IT 

SUGGESTIONS TO TEACHERS 

T is the purpose of this book, through a reading and rereading of its text, through a careful use of the many activities that are suggested, and through collateral reading in the other books that are listed, to give to children of from twelve to fourteen or fifteen years an intelligent idea of the course of American history from 1763 to the present time. It is assumed that the pupils have had the necessary preparation in the European background of American history and in the colonial period, either through the three books in this series which precede this volume or from some other source.

You will observe that the book is divided into twenty-five units. It is desirable that it be used for two years for the seventh and eighth grades; the ground cannot be covered satisfactorily in a single year. If it is so employed, the first twelve units may constitute the first-year course. That will give an average of three weeks' study to each unit; but, since the units are not of equal length or importance, the teacher should allocate the time more flexibly. The first unit, for example, should not detain the class more than a week; but there are units, like that which covers the Revolution, which may easily require four weeks of study. 

The children should read the text of each unit through, twice at least; once at the beginning and once at the end of the time allotted to it. The questions should be used to prove that they have done so intelligently, and that they know the important facts which the unit contains. In this connection it is believed that the outline summaries will be found very useful both to teachers and pupils. They are intended to systematize the contents of the units; to show

iv 

SUGGESTIONS TO TEACHERS 

graphically which are the principal topics treated, and to arrange the subordinate topics in each division in an orderly way. They ought to help the teacher in making clear to the pupils the relations of fact to fact, and they ought to help the children to arrange what they learn in their own minds and so to remember it the better. 

It is through the Activities that the teacher can best display her own directive ability, and the pupils can get a more personal understanding of the events and forces that make up our country's history. In the debates suggested, and the papers and reports to be written, the children should be encouraged to think for themselves and express their own ideas. It may sometimes be necessary for the teacher to correct those ideas; when that is the case it is better to do so by suggestion and persuasion rather than by contradiction. 

Whenever a classroom session is given up to the Activities, it should be made a place for discussion. The effort should be made really to interest the children in what they are studying, so that they will be not only willing, but eager, to talk it over in class, to say what they themselves think on each subject as it comes up, and to reach final conclusions by hearing what each one including the teacher has to say, and then weighing the various opinions in their own minds. That teacher gets the best results who makes herself one of the class and shows that she is not simply telling the children what they ought to think, but is interested in talking over the history of the country with them, listening to their views and impressions and guiding them by her own greater knowledge and experience to a clearer understanding of the meaning of it all.

The pupils can find material for most of the Activities in one or another of the excellent encyclopedias that are available. Two at least are written especially for children; they are included in the school library which is suggested in Ap-

pendix H. They can get still better material from the books listed at the end of each unit if they are within their reach. 

Occasional floor-talks by the teacher are extremely useful even essential. A good many such are suggested among the Activities.

The teacher may easily think of others, and should do so whenever she finds that certain subjects are at all difficult, and require explanation in order to be clearly understood. They should be short and expressed in simple language. They may require careful preparation; for it is not easy to present a subject at once clearly and briefly.

As for the books that are listed for collateral reading, it is too much to expect that they will all be available for any but the most completely equipped school. Some may be found in almost any school library, however; some may be owned by the families of the pupils; and most of them can be found in any fairly good public library. If possible, there should be at hand one of the encyclopedias for children, a good standard history and an economic history like Faulkner's. Get together just as many of the others as you can, either through purchase, borrowing or contribution from the families of the pupils. Encourage the children to read as much as possible outside this textbook. The amount of ground to be covered and the space that is available make it impossible to give more than an outline of our history here. There is an immense amount of interesting reading in the books suggested. Try to show the children that this is so. 

If you can engage their interest in outside reading they will find the course much more delightful, and derive a much more satisfactory idea of the history of their country than any single textbook can give them. 

In the study of American history the things to be chiefly impressed on the pupil are: (1) The nature of our Constitution, a clear understanding

vi 

of its workings, and of the way in which power is divided between the Federal Government and the forty-eight States. 

In this connection trace carefully the story of the differences concerning that division of power which led finally to the 

Civil War. 

(2) The gradual but steady change in the economic structure of the nation, through which a small and scattered colonial population, living almost wholly on the land, became a strong and wealthy people almost as completely industrialized as any of the European nations, and much more so than many of them. (3) The experience of Americans, unknown to any other modern people, of occupying, overspreading and subduing an almost uninhabited continent, with the interesting changes in customs, habits and points of view as the successive generations were pioneers, settled farmers, builders of industry and modern men and women of the twentieth century. (4) The underlying and essential beliefs, ambitions, convictions, and opinions on political, social, and religious subjects which are characteristic of Americans; which make them what they are as a people, different in so many respects from Englishmen or Germans or Frenchmen. Show the children how and why these American characteristics have become fixed. Teach them to be loyal and earnest in allegiance to whatever is admirable in our national character, and to look forward to doing their share to improve it wherever it needs improvement; so that the future of our country may be still more honorable and glorious than its past.

YOU TO THE PUPILS 

YOU are about to read the story of our nation, the 

United States of America. It begins with thirteen separate colonies, often jealous and discordant, which understood at last that they must unite if they were to win and maintain their independence of a mother country that could not realize that they were grown-up children able to govern themselves and entitled to do so. We shall follow the hard road they traveled through bloodshed and suffering to nationhood.

Then we shall see the American people establishing their new government firmly on the shores of the Atlantic and defending it against the interference of older and stronger European powers. That danger over, we shall watch them streaming westward, adding one vast stretch of wilderness after another to their possessions, and filling each new territory with farms and homes, spreading civilization and free institutions over a continent. In the end the narrow strip of land along the Atlantic Coast, where the Nation had been planted, had widened into an empire that reached three thousand miles to the Pacific.

Then we shall read how the United States, in the midst of its growth and its prosperity, was threatened with division and dissolution over the question of human slavery. A great war had to be fought before it was proved that the Union of the States was strong enough to resist any force that would tear it apart. With the emergence of the United

States from the Civil War a nation, united beyond any danger of division, this part of our history comes to an end. 

In the latter half of the book, we shall follow the story of

viii 

TO THE PUPILS 

the nation from the end of the Civil War to the present time. We shall trace the astonishing growth of the country in wealth and population. We shall watch it changing from a nation of farmers, among whom manufacturing was just getting a foothold, into one of the greatest industrial countries of the world. We shall see it becoming a “worldpower," with possessions across the seas, and exercising an influence on mankind of the Twentieth Century equal, at least, to that of any other nation on the globe. What a contrast between the small, weak, disunited colonies of 1763 and the strong, populous, firmly established United States of today!

It is a story filled with the names and the deeds of pioneers and patriots who labored mightily to create the American Nation. It is a story crowded with great events of which we can all be proud — and of some, perhaps of which we cannot; for men are human, and not everything they do is noble. It is the story of the peaceful conquest of a continent and the building of a great nation. 

Through it all run the threads that have always been the characteristic ones in the American fabric; love of freedom, faith in democracy and the common man, and an unresting movement toward better things. I am sure you will find the story absorbing.

INTRODUCTION A REVIEW OF COLONIAL HISTORY 

Bron, the Wenlegi States of America, let us review briefly EFORE we begin to read about the history of our Nathe story of the discovery of America, the planting of the thirteen colonies which later united to form our Nation, and some of the important events which occurred while the Colonies were still subject to the King and Parliament of 

Great Britain.

The Discovery of America 

Four hundred and fifty years ago the existence of the two 

American continents was unknown. Christopher Columbus, a Genoese navigator, sailing in the service of the King and Queen of Spain, first discovered the land of the New World in 1492. He was sailing westward from Spain in the hope of finding a new and more direct way to the shores of Asia. When he came upon the islands of the West Indies, and later the mainland of South and Central America, he thought that it was some part of Asia that he had reached. 

Other explorers followed Columbus, and before long it was proved that a new continent had been discovered. The 

Spaniards were quick to take advantage of their rightful claim to the new lands that their sailors had found. Spanish soldiers and adventurers overran the islands of the West Indies, and then began to invade the mainland as well. 

Cortez conquered Mexico and Pizarro conquered Peru. In both these countries there were Indian people who had reached a considerable degree of civilization. There were also rich gold and silver mines. The Spaniards took possession of these mines. They reduced the natives to the position of slaves, or at least of serfs, and they sent back home such enormous quantities of gold and silver that Spain became the richest country in the world.

INTRODUCTION The French in Canada 

While Spain was building up this rich empire to the southward, North America was for more than a century neglected. The coasts of what are now the United States and 

Canada had been visited and crudely mapped by English and French sailors and explorers by John and Sebastian Cabot, sailing from England, and by Verrazano and Cartier, sailing from France - but no successful attempt to plant. colonies in all this wide region was made until after 1600. 

There seemed to be no evidence of gold or silver there, and no other advantages sufficient to entice Englishmen or 

Frenchmen to go to live in a lonely and savage wilderness.

But as the seventeenth century opened, people in both France and England began to think that it might be worth while to occupy some of this vast New World. The French made settlements on the St. Lawrence River and in other parts of what is now Canada. Samuel de Champlain founded the city of Quebec in 1608; even before that there were Frenchmen in the region we now call Nova Scotia. 

Canada was chiefly valuable to France for its fisheries and its rich fur trade, but some settlers also came over to make farms. Many Jesuit missionaries visited America in the hope of converting the Indians to Christianity.

The Founding of Virginia The English settlements were farther South. King James 

I, on the strength of the discoveries made by the Cabots, claimed for England all the land from what is now Maine to Florida, and he divided it between two commercial companies the London Company and the Plymouth Company - which were formed to make what profit they could out of the resources of the New World. The London 

Company sent out a party of colonists in 1607, which landed in Virginia, and settled at a place on the James River which they called Jamestown in honor of the king.

INTRODUCTION 

3 

JAMESTOWN IN 1622 

(From an early Dutch account of Virginia) This colony had its time of hardship and discouragement, because it was at first made up of "gentlemen" and adventurers, who wasted their time looking for gold that did not exist, and were unable or unwilling to work at raising food on which to live. Fortunately, there was among them a capable leader and sensible man, Captain John Smith, who took charge of affairs at their worst, made the lazy "gentlemen" work, and persuaded the London Company to send out men of a very different kind, farmers and mechanics, who knew how to work and were willing to do so. The colony won through and gradually grew strong. When the 

Virginians found that they could raise tobacco on their land, the success of the experiment was certain; for tobacco was in great demand in Europe, and Virginia became prosperous through its great tobacco plantations. 

Virginia Grows and Prospers 

Tobacco and the river system of Virginia determined the kind of life that the Virginians lived. There are many wide and deep rivers really arms of the sea-emptying into 

Chesapeake Bay. Along these rivers the extensive tobacco plantations grew up. Ships came up to each planter's wharf, took off his tobacco, and left goods that he had or-

INTRODUCTION 

dered from London. There were few towns and no large ones in Virginia; the planters traded directly with the English merchants in London. There were few roads; almost every one used boats to visit his neighbors and friends, sailing up and down the rivers and along the shores of the bay. 

It was hard for the planters to get all the field labor they needed. Not many laborers came out to Virginia except those who were sent out because they had committed some crime, or young orphans who had no one at home to care for them. So the tobacco planters began to buy Negro slaves from Africa, for in those times European sea captains were not ashamed to visit the shores of Africa and buy or seize Negroes to be sold into slavery in the West Indies and the southern parts of America, where the climate was hotter and much more exhausting than that to which white people were accustomed. The first slaves were brought to Virginia in 

1619. As time went on, the practice of keeping slaves spread not only through Virginia, but into Maryland, North and South Carolina, and Georgia as well. There were even some slaves owned in the Northern colonies.

Settlement of Plymouth 

New England was settled in a very different way from Virginia. The Plymouth Company failed in its first efforts to establish commercial colonies in its part of King James's grant (north of the 41st parallel of latitude) and soon lost interest in the undertaking. The actual settlement of New 

England was made by small groups of people whom we call Pilgrims and Puritans. They came to America, not for profit by trade, but to find homes where they could be free from religious and political persecution. The Pilgrims had broken away completely from the Church of England, and refused to worship in the way required by that Church or to recognize the authority of the Church over them. The

INTRODUCTION 

5 Puritans still claimed to belong to the Church of England, but held very different views about religion and forms of worship from those of King James and the bishops. Both 

Pilgrims and Puritans believed in popular government, and objected very strongly to the attempt of King James, and his son King Charles who succeeded him, to rule as absolute 

THE LANDING OF THE PILGRIMS, PLYMOUTH, MASSACHUSETTS, DECEMBER 21, 1620 monarchs, without the help of Parliament. Because of these religious and political differences, both the Pilgrims and the Puritans were in frequent trouble with the Government. The Pilgrims were persecuted to so great an extent that they left England and went to Holland to live. 

There were very few of the Pilgrims; most of them were from one little town in England called Scrooby, but they were very determined to live in their own way and to worship as they thought right. They were not entirely happy in Holland, so in 1620, they secured permission from the successors of the old Plymouth Company, and set sail in

INTRODUCTION 

a vessel called the Mayflower and finally landed on the shores of Massachusetts Bay, at a place they named Plymouth. They faced terrible hardships at first; half of the company died the first year. The survivors remained, however. They built a village and raised crops, fished and shot game, made a living somehow, and as their settlement throve, more Englishmen and women came to join them. 

William Bradford, William Brewster, and Miles Standish were the leaders of the Colony.

The Puritan Immigration to Massachusetts The Puritan immigration came some ten years later, when the quarrel between King Charles and those who hated his ideas of government and religion had become unendurable. This was a more important migration than that to 

Plymouth. A thousand people came over the first year under the leadership of John Winthrop. Many other thousands came between 1630 (when Winthrop landed) and 1640. These people settled a little farther north, in Boston and Salem and along the coast as far as the Merrimac River. 

The Puritan colony of Massachusetts was successful from the first. Many of the settlers were well-to-do. They were all industrious, thrifty, and very much in earnest about making permanent homes. They built substantial little villages, each with its church in the center. They worked hard on their farms, and made money by catching and salting fish. As time went on, they built vessels and traded with the other colonies and the West India islands. 

Other New England Colonies 

The other New England colonies were largely offshoots of the Massachusetts settlements. Rhode Island was settled by people who disagreed with the Massachusetts Puritans about liberty of conscience. The Rhode Islanders insisted

INTRODUCTION 

7 on the right of every man to believe what seemed right to him in matters of religion, while the Massachusetts Puritans wanted nobody among them except those who agreed with them in religious matters. Roger Williams was the leader of the people who founded Rhode Island. 

Connecticut was settled partly by Massachusetts men and women who thought the leaders in Boston and Salem were too strict in letting no one but church members vote as citizens, and partly by an independent immigration from England. Thomas Hooker was the leader of the first group, which settled at and near Hartford in 1636. The other group settled at New Haven. For some years the two colonies were independent, but in 1662 they were united. 

New Hampshire was settled chiefly by an overflow from Massachusetts, though there were some who came directly from England.

The New Englanders, as you will see, did not entirely agree among themselves, but they were all much alike, -hard-working, thrifty, deeply interested in religion, and determined to govern themselves in their own way. 

How New York Began 

New York was settled by Dutchmen, following the exploration of the Hudson River by Henry Hudson, who sailed in the employ of the Dutch West India Company. His voyage occurred in 1609. A few years later, there were 

Dutch trading-posts at New Amsterdam (now New York) 

and Fort Orange (now Albany). By 1623, the fur trade had attracted so many settlers that a governor was sent over and the colony of New Netherland was established; it was controlled, however, not by the nation of Holland, but by the West India Company. Except the port of 

New Amsterdam, which profited by its excellent harbor, the colony grew rather slowly, though farms existed along the shores of the Hudson, all the way to Albany.

INTRODUCTION 

England never admitted the right of the Dutch to settle in this region. For a long time, however, England was too much absorbed in its own troubles at home, where King and Parliament were fighting out their quarrel in a civil war, to bother with the little Dutch colony in America. In 1664, England did send a fleet into New Amsterdam harbor, and the Dutch were not able to resist. The city and colony were surrendered to England and renamed New York. 

Courtesy, Title Guarantee and Trust Co., N.Y. 

THE SURRENDER OF NEW AMSTERDAM 

The meeting of the English and the Dutch commissioners took place at Peter Stuyvesant's country home

The Proprietary Colonies 

Maryland was settled in 1634. The region was granted by King Charles to a nobleman named Lord Baltimore, and he sold or granted the land to settlers who wished to establish themselves there. Lord Baltimore was a Catholic and so were many of the early settlers, but the colony was distinguished for a generous toleration in religious matters. 

Social life in Maryland was much like that in Virginia; there were many large plantations, but few towns until far into the eighteenth century, and slavery existed there.

INTRODUCTION 

9 

Pennsylvania was "granted" to William Penn in 1682.

Penn was a Quaker, and a very wise and tolerant man. He made excellent laws for his colony and granted land on generous terms. Settlers came in large numbers, and 

Philadelphia, which Penn founded, speedily grew to be the largest town in all the colonies. The population of Pennsylvania was increased by a considerable immigration of Germans, who settled in the rich farming country west and southwest of Philadelphia, and by an equally large immigration of people from the north of Ireland — Scotch-Irish, they were called — who went still farther west into the hill country of the frontier.

Delaware, originally settled by a company of Swedes in 1638, and later occupied by the Dutch from New Amsterdam, fell into the hands of the English when New Netherland was surrendered to them. For a time it was part of 

Penn's province of Pennsylvania, but later was set apart as a separate colony.

New Jersey was at first part of the Dutch colony of New Netherland. After 1664 it was granted by King Charles II to his brother, the Duke of York, who subsequently made it over to two noblemen who were his friends. It was settled by people from Connecticut dissatisfied with the union of the two colonies at Hartford and New Haven, by Scotch- 

Irish immigrants, and others from England.

The Southern Colonies 

South of Virginia there were three colonies, North Carolina, South Carolina, and Georgia. The two Carolinas were originally "proprietary" colonies like Maryland and 

Pennsylvania. King Charles II granted the land to eight noblemen who sent over several companies of settlers, the first in 1670, when Charleston was founded. In North

Carolina, tobacco was the chief crop raised. In South

ΙΟ 

INTRODUCTION 

Carolina there were large rice and indigo plantations. 

Slavery existed in both colonies. The earliest settlers were 

English, but in later years many Scotch-Irish and a few Germans found their way to the Carolinas. When the 

Huguenots (French Protestants) were driven out of France, in 1689, some of them came to South Carolina to live. In 

1729, the descendants of the original proprietors found the management of their colonies so difficult and unprofitable that they made their rights over to the King and the 

Carolinas became "crown colonies."

Georgia was the last of the thirteen colonies to be settled. 

It dates only from 1732 when General Oglethorpe persuaded King George II to grant him the land south and west of South Carolina. On this land he planted a colony of 

Englishmen, many of whom were men who, under the cruel laws of the time, had been imprisoned for debt. The colony was still a small one when the Revolution occurred. 

Life in the Colonies 

You will see from what you have read that the thirteen

American colonies differed a good deal from one another, though they all owed allegiance to the British Crown. A majority of the colonists were of English descent, but there were also many people from Scotland, Ireland, Germany, Holland, France, and Sweden among them, especially in 

New York, Pennsylvania, Delaware, and the Carolinas.

Of course their customs and ways of life were unlike those of the English settlers of Virginia, New England, and Maryland. There were differences among these English folk, too, for the Puritans of Massachusetts and Connecticut, with their strict ideas about religion and social life, were by no means like the more easy-going planters of the South. 

The life among the wide plantations of Virginia and the Carolinas, tilled by Negro slaves, was very different from

INTRODUCTION 

II that in the villages and the small hillside farms of New England. In Virginia, the tendency was toward an aristocracy of wealthy planters. New England was socially and politically more democratic. In New York, there were a few "patroons" who owned great estates cultivated by tenant farmers, but small farmers and mechanics and tradesmen were numerous. In many ways the middle colonies were intermediate between the two other sections — the South and New England. The people were less interested in religious and political doctrines than those of the Puritan colonies; they were perhaps less thrifty, and they were certainly more tolerant of all sorts of ideas; but they were more democratic, more industrious, and more commercially minded than the people of the South.

Those who lived on the frontier, however, whether in 

New England, New York, Pennsylvania, Virginia, or the

Carolinas, were much alike— rough, hardy folk, accustomed to hardship and limited means, toughened by a continual struggle with Nature in the wilderness and by border warfare with Indian enemies. 

How the Colonies were Governed

The colonies were at first unlike in their political organization. Some were crown colonies with governors appointed by the King — Virginia and New York, for examples. 

Some were proprietary colonies Maryland, Pennsylvania, and the Carolinas — governed by their proprietors or by governors selected by them. Those in New England were self-governing under charters granted by the King. 

This difference existed down to the time of the Revolution, though Massachusetts, New Hampshire, and the Carolinas eventually became crown colonies, with governors appointed by the King.

England, as you know, was the European country where

INTRODUCTION 

the right of the people to take part in their own government, through representative assemblies - legislature or parliament was most firmly established. So the American colonies, whose inhabitants were largely, and in some cases almost wholly, English, were determined to assert that right. Virginia had an assembly — the House of Burgesses — in 1619, and Maryland had one before 1640. 

All the New England colonies had legislatures from the beginning of their settlement, and more than that, each little town governed itself through the "town meeting," at which all citizens had the right to vote. William Penn granted an assembly to his colony of Pennsylvania when it was founded. New York was less fortunate; it was not until 

1683 that the persistent demand of the people at last obliged King Charles II to permit a legislature to be elected. 

But long before the days of the Revolution, all the colonies had assemblies through which the people took an important part in making the laws which they were to obey. 

The Indian Wars; Virginia

Life in the colonies in the early days was hard and laborious. The clearing of the great forests that covered most of the land and the breaking-up of the rough soil into cultivated farms was the hardest kind of work. In addition, the settlers in many cases were in peril from savage enemies the Indians. In the beginning the Indians were not unfriendly and sometimes they were glad to help the newcomers and to trade with them. As time went on, however, and more and more white men appeared, the Indians grew suspicious and fearful lest the settlers should take all their hunting grounds from them and drive them west across the mountains.

The first serious Indian war in Virginia occurred in 1622; the settlers were already so numerous and so much better 1

INTRODUCTION 

13 armed that they defeated the savages rather easily. Again, in 1675, the Indians rose against the white men. This time the fight was harder, and it led to a revolt of some of the Virginians against the governor, Berkeley, who, they thought, was not active enough against the Indians. This was called Bacon's Rebellion, from the name of its leader, and it was the first case of open resistance to a representative of English authority in any of the colonies. The 

See B

AN INDIAN WAR DANCE The Indian Wars in New England and New York 

Connecticut had a war on its hands as soon as it was settled. The Pequots, a fierce and stubborn tribe of Indians, resented the appearance of the white men in their country. They raided several of the new settlements, and killed a number of men and women. With the help of a tribe of Indians that hated the Pequots, the Connecticut settlers finally defeated the Pequots and almost exterminated them. This was in 1637.

INTRODUCTION 

Some forty years later, New England was again the scene of bloody fighting in King Philip's War. The Indians of all southern New England were on the warpath. They attacked twenty towns and destroyed many of them. The colonists had to unite to form an army of more than a thousand men before they could make headway against the savages. In the end the colonists crushed the red men and killed King Philip, their leader.

New York, too, had its Indian war-in 1641. It was brought on by the blundering cruelty of Governor Kieft, who had a number of innocent red men executed in revenge for the murder of a white man. The Indians were furious. 

For a time there was danger that the Dutch colony would be wholly wiped out. After many settlers had been killed, however, the colonists raised a force strong enough to surround and crush their enemies. Almost all the colonies had to fight the Indians at one time or another. In Pennsylvania, alone, were the savages always friendly. That was because William Penn treated them kindly and justly. 

The Conquest of French Canada 

The colonists were able to get the better of the Indians, but they faced a more dangerous enemy in the French province of Canada to the northward. During the colonial period, when France and England were often at war, the colonists of the two nations fell to fighting in America. 

Between 1689 and 1760, there were no less than four such wars. On this side of the water they were called King 

William's War (1690-1697), Queen Anne's War (17011713), King George's War (1744-1748), and the French and 

Indian War (1754-1759). 

The first three saw little but frontier fighting — raids, massacres, and battles between small bodies of men. The 

French made allies of several Indian tribes, and the savages

INTRODUCTION 

15 brought terror and death with torch and tomahawk to many a frontier settlement. New England and New York, being next to Canada, suffered the most in these bloody little wars, none of which led to any decisive result. 

About 1750, however, the other colonies began to be affected. The French were moving down the Ohio and 

Mississippi Rivers, getting in the rear of Pennsylvania and Virginia, and threatening to seize the whole interior of the continent. It became apparent that the future of America was at stake. The British Government sent over several thousand troops. One expedition, under General Braddock, was ordered to force its way through the forests to the present site of Pittsburgh, where the French had built a fort. It was ambushed and cut to pieces by the French and 

Indians (1755).

Other victories were won by the French, and matters looked gloomy for the British. Then a great statesman, William Pitt, was called to take charge of the British Government. He sent over more soldiers under better generals, the colonists raised armies of their own, and a fourfold campaign against the French was undertaken. This time everything went well. The British took one French fortress after another. In 1759, an army under General 

Wolfe, protected by the British fleet, sailed up the St. 

Lawrence River and attacked Quebec, the capital of Canada. In the face of great difficulties, Wolfe got his men up the bluffs that guarded the city, and Montcalm, the French commander, had to come out and fight him. It was a great victory for Wolfe, though both he and Montcalm were mortally wounded. The French army fled, Quebec was surrendered, and all Canada, as well as the entire continent, as far west as the Mississippi became British territory.

We shall now learn how these British colonies developed into the United States of America.

INTRODUCTION Further Study 

This brief outline of colonial history is intended to recall the principal incidents in a story that has usually been previously studied. In cases where the detailed study of this period has been omitted, this review will serve to give pupils some knowledge of the background against which the history of the United States must be viewed. It should be supplemented, however, by some reading in other books which treat the subject of colonial history more fully. A few such books are suggested below.

UNIT I HOW EUROPEANS IN AMERICA BECAME AMERICANS 

YOU have already learned how our country was discov- Yord ered by European sailors, and how it came to be settled by men and women from England and Scotland and Ireland, from Germany and France and Holland. You have read about the founding and the growth of the thirteen colonies that stretched from New England in the North to Georgia in the South. You have seen these colonies, most of them established by Englishmen, finally all become English possessions. Finally you have read how England, with the help of her colonies, drove the French out of Canada, which they had owned for one hundred and fifty years, so that every part of North America east of the Mississippi,

Florida alone excepted, was counted a part of the British Empire.

You are now to learn about the way in which England lost the greater part of these possessions, which came in time to form the United States. First of all, in this unit, let us see what sort of people the colonists were, in 1763; how they differed from the people of their own blood in Europe, and what causes and influences had brought about the change. 

Results of the French and Indian War

In the year 1763, England and France made a treaty at Paris that brought to an end the long struggle between the two nations for the control of North America, east of the

HOW EUROPEANS BECAME AMERICANS 

Mississippi River. England was victorious. By the terms of the Treaty at Paris, France gave up all her great possessions, and her still greater claims, on our continent. 

The British Empire stretched over half of what is now the United States and all of Canada besides. 

To the English statesmen the future seemed secure. 

They thought North America was safely English forever.

Yet within a dozen years their American colonies were in revolt; and in twenty years England had lost every part of 

America that was originally settled by Englishmen. She kept only Canada, and the Maritime Provinces, all of which had been settled by the French, and ruled by French kings for more than a hundred years. Let us see how it happened that England lost this great territory and how the United 

States came to be born.

Character of the Early Settlers 

The people who lived in the American colonies were of European stock. Most of them were English in blood, though there were also among them people of Scotch, Irish, German, Dutch, and French descent. They had lived long enough under the strange new conditions of the American wilderness to have become different in many respects from their cousins who had stayed at home on the other side of the Atlantic. The early settlers were in a way an unusual people. They had physical hardihood and great powers of endurance. Only those who had such qualities would venture across the seas in those days. Only such men and women could survive the labors and hardships of life in the forests and wildernesses of the new country. 

Freedom of Thought in America 

A great many of the early settlers were also people who thought for themselves and were determined to break away

HOW EUROPEANS BECAME AMERICANS 19 

PILGRIMS GOING TO CHURCH 

(From a painting by G. H. Boughton) from conditions in Europe of which they did not approve. 

They had ideas of their own in both religion and politics. 

They wanted to live where they could express those ideas without interference from the English Government. The freedom of each man to think as he would, and the freedom of each colony to manage its own affairs in its own way, came to be ideals for which the Americans were ready to fight and die.

The colonists believed in education, too. There were not so many learned men among them as there were in Europe, which was natural enough in a new country, still rough in many ways; but there were some schools for the common people, and the ordinary man had about as good an education as he would have been able to get in Europe. 

Moral Qualities of the Colonists The labor of clearing the forests, and of making farms and building towns in the wilderness, brought out certain valuable qualities among the colonists. The necessity of facing hardship and privation, and the frequent danger of warfare

HOW EUROPEANS BECAME AMERICANS 

with savage Indian foes developed these qualities still further. They were courage, perseverance, determination, patience, and faith in God. The men and women who founded our American colonies were sturdy and brave.

They were not afraid of the hardest of hard work, and they did not complain at conditions that we should find almost unbearable.

It is hard for us to put ourselves in the place of these American pioneers, and to realize the kind of life they led. 

Poor in everything but their strong muscles and their stout hearts, they labored year after year, laying the foundations on which our nation rests today. They sacrificed the ease and comfort of their old homes to clear the wilderness and establish new homes where they might live in freedom. Had it not been for the rough hard work of these pioneers our present wealth and civilization, and our religious and political liberty would not have been possible. 

What the Pioneers Learned from the Indians

The Americans of the eighteenth century were different from the Europeans, as we have said, because they grew up under such very different conditions. The continual struggle with nature in a new country bred a kind of people quite unlike those who lived in lands where the fields had been cultivated for a thousand years and where there were cities hundreds of years old. They were rougher in their manners, perhaps, but they were superior in self-reliance, in ingenuity, and in physical strength.

The presence of the Indians among the colonists made a difference, too. In constant contact with the savages, sometimes as friends but oftener as enemies, the colonists could not help being influenced by them. They learned some things from the Indians — particularly their method of forest fighting, which they afterward employed so effec-

HOW EUROPEANS BECAME AMERICANS 21 

tively against the unprepared British soldiers in the Revolution. They learned from them something about hunting and living in the woods also, and about the raising of the simple crops grown by the Indians. The Iroquois were the strongest and most clever of all the Indian tribes. They were brave though cruel in war and they had more idea of

A BLOCKHOUSE OF THE COLONISTS 

(Built near the junction of the Kennebec and 

Sebasticook Rivers, Maine) uniting to form a nation than any of the other red men. They lived in the State of New York, often in close association with the 

English colonists. Fortunately they were usually friendly; but the Americans, who had to deal continually with these savages, were different in their ideas and their practices than they would have been if there had been no red men about.

Americans a Changed People 

The long border warfare, especially with the French, had its effect on the American character, too. For nearly seventy years there was constant fighting or danger of fighting with the French and their Indian allies. It was a kind of fighting unlike any in an old and civilized country, and it developed in the colonists a kind of courage and shrewdness that was new to people of their blood. 

Finally the distance from Europe, and the length and hardships of a voyage thither kept the colonists remote from the mother country. They often did not know what

HOW EUROPEANS BECAME AMERICANS 

was going on there; visitors from abroad were infrequent, visitors to Europe still more rare. The old continent went its own way, and so did the new. Manners, customs, ideas grew more and more unlike on opposite sides of the Atlantic. Before the time of the Revolution the American had become very different from the European. 

Long Neglect of the Colonies by England Naturally, the Americans began to develop ideas and institutions of their own. They were better able to do this, because the English Government for many years neglected the colonies and allowed the Americans to conduct their affairs about as they pleased. In the beginning the colonies were founded, not by the Government at all, but by private citizens who had their reasons, commercial, political, or religious, for wanting to come to America.

For a long time England was so much occupied with her own civil wars between the Stuart Kings and the Puritan Parliament that she had no energy left for the management of colonies three thousand miles away across the sea. Then followed wars with other European nations, particularly with France and Spain, and so England was unable to give the colonies the attention they deserved.

Moreover, only a few Englishmen realized the importance of their American possessions. Because the population was small, and the country was in large part a wilderness of forest, they thought it did not matter much what happened there. Every now and then one of the kings would interest himself enough to take away some of the privileges the colonists had enjoyed during the time when their affairs were quite neglected at London; or he would interfere with the laws the colonial legislatures had made for their own people. But these attempts never went very far, and never grew into a settled policy, until after 1760.

HOW EUROPEANS BECAME AMERICANS 23 

Self-Sufficiency of the Colonists

Meanwhile the colonists had learned to take care of themselves very well. They had managed to hold their own. against their French and Indian enemies. When an expedition of New England colonials actually took the strong TUNG 

AN EARLY SETTLER'S HOUSE 

French fortress at Louisburg, in 1744, they began to feel that they might become able to defend themselves without much help from England.

They built up a very thriving trade of their own by sea, particularly with the West Indies. Though the British 

Government tried to put a stop to that, so that the profit of all such business might go to English shipowners, the colonists were usually able to disobey the laws with impunity, and kept on exchanging their fish and lumber and hoops and staves, for the sugar and molasses of the West Indies. In spite of English neglect, and later of English interference, they grew increasingly wealthy and self-confident.

HOW EUROPEANS BECAME AMERICANS The "Rights of Englishmen" 

All this time, however, they thought of themselves as Englishmen, entitled to all the political rights that the English nation had won through centuries of struggle. 

Those rights were, first of all, that of managing their local affairs in their own way, and that of having to pay only such taxes as were voted by their own representatives. 

They never ceased to insist on those rights, and it was the attempt of the English Parliament to deprive them of them which led in the end to the Revolution. 

At the same time there grew up in America very strong opinions about the freedom of speech and of the press. The colonists stood firmly for the right of every man to say what he pleased about the Government and about the public officials. They sometimes got into serious trouble with the royal governors through exercising that right, but they never admitted that they were in the wrong. Freedom of speech is still, as it always has been, a principle to which all 

Americans are devoted. 

England and America Grow Apart Few

In addition to their habit of independence and their love of freedom, the colonists found a cause for a growing lack of sympathy with their fellow subjects in England, in the failure of each side to understand the other. Crossing the ocean was a serious undertaking in the eighteenth century. 

Few of the colonists in 1760 had ever seen England. 

Englishmen had ever been to America. There were almost no books or newspapers to explain the affairs or the ideas of the people on our side of the Atlantic to those on the other. 

So, when differences arose, the English people easily came to think of the Americans as stubborn and selfish; while the

Americans were sure that the English were grasping and tyrannical.

HOW EUROPEANS BECAME AMERICANS 25 

For many years the mother country and its colonies continued to grow farther and farther apart; though it was not until after the final defeat of the French had put an end to the need that each should stand firmly by the other, that people began to realize how great the difference had become.

UNIT II STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 

TOW that we have made the acquaintance of our NOW colonial forefathers, we shall learn of the causes that led them to quarrel with England, their mother country, and in the end to declare and win their independence. 

You will see that the trouble arose over the way in which the English King and Parliament wished to govern them. 

The quarrel might have been avoided if there had been more wisdom and a better understanding of human nature among the rulers of England; but wisdom and tolerance have never been too common among kings and statesmen anywhere. We shall follow the story of one unpopular and often unjust law after another, and of the rising spirit of protest and indignation among the colonists, until finally they took up arms to resist the Government that they felt was oppressing them.

King George III 

The King of England in 1763 was George III. He was a man who believed thoroughly that God had given to kings the right to rule their people as they thought best. He was honest and industrious, but stubborn and rather dull.

He found himself suddenly at the head of a greater empire than any British king had ever ruled; for by the same war which drove the French out of North America, England had won for herself the control of the vast domain of India in Asia. King George had many plans for making this great empire profitable to England, and in most of them the British Parliament was ready to agree.

STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 29 The Mercantile Theory 

The first thing to be done was to make the colonies more profitable to the mother country, and to govern them from London instead of letting them govern themselves as they pleased. In those days nearly every one in Europe believed in the mercantile theory of trade. According to that theory, colonies existed, not for their own growth and prosperity, but simply for the benefit of the mother country. It was thought right, therefore, to make laws that would compel the trade and industry of the colonies to pay tribute to that of the nation which owned them. Spain used its colonies only to get gold and silver and tropical products out of them.

France used Canada merely as a source of wealth from the fur trade. England had been more liberal, but even England had 

KING GEORGE III 

made laws forbidding its colonists to sell any of their produce except to Englishmen or to buy anything that had not been brought across the sea in English ships. 

The Colonies Must Pay

Now at last the King and Parliament began to realize how prosperous the colonies were likely to become. Accordingly it was determined in London to enforce these laws more strictly than ever, in order to make sure that all the valuable trade with America should be monopolized by England. 

Furthermore, the King and Parliament thought that the colonies ought to pay taxes in return for the military protection they got from the mother country, and to help pay

STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 

the costs of the last great war with France. The war had been very expensive. England found herself burdened with a very heavy debt, a part of which had been contracted to pay for the armies that had fought in America. Why should not the colonies bear their share? King George thought they should, and so did Parliament. 

To be sure, the colonies had paid something. They had raised troops, and their legislatures had granted some money to help pay for the war. But they had not raised as much as the King thought they should; and they had often been slow and grudging about it. If there was to be a great and strong British Empire, it must be able to collect the money it needed promptly and in sufficient quantity. It would not do to wait till the colonies were willing to offer money, and to take only what the colonial legislatures saw fit to give. 

So King George and Parliament reasoned; and they determined to establish a new order of things at once. 

King George's New Laws

First of all the laws governing industry and trade were made more strict, and the British officials in America were told to be more careful to enforce them. The Navigation 

Act, which forbade direct trade with other countries than England, and the importation of goods in other than English ships was to be more strictly enforced. The colonists were also forbidden to engage in any kind of manufacture that would compete with similar industries in England. 

Pig iron, for example, could be made in the colonies, but the iron had to be sent to England to be made into useful articles. Even the making of hats in the colonies was referred to as "an evil practice."

At the same time the Government granted bounties to those who raised products that were much needed in England, such as hemp, flax, lumber, and indigo. It was said

STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 31 

that these bounties made up for the restrictions that were put on American trade in general, but the colonists never admitted that this was so. 

Next, higher duties were laid on most of the articles imported into the colonies, and this money went to the British Government in London. Last of all, it was provided that a standing army of ten thousand men should always be kept in America. Parliament gave as a reason for this army the need of defending the Western frontier; but few of the soldiers ever left the cities on the coast. The colonists believed, and with reason, that they had been sent over merely to help in enforcing the unpopular trade and navigation laws.

The Stamp Act AMERICA 

To raise the money necessary for maintaining this army of ten thousand men, Parliament passed the Stamp Act in the spring of 1765. According to this law every newspaper, pamphlet, bill of merchandise, and legal document must bear a government stamp. These stamps cost from as little as one cent to as much as fifty dollars, according to the importance of the paper on which they were placed. SHILLI 

A STAMP 

The stamps were not like postage stamps but impressed on paper with a seal. 

The black patch is a fastener of tin foil inserted through slits in the stamp and in the paper to which it was

Almost every one was affected by this form of tax; the merchants, lawyers, printed bankers, and publishers were especially hard hit. From the first the law was thoroughly unpopular, and protests rained in upon Parliament. The colonists pointed attached. out that they had no representatives in Parliament, and that it had always been a right of Englishmen to be taxed only by bodies in which they were represented. King George was very angry at these protests.

STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 

He called them disobedient and lawless, and said that he would crush any resistance that was offered to the Stamp Act. 

William Pitt and Patrick Henry

William Pitt (now become Lord Chatham), the great statesman who had led England in its war with France, warned the King and Parliament that the Americans were only standing out for their rights as English subjects, and that harshness and violence were dangerous methods to try with Englishmen either at home or abroad; but the stubborn King would not listen.

He was not prepared, however, for the storm of opposition the Stamp Act provoked. On the day the Act went into effect, bells were tolled as if for a funeral, and public meetings to protest against the “assassination of liberty” were held all over the colonies. Merchants agreed to import no more goods from England till the act was repealed. Societies called the Sons of Liberty and the Daughters of Liberty were formed, the members of which took a pledge to buy no 

British-made articles.

The colonial legislatures nearly all passed resolutions condemning the Stamp Act as a measure of tyranny. In the 

Virginia House of Burgesses, Patrick Henry, an eloquent young lawyer, made a speech that became famous. He warned King George that tyrants always met their punishment from an outraged people. "Cæsar," he cried, "had his Brutus, Charles the First his Cromwell, and George the 

Third may profit by their example." There were cries of "Treason! treason!" from other members of the house, but 

Henry concluded fearlessly: "If this be treason, make the most of it!"

The Stamp Act Congress 

In October, 1765, there was held a Stamp Act Congress in

STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 

33 

New York. Nine colonies were represented. Resolutions were passed declaring the loyalty of Americans to the King, but adding that none but their own representatives should be permitted to lay taxes on them. When these resolutions were laid before Parliament, William Pitt cried out that he rejoiced over the resistance of the Americans; that they would have been fit only for slaves if they had not resisted.

To quiet their protests the Government did at last repeal the Stamp Act. This was the occasion for general rejoicing in 

America. The colonists had at last learned that they had real interests in common, and that by united action they

STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 

could accomplish what no one colony by itself could. Another step, and a long one, had been taken toward union and independence.

The Writs of Assistance 

Everywhere, during these years between 1760 and 1770, the colonists found cause to carry on the struggle for what they held to be their rights as British subjects. In the attempt to enforce more strictly the Navigation Act, which was continually violated, especially by the traders and sea captains of New England, Parliament provided for what were called "writs of assistance."

These writs gave the customs officers the right to enter and search any house on mere suspicion that it might contain smuggled goods. Armed with them the King's officers broke into shops and dwellings on every hand, and people who were only suspected of smuggling were often arrested and held in jail, even when no evidence could be found against them.

A Boston lawyer, James Otis by name, made a very hard fight against the issuance of these writs, and in an eloquent speech pointed out how they violated an old principle of English liberty that no man's house could be entered or searched save by special warrant based on actual information of crime. He did not convince the King's judges, but his speech was sent all over the colonies and made a deep impression. 

Unrest in Virginia

In Virginia there was trouble also, when the King set aside a law passed by the legislature. According to the old arrangement the ministers of the Church of England in Virginia were paid in tobacco-sixteen hundred pounds every year. The legislature passed a law that they should be paid

STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 35 

in money at the rate of twopence for each pound of tobacco to which they were entitled. more than that, and the clergymen protested to 

England. Thereupon 

King George set aside the law and ordered the ministers to be paid according to the market price of tobacco. 

This was really fair enough, but the people of Virginia were very angry, nevertheless, at the royal interference. 

When one of the ministers brought suit for the money owed himthe case was called the 

Parsons' Cause - Pat- 

Tobacco was really worth 

PATRICK HENRY 

rick Henry appeared in opposition, and made a fiery speech which excited the people still more, and gave occasion for the judge to accuse him of speaking treason. 

Massachusetts in Revolt

Having repealed the Stamp Act, Parliament looked about for other means of raising money. It put heavy taxes on almost everything that was imported into the colonies, and provided that the army in America and all the King's officers should be paid from the money so raised. 

Again the colonists were in revolt. Their legislatures voted indignant resolutions of protest, and the Massachusetts legislature sent out a letter inviting all the colonies to unite to oppose the new taxes. The customs officers were

STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 

resisted openly, and smuggling, which was always going on in defiance of the Navigation Act, grew bolder than ever.

Massachusetts was the leading colony in resisting the unpopular laws, and King George lost his temper completely over the "rebelliousness" of Boston. The British soldiers and the citizens of Boston were openly hostile. 

A party of officers assaulted James Otis on the street and beat him so severely that for a time he became insane.

Later, on March 5, 1770, there was a clash between the redcoats and a crowd of colonists, during which the soldiers fired on the citizens and killed four of them. This was called the "Boston Massacre," and it roused excitement and indignation all over the country. 

SAMUEL ADAMS I Committees of Correspondence 

Samuel Adams, clerk of the 

Massachusetts legislature, had the idea of forming committees of correspondence, first in the towns of Massachusetts, and then in those of the other colonies. These committees sent word to one another of of re- what was going on and of what was planned by way sistance to the King's officers. They were one of the most 

I Samuel Adams was born in Boston in 1722. He was a cousin of John 

Adams, later President. He was tax collector of Boston and had unequalled influence over the voters in the town meeting. He has been called the "Man of the Town Meeting" and the "Father of the American Revolution." He was then elected to the Massachusetts Legislature, and as its clerk drew up its addresses to the king. He was a member of the Continental Congress and later lieutenant governor and governor of Massachusetts.

important means of bringing about mutual understanding and coöperation among the colonists. 

The Tea Tax and Tea Parties 

In the end Parliament had to repeal these special taxes, too; for the col- onists stopped buying everything that they could possibly get along without; and the English merchants were again so loud in their complaints that Parliament had to listen to them. One tax 

Courtesy of D. Appleton & Co.

THE BOSTON TEA PARTY 

only was retained, that on tea, in order to maintain the principle of Parliament's right to tax the colonists. 

Of course the colonists were equally determined to defend their principle, that Parliament had no such right. They would buy no tea at all, and when the King sent several shiploads of it across the Atlantic to be sold at a temptingly low price, they would not even let it be offered for sale. In 

New York, Philadelphia, and Charleston the merchants to whom the tea was consigned refused to accept it, and it was either taken back to England or allowed to rot in the warehouse.

At Boston the situation was more difficult. The dealers insisted on their right to receive the tea, and the Governor ordered that it be unloaded. The patriots, as they began to call themselves, were ready for this emergency, however.

While a town meeting was being held in the Old South

STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 

Church, with Samuel Adams directing it, an Indian war whoop was heard in the street outside. 

Fifty men dressed as Indians rushed past the meetinghouse, and down a near-by street to the wharf where the tea ships lay. They swarmed aboard the ships, broke open the chests, and emptied the tea into the harbor, while thousands of their fellow townsmen watched them from the shore. Some $90,000 worth of tea was thus destroyed. 

This was the famous Boston Tea Party, the boldest defiance that had yet been offered to the King and the Government of Great Britain. The encouragement it gave to rebellious colonists elsewhere is shown by the fact that shortly afterward a tea ship was burned in Annapolis, Maryland, and a cargo of tea was thrown into the waters of Charleston Harbor.

The Intolerable. Acts 

The reply of the British Parliament to the Boston Tea Party was a series of new laws so objectionable to the colonists that they called them the Intolerable Acts. One of these was the Boston Port Bill, which closed the port of Boston against all trade until the spilled tea should be paid for.

Another limited the times of meeting and the powers of town meetings in Massachusetts, and provided that all officers of the colony should be appointed by the royal governor instead of being elected by the people. 

A third obliged the citizens of Massachusetts to take the King's troops into their houses and board them for nothing when they were required to do so.

The last the Quebec Act added the whole country west of the mountains and north of the Ohio River to the 

Province of Quebec. Settlers from the other colonies were forbidden to enter this vast region. It was to be kept a virgin forest for the benefit of the fur trade.

STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 39 First Continental Congress 

These laws convinced the colonists that they could not hope for fair treatment from either King George or Parliament, and that if they meant to preserve their rights as Englishmen, they must unite to offer forcible resistance. 

The committees of correspondence took counsel together, and agreed to call a Conti- nental Congress, to meet in 

Philadelphia in September, 1774. To this Congress came many of the leading men of the colonies - John and Samuel Adams from 

Massachusetts; George 

Washington, Richard Henry 

Lee, and Patrick Henry from 

Virginia; John Jay and 

Robert R. Livingston from 

New York; Roger Sherman from Connecticut, among others. 

Benjamin Franklin, then the most famous Ameri 

BENJAMIN FRANKLIN 

can, could not be present. He was in London, acting as agent there for all the colonies, and trying in vain to make the King and his ministers understand the principles for which the colonists were contending. 

The Congress drew up a Declaration of Rights and Grievances, which they sent to King George and Parliament. 

The declaration insisted that only the colonial legislatures should vote taxes which the colonists must pay, and demanded that Parliament repeal no less than eleven laws which were contrary to what the Congress declared to be the rights of the people of America. The Congress also

STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 

voted to recommend that Americans should buy no goods whatever from England until those laws were repealed. 

More Acts of Repression 

The proceedings of the Congress roused a fresh spirit of liberty throughout the country. In England, too, the Concord Lexington Route of British

BOSTON AND VICINITY, 1775 

Declaration of Rights made a deep impression. Pitt (now become Lord Chatham) and the great orator, Edmund

Burke, both praised it, and declared that the Americans were really upholding the old rights of the English people which were in danger from a tyrannical and stubborn King. 

But King George himself only grew more angry. News came that in Massachusetts the people had refused to obey the officers appointed by the royal governor. They would not pay taxes to them, or sit on juries in the courts. Many of the men appointed as councilors or judges or sheriffs by Governor Gage had to resign because the people made their

STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 

4I positions so unpleasant. One man, a respected citizen of 

Plymouth, who had been appointed a councilor, gave up his office because when he entered the church on Sunday everybody else got up and walked out. 

The King called this flat rebellion - as indeed it wasand the majority of Parliament agreed with him in spite of the eloquence of Chatham and Burke. More soldiers were sent to Boston, and it was announced that Massachusetts was to be crushed into obedience.

The Fights at Lexington and Concord 

The colonists on their part prepared to defend themselves, and began to collect guns, powder, and ammunition with which to resist the crushing process. On April 19, 1775, the clash which had long seemed inevitable occurred. General Gage sent out a force of soldiers with orders to arrest 

Samuel Adams and John Hancock, two patriot leaders who were known to be in the village of Lexington. Having seized these men, the soldiers were to press on to Concord and destroy a store of powder that was collected there. 

The march was conducted in the night with great secrecy; but the patriots found out about it, and two of them, Paul Revere and William Dawes, set out to ride about the country and call out the minute men, as the local militia were called.

When the British got to Lexington, they found that Adams and Hancock had been warned, and had disappeared. There were some armed militia awaiting them, however, and shots were exchanged. The colonists were too few to offer long resistance, and the redcoats pushed on to Concord. Here they met with fresh disappointment.

The powder was all removed and hidden, and there was a force of four hundred minute men on hand - enough to give the British a lively battle.

STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 

THE RETREAT FROM CONCORD 

The British troops in full retreat are just entering the village of Lexington The expedition having failed in its object, the commander ordered a return to Boston. The colonists followed all the way, shooting, Indian fashion, at the retreating redcoats from behind trees and stone walls. Their numbers steadily increased as more minute men came pouring in from the towns in every direction. Before long they outnumbered the British. The retreat became a rout, only checked by some reënforcements which General Gage hurried out from 

Boston. Nearly three hundred British soldiers were killed on this 19th of April, which is still celebrated in Massachusetts as Patriots' Day. The colonists lost ninety-three men.

The Battle of Bunker Hill 

The news of this fighting ran like wild fire over the colonies. Almost every one was now convinced that the differences between the King and his American subjects could only be settled by actual warfare. Until now most of the

STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 43 

colonists had looked forward to a settlement that would leave them still English subjects. Now they began to talk of independence, for which some of the shrewdest leaders had been quietly working all the time. 

From every part of New England companies of militia began to stream toward Boston. Within a week there were sixteen thousand of them gathered there, and Governor Gage, instead of crushing the colony, as he had been ordered to do, found himself and his garrison of soldiers shut up, under regular siege, in Boston.

Meanwhile the Continental Congress, now in session, voted to assume the cause of Massachusetts as that of the 

BOSTON CHARLES TO THE BATTLE OF BUNKER HILL 

From a contemporary engraving. The British batteries in Boston are bombarding and setting fire to Charlestown. British soldiers are crossing in boats to the attack or gathered at the foot of the hill and firing on the Americans on the crest. entire country. The force of militia round Boston became the Continental Army; and Colonel George Washington, of

Virginia, who had seen plenty of active service in the French and Indian War, was chosen to command it.

STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 

Before he reached his post, another battle had been fought. The colonial troops had taken possession of some high land in Charlestown overlooking Boston and its harbor, and General Gage thought it necessary to drive them out. Bombarded by the British men-of-war, and facing three fierce charges by the best soldiers of Europe, the colonists stood their ground until their ammunition gave out, when the British at last took the hill. 

This was the battle of Bunker Hill. The British lost more than one thousand men a third of the entire attacking force; the Americans killed and wounded numbered some four hundred. The battle was important because it proved to the world that the colonial militia could and would face British regulars coolly and bravely. This was to be a real war. The British army would not overrun a helpless country. It would have to fight for every inch of ground, and fight hard. 

Capture of Ticonderoga

Meanwhile another aggressive movement, this time against the fortresses of Ticonderoga and Crown Point, which commanded the best route from Canada to the Hudson, showed how much in earnest the New Englanders were. This campaign was undertaken by volunteers from 

Vermont and Connecticut, led by the famous Ethan Allen, chief of the Green Mountain Boys. It was a complete success. The small British garrisons were taken by surprise and surrendered without even a pretense of fighting. A great quantity of ammunition and supplies fell to the victors, besides a number of cannon which were dragged to Boston and used against the British forces besieged there.

Washington Takes Command 

A few weeks later, on July 3, 1775, Washington reached

STEPS THAT LED TO THE REVOLUTION 45 

Cambridge, just outside of Boston, and took command of his new army. The war of the Revolution had begun. The colonies were fortunate indeed to find for their ill-armed, hastily-equipped forces a leader with the military skill, sound judgment, high character and patient, resourceful determination of Washington. Without him it is hard to see how America could have won its independence. He is by common consent one of the greatest figures in all history.
