UNIT III HOW THE REVOLUTIONARY WAR WAS FOUGHT AND  

E are now to take up the story of our Revolution, by which, after six years of fighting, often under the greatest difficulties and discouragements, independence was achieved. You will learn from this unit how much the country owed to the patience, firmness, and constancy of George Washington; and you will also learn of the very valuable aid we received from France during the closing years of the war. Perhaps we should not have won without it, though it was rendered less from love of us than from hostility to England. 

An Unequal Struggle

The struggle thus begun seemed a most unequal one. 

The thirteen colonies, with their small and scattered population, were facing the strongest and richest nation in the world. They were not even united in the undertaking, for besides the jealousies and suspicions that continually made it hard for the thirteen little colonies to act as one, there were many among the colonists who were not in favor of resistance at all. The loyalists- men and women who sympathized with England instead of with the rebels numerous everywhere, but especially in the important middle colonies of New York and Pennsylvania.

The Loyalists 

were

Wherever the loyalists were found, they were likely to be the richest and often the most influential and best educated

then Jon

From the painting by Gilbert Stuart in the Museum of Fine Arts in Boston

people of their neighborhoods. They were the merchants who had everything to lose by war; the public officials who were appointed by the King, and were of course on his side; the great landowners and rich men, who were afraid of democratic rule and were naturally in sympathy with the King and the aristocratic Government of England. 

The early history of the Revolution is full of conflicts both in politics and on the battlefield between these loyalists whom the patriots called Tories — and those colonists who were determined to gain their independence. Each side came to hate and despise the other, and when the patriotic party won, thousands of the loyalists left the country rather than remain to be ruled and perhaps punished by their opponents. Some went to England, but most of them removed to different parts of Canada, where their descendants live to this day.

Weaknesses of the Colonies Besides this lack of union among themselves, the colonists had few facilities for manufacturing the clothes, guns, ammunition, and cannon they needed; they had not a great deal of money, and very insufficient means of borrowing. 

As the war went on, they were reduced to using paper money with nothing behind it but the promise of Congress to pay, and at times that promise seemed so little likely to be kept, that the Continental money was worth almost nothing at all.

Since there was no strong central government - for each colony was still afraid and unwilling to let any of the others have anything to say about its own laws or its own taxes the war was fought without any proper coördination. Soldiers went and came as they pleased, often refused to fight outside their own colonies, or under officers from other parts of the country. Congress had no effective

way of raising the money to pay and equip its troops - so the troops often went unpaid and were poorly equipped. 

Advantages of England

On the other hand, England was the richest country in the world, with a strong army, ably officered, and supplied with the best of arms and equipment. English manufacturers could furnish at short notice whatever was needed by the army.

The soldiers were mostly veterans who had beaten the best armies of Europe in previous wars. Finally

England commanded the sea with her navy and had all the ships she needed to bring men and military supplies across the ocean. It is not surprising that King George and his advisers thought the war would soon be over and the colonists beaten into submission.

Where the Strength of the Americans Lay 

The affair was not so one-sided as it seemed, however. 

For one thing the colonists were fighting for their own country, and were much more in earnest than the British troops who were paid fighting men, sometimes from countries other than England, without much personal interest in the struggle. Then, too, the American country was a difficult one in which to carry on war, especially to armies used to European conditions. It was all "forest and marsh and wilderness," as one British officer complained. There were poor roads or none at all, and wide stretches of thinly settled land to be marched over, with no large towns to be looted or to furnish supplies.

The Americans knew this difficult country well, and they had learned from the Indians how to fight in it to advantage. 

The British soldiers, heavily clad in the clumsy uniforms of that day, and accustomed to regular formations in open country, instead of scattered fighting behind trees and stone

walls, were often at a loss what to do when they were so attacked.

The colonists, man for man, were perhaps physically stronger than their enemies, for generations of pioneer life out of doors had given them good constitutions and great physical endurance. They could throw up earthworks in a night that would take Europeans several days.

The Enemies of England 

England was not wholly united. 

There was a strong party, led by Lord Chatham and Burke, who opposed the 

WILLIAM PITT, EARL OF CHATHAM 

King's policies and openly sympathized with the rebellious colonists. This party was never a majority, but it was able enough to lead many 

Englishmen to question the wisdom and justice of the King's course, and to make the war far from popular in England. 

England had also to face the enmity of other nations, which were jealous of her power and ready to injure her. ways to help the colo- France, Spain, and Holland all found nists. At first they did so secretly, by sending supplies and arms to them; but before the war was over they helped by going to war openly. France, in particular, eager to re-

venge herself for the loss of Canada, gave the colonies military and naval support without which the victory might never have been won. 

Howe and Washington

The first plan of the British Government was to end the rebellion by crushing Massachusetts, the most noisy and defiant of all the colonies. If that "wasps' nest" could be smoked out, they thought, the rest of the country would submit. So they sent a famous general, Sir William Howe, with more troops, to take charge of the campaign. This plan did not turn out as expected, however. The "wasps" proved more numerous and more dangerous than they were supposed to be. At Concord and at Bunker Hill they fought the King's troops to a standstill and then they poured in from all New England in such numbers that 

Howe found himself shut up in Boston.

Howe was an able soldier on the battlefield, as he often proved, but he was indolent and slow to act; perhaps, too, he was contemptuous of the "raw militia," as he called the colonial soldiers. He lingered in Boston, doing nothing, while Washington disciplined and armed his rough troops. 

Washington even grew so sure that Howe would make no move that he let some of his best soldiers, including Morgan's Virginia riflemen, go to take part in an expedition against Quebec led by the daring and brilliant Colonel 

Benedict Arnold.

The Quebec Expedition 

The colonists were very hopeful that the Canadians would join them in their opposition to England, and that if they sent a military force against the British garrisons there, the French settlers would come to their support. One little army, led by brave General Montgomery marched

northward by the Lake Champlain route against Montreal. 

Arnold led another through the thick forests and swamps of 

Maine to assault Quebec.

Montgomery did take Montreal, which offered little resistance; but Arnold's men were delayed by the difficulties of their march and their number was greatly reduced by hardships and disease. When they finally reached Quebec, they found Sir Guy Carleton, the Governor, shut up in the citadel with his men. Carleton would not come out and fight, and when Montgomery arrived from Montreal the Americans decided on the rash plan of assaulting the fortress. Their supplies were running low, the French people were hostile instead of friendly, and something had to be done. 

Arnold Gives up Quebec

The assault was made in a snowstorm on the last day of 1775. It almost succeeded, but unfortunately Montgomery was killed, and Arnold was so badly wounded he had to leave the field. Without their leaders the Americans lost heart and fell back. For several months Arnold maintained a siege, but his little force was reduced so fast by exposure and disease that he had to retreat, without having provoked 

Carleton to another fight. Canada remained in the hands of the British throughout the war. 

Massachusetts is not Crushed 

Meanwhile the siege of Boston dragged on. Howe had been too careless to seize and fortify Dorchester Heights, just south of the city; and Washington saw that if he could occupy that position he could drive the British out of Boston. In a single night his soldiers took the heights, threw up breastworks and mounted the cannon that had been brought from Ticonderoga. That settled it; Howe had no

mind for another battle like that of Bunker Hill, now that the Americans had cannon to defend their position. So he quickly loaded his soldiers into the British ships that crowded the harbor, and sailed away, taking about a thousand of the Boston loyalists with him.

The crushing of Massachusetts had failed, because the British had underestimated the determination and the strength of the colonists, and because the British officers had been slow and uncertain in their conduct of the campaign. 

Again and again while he was in command in America, Howe was accused of failing to use his full strength against the colonists. There is some reason to believe that if he did not to some degree sympathize with the rebels, he did not particularly enjoy the task of reducing them to submission. 

The second plan of the British Government was to cut the line of the colonies in two by seizing New York and Pennsylvania. This would make it impossible for the Northern colonies to send help to the South, or for the Southern colonies to send their troops into New England. After the middle colonies had been conquered, it would be easy, the British leaders thought, to overrun first New England and then Virginia. This plan seemed especially hopeful because there were a great many loyalists in the middle colonies, and it was believed that they would come to the aid of the British troops and help to overcome the patriotic resistance. 

The Second Congress Meets 

Before General Howe had an opportunity to carry out this new plan of campaign, however, some very remarkable things had been happening in Philadelphia, where the 

Second Continental Congress was in session. Until the beginning of actual fighting in Massachusetts, Congress, like the people at large, had hesitated to go so far as to talk seriously of independence. They had hoped that King

George, when he saw how much in earnest the colonists were, would yield. 

But the King did not yield. He would not even listen to the petitions and declarations of grievances that Congress sent him. Moreover, since the young men of England did not seem eager to enlist to fight the patriots of America, he hired some sixteen thousand Ger- man soldiers from one of the princes 

From an old engraving

INDEPENDENCE HALL IN PHILADEL- PHIA IN 1776 

of Hesse, and sent them over to fill up his army here.

The Declaration of Independence 

Congress now determined to declare the independence of the colonies. It appointed a committee, on which were 

Benjamin Franklin, John Adams, Thomas Jefferson, Roger 

Sherman, and Robert R. Livingston, to draw up a Declaration. Jefferson, a young Virginian whose head was full of democratic ideas, and who knew how to write eloquently, did most of the work. In ringing phrases he set forth all the reasons America had for renouncing its allegiance to

King George, and maintaining its independence of Great Britain.

Congress debated the matter solemnly, for the delegates knew that if they signed the Declaration they became hopeless rebels in the eyes of the British King; and might pay

with their lives if the Revolution failed. "We must hang together," said Franklin with grim humor, "or we shall hang separately."

How the Declaration Was Received 

In the end independence was voted almost unanimously, and on the 4th of July, 1776 — the most memorable day in our history the members of the Congress authorized the 

A Declaration by the Representatives of the UNITED STATES OF AMERICA, in General Congress assembled.

Declaration. The delegates from New York, who had not yet received instructions to do so from their legislature, did not at first sign the Declaration. Later, however, they, too, set their names to the document.

The news of the Declaration was soon spread far and wide by messengers riding at top speed along the rough roads of that period. Everywhere the patriots received it with cheers and rejoicing. Bells were rung, bonfires were lighted, public meetings and church services of thanksgiving were held.

In New York City a leaden statue of King George was pulled down, melted, and made into bullets to be fired later at his own troops. From this time it became

more dangerous than ever to be a loyalist, for such people were now regarded not as lukewarm Americans, but as public enemies. 

Fighting for New York

A few days after the Declaration was signed, General Howe landed his troops on Staten Island opposite New York to begin the work of carrying out the second British plan referred to on page 56. He had a splendid army of twentyfive thousand men and his fleet of men-of-war and transports numbered some four hundred sail. Washington had hurried down from Boston to defend New York, but his army was much smaller than Howe's and not nearly so well equipped. When the British left Boston, thousands of the 

New England militia had gone home to work on their farms, and others had disappeared when their year of enlistment was over.

Not only were the Americans outnumbered, but the 

British fleet also commanded New York Harbor and the rivers on either side of the town. Washington tried to check Howe by holding Brooklyn Heights, but Howe had men enough to get in the rear of the Americans and they were soundly beaten. Washington withdrew to the north of the city, but his little army was beaten again at White Plains, and he had to yield New York to the British and cross the Hudson into New Jersey.

The Battle of Trenton 

Howe was slow, as usual. He neither pursued Washington with vigor nor hastened to take Philadelphia as he was expected to do. When winter came on, after driving Washington out of New Jersey into Pennsylvania, he withdrew into comfortable winter quarters in New York, leaving sev-

eral bodies of troops along the Delaware River to keep 

Washington from coming back into New Jersey.

A thousand or more of these troops, largely Hessian soldiers whom the British Government had hired to help fight 

Painting by Leutze

WASHINGTON CROSSING THE DELAWARE BEFORE THE BATTLE OF TRENTON 

the Americans, were stationed at Trenton. On Christmas night, while the Hessians, entirely unsuspecting, were celebrating the holiday in the German fashion, Washington, with a few hundred men, crossed the Delaware in a little fleet of boats. He fell on the astonished Hessians in the midst of their feast and killed or captured them all. 

Battle of Princeton

A few days after the Battle of Trenton, Washington surprised another British force at Princeton, and defeated that also. In spite of Howe, Washington had succeeded in getting his men back into New Jersey and his army passed the winter among the hills only twenty miles from New York. 

These two victories put new heart into the Continental army, which had been in serious danger of breaking up al-

together after the defeats of Long Island and White Plains. 

The chief trouble was that there was no money with which to pay the soldiers or to feed and equip them properly. 

Congress had no right to lay taxes and found it impossible to get money in sufficient amounts from the colonies. The Continental currency was already almost worthless.

If it had not been for the services of Robert Morris, a wealthy Philadelphian, who gave or borrowed on his own notes a sum of fifty thousand dollars, and for the generosity of Washington, who advanced money of his own for the soldiers' pay, the army could hardly have held together. 

With the paymaster again able to find something in his chest for the soldiers, and the increased confidence in Washington, due to his skillful generalship in New Jersey, the spirits of the army began to rise. 

Volunteers from Abroad

At the end of the winter Washington was joined by several able officers from Europe, who had become interested in the American cause, and came to offer their services. 

These men were of great value in drilling and organizing the raw recruits as they joined the army, in designing fortifications and in instructing the younger officers in their duties. They were Baron Steuben, a celebrated engineer officer from Prussia, Baron de Kalb, also a German, Count 

Pulaski and Thaddeus Kosciusko, two clever Polish soldiers, and most famous of all, Marquis de Lafayette, a nobleman of France.

Lafayette was younger and less experienced than these other volunteers he was in fact only nineteen years old — but he came with a burning enthusiasm for the cause of liberty and democracy which made him particularly welcome. He and Washington became deeply attached, and

his winning personality and generous nature made him a popular hero with the Americans. He gave liberally of his money and served bravely in the field. Almost fifty years after the war in 1825

LAFAYETTE ¹ 

- - - he revisited the United 

States, an old and distinguished man; and he was received with extraordinary enthusiasm.

The British army remained comfortably in winter quarters in New 

York until the spring of 

1777. General Howe and his officers enjoyed themselves almost as much as if they had been in London. There were many wealthy and socially ambitious loyalists in the little city; and dinners, balls, and even theater parties were given for the entertainment of the King's officers.

Meanwhile Washington's little army in the New Jersey hills was getting through the winter as best it could, still underpaid and not too well clothed. However, it was being drilled and disciplined till it became a body of veteran soldiers, needing only sufficient numbers to face the British with confidence. 

¹ Lafayette was born of a noble French family in 1757. After the Revolution he rose to the rank of general in the French Army; he took an active part in the early years of the French Revolution; but he was not radical enough to suit the later leaders and had to escape to Belgium. He was a political prisoner in Austria for a while but when France accepted Napoleon as ruler, Lafayette returned to France and held important offices there. He died in 1834.

Burgoyne's Expedition 

A year had passed since the plan of the British to cut off New England by conquering the middle colonies, had been undertaken. Howe held New York, but he had made no further progress and did not control the important line of the Hudson River and Lake Champlain. The British must seize that line if their plan was to succeed. 

So another army marched southward from Montreal commanded by General Burgoyne. He had about eight thousand regular soldiers, and he hoped for military assistance from the loyalists of central New York and from the Iroquois Indians. As a matter of fact he got far less help than he had expected. Sir John Johnson and Colonel 

Butler, two influential Tories, raised a regiment or two of loyalists, but they could not induce the Iroquois to join Burgoyne. Some of the red men were willing to do so, but others of the Six Nations were friendly to the colonists, and while the discussion went on the critical moment passed. 

A few Mohawks and Senecas and some Wyandots who were not Iroquois at all— joined the British army, but they did little good. Burgoyne could not keep them from killing and scalping inoffensive people, even including women, and that aroused and angered the Americans so much that they fought the British with all the more determination. 

Ticonderoga Falls 

Burgoyne came marching down from Canada, and began by taking the important fort of Ticonderoga, without much trouble. General Schuyler, a sound patriot and brave man, was in command of the Continental forces in

New York. He was unjustly blamed by Congress for the loss of Ticonderoga, and General Gates, a much less able officer, was put in his place.

From Ticonderoga, Burgoyne came marching down toward the Hudson. He expected Howe to come up the Hudson at the same time so that the little American army might be attacked both in front and rear. Of course Howe should have done so; it was the only way to make sure of the success of the campaign. However, he did nothing of the kind, and at the time when he was expected at Albany, he was loading most of his army into ships to sail around into 

Chesapeake Bay and then to capture Philadelphia. What he was thinking about to act in this way has never been explained. 

Bennington and Oriskany

Meanwhile Burgoyne was having the trouble that British commanders always had in carrying on operations among the roadless forests of America. He made slow progress. 

Thousands of eager patriots from New England and upper New York, excited by the news of the cruelties practiced by Burgoyne's Indian allies, hastened to block his advance.

One body of a thousand men that the British general sent to Bennington in Vermont to seize some military supplies was met and defeated by a force of Vermont and New 

Hampshire volunteers led by Colonel John Stark, a brave frontier fighter.

Another force of loyalists and Indians, which was expected to join Burgoyne after capturing Fort Schuyler, at what is now Rome, New York, was beaten and driven back at Oriskany by a body of backwoods riflemen under General Herkimer, aided by a sortie made by the defenders of the fort. It was on this day that American soldiers first fought under the Stars and Stripes; Congress had but a month earlier decreed that flag to be the banner of the New Nation.

Burgoyne Surrenders at Saratoga 

Burgoyne pushed on slowly to Saratoga, where he found himself facing a superior force under Gates and Schuyler. 

Now was the time for Howe to appear in the rear of the 

Americans; but Howe was on the sea sailing away for Phil- 

Painting by F. C. Yohn. Courtesy, Glens Falls Insurance Co. 

THE SURRENDER OF BURGOYNE 

adelphia. Burgoyne's supplies were running low. He was forced to fight. He began by attacking a position fortified by the Polish volunteer officer, Kosciusko, and defended by General Benedict Arnold. He was repulsed. Later he tried to turn the American position; this time he failed completely. Arnold led in the fighting; the British were completely defeated, and on October 17, 1777, Burgoyne, all his plans gone wrong, had to surrender.

This victory was really the turning-point of the war, for it upset all the military plans of the British, and, as it proved, persuaded France to make a military alliance with the colonies, who now for the first time seemed really likely to win. 

While the fighting was going on at Saratoga, Howe was ad-

vancing on Philadelphia. Washington faced him, first at the Brandywine and then at Germantown, but was not strong enough to stop him. Congress hastily fled from 

Philadelphia to York, Pennsylvania, and Howe entered the city, where he settled down once more in "winter quarters."

The Winter at Valley Forge 

The fact that Saratoga had wrecked the second of the military plans of the British did not disturb him. He and 

THE AMERICAN CAMP AT VALLEY FORGE 

his officers enjoyed another lively social season in Philadelphia; while Washington's little army, more or less broken by its defeats before Philadelphia, passed the most distressing of winters at Valley Forge.

The weather was unusually cold, and the soldiers, poorly clad and half fed, suffered severely. Crowded in damp unsanitary log shelters, they fell sick and died in great numbers. They could not get good food because the Pennsylvania farmers preferred to sell their produce to General

Howe for good British gold rather than to Washington for the almost worthless paper money of Congress.

We do not like to recognize the fact, but a good many of the colonists were still indifferent or actually hostile to the patriot cause. It was that which made it so hard to get the necessary money and supplies with which to maintain an army. A less courageous and determined leader than 

Washington might have given up the task as impossible. 

Even he lost patience now and then; but for a moment only. 

He heartened his men by his indomitable spirit, and by his example showed them how to bear their hardships. Somehow or other they won through that terrible winter; but 

Valley Forge was long a sad word in American ears. 

It was there that the cause of independence came nearest to hopeless discouragement.

The French Alliance 

With the spring of 1778 came better news. The colonies had long cherished the hope that France would help them against England, her old enemy. Benjamin Franklin, the ablest diplomat and statesman in America was in Paris, trying to persuade the King to assist the struggling colonists. 

Franklin made a great impression on the French, and he was received with honor at court; but for a long time the 

French Government would not risk going to war. 

It was the news of Saratoga that convinced the King and his ministers that the colonists had a chance to win. time they saw an opportunity of striking a nation that had taken Canada from them. 

At the same blow at the

In February, 1778, France signed a treaty of alliance with the United States of America, and promised to send over to this country both money and troops. This treaty put a new face on affairs. The Americans took fresh courage and hope, and celebrated the new alliance with great enthusi-

asm. The British Government grew uneasy. It tried to bring the war to an end by promising to repeal all the laws the colonists had objected to and offering to let them have representatives in Parliament.

But it was too late for that. The United States was fairly launched as an independent nation and would not listen to any offers of compromise. 

Battle of Monmouth

The next important event was the evacuation of Philadelphia. Howe was afraid the French fleet would cut off his communications with New York, so back he marched to that city. On the way Washington attacked his rear at Monmouth, and only the flat disobedience of General Charles Lee, his subordinate, prevented him from winning a complete victory. Lee, who was an unreliable, if not actually treacherous officer, was court-martialed and discharged from the army. 

Howe Recalled

Two years had now passed since the British undertook to cut the rebellious colonies in two by the conquest of New 

York, New Jersey, and Pennsylvania. All they had to show was the possession of the city of New York. General Howe had failed; he was soon recalled to England and Sir Henry 

Clinton was raised to the chief command.

Clinton undertook a third plan of campaign. He believed that the Southern colonies could easily be conquered, since there were many loyalists among them. His idea was to overrun Georgia and the Carolinas, and then sweep northward pushing the beaten Americans before him. We shall soon see what success he had, but first we must look at what was going on among the forests and scanty clearings of the West.

HIGH STREET, PHILADELPHIA IN 1778 

Showing the well-built appearance of the city when Howe occupied it 

The Tories in New York 

The collapse of Burgoyne's expedition left the loyalists (or Tories) of New York unsupported by any British force, but they did not therefore give up the fight. The Tories were numerous in central New York, and they had bold, determined leaders - Sir John Johnson, son of Sir William

Johnson whom we read about last year, and the Butlers, father and son. Many of them were rough frontiersmen, tenants of Johnson and Butler, and quite as good at forest fighting as any of the patriots.

They had, as allies, the Mohawk and Seneca tribes of the Iroquois, led by Brant, a famous Indian chieftain, who had got a good education in American schools and was more intelligent and less cruel than most of the red men. 

Wyoming and Cherry Valley

In July, 1778, a large party of Tories and Indians, led by Colonel Butler, swooped down on the Wyoming Valley, a

beautiful spot on the Susquehanna River just over the Pennsylvania line. This valley had been settled by men and women from Connecticut. The unsuspecting people were taken by surprise, and almost all massacred or driven out to die in the wilderness.

A few months later, another similar raiding party attacked the settlement of Cherry Valley in central New York. Again the rifle and tomahawk were employed on the helpless settlers, fifty of whom were killed. These were the most bloody events of the border warfare; but all through the year 1778 there were skirmishes and raids along the frontier, in which there was as much savagery as in the battles of the French and Indian War. The hatred between the patriots and the Tories grew more and more intense, while the Indians turned their bloody hatchets not only on the whites but on one another; for some of the Iroquois refused to join in the war on the patriots, and consequently were attacked by Brant and his savages. 

Punishing the Iroquois

The news of Wyoming and Cherry Valley convinced Washington that the Tories and Indians must be punished. 

He spared as many men as he could for an expedition against them, commanded by General John Sullivan, one of his best lieutenants. Advancing in two bodies up the 

Mohawk and Susquehanna Rivers, the American forces united and met the enemy at Newtown, where Elmira now stands. Johnson, the Butlers, and Brant were all there, but they were soundly beaten. Then the Americans laid waste a great part of the cornfields of the Iroquois and burned many of their villages.

The Iroquois never recovered their old strength after this defeat, though they were still numerous enough, with Tory help, to keep the border uneasy till the end of the war. The

settlers had to live in blockhouses, and plow their fields under the protection of armed guards; even then a good many lost their lives from the raids of the Indians. 

George Rogers Clark in the West

The situation was similar all along the frontiers of Pennsylvania, Virginia, and North Carolina. Before the war began, settlers had begun to pour across the mountains to take up the fertile lands in Ohio, Kentucky, and Tennessee. The British had few soldiers in that vast country, but they had Indian allies who were only too glad to take up the hatchet against the settlers. The history of those States is full of the tales of massacre, bloodshed, and forest warfare between whites and Indians.

The settlers held their own, however. They did

VIRGINIA MAP OF CLARK'S CAMPAIGN IN THE WEST 

better than that when George Rogers Clark, a young surveyor-soldier, organized an expedition of volunteer frontiersmen and led it against the British posts, Kaskaskia, Cahokia (near St. Louis), and Vincennes.

In a flotilla of boats Clark floated down the Ohio and rowed up the Mississippi. He had no trouble at all in taking the posts on the latter river, but in order to attack Vincennes he had to lead his men overland in the depth of winter, through forests and swamps that were flooded by the icy waters of an unusually wet season, for one hundred and thirty miles. It was a hard and exhausting march, and at the end he had to face a strong garrison led by Colonel 

Hamilton, the chief British officer in the West.

Clark and his men assaulted the fort, aided by a few cannon which Clark had sent around by way of the Ohio and Wabash Rivers. After a hot fight the British surrendered. 

Except for Detroit they now held no posts in the West. 

The victory of Clark was of great importance, for it left the great territory of the Mississippi Valley in American hands. 

CLARK'S MEN ADVANCING ON VINCENNES By permission of the Yale University Press. From The Chronicles of America Motion Pictures 

If the British had continued to hold Cahokia and Vincennes until the end of the war, they might have claimed that the interior belonged to Canada instead of to the United States. They did in fact make that claim; but they could not sustain it, for Clark had outwitted them. The 

Americans had actual possession of the immense territory, and insisted on keeping it. 

The War on the Sea

Before returning to the story of the third British plan of

campaign, which took the fighting into the Southern colonies, we ought to learn something about two famous figures in American history, John Paul Jones and Benedict Arnold. 

Although the colonies had no navy when the war began, while Great Britain was the strongest of naval powers, there was a surprising amount of fighting at sea. This was because the Americans fitted out a great number of swift, though small vessels, built mostly in New England and manned by hardy brave sailors from the Northern colonies.

These vessels could not face British men-of-war, but they acted as privateers. They attacked British merchant ships wherever they could be found, and burned them or seized their cargoes, which were often of great value. After France became our ally the privateers became especially troublesome to English commerce, because the French harbors provided safe places in which to hide and refit. It is said that more than six hundred English ships were captured in this way, and merchant vessels dared not sail through the 

English Channel without a naval convoy. 

Unfortunately, this was a game at which two could play.

The English frigates also caught and destroyed many American ships and made it quite impossible for Massachusetts fishing boats to ply their trade off Newfoundland. 

John Paul Jones

John Paul Jones, a Scotchman and a sailor, who was living in Virginia when the war began, was the boldest and ablest of the men who helped to form a little American navy. 

He was the most brilliant and famous of early American sea-fighters. In his little cruiser, the Ranger, he raided British vessels and even British coast towns with much success. 

Jones later had a larger vessel, a made-over French merchant ship that had been used in the India trade. The

French named it "Bonhomme Richard" out of compliment to Benjamin Franklin, who wrote "Poor Richard's" sayings.

His most famous exploit was the battle between the Bonhomme Richard and the British frigate Serapis which he overtook, convoying a fleet of merchantmen, off the coast of England in the North Sea.

Jones's ship was no match for the Serapis in gunfire; but he promptly laid it alongside the British ship and fastened the two together so they could not drift apart. The battle was mostly hand-to-hand fighting on the decks, though each ship could use part of its cannon at point-blank range. 

This battle was one of the bloodiest of naval engagements. 

It lasted for hours, and was fought largely by moonlight. 

Finally the Se- rapis struck its colors after almost the entire 

Painting by Patoi

THE BONHOMME RICHARD AND THE SERAPIS 

crew was killed or wounded. Jones transferred his men to the Serapis, for his own ship was on fire and a little later it sunk. 

This battle had no particular bearing on the war as a whole,

The ship at the right is a French ship whose captain was ac- but it made a cused of treacherously firing on the Bonhomme Richard. great stir in Europe, and convinced some of the European nations who had been hesitating to take sides, that the new American nation was a power to be reckoned with.

England Makes More Enemies 

England had indeed succeeded in making enemies of all the other maritime countries. Her captains claimed the right to seize and search the vessels of neutral countries. If they found any cargo on board that came from America or France, or was going to either of those countries, they would seize not only the cargo but the ships as well. Protests did no good, for King George was as obstinate in such matters as he was in his dealings with the colonies. 

So not only France, but Spain and Holland ended by declaring war on Great Britain, and Russia and Denmark were thoroughly unfriendly. During the last years of the 

Revolution, England had not only America, but these other countries to fight - mainly by sea. This situation helped the Americans, because England had less money and less energy to spare for the fighting in this country. 

The Treason of Arnold 

The year 1780 was not a hopeful one for the patriots. 

One of the most discouraging events of the year was the treachery of Benedict Arnold. This man was a very daring and brilliant soldier. At Ticonderoga, at Quebec, above all at Saratoga, he had rendered great services to the patriot cause. Washington admired and trusted him.

But Arnold was vain and hot-tempered as well as brave. 

He made enemies in Congress, in the army, and in Philadelphia, of which he was for a time military governor. He was offended because Congress did not vote him the promotion he had earned, and his quarrel with the Philadelphia civil authorities led to a court-martial, by which he was acquitted of any real misconduct.

He brooded over his wrongs, real and fancied, till he determined to be revenged by betraying the American cause.

Washington had made him commander at West Point, a

strong fortress that overlooked the Hudson River. Arnold offered to betray this important post to the British. Sir 

Henry Clinton listened to him, and Major John André was the go-between. The treason was on the point of success when the disguised André fell into the hands of a volunteer patrol of patriots. The papers that proved the intended crime were found in his boots.

Word of André's arrest reached Arnold in time for him to make his escape to New York, but André was hanged as a spy. There was much sympathy for André, but none for Arnold, whose name became then, as it has since remained, hateful to all Americans. Washington was stunned by his treachery. "Whom now can we trust?" he cried to one of his officers. 

Gates Beaten in the South

Meanwhile in the South the British seemed to be making headway at last. Clinton had moved a good-sized army by sea from New York to Savannah. With it, he and Lord 

Cornwallis, his second in command, overran both Georgia and South Carolina. Charleston, as well as Savannah, was taken.

The militia of the South were quite unable to face the British regulars; though their brave and dashing leaders, Francis Marion and Thomas Sumter, managed to avoid capture. With their small but active cavalry forces they annoyed the British commanders by destroying supplies, cutting communications, and even surprising detachments of soldiers on the march with sudden and daring attacks. 

Things looked so promising for the British in the South that Clinton returned to New York, leaving Cornwallis in command. In the hope of regaining the lost ground, an army of Continentals and militia was raised by Washington and dispatched into South Carolina. Congress insisted

that Gates should command and Washington unwillingly yielded.

Gates lost no time in proving that the reputation he had won at Saratoga was undeserved, and that he was a coward. He made blunder after blunder, and when he had to fight Cornwallis at last at Camden, he was soundly beaten and his force dispersed. This was on August 16, 1780, only six weeks before 

Arnold's treason was uncovered. Certainly it was a dark moment for the patriot cause. 

There was discouragement everywhere. Financially the Govern-

The Continental paper ment seemed breaking down. money had become so worthless that by the end of 1780 a paper dollar was worth less than one cent! Butter was twelve dollars a pound; flour, $1575 a barrel. Samuel Adams paid $2000 for a suit of clothes and a hat!

General Greene Takes Command 

Another small 

Better things were in store, however.

American army was raised and sent southward. This time it was commanded by General Nathanael Greene, the 

Rhode Island blacksmith, who had more military genius than any American officer except Washington himself, and who had been his chief's "right arm" during four years of wearisome warfare.

The patriots took heart. When Cornwallis undertook to invade North Carolina, he got a severe check at King's

Mountain, where part of his army was badly defeated by a force of backwoods marksmen. 

When Greene took command, he had with him Daniel Morgan, the Virginian who had won fame at Quebec and 

Saratoga; Kosciusko, the clever Polish engineer; and “Light

Horse Harry" Lee, with his splendid regiment of Virginia horse. His army was small, but splendidly officered. 

The Tide Turns 

Early in the campaign a force commanded by General Morgan defeated a part of the British army under Colonel 

Tarleton at the Cowpens, and in that battle Cornwallis lost the best of his cavalry. Then Greene, pretending a retreat, drew Cornwallis north into North Carolina. Almost all the way across that State the two armies marched, until at 

Guilford Court House Greene turned and struck Cornwallis a blow from which he found it hard to recover. He was so far north already, and so far from the sea, by which only could his supplies come, that he determined to keep on into Virginia, where reënforcements and supplies could reach him by ship from New York. 

As Cornwallis turned north, Greene turned south, marched quickly back to South Carolina, and in a brisk campaign cleared all the British garrisons except that at Charleston out of that State and Georgia. So the British plan of winning and holding the far Southern colonies was also a failure. 

Cornwallis Surrenders at Yorktown

Meanwhile Cornwallis marched up into Virginia, tried in vain to make General Lafayette fight him with the small army he had in that State, and ended by taking up his position at Yorktown, at the end of the peninsula between the York and the James Rivers. He sent word to Clinton

in New York, that men and supplies must be sent to him by ship. 

While he waited he permitted Lafayette to “bottle him up" by pushing his troops across the neck of land which led to Yorktown. Worse still, it happened that England had 

THE SURRENDER OF CORNWALLIS AT YORKTOWN 

Painting by Trumbull in the Capitol at Washington for the moment lost control of the sea. A French fleet under Admiral de Grasse was near by. It was stronger than any English fleet in the neighborhood. It sailed into Chesapeake Bay and cut off any hope of reënforcements reaching Cornwallis.

Then Washington, who was watching Clinton in New 

York, made a very bold and clever move with his army.

He pretended to be preparing an attack on New York; but suddenly he withdrew his men and marched them quickly to the head of Chesapeake Bay, where they took ship for

Yorktown. Before either Clinton or Cornwallis knew what was going on, he had joined Lafayette. A force of Americans three times as many as the British faced Cornwallis. 

The British commander made a brave fight for seventeen days, but, attacked in front by the Americans and French, and bombarded from the rear by the French fleet, his position was hopeless. On October 19, 1781, he surrendered his army.

Peace Signed at Paris 

The blow was a crushing one to English hopes. After seven years of costly, bloody warfare the British held nothing in America but the cities of New York and Charleston. It was clear that the attempt to conquer the united colonies now the United States - was a complete failure. 

Lord North, the King's Minister, cried out when he heard the news, "It is all over! It is all over!" Parliament, which had long been restless under the mounting expenses of a losing war, would grant no more money. King George, still obstinate, talked of fighting on; but the British nation would support him no longer. With tears in his eyes, he told Parliament that he was at last willing for the American colonies to form an independent nation.

The commissioners to make a treaty of peace met in 

Paris. Franklin and John Adams represented the United

States. It took a long time to agree on all the terms of the treaty, but it was signed at last on September 3, 1783. 

Some two months later the last of the British troops sailed away from New York. Washington marched into the city at the head of his soldiers, amidst the cheers and rejoicings of the people, the display of fireworks, and the crackle of huge bonfires.

THE "EXTRA" THAT BROUGHT THE NEWS TO BOSTON

Washington Bids Farewell to His Officers 

After bidding an affectionate good-bye to his officers, the general, whose constancy and military skill had made the victory possible, went to meet the Congress at Annapolis.

To that body he surrendered his commission, and having won the admiration and love of the whole people he retired to his home at Mount Vernon. He would not take any pay for his invaluable services and asked only that the money he had again and again advanced to pay and feed his army be repaid, when convenient, by the young Republic. 

Results of the War

The treaty of peace made the Government of the United States supreme in all the great territory between the Atlantic and the Mississippi, from the Great Lakes to the borders of Florida, which still belonged to Spain. 

The result of the American Revolution was a serious blow to the prestige of England, and many Englishmen thought that it marked the end of a strong British Empire. At the same time France seemed to have won ample revenge for her defeat of twenty years before.

But events proved that the British Empire had plenty of vitality left. Its greatest days were still before it in the nineteenth century. Parliament never again permitted an obstinate king to get the nation into so unhappy a situation. Indeed, King George's mind soon after became affected, and he ceased to have any influence at all on public affairs which went on much better without him. 

On the other hand, France, heavily burdened with debt, and troubled with an incompetent king and a corrupt government, slipped steadily down into her own Revolution. 

Within ten years from the signing of the Treaty of Paris, King Louis was beheaded by his rebellious subjects. No doubt our Revolution, with its victorious assertion of the

rights of the people against the will of the King, had something to do with arousing that spirit of revolt among the French.

Questions 

1. Who were the loyalists? Where were they most numerous? 

What did the patriots call them? 

2. What were the reasons why the colonists were not prepared to carry on a long war? 

3. What advantages did England possess at the beginning of the war? From what disadvantages did she suffer? What, if any, were the advantages of the colonists? 

4. What opposition did the British Government face at home?

What enemies did it have in Europe, and how did they act during the war?

UNIT IV  

N this unit we shall follow the trials of the new Nation, IN not yet firmly united under a government strong enough to guide its affairs with decision and success. We shall observe the weaknesses of that government which almost brought the experiment of union to failure. Indeed, the 

United States narrowly escaped breaking up into a number of small nations like those of Central and South America. 

In the end, however, the leaders of the people were wise enough to see what was needed and determined enough to do it. They made a new Constitution, by which the thirteen separate States were at last joined together under a central government with real and sufficient powers, and then they persuaded a rather reluctant people to accept it. 

The colonies had won their independence; they were no longer colonies but States — the United States of America; yet for a time they seemed not to know how to make a proper use of their newly won freedom. Before the end of the fighting, the States, at the suggestion of Congress, had agreed upon certain Articles of Confederation; but the Confederacy so formed was of a very loose sort and proved to be of almost no value as a form of government. 

The Steps Toward Union 

The States were still like thirteen separate little nations only bound together by the lightest of ties for mutual defense. They were, as they always had been, jealous and suspicious of one another, and unwilling to give up to a common government any part of their independence. It

took a long, long time to teach them the need of genuine union. The various steps in the process occupied almost one hundred and fifty years.

First there was the New England Confederation of 1643, a plan for uniting four New England colonies for defense against the Indians. Next there came the Albany Congress of 1754 to secure coöperation among all the colonies in the French and Indian Wars. Then followed the Stamp 

Act Congress (1765), för united action in resisting the unpopular laws that Parliament was making; and last, the 

Continental Congress (1774) and the Articles of Confeder ation (1781) to hold the colonies together during the War of the Revolution.

Each was a step in advance. Each union was a little stronger than the previous one, yet none was strong enough to constitute a nation. 

Weakness of the Confederation

The trouble was that each colony or State kept a firm grasp on all the essential powers of government, and would give up as little as possible to the Congress. It is surprising that Congress did as well as it did in carrying on the war, in borrowing money from foreign nations, and in maintaining something like a working union of the thirteen States. 

But after peace was declared, and there was no need to "hang_together," in order to keep from “hanging separately," as Franklin had said, things went from bad to worse.

Congress could pass laws, but only when at least nine of the thirteen States agreed to them. When the laws were passed, there was no President and no executive department to see that the laws were enforced. The States could, and often did, ignore them if they felt like it. Moreover, the States had each one vote in Congress. Little Delaware

and Rhode Island had exactly the same power there as 

Virginia or Pennsylvania or Massachusetts or New York; which to most people seemed unfair. 

THE CAMPUS MARTIUS AT MARIETTA, OHIO 

Marietta was one of the first towns established west of the Alleghanies. Observe the stockade and the fortified blockhouses at the corners of the lines of dwelling-houses, for protection against Indian attacks.

Congress could only advise the States or ask their assistance. It could not raise taxes, and could get only what money the States slowly and grudgingly voted to give it. So it could not pay its expenses promptly. It could not find the money to pay back what it had borrowed, during the war, from France and Holland; it could not pay the soldiers who had fought the battles of the Revolution what it owed them; and it could not even get any real money with which to redeem the paper currency it had issued. Altogether it was a weak and helpless kind of body to be in charge of a young nation's affairs. 

Jealousies of the States

Meanwhile the States were putting all sorts of obstacles in the way of a real union. Each State insisted on issuing its own money, of different values, of course or, it may 

---

better be said, different in the lack of value — which made trade between the States very difficult. This situation was made still worse by the fact that individual States often taxed heavily every kind of trade with other States. 

For example, farm produce from New Jersey was taxed on entering both Philadelphia and New York. In retaliation 

New Jersey put a heavy tax on a lighthouse that stood on its shore, which had been built to help ships into New York Harbor. 

Evils of Cheap Money

With our foreign trade largely gone, since the English markets were no longer ours; with our domestic trade hampered by high taxes and almost worthless paper money, every one was poor or felt himself so, and most people were in debt. Some States tried to relieve the debtors by issuing more and more cheap paper money. When Massachusetts refused to do so, an actual uprising of people, who demanded that all debts be wiped out, occurred. This was called 

Shays's Rebellion, because a man named Daniel Shays was its leader.

For a time the situation was alarming. The courts were broken up, property was destroyed, and the militia was put to flight by the rebels; but the sober sense of the Massachusetts people prevailed, and the rebellion was put down. 

Similar riots, though on a smaller scale, occurred in other States. It was clear that things could not go on in this way. 

Every year the States were drifting farther and farther apart. Every year business became worse and worse, for many merchants and shopkeepers closed their stores rather than sell their goods for the valueless paper money. Every year disorder and even crime increased. There was danger that all government would break down, and the country fall into a state of anarchy.

Some people began to talk of appealing to England or some other strong foreign country to take charge of our affairs; but that humiliation would have been too great for 

Americans to endure. Daniel Webster was right when, in after years, he called the union under the Articles of Confederation a "rope of sand."

The Northwest Territory 

Lake of the 

Woods C

MINNESOTA 

L Superio 

In one respect at least the States showed true patriotism and Congress true wisdom. That was in their conduct with regard to the great Western territory which the peace treaty had left in 

American hands.

Some of the original colonies had charters that extended their boundaries to the 

Pacific Ocean, and at first they were inclined to insist that this gave them the right to claim the land to the West as far as the Mississippi.

KENTUCKY 

It was at last agreed that these 

States should give up their rights to 

Congress. The vast territory north of the Ohio and east of the Mississippi was to become public land, which could be sold to settlers, and so give Congress money with which to pay its heavy debts. 

New States were to be formed as fast as the population 

THE NORTHWEST TERRITORY 

The shaded portion represents the territory, also its later division into states.

grew sufficiently. This agreement showed that the States were not wholly selfish. Moreover, it gave the people of all the States an interest in these public lands, in which they had a common ownership, and it assured a great increase in the population and prosperity of the new Nation if only its present difficulties could be overcome.

The Ordinance of 1787 

In 1787, Congress passed an ordinance for the government of this great Northwest Territory. It was a wise and generous piece of legislation. Three things of especial importance were provided for. First, there was to be complete religious freedom everywhere throughout the territory. Second, slavery was forever forbidden within its borders. Third, education was to be encouraged by the sale of part of the public land for the benefit of schools and colleges to be established there.

Under this wise and liberal law the Northwest Territory soon began to fill with settlers. Many of them were Revolutionary soldiers, who took land instead of the money that

Congress still owed them for their services. The soil of this region was rich and covered with forests which contained much valuable timber. The present States of Ohio, Indiana, Michigan, Illinois, and Wisconsin, with part of Minnesota, have been carved out of this great domain. Fully one fifth of all the people in the United States live in these States.

The Constitutional Convention Meets 

Meanwhile events were moving toward a real national union of the thirteen States. A convention of some of the leading men of the country met at Annapolis in 1786. It issued a statement setting forth the unhappy conditions all over the United States and asked Congress to call a general

Congress did so and the Constitutional Convention, as it came to be called, met at Philadelphia May 25, 1787.

This Convention brought together a remarkable body of men.

THE WESTWARD MOVEMENT OF THE FRONTIER, 1775-1800 

70°

Washington and Franklin, in whom every one had confidence, were members, and Washington was chosen to preside. John Adams and Thomas Jefferson were not there because they were both abroad, one as Minister to Great Britain and the other as Minister to France. Two brilliant young men, Alexander Hamilton, of New York, and James

Madison, of Virginia, were delegates. Hamilton was an authority on financial matters and Madison was the most learned student of government in the Convention. He had so much to do with writing the new instrument of govern-

ment that he has been called the "Father of the American Constitution."

Other eminent men in the Convention were John Langdon, of New Hampshire; Rufus King, of Massachusetts; 

Roger Sherman and Oliver Ellsworth, of Connecticut;

Robert and Gouverneur Morris, and James Wilson, of 

Pennsylvania; George Wythe, Edmund Randolph, and 

George Mason, of Virginia; John Rutledge and the two 

Pinckneys, from South Carolina; John Dickinson, of Delaware; William Paterson and William Livingston, of New 

Jersey; Daniel Carroll, of Maryland; and many more.

Debating the Constitution 

There were some delegates two of them were from New

York - who wanted to do nothing more than to revise and strengthen the Articles of Confederation. Most of these men refused to sign the Constitution when it was finally adopted. All the most influential members, however, were agreed that it was necessary to create a wholly new form of government; one that would be strong enough to maintain and rule the union of States as a nation. 

More than once

The debates were long and often warm. the Convention seemed on the point of breaking up, because the differences among the delegates were so sharp. There were men who feared any kind of strong central government, because they thought it might turn into a despotism and destroy the rights both of the States and of the people. There were also men from the smaller States who were afraid larger States would have too much power under the new government. Then there were endless disputes over the institution of slavery, which some wanted to do away with at once, while others resented any interference with it. There was continual need of patience and reasonableness among the men who directed the deliberations of the convention.

The Three Great Compromises 

In the end three great compromises were reached. It was voted to have a Congress of two houses. One was to be elected on the basis of population, so the larger States would have their proportionate influence there. In the other the Senate each State, large or small, was to have two votes, no more and no less. That arrangement quieted the fears of most of the delegates from the small States. 

The second compromise was over the question of whether slaves should be counted in determining the representation of each State in Congress. The Northern States objected that since the Negroes were not voters, to include them would give the white voters of the South an unfair representation. This argument was settled by agreeing to include three fifths of the slaves in the estimate of population on which seats in Congress were.apportioned. 

The third compromise grew out of the proposal to give Congress entire control over foreign trade and to forbid the States to levy any tariff taxes on trade either with other countries or among themselves. The Southern States hesitated over this. Their consent was purchased by an agreement to permit the slave trade to continue for twenty years more, though most of the Convention would have preferred to stop it at once. 

What the Constitution Provided

By these compromises enough votes were finally secured to pass the new Constitution and submit it for ratification by the different States. The final vote was taken on September 17, 1787. The Convention had been four months in session.

The important features of the new government proposed were these:

There was to be a strong central government with a Presi-

dent, elected for four years, to have power to execute the federal laws. (These laws were spoken of as federal rather than national to satisfy some of the delegates who still thought of the union as a federation of States rather than a true nation.)

Congress was given power to get the money it needed by taxation of the people, instead of by asking the several States to give it. At the same time absolute freedom of trade between the States was enacted.

A Federal Supreme Court was established to decide all questions between citizens of different States, and to determine any disputes over the extent of the authority of the Federal Government and the States under the Constitution. 

This court later assumed the right to decide whether a law passed by Congress was in accordance with the Constitution or not. Many times it has set aside laws that it found unconstitutional. 

Checks and Balances

The three departments executive, legislative, and judicial were declared to be independent of one another, according to a theory, popular in those days, that tyranny always grew out of the union of these powers under one control.

There was also an elaborate system of checks and balances in the Constitution by which the different departments could exercise some control over one another to prevent any from becoming too powerful. The President could veto a law passed by Congress, and it took a twothirds vote of both House and Senate to repass it over his veto. The Senate was given power either to confirm or reject the President's appointments to important offices. 

The House of Representatives could also impeach a President if it thought he was misusing his authority, and by a

two-thirds vote of the Senate he could be turned out of office. The federal judges could be impeached in the same way; while the justices of the Supreme Court in turn could interpret and, as it turned out, even set aside, acts of Congress which they thought contrary to the Constitution. 

The Bill of Rights 

The new Constitution was criticized by many because it contained no bill of rights. By a bill of rights is meant a series of provisions securing the rights of citizens as to life, liberty, property, and fair treatment in the courts against any possible action of the government. This was a matter which had been simply overlooked by the Constitution makers. No one objected to a bill of rights, and one was promptly supplied by the first ten Amendments to the Constitution.

The Convention in New York 

The new Constitution was presented for adoption or rejection, to conventions specially elected in each State. It was by no means certain that it would be accepted; in some 

States, indeed, there was from the first a popular majority against it. Only the more intelligent and unselfish Americans really understood how necessary it was to have an effective central government. There were many who still feared that such a government would prove tyrannical, and many others who could not bear the idea of surrendering any of the independence which their native States possessed.

There were, therefore, two parties among the people, the Federalists who stood strongly for the Constitution and the Anti Federalists who opposed it. The contest grew warm. 

On one side were men like Washington, Franklin, Madison, Hamilton, John Adams, Sherman, and John Jay. On the

ΙΟΟ  

other were men of equal patriotism but narrower views, like 

Samuel Adams, Patrick Henry, Richard Henry Lee, and 

George Clinton.

The struggle in New York was of especial importance, for if New York stayed out of the new union, the chief seaport of the country would be lost, and the proposed nation would be cut in two in the middle with no connection between the two parts. 

HAMILTON PARADE IN NEW YORK, IN CELEBRATION OF THE ADOP- TION OF THE CONSTITUTION 

At first the chances were all in favor of rejection. Two of 

New York's three delegates at Philadelphia had refused to sign the Constitution, and Governor Clinton, who had great political influence in the State, was opposed to adoption. 

The Federalist

As it happened, the Constitution was finally ratified by the convention in New York almost entirely because of the efforts of Alexander Hamilton. This brilliant young man, who had been Washington's favorite aide-de-camp during

ΙΟΙ the war, and who was now a successful lawyer, threw himself into the fight with enthusiasm and intelligence. 

Hamilton, together with Madison and Jay, wrote the series of newspaper articles, since collected into a book called The Federalist, explaining and defending the Constitution. They were extraordinary articles, as clear and forcible as anything that has ever been written on the subject of government, and they made a very deep impression. 

They were the more effective because every one knew that the Constitution was not at all what Hamilton would have liked it to be. He was in favor of a very much stronger national government than the one that was actually provided. His enemies even said he would like to have established a monarchy. 

The Constitution Ratified

Having prepared the ground with these articles, Hamilton went to the State convention. The majority was at first hostile, but by the solidity of his arguments and his eloquence he actually managed to win over enough votes to bring about ratification. One by one the smaller States adopted the Constitution, though often by slender margins and after long and heated discussion. By June, 1788, the nine necessary ratifications had been voted, and the Constitution was declared to be in effect. Neither Virginia nor 

New York was among this first nine; but they followed quickly. Rhode Island, the last State to ratify, did not do 

30 until 1790.

Doubtful as the issue had been, the result was accepted by every one, and in many parts of the country there were great rejoicings over the foundation of the new Nation. In 

Philadelphia the celebration of the event on the 4th of July, 1788, was especially noisy and enthusiastic. 

Bells were rung, cannons were discharged, and there was a great pro-

cession of people. Many public meetings were held where orators declaimed over the glorious future of the United States. At night the houses were illuminated with candles in every window.

Washington Elected President 

By common consent George Washington was chosen to be the first President, and though he would have preferred to remain quietly in his beautiful home at Mount Vernon, he felt that it was his duty to accept. John Adams, of 

Massachusetts, was elected Vice-President.

Activities Compare the Articles of Confederation with our present Constitution with regard to the powers of Congress in raising and supporting an army; with regard to the power of taxation and the financial support of a government; with regard to executive and judicial authority.

The people of 1781 feared despotism rather than anarchy. 

Learn what these words mean; discuss in class the dangers of each.

As the son of a soldier killed in the Revolution, write a letter to a friend telling how much trouble you have because the Continental money will buy so little; tell of your joy at finding a hundred silver dollars your father has hidden.

On an outline map show the boundaries of each of the thirteen States and of the Northwest Territory. Show how the Allegheny 

Mountains form a barrier between the coast regions and the interior of the country.
