UNIT V HOW THE FEDERALISTS CARRIED ON THE NEW GOVERNMENT 

HIS unit will show you how President Washington and

TH his advisers undertook the difficult task of actually making the new Government, outlined in the Constitution, work. We shall read of the remarkable ability which 

Alexander Hamilton displayed in setting the Government firmly on its financial feet, and convincing the world of its ability and willingness to maintain order and pay its debts. 

We shall see how the people began to divide into political parties, and how the Federalist Party, which Hamilton led and with which Washington was in sympathy, so conducted the affairs of the Nation that a strong foundation was laid for its future strength and prosperity. 

Washington Inaugurated 

In April, 1789, Washington left Mount Vernon for New

York, which was to be the temporary capital of the new Nation. He rode all the way on horseback; every town and village through which he passed greeted him with displays of affection and marks of honor. Over the bridge at Trenton, which he had crossed to fight the battle of Princeton, there was thrown a handsome triumphal arch, which had been designed by the women of the town. bore the words, "The Defender of the Mothers will be the 

Protector of the Daughters." It 

At New York he was received with salutes of cannon and cheering crowds. His whole journey was one long demonstration of the love and admiration that the people felt for him.

On April 30 he was inaugurated on the balcony of

Federal Hall in Wall Street. To this day his bronze statue stands on the steps of the Sub-Treasury building, which now occupies the ground where Federal Hall once stood. 

FEDERAL HALL, NEW YORK CITY 

This building, the first Capitol of the United States, stood on Wall Street, where the Sub-Treasury building now stands The new Government did not get very promptly under way. Congress had first to pass laws creating the various departments, and defining the powers of the officers who were to conduct them. It was almost five months after the inauguration of President Washington before Alexander

Hamilton, Washington's choice for the important post of Secretary of the Treasury, took office, and it was still longer before Thomas Jefferson began to serve as our first Secretary of State, or Foreign Minister.

The First Cabinet 

The two other members of Washington's Cabinet were Henry Knox, of Massachusetts, who had been chief of

artillery and one of General Washington's most faithful lieutenants during the Revolutionary War, and Edmund 

Randolph, of Virginia. Knox was Secretary of War and 

Randolph was Attorney-General.

The President also had to appoint a number of federal judges, including an entire Supreme Court. For Chief 

Justice he chose John Jay, an eminent lawyer and earnest patriot of New York.

The first Congress had many very important matters to decide. It had both to create an entire National Government, and to establish an orderly method of raising the money to carry it on. Fortunately it was made up of unusually able and hard-working men, and within its term of two years it laid the foundations of our system of government so firmly that few important changes have ever had to be made in it. 

Hamilton's Financial Policy

The most pressing problem was the financial one. The 

United States was heavily in debt. It owed more than fifty millions of dollars, the cost of the Revolutionary War, which the old Confederation had never been able to pay. It had no credit either, for no one would lend money to a country that could not pay its debts.

It was the duty of Hamilton, as Secretary of the Treasury, to find a way out of this unhappy situation. He acted boldly, and, as it proved in the end, with brilliant success. 

First of all he insisted that Congress must agree to assume all the old debts of the Confederacy, and to pay them in full. 

Not only that, but the unpaid debts of the various States that they had incurred in raising, feeding, and equipping troops, must also be assumed and paid, he said. Nothing else would be honest and nothing else would restore the lost credit of the Nation.

Hamilton did not find it hard to persuade Congress to pay the debts of the Confederacy, but difficulty arose over the state debts because some of the States had paid off most of their debt, while others still owed very large sums. 

ALEXANDER HAMILTON 

Naturally the people of 

States no longer in debt objected to being taxed to pay the heavy obligations of the States that were still in debt. 

Locating the New Capital 

For a time it looked as if 

Hamilton would fail to carry this part of his plan; but he found a way to do it. Congress was debating at this time where the permanent capital of the United States should be placed. It was determined not to have it in either Philadelphia or New York, but to build a new city to be the capital. The Northern people wanted it somewhere on the Delaware or Susquehanna Rivers; the Southerners wished it to be on the Potomac. 

Hamilton was shrewd enough to arrange an exchange of a number of Northern votes for the Potomac site for some I Hamilton was born in the West Indies in 1757, and was educated at Kings College (now Columbia) in New York. At nineteen he entered the patriot army and became Washington's military secretary. After the war he became a lawyer and entered politics, working both in the New York legislature and in Congress for a stronger union under a Constitution. 

He was Secretary of the Treasury from 1789-1795. As the result of a long political feud with Aaron Burr, Hamilton, in 1804, became involved in a duel with Burr and was killed. Hamilton was by nature an aristocrat and doubted the ability of the common people to sustain a proper government.

Southern votes for his financial plan. In this way he got 

Congress to agree to take over the twenty-five million dollars of state war debts, while it was determined to put the federal city which was to be named Washington on the banks of the Potomac. 

Tariffs and Excises

As soon as the matter of state debts was settled, it was necessary to find ways of raising the revenue that would be necessary, not only to pay the running expenses of government, but also the interest and the principal on this great debt (including the state debts) of seventy-five million dollars. To meet this need, Hamilton proposed and Congress voted a number of taxes.

One was a tariff tax on articles imported into the country such as wine, tea, coffee, spirits, sugar, molasses, and manufactured articles of all kinds. It is interesting to notice that even in 1790, when this first tariff law was passed, it was drawn, not only to produce revenue, but also to "protect" our manufacturing industries, which were then small and weak, against the competition of goods made in Europe. The duties were at first low, but they were increased until they brought in a great deal of money. 

Another important tax was the excise tax on liquor that was made in this country. Hamilton was opposed to a land tax which many people wanted, because land was sufficiently taxed by the States.

Public Credit Restored 

The result of these measures was just what Hamilton had expected. As soon as people with money, at home and abroad, learned that the United States meant to pay all its obligations and was getting the money to do so, our national credit became almost as good as that of England. We could

IIO  

borrow all the money we needed on reasonable terms. Our new tariff law enabled foreign merchants to send their goods to any port, and have a uniform duty to pay on them instead of having to reckon on different duties in New York, Philadelphia, Boston, or Charleston. So trade revived, and money began to circulate once more. 

A National Bank

Hamilton followed these measures with one for the establishment of a National Bank. He had no little difficulty in getting Congress to agree, for the existing state banks were very jealous of his "Bank of the United States," which they thought would put them out of business. But the business men were in favor of it, for the new bank could issue bills that were good everywhere, while the bills of the state banks were of differing value and often could not be passed outside their own State.

A mint was established in Philadelphia to coin silver and gold money, and at the same time the decimal coinage of dollars and cents was adopted. Up to this time Americans had reckoned their money in the English way, by pounds, shillings, and pence, though the values of those coins were not always exactly what they were in England. 

Altogether the achievements of Hamilton in establishing our finances on a firm and lasting foundation were remarkable. In later years Daniel Webster said of them without exaggeration: "He touched the corpse of the public credit, and it sprung to its feet. He smote the rock of public revenue, and copious streams of wealth gushed forth." 

Two Parties are Formed

From the first the people were divided by a well-marked difference of opinion concerning the powers of the States and of the Federal Government under the new Constitu-

III tion. Because of this difference of opinion political parties arose.

The Federalists, who believed in a strong central government, took what is called a broad construction of the Constitution. They held that the Congress and the President ought to have authority to do everything that the Constitution did not forbid them to do. They said that there were all sorts of rights implied in the language of the Constitution, and that Congress could pass laws based on these implied powers, as well as on those that were granted in so many words.

The other party, called at first Anti-Federalists, but later Republicans or Democratic-Republicans, was jealous for the rights of the States, and favored a strict construction of the Constitution. They said that the Federal Government had only such powers as the Constitution expressly gave it, and no more; and they were in constant fear lest the central government should come to have more power than the States which had created it. 

Hamilton and Jefferson

Hamilton was the real leader of the Federalists and Jefferson of the Republicans. They were very remarkable men, thorough patriots both, but singularly unlike in their temperaments, their ideas, and their political principles. Hamilton always distrusted the wisdom and even the intelligence of the mass of the people; Jefferson believed the old saying that the voice of the people was the voice of God. Hamilton thought that government ought to be kept in the hands of the leaders, and used to direct and control the rest. Jefferson was for as little government as was consistent with order and public safety. Tyranny, the oppression of the 

I Do not confuse this party with the present Republican Party, which is descended from the Federalists rather than from their opponents.

common man, was what he was afraid of, as Hamilton's constant fear was anarchy and the breaking up of society into small and quarrelsome fragments. 

Federalists and Republicans

Naturally enough the wealthy, the conservative, and the intellectual people were mostly Federalists. They shared 

Hamilton's fear of the common people. They wanted quiet, orderly, steady conditions, under which trade would be profitable, and life would go on as they were accustomed to have it.

The poorer members of the community, and especially the pioneers and backwoodsmen who were opening up the wilderness, were enthusiastic Republicans. They often owed money the richer and more prosperous Federalist merchants, and they resented very deeply the Federalists' attitude of superiority. Without being disorderly, they had no such attachment to established things as the Federalists had. Change of one sort or another might often 

THOMAS JEFFERSON 

to ¹ Thomas Jefferson was a lawyer and a patriot. He was born in Virginia in 1743, and was educated at William and Mary College. He became in succession member of the Virginia House of Burgesses, member of Congress, Governor of Virginia, Minister to France, Secretary of State, Vice-President, and President. He was the author of the Declaration of Independence. He was not an orator, but he was unrivaled as a politician and manager of men. 

He was a convinced liberal and a firm believer in the collective wisdom of the people. He was simple in his tastes and his manners, and as President dispensed with most of the forms and ceremonies that Washington and 

Adams had adopted.

be to their advantage, and they had a stubborn dislike of being governed too much, which they got from their colonial ancestors.

What the Republicans Objected to 

The Republicans were often in revolt against certain undemocratic features of the Government. One of these was the limitation of the suffrage. By no means every citizen could vote. The laws varied in the different States, but in all of them there was some property qualification - usually the ownership of a certain amount of land—which the voter had to meet. It is said that in 1790 there was only one voter to every fifteen persons. Today between one third and one half of the total population are voters. 

The indirect election of the President was another check on democracy. Not only was he chosen by an electoral college, small in number, but in many States these electors were chosen, not by the people, but by the legislatures. 

Gradually that has been done away with. The electors are now chosen by popular vote, and they can vote only for the candidates preferred by the party which elects them. 

Rise of Party Feeling 

The party quarrels of Federalists and Republicans came to be carried on with a great deal of bitterness. The Federalists were accused of being monarchists, enemies of liberty, and oppressors of the common man. They retorted by calling the Republicans traitors to the new Union, enemies of law and order, and other hard names.

The newspapers of the day were full of abuse of the public men of the opposite party, and before he left office, Washington himself, whom every one had welcomed to the presidency, was so venomously attacked that he said, "a Nero, a notorious defaulter, or a common pickpocket" could not have had worse said of him.

The Whiskey Rebellion 

The opponents of Federalism were by no means ready to obey all the laws which Congress passed whether they liked them or not. Among the mountain people of Pennsylvania and Virginia there was determined resistance to Hamilton's excise tax on liquor. These mountaineers had long been in the habit of turning much of the corn they raised into whiskey, and they thought the Government had no right to tax the liquor they made in their homes. They drove off the agents sent to collect the tax, and even tarred and feathered some of them. The disorder increased until there were serious riots which the State Government of Pennsylvania could not, or at least did not, suppress.

So President Washington sent a military force into the mountains which soon put down this Whiskey Rebellion, as it was called. After this exhibition of federal power, there was less open resistance to the laws that Congress passed. 

Virginia and Kentucky Resolutions

A few years later, in 1798, some unpopular measures called the Alien and Sedition Laws were passed. You will read more about them in the next unit. The Republicans were very angry at these laws, which, they said, showed that the Federal Government meant to interfere with freedom of speech and of the press. 

The legislatures of Virginia and Kentucky passed bold resolutions declaring that these laws were contrary to the Constitution and that there was no obligation on any one to obey them. The Kentucky Resolutions are said to have been written by Jefferson himself, and the Virginia Resolutions were certainly written by Madison. As it happened, Congress repealed the laws the very next year; so the dispute about them died out; but these resolutions are of importance because they first declared the States' Rights doc-

trine that a State could refuse to recognize or enforce a federal law of which its people did not approve. 

Why the Federalists Fell

The Federalist Party remained in power twelve years. 

Washington was President for eight years. He refused to accept a third term - which he might have had — and John Adams was elected to succeed him. The services of the party to the country were great, in spite of some mistakes and some unwise legislation; for it accomplished the organization of the government, and made it so efficient, financially and politically, that there was no longer any doubt about the ability of the once discordant colonies to make a single strong and prosperous nation.

There were a great many able men in the Federalist Party; indeed, it suffered from too much talent and ambition on the part of its leaders. It finally fell from power more on account of quarrels within its own ranks, between men who felt themselves fit to take the lead in government, than for any other reason. Adams and Hamilton were especially hostile to each other; if they had worked well together, the Federalists might have remained in power at least another four years. However, the increasing power of the masses of the people, who were followers of Jefferson because he so heartily believed in them, would sooner or later have put the party he led into power. 

Washington's Farewell Address 

It

On his retirement from office, Washington published a 

Farewell Address to the people of the United States. was a remarkable paper, full of warm patriotism, sound sense, and wise counsel. He begged Americans to stand for high principles in government; to avoid faction, corruption, and unfair party advantage. He urged them to cultivate

a "cordial, habitual, and immovable attachment" to the Union. He advised them always to treat other nations in a fair and friendly spirit, but to be careful not to make any permanent alliances with any portion of the foreign world." All Americans ought to read and remember the 66

UNIT VI HOW THE UNITED STATES GAINED THE 

TH 

RESPECT OF FOREIGN NATIONS 

HE United States was formed, supplied with a sound government, and launched on its career as a nation. 

It was still small and weak as compared with many of the older nations of Europe, and our democratic form of government was still an experiment, which those older nations did not expect to succeed. In this unit we are to see how the 

United States, under the leaderships of Washington, Adams,

Jefferson, and Madison, gradually won the respect of the world; how it showed that it dared resist ill treatment, and was strong enough and determined enough to do so successfully. In the process it was necessary to declare our war against France - which came to little- and to fight a second war with Great Britain. Out of these troubles we shall see our country emerging more united and more selfconfident than ever before.

The French Revolution 

The early years of our existence as a nation came at a time when the world was upset as a result of that extraordinary event called the French Revolution. It required all the tact, good judgment, and patience of Washington and Adams to keep us out of serious trouble with one or another of the European nations.

The violent phase of the revolution in France began almost as soon as Washington was seated in the presidential chair. Affairs went on from the overturning of all the old laws of France by the National Assembly, and frequent

disorders in Paris and elsewhere, to the execution of King Louis XVI and his queen, Marie Antoinette. Then came the formation of a republic, and a reign of terror, in which some thousands of the nobility and of those who objected to the violence of the revolutionary leaders, lost their heads by the guillotine.

Following these bloody events, France found herself involved in war with the countries around it. England, Prussia, and Austria were all very much afraid that the spirit of the Revolution would spread to their own people, and their rulers were terribly shocked at the way in which royal persons and others were being put to death by the revolutionists.

Our own troubles began when France and England went to war. Though we claimed the right to trade freely with both countries, England seized all of our ships that her navy could catch on their way to France, and the French in return captured any that they could find trading with England.

The French tried also to force us to take up arms as their allies; and pointed to the treaty of alliance made in 1778, in which the two countries promised to come to each other's aid when either was attacked.

The United States is Neutral 

Washington, however, was determined not to get drawn into the fight. He felt wisely that the United States was not yet sufficiently strong nor firmly enough established to afford another war. He knew that the country would suffer severely, whichever side it took; and that if it once permitted itself to get involved in the quarrels and enmities of the European nations, it would never be able to keep out of them.

Accordingly, he took the ground that the Treaty of 1778

was no longer binding, because it had been made with King Louis, who had died and left no successor; and he issued a proclamation of neutrality warning all American citizens against taking the part of either France or England. This was in 1793. It was with this proclamation that the American policy of isolation, or careful avoidance of meddling in the politics or the disagreements of Europe, began. 

The Episode of M. Genet 

Most of the people recognized the wisdom of Washington's position; but the ardent Republicans attacked it bitterly. They sympathized warmly with the French, who, as they said, were fighting the battles of democracy in Europe, and they thought we ought to go to their assistance. 

They formed Jacobin clubs, named in imitation of the revolutionary clubs of Paris, wore the three-colored cockade of red, white, and blue in their hats, and were loud in their abuse of Washington. Jefferson himself was in hearty sympathy with France, though as Secretary of State he was wise enough to do nothing in active opposition to the President's policy of neutrality. 

The Revolutionary leaders in France were angry enough at our unwillingness to fight on their side. Their Minister to this country, Edmond Genet, did his best to defy the President, and to stir up the American people against him. 

He helped to fit out privateers in our ports, which were to sail secretly and make war on British merchant ships; and he addressed the democratic Jacobin clubs, with very improper abuse of the President.

He became so offensive that Washington had to request the French Government to recall him. They could do nothing else; but although Genet ceased to be Minister, he did not return permanently to France. He married an American woman, settled down in this country, and finally be-

came a naturalized citizen. He lies buried at East Greenbush, New York, not far from Albany. 

Trouble with Great Britain

At the same time that we were having difficulties with France, we were having even greater trouble with Great Britain. The navy of that country was exceedingly busy seizing our ships that tried to trade with France. What was more intolerable, the crews of her ships boarded our merchant vessels and took off any sailors whom they believed to be natives of Great Britain, whether or not they were naturalized Americans.

Besides all this, British garrisons still held a few posts on our side of the Canadian boundary line. They refused to give up these forts until the United States agreed to pay damages to Loyalists whose property had been seized or destroyed during the Revolution.

The Jay Treaty 

To settle these questions, which were quite capable of ending in war, Washington sent John Jay, the Chief Justice, to London to negotiate a treaty. The treaty, signed in 

1794, did settle some of the matters in dispute. The United 

States agreed to pay the Loyalist claims, and Great Britain agreed to evacuate all posts on American territory and to pay for the American ships her navy had seized. Jay could not get the British to give up taking naturalized American sailors off our ships; for they still held to the principle once an Englishman, always an Englishman." Neither would they promise to seize no more of our ships trading with France. 66 For these reasons Jay's treaty was unpopular enough. 

He was burned in effigy in some places; there were street riots before the houses of British consuls in New York and

other cities, and Hamilton was actually stoned while speaking to a crowd in defense of the treaty. 

In the end the President's influence was strong enough to get the treaty ratified by the Senate. An unsatisfactory treaty, he said, was better than a disastrous war, and sensible people agreed with him.

The Battle at Falling Timbers 

To add to our difficulties a serious uprising of Indians broke out just at this time in Ohio. The red men were greatly alarmed at the way white settlers were pouring into that country. They dug up the war hatchet and began to raid the white settlements, burning and killing in the familiar Indian fashion. They were finally beaten by General 

Wayne, the hero of the attack on Stony Point during the 

Revolutionary War. This battle at Falling Timbers, on the

Maumee River, broke the spirit of the Indians for at least fifteen years. 

Trouble with France

For the last four years of his term, Washington, by his prudence and patience, kept the young republic out of war. 

His successor, John Adams, was less fortunate. Soon after his inauguration, Adams sent three commissioners, John 

Marshall, Charles C. Pinckney, and Elbridge Gerry, to

Paris to see if we could not persuade the French to stop seizing our merchant ships as they had been doing. 

The commissioners had no success, and they were informed by secret agents of the government that they could not hope for any unless the United States would pay a considerable bribe to the members of the Directory which then ruled France.

The Americans were indignant, of course; one of them, Pinckney, is said to have exclaimed, "Millions for defense;

not one cent for tribute!" These words became the rallying-cry of patriotic Americans when the facts became known.

The X Y Z Papers Lead to War 

The President himself sent the commissioners' report of the attempted bribery to Congress, but without giving the names of the French agents, whom he called X, Y, and Z. 

For this reason the reports were called the "X Y Z papers.' The affair aroused the country, and there was a demand for war with France.

Arrangements were made to enlist men and reorganize our little army. Washington left his retirement to become its chief, and at his request

Adams unwillingly appointed 

Hamilton second in command. 

There was no fighting on land, however; though each nation did all it could to interfere with the commerce of the other. American warships or privateers captured no less than eighty-four prizes. 

France soon found it was getting nothing out of a war with us, when it needed all its strength for its wars in Europe; 

JOHN ADAMS 

I John Adams was born at Braintree, Massachusetts, in 1735. He graduated from Harvard and became a lawyer. He was among the most earnest of patriots, and eminent in the Continental Congress for his power in debate. He was one of the Peace Commissioners in 1783, and served as our envoy to France and Minister to Great Britain. He was Vice-President, 1789-1797, and President, 1797-1801. He lived to the age of ninety, dying on July 4, 1826.

and when, in 1799, Napoleon Bonaparte rose to power as First Consul of France, he lost no time in making peace. 

Alien and Sedition Laws 

It was while this little war was in progress that the Alien and Sedition Laws, about which you read on page 114, were passed. The Alien Law authorized the President to send out of the country any foreign-born person whose presence he thought in any way dangerous. The Sedition Law provided for the punishment of any one who spoke or wrote maliciously or abusively about the President or the Government of the United States. 1 The severity of these laws and particularly the interference with free criticism of the Government aroused a great deal of opposition. The Republicans were especially indignant. You have read how the legislatures of Virginia and Kentucky voted that no State was bound to obey and enforce these laws;¹ and although they were repealed before the presidential election of 1800, they were one of the chief issues in that campaign. They played a part in causing the defeat of Adams, the overthrow of the Federalists, and the choice of Jefferson as President. 

France and Great Britain Seize Our Ships

Jefferson was by nature a man of peace, and during the eight years that he was President he managed to keep the United States out of actual warfare. That was not an easy thing to do; for France under Napoleon — who made himself Emperor in 1804 – and England under its great War Minister, William Pitt a son of the William Pitt who was so good a friend to America during the Revolution were almost continually at war. 

Each nation tried to injure the other by keeping other

For a ship sailing in 1796 to the West Indies lon Juty with very busily engaged in trade with both France and Great Britain, and if their ships had been let alone, they would have made a great deal of money out of this trade. 

Jefferson Complains in Vain

But both Great Britain and France captured our ships and sold their cargoes whenever they got a chance. President Jefferson complained in vain; neither nation paid any attention to him. Hundreds of vessels were seized in this way, contrary to all law and justice as the Americans believed. There were repeated occasions for going to war, and it began to appear that only by fighting could the United

States make these European nations respect its rights; but 

Jefferson would never go to the point of declaring war. 

The Territory of Louisiana 

Quite early in his administration, Jefferson had an opportunity to do something of the greatest importance for the future peace and prosperity of the country. The vast territory of Louisiana, which stretched from the Mississippi River to the Rocky Mountains, was suddenly offered for sale. Louisiana had passed back and forth between France and Spain. First one country owned it and then the other. 

In 1800, Napoleon obliged Spain to cede all her rights in this great territory to France.

The Americans who lived in the Mississippi Valley were much alarmed at this. They had always had trouble with 

Spain over the navigation of the Mississippi River, which was their natural way of reaching the sea; and they were afraid they would have even more with France, a far stronger and more ambitious power. 

Napoleon Sells Louisiana to the United States

But Napoleon soon decided that Louisiana would be more trouble than benefit to him. It was too far away; he was so busy fighting his great wars in Europe that he had no men or money to spare for defending Louisiana. He was afraid England would send an army down from Canada to seize it, and so, in order to keep Louisiana out of the hands of his enemy, England, he offered to sell it to the 

United States for fifteen million dollars.

Jefferson was only too glad to have the chance to add this immense territory to the United States, especially because its purchase would give us complete control of the Mississippi River from its source to its mouth. The treaty was quickly negotiated, the money was paid over, and in 1803

we took possession of New Orleans and St. Louis, the two most important places in Louisiana.

This transaction almost doubled the area of the United States. Napoleon is said to have declared when he signed the treaty, "By this treaty the power of the United States is established forever. I have given to England a maritime rival that will, sooner or later, humble her pride."

The Importance of Louisiana 

Most Americans did not look so far into the future as Napoleon, but they saw that the possession of Louisiana meant that one possible enemy, France, was no longer a neighbor; that another possible enemy, Great Britain, could not now encircle us; and that we had gained complete control of the great river, down which American trade could pass to an American port at its mouth.

It is interesting to notice that, in making this purchase on his own responsibility as President, Jefferson did something that many persons thought he had no right to do. 

According to his own earlier views of the Constitution, the Nation could not do anything of the sort without the consent of the separate States. When the opportunity came, however, Jefferson was too wise to hesitate. He saw at once the immense value of Louisiana to his country, and he forgot his constitutional scruples. The purchase of Louisiana was a long step toward the strengthening of the National Government at the expense of the States. 

The Embargo

Meanwhile our troubles with France and Great Britain on the sea remained unsettled. Our ships were being seized by both navies; while the British continued to take American sailors off the ships so seized, and make them serve in the British navy, on the pretense that they had been born in Great Britain.

Jefferson would not fight. He thought that he could make France and Great Britain come to terms by forbidding all trade of any sort with Europe. The European countries were now buying a great part of their food supplies from us; and they depended on our merchant ships for a good deal of the business of carrying cargoes from one country to another. Jefferson's idea was that if we refused to do any business at all with Europe, it would make life so uncomfortable there that France and Great Britain would be driven to letting our ships alone. 

Accordingly he got Congress to pass (in December, 1807) an Embargo Act that forbade American ships to sail to any European country. This law did not work at all as he hoped it would. Neither France nor England altered their behavior in the slightest. Somehow or other Europe managed to get all the food she needed, and no nation interfered to make France and England come to terms with us. 

The only people who were hurt were our own merchants, farmers, and shipowners. Our trade went all to pieces; prices fell because we could not sell our food and other articles of commerce abroad; our seaports were deserted and our vessels lay idle at the wharves. The effect was especially serious in New England, for most of the ships in our foreign trade were built and owned there. Many merchants and shipowners, previously wealthy, were quite ruined. 

The Embargo Fails

The Embargo Act was so complete a failure that Congress soon repealed it; and in place of it passed a Non-Intercourse Act, which forbade trade with Great Britain and France only. That helped matters a little, but our commerce amounted to only a fraction of what it had been before the rash experiment of Embargo.

Jefferson found much of his popularity gone as a result of these measures. Still his party was so much stronger now than the Federalists that he might have had a third term if he had wished for it. However, he refused to consider another reëlection. He believed that no President should have more than two terms in office. When he followed 

Washington's example in declining a third term, he established a tradition that has lasted till the present day. No 

President has ever served more than eight years. Jefferson was succeeded by James Madison, his Secretary of State. Madison was also a Virginian. Do you remember that it was he who was called the "Father of the Constitution," because of the important part he took in drawing up that instrument? 

President Madison tried to preserve peace as Jefferson had done, but events proved too strong for him. The resentment that Americans felt at the treatment their ships and their commerce received at the hands of the fighting nations of Europe grew more and more intense. At last, in 

1812, war broke out. There had been some doubt whether 

France or Great Britain should be the nation we were to challenge; but Great Britain had given us even more provocation than France. Her practice of impressing sailors for her navy, out of American ships, made our people furiously angry, and it was Great Britain against whom war was finally declared.

A World War in 1812 

Our War of 1812, as it is called, was really part of a great James Madison was born in Virginia in 1751. He was a graduate of 

Princeton, a lawyer and a student of law and government. 

He was a leader in the Virginia House of Burgesses and the Continental Congress; and so influential in the convention that drew up the Constitution that he has been called the "Father of the Constitution." He was later in Congress again, Secretary of State under Jefferson, and President, 1809-1817.

MAP OF THE FIELD OF WAR, 1812-1814 

World War almost exactly one hundred years before the great struggle of 1914-1918. Almost all Europe was involved in it. Napoleon, Emperor of France, with the help of some of the nations he had overcome or made his allies among them Italy, Holland, Poland, and Saxony - was fighting Great Britain, Prussia, Russia, Austria, and Spain.

Great armies fought all over Europe from Moscow to the mountains of Spain. There were battles at sea, too, though there the victories of the English Admiral Nelson had broken the French naval power. Europe believed that 

France threatened to become the master of the whole Continent, as she believed, in 1914, that Germany was making the same threat.

Our declaration of war with Great Britain really gave some help to Napoleon, though we made no alliance with France. We insisted that we took no sides among the 

European powers, and were fighting only because we could no longer endure Great Britain's treatment of us.

The "War Hawks"

However, the fact that we were, in a way, helping France made the war popular in some parts of the country, and unpopular in other parts. The war party was very strong in the West and South. Its leaders were able, if rather hotheaded young men. Henry Clay, of Kentucky, and John C. Calhoun, of South Carolina, were among the most influential leaders.

These young men, who were now pushing the older statesmen off the stage, were called "war hawks." It was their eloquence and activity that forced Congress into declaring war. They believed that when the fighting was over, we should have conquered 

Canada, and forced Great Britain to promise never to take another sailor off an American ship, whatever his nationality might be. "Free Trade and 

Sailors' Rights" was their battle cry. 

HENRY CLAY I 

In the commercial States, and particularly in New England, the war was never popular. The people there were suspicious of France, which, under Napoleon, seemed to them the enemy of freedom. They were also afraid that after war was declared the British navy would complete the ruin of our merchant fleet which the Embargo had begun, and so destroy the prosperity of New England. 

I Henry Clay was a Virginian, born in 1777. After studying law, he removed to Kentucky, where he practiced law and engaged in politics. He was for many years in Congress and Speaker of the House, one of the negotiators of the Treaty of Ghent, Secretary of State under John Quincy Adams, and long a member of the United States Senate. He was three times an unsuccessful candidate for President. Clay had great charm of manner and no little eloquence. His followers almost worshiped him.

Secession Talked in New England 

As the war went on, this feeling of opposition to the war grew stronger; for a great many of the fears of the New- 

Englanders were realized. In 1814 a convention was held in Hartford, attended by many of the leading Federalists in New England. This convention passed resolutions, somewhat similar to the Kentucky and Virginia Resolutions, looking toward the secession of the New England States unless they were permitted to keep for their own protection during the war the customs duties collected in their ports. Some of the delegates thought secession would be the best plan in any event.

The war came to an end before anything was done about these resolutions; but it is interesting to observe that in 

1814 it was New England and not South Carolina that was talking about secession as a remedy for national conditions to which it objected. 

Our Unpreparedness for War

The "war hawks" had hurried the country into war before we were at all prepared to fight. We had only a very small army, and few officers who had any real experience in the field; for the veterans of the Revolution were too old to fight. Of course the volunteers and the militia, who were called upon to fill up the ranks, had had even less military training. We were without proper supplies and ammunition, and a great deal of money had to be found or borrowed to feed and equip our forces. 

We were a little better off on the sea; for we had a few frigates and sloops-of-war, and more were quickly added by the shipbuilders of New England. In those days warships were built of wood, and they could be constructed quickly, by men who understood their business, from the timber growing along our shores.

Our little navy had had some useful experience in fighting, too; for a few years before this war broke out with Great 

Britain, several vessels had been sent across the sea to punish the Barbary pirates. These lawless people, who lived along the northern coast of Africa, had gone into the 

"OLD IRONSIDES" From a lithograph by Wales. Courtesy of Goodspeed's Book Shop, Boston. 

business of preying on the commerce of all nations who sent ships into the Mediterranean. Many European countries actually paid bribes to these pirates to let their ships alone. 

Our own government had often agreed to pay ransom for Americans who had been taken as prisoners by them. 

Fighting the Barbary Pirates 

President Jefferson made up his mind to pay no more tribute money. In 1804 he sent over a squadron of warships, and they made enough impression on the Bashaw of Tripoli, the chief of the pirates, to persuade him to sign a treaty that gave our ships and seamen some protection against capture and slavery.

In the course of this little war Stephen Decatur won fame by a deed of great boldness. An American frigate, the

Philadelphia, had gone ashore off the coast of Tripoli. The crew abandoned it; but the pirates got it off the reef, towed it into the harbor of Tripoli, and were refitting it to fight the Americans. Decatur sailed a little vessel into the harbor, under the guns of the pirate fleet, laid his craft alongside the Philadelphia, drove off the pirate crew, and set the ship on fire. Then he made his escape. Lord Nelson, the great British Admiral, is said to have called this the most daring naval exploit of his time. 

The Invasion of Canada Fails 

As soon as war was declared with Great Britain, the Americans prepared to invade and conquer Canada. General Hull was to cross the Detroit River and move eastward through Ontario. General Van Rensselaer was to cross the 

Niagara River, seize York (now Toronto), and join his force with Hull's. Both were then to march eastward and meet 

General Dearborn, who was to come up by way of Lake Champlain. The united forces were to attack and, if possible, capture Montreal and Quebec.

This excellent plan fell through, however; for Hull was forced to surrender to a superior force of Canadians near 

Detroit and Van Rensselaer was beaten before he had got far into Canada. Hearing of these disasters, General 

Dearborn fell back without entering Canada at all.

It was a most discouraging year for the Americans on land. "Old Ironsides" Wins a Sea Fight

On the sea, however, things went better. The frigate 

Constitution, commanded by Captain Isaac Hull, met the British frigate Guerrière off Nova Scotia. After a hot fight,

in which the Guerrière was shot to pieces and brought to the point of sinking, the British captain hauled down his flag. 

The Constitution was so well handled that she was very slightly damaged.

This victory aroused great enthusiasm in the United 

States. The Constitution was called "Old Ironsides," because it had defied the British gun-fire so well; and it became the bestloved ship in our little navy. 

It was preserved, like Nelson's flagship, the Victory, after all other wooden warships had been dismantled and sunk. It has lately been com- 

Courtesy N.E. Mutual Life Ins. Co. 

THE SEA BATTLE BETWEEN CONSTITUTION 

pletely restored,

AND GUERRIÈRE 

and may be seen today in the navy yard at Boston almost exactly the same in appearance as when it met the Guerrière. 

Other Victories at Sea

Other single American warships won victories almost as striking. The sloop Wasp captured the British brig Frolic; the frigate United States defeated the British frigate Macedonian off the coast of Africa; the Hornet sank the Peacock in South American waters. The Essex cruised in the Pacific, destroying the British whaling fleet in that ocean, and was only beaten when attacked by two British ships at once.

The Constitution fought and destroyed the British frigate 

Java off Rio Janeiro. 

These victories aroused the pride of the Americans and revived their confidence in the navy. They proved that man for man and ship for ship our navy was at least the equal of the British — an unpleasant discovery for the British, who thought that no other nation could fight them successfully at sea.

The British Blockade Our Ports 

However, the British navy was so much larger than ours that in the end its numbers began to tell. It was able to blockade our ports and prevent our newly built warships from getting out to sea. Once in a while one sailed out to meet the enemy, as the Chesapeake did out of Boston Harbor; but the Chesapeake was engaged by the Shannon, a larger vessel, and forced to surrender after a brave fight. 

Captain Lawrence, its commander, was mortally wounded in the battle; his dying words, "Don't give up the ship!" have become the battle cry of the American navy. 

Indecisive Fighting

On land the war dragged on without much credit to either side. An American expedition sailed across Lake Ontario, took the town of York, the capital of Ontario, and set fire to the Parliament House. Then a British expedition crossed to Sacketts Harbor in New York State, and the Americans had to hurry back from York, to defend their own territory. 

There was marching and counter-marching and some fighting in the neighborhood of Niagara, and along the shores of Lake Champlain, but neither Americans nor British won any decisive advantage over the other.

The Battle of Lake Erie 

After the failure of General Hull at Detroit, there was serious danger that the British might get control of all the upper Great Lakes, and invade our country by an expedition across Lake Erie. Accordingly our navy was called to the rescue, and Captain Oliver H. Perry was sent to take command on Lake Erie. He had almost nothing in the way of an armament to start with, but he set his men at work cut- ting down trees 

Painting by Powell

COMMODORE PERRY AT THE BATTLE OF LAKE ERIE 

and building vessels, until he had a fleet of nine small warships, fairly well armed.

On Septem ber 10, 1813, Perry was attacked by a British fleet of six ships, which, however, carried more guns than his little squadron. The battle was fought at Put-In Bay, and it was an extremely hot engagement. Perry's flagship was sunk, but he escaped from the wreck, and was rowed in a small boat through the thick of the fight to another vessel. 

His twelve-year-old brother was in the boat with him. The boat was hit again and again, and the boy's cap was torn from his head by a bullet, but they gained the deck of the Niagara, from which Perry carried on a winning fight. The 

British ships were all sunk or captured. "We have met the enemy, and they are ours," wrote Perry to General Harrison, who commanded on the land.

After this victory, which made the British cause in the

West well-nigh hopeless, Harrison moved forward against Detroit. He defeated the British, who were aided by a large force of Indians under the great chief Tecumseh, at the battle of the Thames, and recaptured Detroit. The 

British made no further military effort in this part of the country.

Battle of Lake Champlain 

Meanwhile Napoleon had been defeated in Europe and forced to give up the throne of France. That meant that 

From an old engraving

BATTLE OF LAKE CHAMPLAIN 

Notice the strings of open barges which formed a part of both fleets. thousands of British soldiers, who had been fighting under Wellington in Spain and France, could now be spared to fight in the United States. It was planned to use these veterans to invade our country at several different points. 

One force of twelve thousand men moved down from Canada by way of Lake Champlain. Lieutenant (afterwards Commodore) Macdonough was in command of an

American squadron of small vessels, and the British commander, Prevost, who wished to transport his army by water, ordered the British squadron to clear Macdonough out of the way. A lively battle followed off the town of 

Plattsburg. There was little maneuvering. The sailors crowded the decks of their small craft which lay almost side by side, and fired at point-blank range. The losses on both sides were heavy, of course; but the American victory was complete. The entire British squadron was destroyed. 

Without its necessary support Prevost's army was helpless. He made one unsuccessful attempt to take Plattsburg by assault, and then retreated to Montreal. This part of the plan of invasion was a complete failure. 

Washington Taken and Burned

The British had better success farther south. A large fleet suddenly appeared in Chesapeake Bay, and landed an army of veteran soldiers which began to march on Washington, the capital of the country. 

An American force opposed them at Bladensburg; but it was easily brushed aside, and the British moved forward to Washington. Congress and all the federal officials fled in a panic. Mrs. Madison, the wife of the President, loaded what furniture and silver she had time to save from the

White House, and hastened to join her husband, who was with the beaten American army. It is said that Mrs. Madison at this time saved Gilbert Stuart's famous painting of

Washington and the original copy of the Declaration of Independence, which otherwise would have been destroyed; for the British set fire to both Capitol and White House. 

A few weeks later, the British fleet bombarded Fort Mc- 

Henry in Baltimore Harbor, but withdrew without capturing it.

This cannonade is worthy of remembrance, for it was while he watched it that Francis Scott Key wrote the

words of "The Star-Spangled Banner," now the national anthem of our country.

The Battle of New Orleans 

After destroying the city of Washington, the British army took ship and sailed to New Orleans. They planned to seize this city and so control the Mississippi River, up which they could move into the very heart of the country. 

There was a small army of Americans ready to face the British. Its commander was Andrew Jackson, of 

Tennessee, who had been a judge and served in Congress, but had now turned soldier. He had lately beaten the Creek Indians, who had gone on the warpath

against the Americans in a battle in Alabama; but he had never seen service against such an army of trained veterans as the British brought to New Orleans.

His own force consisted of some five thousand frontier militia, backwoodsmen all, without military discipline, but hunters and sharpshooters who knew perfectly well how to handle a rifle. The British commander Pakenham had a somewhat larger army of the best soldiers in Europe. 

He had no doubt of sweeping the buckskin-clad backwoodsmen out of his way. Instead of maneuvering to get in Jackson's rear, he ordered an assault on the breastworks of earth and logs that Jackson's men had thrown up. As the British advanced, the cool-headed Americans held their

fire until the enemy was almost upon them. Then they fired, and almost every bullet accounted for a man.

The British were forced to retreat; but advanced again, and were once more shot to pieces. Pakenham and more than two thousand of his men were killed. The remnants of his fine army fell back to their ships, and not long afterward sailed away, completely defeated.

Peace is Made 

The news of this battle filled the United States with rejoicing, for the Americans had had few victories on land, and none so overwhelming. Andrew Jackson became the popular hero everywhere, but especially in the West and South, where the people almost idolized him, as one of their own, who had saved their homes from invasion and destruction.

As a matter of fact, the battle of New Orleans was fought after the treaty of peace had been signed at Ghent in Belgium by the British and American representatives; but news traveled so slowly in those days that, although the treaty was signed on Christmas Eve, 1814, nothing was known of it in Washington until early in February. The battle of New Orleans was fought on January 8, 1815. 

Results of the War of 1812

Both Great Britain and the United States were glad enough to stop a war which seemed likely to drag on without really settling anything. The treaty of peace did not even refer to the causes of the war- the impressment of sailors and the interference with American trade; but after the overthrow of Napoleon, Great Britain had no occasion to disturb our ships, and it never again tried to take any sailors from them.

The War of 1812, in spite of some brilliant exploits, was

not an especially glorious one for us. However, it had some worth-while results. It helped to bind the States more closely together and aroused an amount of patriotic feeling for the Nation that was quite unknown before. Moreover, it proved that we could and would defend ourselves against insults from other nations; Europe had much more respect for us when that fact was made clear. Finally the necessity of providing articles that had previously been imported led to the establishment of a great many new industries, which, once founded, continued to grow.

The Holy Alliance 

Although the war was over, foreign affairs still occupied much of our attention. Following the overthrow of Napoleon, some of the European nations — Russia, Austria, and Prussia formed what they called the Holy Alliance.

This was really designed to prevent the growth of democratic ideas in Europe and to make still stronger the position of the royal governments in those countries. 

Americans were naturally suspicious of this Alliance, all the more because it threatened to take the side of Spain, whose colonies in Mexico and South America declared their independence from 1815 onward, and soon proved themselves able to win and maintain their freedom. People in 

America were very much afraid that some of the other nations, which were stronger than Spain, would interfere to crush the South American patriots, and either restore the Spanish power or seize the continent for themselves. 

The Monroe Doctrine 

The Americans had always done their best to keep out of European politics and European quarrels. Both Washington and Jefferson had seen that it would be dangerous if not fatal for our young country to take sides in such matters,

and so get involved in the frequent wars of Europe. But as Americans meant to keep out of the affairs of Europe, so they intended that Europe should not interfere with what went on on their side of the water. They were especially determined that no more European colonies and no monarchies of the European sort should be created in either 

North or South America.

Accordingly President Monroe - who had succeeded 

President Madison in 1817-sent a message to Congress 

JAMES MONROE

in 1823 in which he stated what has come to be called the "Monroe Doctrine." It was, in effect, a warning to the European nations to keep their hands off North and South America. These continents, he said, have won their independence, and are not to be considered as subjects for future colonization by any European power. The United 

States, he went on to say, would regard any attempt to extend the monarchical system of Europe to America as an unfriendly act, which it would resist, by war if necessary. 

Effect of the Monroe Doctrine 

Fortunately, in announcing this doctrine, President Monroe had the quiet support of Great Britain. That country 

He served as a soldier in 

He was a member of the 

¹ James Monroe was a Virginian, born in 1758. the Revolution and became a lieutenant-colonel. 

Congress of the Confederation and of the United States Senate after 1789. 

He was also our Minister to France, Governor of Virginia, and for eight years Secretary of State under President Madison. He was himself President, 1817-1825.

was not a member of the Holy Alliance, and did not like the ideas of absolute monarchy that the Alliance was formed to encourage, much better than the people of the United States did. Moreover, Great Britain had all the colonial territory she wanted on this side of the water, and did not care to have any European rival establish itself in America. 

Accordingly, the Monroe Doctrine actually accomplished something. If any of the countries in the Holy Alliance really had it in mind to seize any land for themselves in South America, they had no desire to go to war about it with the United States, and probably with Great Britain as well. The Spanish colonies everywhere in America went on to make their independence sure. Neither then, nor for many years later, did any European nation try to defy the Monroe Doctrine, by interfering in political affairs on the 

American continents.

2. Louisiana. The Embargo (a) Napoleon sells to the United States the territory of Louisiana (b) Jefferson reverses his views of the Constitution in order to buy Louisiana (c) Jefferson proclaims an embargo on all trade with France and England (d) Distress caused by the Embargo Act; it fails to bring either 

France or Great Britain to terms

3. The War of 1812 

(a) The second war with England part of a World War (b) The "war hawks"; they force a declaration of war (c) Unpopularity of the war in New England; secession is discussed there (d) Our unpreparedness; fighting record of our navy against the Barbary pirates (e) American invasion of Canada fails (f) Bravery and skill of the American sea fighters (g) Battles of Lake Erie and Lake Champlain (h) The British invade the United States; Washington burned (i) Defeat of the British at New Orleans (j) Peace is signed at Ghent 

4. The Monroe Doctrine 

(a) Results of the war; the United States wins the respect of Europe (b) The Holy Alliance; America is suspicious of it (c) President Monroe announces his famous "Doctrine" 

(d) What the Monroe Doctrine accomplished 

Questions 

I. What caused the French Revolution? Tell as much as you can of what happened in it. 

2. What was Washington's policy toward France during the 

French Revolution? Was it a wise policy? How has it affected our later history? 

3. Who was M. Genet? What of his experiences in the United States? 

4. What was the Jay Treaty? Why was it unpopular? Why did Washington want it ratified? 

5. What was the battle of Falling Timbers, and what result did it have? 

6. What were the X Y Z papers? Why did they rouse so much indignation? 

7. What were the Alien and Sedition Laws? Tell whether you think they were wise and proper laws. 

8. Describe the way of electing the President in 1800 when

Jefferson was elected. Compare it with the present way.

9. How did France and Great Britain combine to injure our trade? Why did they so behave when they were at peace with us? 

10. Why was Napoleon willing to sell Louisiana to the United 

States? What did he say he had accomplished by signing the treaty? 

II. Why was the purchase of Louisiana so important? Did

Jefferson act in any way contrary to his previous views in making the purchase? 

12. What was the Embargo? What did Jefferson hope to accomplish by it? 

13. Did the Embargo work as he hoped it would? What effect did it have on merchants and shipowners? On public opinion, especially in New England? 

14. Do you think the Embargo and Non-Intercourse Acts were wise? 

15. Compare the world war of 1812-1815 with that of 1914-1918. 

16. Who were the "war hawks"? What did they hope to win through fighting Great Britain? 

17. Why did New England people talk secession during the war? 

Were their reasons good? 

18. What service had our navy seen before the War of 1812? 

Tell the story of Decatur and the Philadelphia. 

19. What was the name of the ship called "Old Ironsides"? 

Tell about her battles. 

20. Tell the story suggested by the phrase, "Don't give up the ship!" 

21. Why did the British burn the public buildings in Washington? Would a present-day army destroy an enemy's capital if it entered it? 

22. Tell the story of the battle of New Orleans. Of what battle in the Revolution does it remind you? 

23. The battle of New Orleans was fought three weeks after peace had been signed. Would that be possible today? If not, what has made the difference? 

24. What is the Monroe Doctrine? Does the United States still adhere to it? How do other nations — especially the South 

American nations now regard it?

Activities Make an oral report on the chief events of the French Revolution to the rise of Napoleon. 

Two men, one a New York merchant, the other a backwoods farmer, meet. The former upholds Washington's neutrality; the latter thinks we should help France. Dramatize the argument. 

As a boy visiting the new town of Washington in 1801, write letter to a friend describing the appearance of the place. Debate the question: Should the United States have joined 

England in fighting Napoleon rather than have declared war against her in 1812?

Using an outline or blackboard map, show clearly the limits of the Louisiana Purchase, and the location of New Orleans and St.

UNIT VII CHANGES THAT TOOK PLACE IN OUR  DURING OUR FIRST FIFTY YEARS 

HE United States of 1830 was a very different country TH from that which had taken the desperate step of declaring its independence in 1776 and which had virtually won that independence at Yorktown. It was a time of great change everywhere in the Western World; it was the time of the French Revolution, of the industrial revolution which substituted machinery for much work that had hitherto been done by hand, and of widespread changes in the social life of every civilized country. In the United States those changes were made more conspicuous by the fact that we were at the same time overrunning a vast forest wilderness in the West and establishing civilization there. In this unit we are to look over the entire American scene and observe the astonishing progress that was made in industry, transportation, education, culture, and social life during that half-century.

During these fifty years that followed the battle of Yorktown the thirteen separate and often discordant colonies had become one nation, conscious of an honorable past and of a greater destiny. To the original thirteen States eleven others had been added before 1830, most of them formed from the western territory beyond the Allegheny Mountains, which had been an almost unbroken wilderness when the Revolution was fought.

Growth of the Country 

The population had increased from a scant three millions

in 1781 to nearly thirteen millions in 1830. A steady stream of immigration was pouring into the country. Reënforced by many people from the older States who hoped to find richer land and greater prosperity in the West, this stream was spreading out over the wide areas of the Ohio and Mississippi Valleys. New towns were springing up overnight, and old ones were growing rapidly larger. 

Trade and commerce were increasing every year, and manufactures, with which the early States had been poorly danoor

FORT DEARBORN, THE BEGINNING OF CHICAGO IN 1831 

supplied, were everywhere being established. The future seemed certain to be still more remarkable, for the opening of the great Northwest Territory, and the purchase of Louisiana had extended our frontiers two thousand miles, to the peaks of the Rocky Mountains; and the possibilities of wealth in such a vast region were boundless. Americans looked forward in 1830, not only with hope, but with confidence and enthusiasm, to the years that were to come.

The Turnpike Era 

The first necessity was for better means of transportation.

At the end of the Revolution there were no first-class roads in the United States and not many that were even passable. 

If the young Nation were to be really united; if the many products of its soil were to move eastward to the sea, so that they could enter the trade of the world and bring in money to the men who raised them, there must be proper means of communication between the different States and between the seaboard and the interior.

The States and the counties within the States promptly set to work to improve old roads and to build new ones; but they had not enough revenue to carry on the work as fast as it was needed. Some States got money for road-building by establishing lotteries, which was a favorite way of raising money for all kinds of purposes in the eighteenth century. Even colleges often resorted to lotteries when they were in need of funds.

Other States followed the example of Pennsylvania, and permitted private citizens to build good turnpike roads, and to get their money back by charging a toll on the persons who used the road. The first successful turnpike road, built by a private corporation, ran between Philadelphia and Lancaster in Pennsylvania. It was constructed in 17911792.

Within twenty years the practice had spread all over the country, and similar roads, often built of planks, were extending in every direction. One hundred and eighty turnpike corporations had been chartered in New England; one hundred and thirty-seven in New York. Eight turnpike roads led out in as many different directions from Albany, like the spokes in a great wheel. Five others joined Newburgh, Kingston, and Catskill-on-the-Hudson with the back country on the Delaware and Susquehanna Rivers. 

The Mohawk Valley and the wide interior of the State were criss-crossed with turnpike roads.
