SCALE OF STATUTE MILES THE NATIONAL, OR CUMBERLAND, ROAD 

It seems strange today to think of paying toll for the right to use a road; but people were glad enough to do it in those days, for the new roads made it possible for them to get their goods to market, and consequently to make a great deal more money.

The National Road 

The most famous of the turnpikes was not a private enterprise, but a government-built road. That road was the 

Cumberland (or National) Road, which ran from Cumberland in Maryland to the Ohio River at Wheeling. It was begun in 1811, and after various extensions across Ohio and Indiana, it reached Vandalia in Illinois in 1838. It was over this road, which cost in the end almost seven million dollars, that the greater part of the immigration into the new States of the Northwest passed; and it was over this road that much of the trade between East and West was carried on, before the building of the railways. 

Stagecoaches, drawn by teams of horses, often changed, rolled along the Cumberland Road, carrying their passengers from one log-built tavern to another, or from one raw frontier town to the next; while stout, canvas-covered, Conestoga wagons, heavily loaded with freight, rumbled back and forth, bearing within their sturdy boat-shaped frames the trade of a nation.

River Transportation 

Even with these improvements transportation was still extremely expensive. It cost a dollar and a half to get a barrel of flour from the farms in central New York to Philadelphia. To send a bushel of salt two or three hundred miles, a man had to pay two dollars and a half. A hundredweight bag of sugar could not be moved the same distance for less than five dollars. It cost one hundred and twentyfive dollars to take a ton of freight from Philadelphia to Pittsburgh.

Accordingly people turned whenever they could to im- 

A RIVER FLATBOAT 

proving the water routes; for it is obviously cheaper to float heavy cargoes down a river or on a canal than to drag them with horses over a road that is often hilly and rough. 

From the beginning, a great deal of use had been made of such rivers as the Connecticut, the Hudson, the Delaware, the Susquehanna, and those of Virginia; and as the 

Western country filled up, the same use was made of the Ohio, the Wabash, the Scioto, the Tennessee, and the Cumberland.

Some of these rivers were wide enough and deep enough for sailing vessels; on others people had to depend on flatboats. These boats could carry a heavy load of freight without drawing much water, because they were broad and flat, and lay on top of the water, instead of reaching deeply into it.

It was easy to float a cargo downstream in one of these flatboats, but the return trip was harder, for the crew had to force the boat against the current by digging long poles into the bottom of the river and pushing hard against them. 

Sometimes the timber of which the boat was built was sold, and the crew returned on foot or on horseback. 

The First Steamboat 

In 1807, something happened which made an immense difference in river navigation. The first successful steamboat, the Clermont, made its appearance on the Hudson River. 

Robert Fulton, who built the Clermont, was not really the inventor of the steamboat. Another American, John 

Fitch, who died in 1791, has a better right to that title; but Fitch, though he built a vessel that would move under steam, could never get the necessary financial backing to make his work successful. Fulton was more fortunate. Robert R. Livingston, a wealthy citizen of New York, who had been one of the signers of the Declaration of Independence, and had held public offices, including that of Chancellor of New York, became deeply interested in Fulton's plans and furnished the money to give the boat a proper trial.

THE CLERMONT STEAMING UP THE HUDSON RIVER 

On August 7, 1807, the little Clermont, a craft one hundred and thirty feet long and eighteen feet wide, steamed up the Hudson from New York to Albany, a distance of over

That one hundred and forty miles in thirty-two hours. seems very slow progress today; but the people of 1807 thought it astonishing. The New York Legislature rewarded Fulton and Livingston by giving them a twentyyear monopoly of steam navigation on the lakes, rivers, and harbors of the State. 

Steamboat Days

Improvements in the construction of the steamboat quickly followed, and within a few years, crafts propelled by steam were to be seen on all the principal rivers of the country. As early as 1811, one plied up and down the Ohio and ventured into the Mississippi. These early steamboats were quaint affairs, with clumsy, noisy paddle-wheels splashing away under the stern or on opposite sides of the boat; but they could carry so much larger cargoes than the flatboats and at so much greater speed- particularly upstream that they rapidly came into use on every river that was deep enough to float them. In course of time they became the chief carriers of trade along the Ohio and Mississippi Rivers, and they remained so until after the Civil War. 

As time went on, larger and larger steamboats were built, and at last men ventured to use them to cross the Atlantic

Ocean. The Savannah, built in this country, was the first steamship to do this. That was in 1849; and the little 

Savannah, with its paddle-wheels on either side of its hull, was the forerunner of the immense floating palaces that today cross from Europe to America in hardly more than four days. 

The Clipper Ships

It was not until after the Civil War, however, that steamships began to crowd the sailing vessels from the sea. Until 

1860, at least, most of the trade of the world was done under

sail, and some of the finest and swiftest sailing ships in the world were built and owned by Americans. These "clipper ships," as they were called, were marvels of beauty and grace. They were designed for speed, with deep, slender hulls, tall masts, and an immense spread of sail. Occasionally they were known to cover four hundred miles of sea in a day. 

THE FAMOUS CLIPPER SHIP SWEEPSTAKES 

They were used in the trade with European ports, and with our own Pacific Coast, but the most famous of them were those which sailed to the Orient and brought back rich cargoes of tea or silk from China. Some of their names are still remembered - Flying Cloud, Sovereign of the Seas, Lightning, Red Jacket. Steam in iron vessels has pretty nearly driven sails and wooden ships from the sea, but the fine old clippers will never be forgotten. 

The Erie Canal

As a means of extending the use of interior waterways for

the transportation of freight, canals were built here and there throughout the country. The most famous of them all is the Erie Canal which extends from Lake Erie, near Buffalo, all the way across the State of New York to the Hudson River opposite Troy - a distance of nearly four hundred miles.

The route followed by the canal is of very great commercial importance, for it is the only pass between tidewater and the great interior of the country which does not climb over mountains. It is therefore the most economical way by which to move freight either eastward or westward.

People did not realize this at first, and it was thought that the Cumberland Road would always be the chief trade route between the seacoast and the interior. There were some far-sighted men, however, who understood the advantages of the New York route. One of them was De 

Witt Clinton, who was several times elected Governor of New York. It was largely owing to his energy and determination that the State appropriated the necessary money and began to build the canal. 

The undertaking was so costly and so unusual that many people would not believe that it could be successful. They scornfully called it "Clinton's Big Ditch" and declared that five million dollars would be buried in it. However, the work went on. Capable canal engineers from Europe helped in surveying the route and designing the many locks that were necessary to lift the canal boats from one level to a higher one. 

Canals and Railways

In October, 1825, the big ditch was finished and the water let into the final section. A gayly decorated canal boat bore Governor Clinton from Buffalo to New York,

cheered by the thousands who lined the banks. The enthusiasm was at its height when he poured two kegs filled with water from Lake Erie into New York Harbor, and pronounced the marriage of our inland seas (the Great 

Lakes) with the ocean completed. 

THE OPENING OF THE ERIE CANAL 

From the painting by Turner in the De Witt Clinton High School, New York City.

Courtesy of the New York Board of Education. 

The Erie Canal was a great success until the railways came into competition with it. An immense amount of freight passed over its waters at a mere fraction of what it would have cost to move it over the roads. Even today it is in constant use, having been widened and deepened. 

Some people think that in the future it will be still further enlarged, so that good-sized ships can make use of it in

sailing from the farther end of Lake Superior to ports on the other side of the Atlantic.

The Erie was by no means the only canal built during these years. There was, indeed, an epidemic of canal planning all over the country, and a number of them were actually built. A very important one ran from the Dela- EMPICK 

A CANAL WITH LOCKS 

ware River to the Raritan River which empties into New York Bay; so that people could travel from New York to 

Philadelphia wholly by water. Another led from Lake 

Erie at Cleveland to the Ohio River at Portsmouth.

In the midst of this fever of canal construction, however, the steam railway was introduced. It soon proved that the railways could carry traffic much more speedily and nearly as cheaply as the canals. Few of the latter were built after that, and most of those already built gradually fell into disuse.

The Industrial Revolution 

The political revolution which made the United States independent, was accompanied and followed by an industrial revolution no less important. The manufacturing industries of the colonies had been few and weak; partly because the colonists had little capital to invest in such businesses, but chiefly because the laws of Great Britain were so drawn as to discourage, if not actually to forbid, the growth of manufacturing, and to oblige the colonists to buy goods made in England.

With independence came the ambition to build up our own domestic industries. Hitherto there had been almost no cotton-weaving in this country; all our cotton cloths came from China or India or England. There had been much weaving of wool and flax in the homes of the people on the old-fashioned spinning-wheels and hand looms; but such homespun fabrics were coarse and far from being as attractive as the woolens and linens that came from foreign mills.

An industrial revolution had begun in England, too; for the inventions of Arkwright, Hargreaves, and Crompton had made it possible to weave cloth by machinery in great quantity and very cheaply. American business men began to wonder why they could not use similar machines on this side of the ocean, and get for our own workmen some of the money that was being paid every year for imported cottons, linens, and woolens. 

Samuel Slater's Services

Some New England men became interested, and they persuaded a young Englishman named Samuel Slater to come over to this country in 1790. Slater had worked with 

Arkwright, who had helped to invent the spinning jenny. 

He understood how the new spinning and weaving machines

were made. He was not allowed to take any plans to 

America with him, but he carried all the details of the machines in his mind so perfectly that he was able to build several of them in Rhode Island, and to equip an American cotton mill with them.

They worked well, and gradually more and more mills for 

MODEL OF THE SPINNING JENNY AS INVENTED BY HARGREAVES 

weaving both cotton and woolen goods were built and set to making cloth. Most of them were in New 

England, for the business men of that region were especially enterprising, and there was also a great deal of cheap water power to be had from their rivers. There were mills elsewhere, too; in 

New York and Pennsylvania and New Jersey and Ohio everywhere except in the South, whose people preferred to be farmers and planters rather than manufacturers. 

Invention of the Cotton Gin

The South, indeed, was just beginning to discover how profitable a crop cotton might become. Cotton had long been raised there; but it was the invention of the cotton gin by Eli Whitney in 1793 that made cotton-growing the great industry of the Southern States. 

The gin is a machine that quickly and easily separates the cotton fiber from the many seeds that are entangled in it. 

When that work is done by hand, it is a slow, and so a costly,

process. A negro could clean only five or six pounds of cotton a day by hand. With the help of Whitney's machine he could clean five hundred pounds or even more in a day.

This machine made a tremendous difference in the profits of cotton-growing. Plantations spread more and more widely over the 

South. Negro field hands were needed in increasing num- bers to plant, hoe, and pick cotton. If there had ever been any chance of abolishing slav- ery in the South, it disappeared when Whitney 

Courtesy of the Library of Congress

A COTTON GIN 

invented the cotton gin; for cotton became the great moneymaking crop, and the Southerners insisted that cotton could not be raised without slave labor. 

The age of machinery had begun. Whitney's machine was making the Southern planters rich, and Slater's machines were filling the Northern mills, making the manufacturers wealthy and causing new and busy factory towns to arise on many a Northern river-bank. 

The Coal and Iron Industries 

There were other new and growing industries, too. In the hills of Pennsylvania great quantities of coal and iron were discovered. Hitherto a little bog iron had been found here and there in the United States, and a few small iron

works had been started, which made enough iron to supply the people with most of the nails and rough hardware they needed. 

Now, however, there was iron in abundance, and along with it there was plenty of coal to use for fuel in smelting the ore. Iron works and foundries began to appear in the 

Western country, especially in the neighborhood of Pittsburgh; and enterprising men began to make all sorts of iron articles, which had previously been imported from England. 

Competition in Trade 

American manufacturers, however, did not find it easy to sell their goods in competition with the imported articles. 

AN EARLY FACTORY IN NEW ENGLAND 

Labor was comparatively costly in this country, and so was transportation. Labor was not so efficient as it was in 

England, for Americans had not yet learned how to work in textile mills and iron works, while in England there were thousands of men who had been familiar with such work all their lives.

The English articles were not only better made and more attractive, but they were generally cheaper, too. There

were attempts to persuade people that they ought to buy American goods for patriotic reasons, even if they were more expensive and not quite so good, but they did not meet with much success. The imported cloth and hardware still sold better than the domestic goods. 

The Protective Tariff 

So the government, which was interested in building up American industries, took a hand in the matter, and tried to help by increasing the tariff duties on articles imported from abroad. A tariff law, as you know, is one which provides that foreign goods cannot be brought into the country until their owners have paid a sum of money for the privilege of selling them. This sum of money is the "duty."

The first object of a tariff is to raise money for the expenses of the government; but many tariffs are also arranged so as to protect native industries against the competition of foreign goods. Our first tariff law, passed in 

1789, was so arranged. It was agreed that our infant industries needed the encouragement they would receive from duties that would make imported articles more expensive than they would otherwise be.

The duties were not at all high, according to present-day ideas, and it was understood that when our manufacturers were well established, the protection they gave would be withdrawn. That was the idea that Hamilton himself expressed. 

The "Tariff of Abominations"

But the foreign goods continued to sell more cheaply than we could manufacture them, and when another tariff bill was passed in 1816, the duties were raised still higher. Still they were not high enough to stop the flow of foreign goods, and in 1828 they were again raised. This time they were

put so high that people who did not like the law called it the "Tariff of Abominations," and South Carolina, as you will read later, refused for a time to obey it. 

The policy of protecting American industries, by laying heavy duties on imported goods, has come to be generally agreed upon. Today, although our infant industries are certainly well-grown and strong, our tariff laws continue to afford them the protection of substantial duties on the products of foreign competitors. 

You can see from what you have already read that the

United States was expanding in every direction during these years from 1781 to 1830. Its area was more than doubled, its population increased fivefold, its trade and manufactures grew by leaps and bounds, and all sorts of improvements in transportation and communication were made. All this meant the need for money and for money of sound and uniform value. 

Need of a Sound Currency

Fortunately, there was during most of this time a great national bank, which furnished a good sound currency to the Nation. You will remember how much trouble the colonies and the Confederation had with their money during and after the Revolution. That was because each State insisted on issuing its own kind of money - often paper money, with no real value behind it. A dollar bill was worth one amount today and often much less tomorrow. 

It would be taken by the merchants of one State and refused by those of another. No one could be sure just how much the money in his pocket would buy. It was hopeless to think of bringing prosperity and business confidence to a country with so poor a supply of money. 

One of the first things Hamilton did as Secretary of the 

I See Unit III, page 77 and Unit IV, page 92.

Treasury was to plan a national bank, which could issue money, good everywhere in the Union, and base it on sound security. His plan met opposition in Congress, for not many men understood how the bank was to work; and, not understanding, they were afraid that it would give too much power to men of wealth and in some way or other oppress the common people. 

The Bank of the United States

The bank, called the Bank of the United States, was chartered, however, and it was of the greatest service. It helped the Federal Government in the raising of money by loans, and it was of still more benefit to business men and to the people at large, for it established and maintained a truly national currency, that could be depended on to pass everywhere, and for a uniform value.

Without such a currency the great projects of national development of which you have read could hardly have been carried out. The enemies of the bank managed to prevent its recharter in 1811; but the experiences of the war with England showed people its value, and a second bank was chartered in 1816, which also survived for twenty years. In 1836 President Jackson caused the Bank of the 

United States to close, by persuading Congress not to renew its charter. 

Early American Schools

The period about which you are reading was noted for a great increase of interest in education. Schools in the colonial period had been few and poor. Very few taught more than a little reading, writing, and arithmetic, and they were held in log cabins or small one-room buildings with nothing to make the lessons attractive. 

There were not books enough to go around, and the

schoolmaster, who collected his pay, not from the town, but from the parents whose children he taught, was often more famous for his ingenuity in devising punishments, or his strength with the birch rod, than for his learning. Outside of the schoolroom there were not many books to be seen, WUSYM ALDWEBC 

CARPETING BROADWAY, NEW YORK AND ST. PAUL'S CHURCH IN 1831 

except in the homes of some wealthy people. Almost none had then been published in this country, and whole villages did not possess a dozen books except the Bible. There were no public libraries.

With the winning of independence, schools began to improve, at first principally in New England, New York, and Pennsylvania. The first steps toward improvement, however, were not in the elementary schools, but in high schools and academies for the older children. Hundreds of these

were opened during the early years of the nineteenth century.

Public Schools are Established Most of the schools were "pay schools" where the pupils were charged tuition; but the movement for public schools 

A ROADSIDE TAVERN IN 1839 

This house stood in what is now the heart of New York City, at 88th Street, and Third Avenue soon began. In New York, De Witt Clinton, whom you read of in connection with the Erie Canal, was among the leaders, and the first public schools in New York City were due to the work of the Public School Society which he organized.

Philadelphia soon followed. New England had begun to have such schools in colonial days, and gradually they were introduced all over the country. The improvement in buildings and in teaching was slow. It was not until after 

1830 that the schools were separated into grades even in the towns. Before that date there were only "district schools," attended by every pupil in the district, whatever his age.

It was not until 1839 that the first normal school for the training of teachers was established; so you can see that the kind of schools and the kind of teachers you know were quite unheard of when the United States was young. 

Webster's Dictionary 

Even after public schools were opened, the teachers were poorly paid, and in many cases they used to eke out their scanty salaries by "boarding round" among the families in the school district, staying a week at each house, and getting their room and food for nothing. 

There were few good schoolbooks. Until Noah Webster published his famous spelling book, Jedediah Morse his geography, and "Peter Parley" (Nathaniel Goodrich) his many clever books on all sorts of subjects, there were almost none of American origin. Webster sold millions of his blue-covered Speller, and made so much money from it that he could give all his time to making a great dictionary. 

He worked for twenty-seven years on this book, and when he had finished it (in 1828), it was by far the best and largest dictionary of the English language that had ever been written. It contained thousands of words and definitions that no other dictionary included. 

The Early Colleges 

Considering the backwardness of elementary education, college education was very liberally provided for in the early United States, because the churches were interested in educating their young men for the ministry. Most of the early colleges were founded and largely supported by one religious sect or another. 

There were two striking exceptions — the University of 

Pennsylvania at Philadelphia and the University of Virginia at Charlottesville. One had Benjamin Franklin as its

founder and the other Thomas Jefferson. Neither Franklin nor Jefferson was very religious. They were both interested in science and not much interested in the classics and the religious philosophy that the other colleges taught. 

So they introduced all sorts of new subjects into their universities, which were much more like the modern type of college than the other early institutions. 

BOWDOIN COLLEGE IN 1822 

At this time, Longfellow and Hawthorne were both students here. Chartered in 1794, and named for a governor of Massachusetts, Bowdoin College has always maintained a leading position among the small colleges of the country. 

When the Revolution broke out, there were eleven colleges in the country. By 1830, the number had increased to about fifty. Today there are nearly six hundred!

An American Literature 

The renewed interest in education had a natural result in the beginnings of an American literature. Previous to this time American contributions to literature had been largely collections of sermons, or political pamphlets, with a few attempts at poetry that were not much above doggerel. 

Franklin's Autobiography was the most interesting book of the colonial period.

Now authors with imagination, who had studied the art of writing pleasantly, began to appear. In New England there were the "Hartford Wits"-Trumbull, Joel Barlow, and Dwight - who wrote prose and verse that were popular in their day, but which are practically forgotten now, except among scholars. 

Irving, Cooper, Bryant

The most remarkable books of the period were written by men who lived in or near New 

York. Washington Irving wrote his humorous Knicker bocker History of New York which is still read, not as history, but for its amusing, satirical description of life in the old Dutch colony. He wrote the Sketch Book in which he told a number of old legends of the Hudson River region with real genius. Rip 

Van Winkle and the Legend of 

Sleepy Hollow are the most familiar of these stories. He wrote also an excellent Life of 

George Washington. Irving was the first really graceful and polished writer America produced. 

WASHINGTON IRVING 

Another New Yorker whose books became famous was 

James Fenimore Cooper. He wrote some of the best sea tales ever written and the Leatherstocking Tales, which give a romantic picture of life on the old New York frontier, with some interesting Indian and pioneer characters particularly Natty Bumppo, called the Leatherstocking, and his Indian friends Uncas and Chingachgook. Cooper's

tales were read in Europe as well as in America, and they were highly praised by foreign writers. 

Then there was William Cullen Bryant, a Massachusetts boy who lived most of his long life in New York City, where he edited the Evening Post. He was a poet, too, who wrote the first really beautiful verses ever produced in the United

States. His poems are serious and thoughtful, often dwelling on the beauties of Nature, and many of them are favorites today. Newspapers

These years saw also a great and progressive increase in the numbers and the influence of newspapers. The newspaper of the colonial days was a little four-page sheet, with mere fragments of news, and mostly filled with letters to the editor or articles of information or of moral instruction. 

With the coming of independence, the newspapers became political sheets and party organs. There was more news - particularly political news and the editorials were loud in praise of the men and measures of one party, and equally loud in abuse of the men and measures of the opposite party.

They were more interesting reading than the earlier papers had been, and they had no little influence in shaping the opinions or stiffening the party prejudices of their readers. 

After the invention of the telegraph, the news of the day took up more and more space, until today we have great newspapers of from twenty to forty pages, filled with reports of happenings from one end of the world to the other, and all printed within twenty-four hours of the event, however far distant.

Introduction of Railways 

You have already read of the appearance of the steam-

boat in American waters in the years following 1807. In 

1830, the first railways began to be built-only a few years after the English engineer, Stephenson, had shown the possibilities of steam transportation on a road of iron rails. 

Gradually the railways spread northward, southward, and westward.

At first people laughed at them or feared that 

AN EARLY RAILROAD TRAIN 

This train ran on the Mohawk and Hudson Railway from 

Albany to Schenectady in 1831. they would lead to all sorts of disasters. 

It did not take long to prove that the railways were to be of the greatest use. 

They could transport passengers from one end of the country to the other in a tenth of the time it used to take. They could convey great loads of freight from one city to another at an extremely low cost and with unheard-of speed. 

Before the opening of the Civil War there were about thirty thousand miles of railway in the United States. 

Traveling by rail made an extraordinary change in the life of the people. It hastened the flow of immigration into the West, and it made the Western lands far more valuable, since their produce could now be drawn quickly and cheaply to market. It stimulated trade between 

East and West and drew the whole country nearer together. 

I See Unit VII, pages 155, 156.

What Railways Did for the Country 

Indeed, it is doubtful whether the United States could have lasted as a single nation if the railways had not been built.¹ If it had continued to take weeks to go from New 

England to Georgia, and months to go from New York to the Pacific Coast, the difficulties of trade, of administration, and of mutual communication would have been so great that the country might easily have broken up into three or four or half-a-dozen separate nations. Because of our railroads a Boston man is today nearer San Francisco, in time and in ease of travel, than his great-grandfather was to New York. It takes no longer to go from Chicago to

New Orleans than it used to take to go from New York to Philadelphia. 

The Telegraph

One more invention that had the greatest effect in making communication swift between the most widely separated parts of the country was the electric telegraph, which 

Samuel F. B. Morse invented in 1844. This invention. 

made it possible to send messages almost instantaneously from one end of the country to the other. It is hard for us, who have never known what life is like without the railway, the telegraph, and now the telephone and the radio, to imagine what an astonishing difference these inventions all made in the life of the people, drawing them together into one solid political and economic union. 

Social Reforms; Religious Freedom; the Suffrage 

The first half of the nineteenth century was also a time of great social reforms. The movement for religious freedom, 

¹ Eminent men of this time, like Josiah Quincy and Daniel Webster, doubted the wisdom of expansion toward the West, lest the country should become too large and too diverse in its interests to hold together.

which had begun here and there in some of the colonies, grew stronger and stronger. It did not rest until everywhere in the United States men could worship freely according to the practice of any church they preferred, and until a man's religious belief nowhere prevented him from being a citizen and a voter.

The right of suffrage was widely extended, too. In colonial times people were not generally allowed to vote unless they owned a substantial amount of property, either in land, money, or goods. It is said that in some of the 

States not more than one man in ten could vote when the 

Constitution was adopted. 

By the

Steadily the restrictions were lightened or removed, and more and more people were permitted to vote. time of the Civil War most free persons were voters. 

Labor Reforms 

There was no real labor problem in colonial days, for there were no factories and no great employers of labor. 

Workmen were usually independent, and sold their time and skill at such prices as they could get to whoever wanted to make use of them. With the advent of machinery and the factory system, trouble began. Wages were small and hours long in the early days of the nineteenth century. 

Men worked twelve hours for a dollar a day, or a little less; and children not more than five or six years old were employed in some of the first cotton mills. As late as 1848, a girl operative in the cotton mills at Lowell could earn only $3.45 a week.

Under these circumstances, labor became naturally dissatisfied. We hear of strikes and labor unions very soon after 1800. The strikers demanded a ten-hour day, better wages, and the employment of no child under ten years of age. It seems strange to us that they should have to ask

A NEW ENGLAND VILLAGE (CONCORD, MASS.) IN 1839 

for such reforms as these; but they did, and they had no easy time getting them. Still, there was a steady improvement in labor conditions as time went on. The ten-hour day was established by law, and the right of the labor organizations to agitate for better wages and working conditions was admitted. 

Prison Reform 

There were two other interesting reform movements that began at this time. One was that for less cruel laws and better treatment of men and women in prison. The other was the temperance movement. There was need for both. 

In 1790, there were probably fifty or sixty crimes that were punishable by death in one or another of the United States. Moreover, people could be, and often were, sent to prison because they owed money to some one little as five or ten dollars; and they were kept in prison until they paid their debt — which sometimes meant for years and years. 

even as

The prisons were wretched places, filthy and unsanitary. 

Often they were overcrowded and all sorts of criminals

were herded together in a single room. All this had to be changed; and it was changed though it took years to do it. 

Gradually the laws were changed so that people could not be put to death for any crimes except murder and treason. 

Imprisonment for debt was forbidden in one State after another, and it is not now practiced anywhere in the United States. New and better prisons were built; the convicts had separate cells, with no more than two in a cell, and they were allowed to occupy themselves with work, instead of being kept in idleness or solitary confinement. All this took time, and some States lagged far behind others; but the improvement went on steadily all through these early years of the Republic. Even now there remains much to be done, for many American prisons are overcrowded, ill lighted, and unsanitary.

Temperance Reform 

Liquor-drinking and drunkenness were very common vices a hundred years ago. Almost every one drank; even ministers, women, and children. Liquor could be bought in any grocery store, and there were often twenty such places in a small village. Drunkenness was so serious a problem, since it led to widespread shiftlessness and poverty, that people everywhere began to realize that something must be done about it. Laws were made to control liquor-selling, and to punish those who sold it illegally.

A great temperance movement began in Boston in 1824, and it swept over the country. Temperance societies were formed in thousands of towns, and their members signed pledges promising to drink no liquor. The work of these societies was of the greatest value; they turned the tide against the liquor traffic. From the day when the first one was formed, intemperance has steadily become less and less common among Americans.

UNIT VIII EFFECTS OF  

NE of the most dramatic periods in American history or, indeed, in any history—is the story of the winning of the great West. It seems incredible that so vast a wilderness of forest and swamp and prairie could so quickly have been overrun, cleared, occupied, and reduced to the uses of civilization. Let us read in this unit the remarkable story. We shall see, too, how the growth of this new West, a rather rough land of pioneers and adventurers at first, had its effect on the country as a whole. It changed political and social standards more or less, and created a new sort of democracy, a far more thoroughgoing and selfreliant democracy than the world had ever seen. Andrew

Jackson was the type and product of this Western democracy. Compare what you are to learn about him with what you know of Washington or Hamilton or Jefferson and you will see what a difference the opening of the West made in the development of our country. 

Daniel Boone

The very first attempts to penetrate into this great Western country - the Ohio and Mississippi Valleys were made before the Revolution, when hardy pioneers from Virginia and Pennsylvania crossed the mountains to hunt animals and get furs, which they could sell in Philadelphia or Richmond. Many of these frontiersmen were of the restless, daring Scotch-Irish blood.

The most famous of them all was Daniel Boone, a cele-

brated woodsman and Indian fighter who was born in Pennsylvania but moved in youth, first to the Shenandoah Valley in Virginia and then to 

North Carolina. Leaving that colony in 1769 he led a party of five men through the Cumberland 

Gap in the Appalachian 

Mountains into the bluegrass region of tucky. There he founded the settlement of Boonesboro, and a few years later the town of Lexington. Ken- 

Only a few years later, James Robertson, a 

Scotch-Irishman from 

Virginia, led a party to 

Watauga in Tennessee, and made a settlement in the unbroken wilderness, to which came presently

DANIEL BOONE 

John Sevier, another Virginian, who became a hero among the Tennesseeans. 

Kentucky and Tennessee Settled

These brave men, with the small groups of settlers they gathered round them, were for years engaged in constant warfare with the Indians, who were determined not to let the white men get a foothold in the magnificent forest country of Kentucky and Tennessee. The white men were too strong for the savages, however. They broke the power of tribe after tribe, and as news of the fertile lands beyond

the mountains reached Virginia and Pennsylvania, thousands of restless, land-hungry pioneers came pouring westward to join the first comers.

Boone was always a wanderer, and a poor man of business. 

He got little but excitement and fame from the founding of the great State of Kentucky; but Robertson and Sevier had more solid qualities. Both served in the Revolution, and Sevier was again and again elected governor of Tennessee.

Slavery Crosses the Mountains 

These two regions had enough inhabitants to apply for admission to the Union as separate States soon after the Constitution was adopted. Kentucky was admitted in 1792 and 

Tennessee in 1796. Their growth was slow at first because it was not an easy matter to make one's way into them over the difficult mountain passes. After the National 

Road was begun, immigration into the new West increased greatly. Kentucky got more benefit from this road than 

Tennessee, and less perhaps than Ohio and Indiana; but both States grew rapidly. Kentucky was filled with Virginians, many of whom cleared the rich soil and planted tobacco, as they had in Virginia. The land in that State was already showing signs of exhaustion, and the fertile virgin soil of Kentucky offered every temptation to men who were willing to face the hardships of frontier life and the perils of 

Indian hostility.

Those settlers who owned slaves took them with them across the mountains, and as fast as others grew prosperous they bought slaves and used them in their fields of tobacco, cotton, and corn.

Growth of Cotton Culture 

The invention of the cotton gin, of which you have al-

ready read,' helped to hasten and increase the immigration into the Southwest. Cotton-growing suddenly became far more profitable than before; wherever the soil was favorable the Southern people turned to raising cotton. 

The soil of the lower Mississippi Valley was perfectly adapted to cotton, and the opportunity for getting rich quickly by planting cotton in what are now the States of 

Tennessee, Alabama, and Mississippi drew in that direction thousands upon thousands of people from the seaboard States.

Removal of the Indians 

At first these people traveled westward into Tennessee before turning South, for western Georgia was still in the hands of the Creek and Cherokee Indians, with whom the United States had made treaties, recognizing their right to the soil. During the War of 1812, however, the Creeks rose against the United States and had to be severely punished by General Jackson. A few years later, they sold a great part of their lands to the government. Then the Cherokee 

Indians were peaceably removed to lands west of the Mississippi, in that part of the Louisiana Purchase which is now the State of Oklahoma. These lands were granted to them in exchange for their territory in northern Georgia.

With the Indian tribes out of the way a fresh flood of immigration rolled into western Georgia and Alabama. By 

1817, Mississippi, and by 1819, Alabama, were sufficiently populous to become States.

The Purchase of Florida 

In 1819, another great addition was made to the territory of the United States by the purchase of Florida. This peninsula had been occupied by the Spanish for nearly 

¹ See Unit VII, page 162.

three hundred years; but Spain, on the verge of losing all her American colonies, was by no means strong enough to keep Florida away from the vigorous young republic, which was spreading out over the continent. 

The Spaniards got into serious trouble with the United States during the War of 1812, when they permitted British agents to enter the province and organize Indian raids on the settlements in Georgia and Alabama. General Jackson, with his customary efficiency, put a stop to that by hanging two of the British agents whom he caught; but the affair left a great deal of enmity between the Americans and the Spanish.

Rather than run the risk of losing Florida in war, Spain agreed to sell all her rights there to the United States for five million dollars. This added another seventy thousand square miles to our area, and opened a wide expanse of land for the extension of slavery.

By 1830, the immense territory south of the Ohio and east of the Mississippi was already pretty well settled and much of it was under cultivation-chiefly with cotton. 

It was indeed the large profits offered by this crop that made the conquest of this part of the wilderness so rapid. 

Settlement of the Northwest

The settlement of the Northwest Territory, north of the Ohio River, was of a different character. It was begun by the fur-traders who ranged the woods of Ohio, Indiana, and Michigan, and brought their catch back to market by way of Pittsburgh or Detroit.

Settlers who meant to become farmers followed them, but more slowly than in the South. There were the same or greater hardships to be faced and there was not the immediate wealth to be hoped for from the growing of cotton and tobacco.

The Northern Indians also were a dangerous lot, and they had in Tecumseh a leader of more than common daring and ability. It was not until he and his confederation of tribes were beaten by General W. H. Harrison at the battle of 

Tippecanoe in Indiana in 1811, that life on the Western frontier was at all safe for white men.

Once the Indians were subdued, and the great National Road built, immigration increased. Then came the appearance of steamboats on the Great Lakes and the building of the Erie Canal. It became possible to make the long journey to the West with less difficulty and delay — though it was still an undertaking that we should think tedious and uncomfortable enough.

The Westward Migration 

The Embargo, the War of 1812, and the consequent confusion in trade and industry had brought about a period of "hard times." Thousands were anxious to get away into the rich new West, where land could be bought for as little as two dollars an acre, and to make a fresh start in life there. 

To these people from the seaboard States were added other thousands from Europe, who had heard of the cheap land to be had in America, and had hurried across the sea to get their share. The Southwest had filled almost entirely with men and women from the older States; but to the 

Northwest there came not a few Germans, Norwegians, and Swedes, besides immigrants from Great Britain and Ireland.

There were plenty from New England, New York, and Pennsylvania, however. So many left these States that many towns not only ceased to grow but lost population. 

The "abandoned farms," of which we hear so much, began to be abandoned at this time; for already the thin soil of many an Eastern farm had begun to fail, and the reports of

CHICAGO IN 1834 South Water Street now stands on this spot. From an old drawing in the possession of the Chicago Historical Society. the richness of the Western soil set many discouraged or ambitious Eastern farmers on the road to the Westward. 

Scenes on the National Road 

The Cumberland (or National) Road was thronged with families pressing to the West. "Old America seems to be breaking up," wrote one observer who watched the endless stream of wagons, ox carts, pack horses, herds of cattle, and men on foot, moving toward the Ohio Valley. The wagons were loaded with furniture, bedding, and household supplies, among which rode the mother and the smaller children.

The men added to the supply of food brought with them by hunting and fishing as they went along; at night they camped beside the road, and slept on the grass beneath

their wagons. A people was in migration, and the movement has been compared to the old stories of the wandering of the German tribes that streamed into Western Europe and overthrew the Roman Empire more than a thousand years before. Only in this case the wanderers were bringing civilization into wilderness, and not threatening an old and rich civilization with extinction.

The Conestoga Wagons 

Behind the throng of settlers came the great lumbering freight wagons- the Conestoga wagons-drawn by their 

A CONESTOGA WAGON 

teams of powerful horses and carrying the freight that would be needed as the new towns and villages rose among the clearings in the forests. Until the railways came, years later, these wagons and the barges on the Erie Canal were the chief means of bringing the produce of the West to market, and of supplying the Western people with farm implements, furniture, imported food products like sugar, tea, and coffee, and the thousand articles of commerce that civilized people require. 

The southern parts of Ohio, Indiana, and Illinois filled first. The National Road led thither, and those who came across Pennsylvania to Pittsburgh often traveled westward by the Ohio River which took them still farther south. After the Erie Canal was opened and steamboats began to ply on

the Great Lakes, an even greater flood of emigration poured into the northern part of those States and into Michigan. 

Ohio was admitted as a State in 1803, Indiana in 1816, Illinois in 1818, and Michigan in 1837.

Public Land Policies 

The Federal Government was extremely generous in disposing of the public lands, which came into its hands when the States agreed to turn over to it all their rights in the great Northwest Territory, and those which it possessed as a result of the purchase of Louisiana. Most of these lands were sold to settlers at the extremely low price of two dollars an acre, or even less; and yet the amount of land was so enormous, and the demand for it so great, that the money which came into the Treasury was sufficient to pay off the last of our national debt by 1835.

Of course there were at first no surveys of these lands. 

The government sent out as many surveyors as it could get to draw the lines and lay out the sections of farm land; but people poured westward faster than the surveyors could do their work. Often they would settle down on a piece of land they liked before it had been surveyed, with the intention of paying for it when the surveyors had done their work. It happened not infrequently that some one else actually claimed and paid for the land when the surveyor's plans were ready, and then there were disputes and lawsuits between the original occupier of the land and the purchaser. 

So the titles to a good part of the farm land in the Ohio Valley were fought out in the courts, and it was many years before everything was settled.

School Lots 

According to the laws passed by Congress, one section of 640 acres in every township was set apart as a "school" lot,

Un- for the support of a school in that neighborhood. shows that the need of education was understood. fortunately, the system did not always work well. The lots were of varying value, depending upon the soil they contained, and some were not worth enough to support a good school. Besides, there was no state system of control. 

Each neighborhood ran its school in its own way, and many of them neglected to keep them up properly. The standards of instruction and the ability of the teachers were not high, which is not surprising in a new and rough country. For a good many years the best schools were not public schools, but those maintained by the churches, in which the ministers were often the teachers. 

Speculation in Land 

One unfortunate thing about the rush of people into the West was the inevitable speculation in land. A great many persons saw a chance to make money by buying more land than they could use. The law permitted them to pay only a part of the purchase money down, the rest to be paid in installments. They hoped, of course, to sell the land to some one else at a higher price before the later payments became due.

At first this sort of thing was profitable; but so many people engaged in speculation, usually with borrowed money, that too much of the credit of the Nation became locked up in land. The demand for money with which to buy land also encouraged dishonest or inexperienced men to go into the banking business. Banks were not properly inspected and controlled by law in those days. These "wildcat" bankers, as they were called, would issue bills to borrowers without any sound security behind them.

As long as the boom continued and prices were rising, the banks and speculators made money. But when times be-

came hard, as they did in 1837, there was a sudden crash. 

The land speculators could not sell their land at the expected profit, they could not pay what they owed the banks, and the banks themselves could not get real money- gold and silver to redeem the notes they had issued so lav- 

THE CITY OF WASHINGTON IN 1839 (From the White House) 

ishly. Banks and landowners failed and went into bankruptcy, and there was a great deal of distress as a result. 

However, this did not seriously arrest the growth of the great West, which continued at such a rate that the people of the older States in the East began to fear that their villages and farms would be deserted and their prosperity seriously affected. Fortunately that did not happen, for a steadily increasing immigration from Europe filled up the gaps that the westward-moving host had left in New England, New York, and Pennsylvania. It was at this time that the change began which has left the old colonial stock only one among many elements of the population in the 

Eastern States.

The Spirit of the New West 

The effect of the settlement of the new West on the politics and the economic ideas of the country was very great. 

In one respect it strengthened the national feeling throughout the Union. The people of such States as Kentucky, Tennessee, and Ohio did not have the strong feeling for their young commonwealths that the people of old States like Massachusetts and Virginia and New York had for theirs. 

They thought of themselves as Americans first of all, and they did not put the rights of their States above that of the 

Federal Government.

It was largely the enthusiasm of the Western people for war with England that forced on the War of 1812 against the protest of some of the Eastern States. After the war was over, these Western States insisted that the government should be generous in building roads, canals, and other internal improvements which many Eastern and Southern statesmen thought it had no right to do, under the Constitution. They carried their point, too. Under 

Presidents Madison and Monroe, who were both originally States' Rights men, with conscientious doubts about granting too much power to the Federal Government, Congress voted to undertake a number of projects that would have been thought quite unconstitutional a few years before. 

In all these activities, Henry Clay was a leader. He was, perhaps, as good a representative of the new West in politics as any. A brilliant orator and a clever politician, he expressed the ambitions and demands of the West with great power in Congress. He was for many years Speaker of the 

House of Representatives, and his influence over his colleagues there was so great that he was able to carry through many things the West wanted the War of 1812, the policy of internal improvements, the protective system, and an increasingly liberal way of distributing the public lands. 

---

Andrew Jackson Becomes President 

It was not Clay, however, but General Andrew Jackson, who was the first President from beyond the mountains. 

When Monroe's second term drew to a close, in 1824, Jackson was a candidate to succeed him. So was Clay, so was 

Crawford, Secretary of the Treasury, and so was John 

Quincy Adams,' the Secretary of State, and the son of John 

Adams, our second President.

Among so many candidates there was no majority in the Electoral College. The Constitution provides that in such a case the House of Representatives shall elect the President from among the three leading candidates, each State having one vote. Although Jackson had had more electoral votes than any other candidate, the House of Representatives did not choose him. The friends of Clay (who could not be voted for, since he was not one of the three leading candidates) united with the friends of Adams to elect the latter. 

Jackson was only a rough Western soldier, they said, while Adams was a trained and educated statesman, who had been a Senator, Minister to no less than five foreign countries, and Secretary of State.

This was all true, but Jackson resented his defeat, and the common people of the West and South who had voted for him resented it still more. When Adams made Clay his 

Secretary of State, they said that that was the price of his I

John Quincy Adams, son of the second President, was born in Massachusetts in 1766. He graduated from Harvard College, was a lawyer by profession, and was in public service almost all his life. He was at different times 

United States Minister to Holland, Portugal, Prussia, Russia, and Great

Britain; served a term in the United States Senate; helped negotiate the 

Treaty of Ghent, and was for eight years Secretary of State before being elected President in 1824. After his term as President he was elected to the National House of Representatives, where he sat until his death in 1848. He won fame during these years in the defense of the right of the people to petition Congress on the dangerous subject of slavery.

help in electing Adams. "Bargain and corruption," they called it. The charge was not true, but it had a great effect upon the voters.

Adams made a good President, but when his term expired, Jackson was elected in his place. Of course Jackson's military fame you remember how he defeated the

1815 helped him; and he had the aid of some very clever politicians who managed his campaign in New

ANDREW JACKSON 

ready man of the people who had come up from poor beginnings, and who was not, like the earlier Presidents, a gentleman and a man of a well-known family. 

What Jackson was Like

Jackson was the son of poor parents from the border between North and South Carolina. Left an orphan by the 

I Andrew Jackson was born of immigrant Scotch-Irish parents, in 1767, in a settlement on the border between North and South Carolina. He was bred in poverty and had little education, but he excelled in riding, shooting, and feats of strength. He became a lawyer and went to Tennessee as 

United States District Attorney. Later, he went to Congress and the Senate, and was for a time a judge. He was also general of the Tennessee militia, which led to his service in the War of 1812 and a general's commission in the United States Army. He was President from 1829 to 1837. 

A man of strong will and violent passions, he was not an accomplished statesman, but a devoted patriot and a remarkable leader of men.

early death of his father, he got what education he could by his own efforts. He moved to Tennessee at twenty-one, and by his own energy and force of character made himself a leading citizen. He was elected to Congress, became a judge, and then left civil life for the army, where his courage, determination, and sound military sense made him famous. He was the first President there have been several since - whose humble start in life was used by his political friends to recommend him to the voters, the common people" of the country. 66 

His inex Jackson was a quick-tempered man and often vindictive toward those whom he considered his enemies. perience in financial and diplomatic matters sometimes led him into mistakes in policy; but he was honest, strong of will and purpose, and devoted to the Union. He was reelected in 1832, served eight years in the Presidency, and was able to make his friend and follower, Martin Van Buren, of 

New York, President after him. 'Jacksonian Democracy"

The scenes in Washington when Jackson was inaugurated were enough to make some of his dignified predecessors in the Presidency wince. The streets were crowded with people, shouting, cheering, and pushing one another about in their anxiety to see the "hero of New Orleans,' "Old Hickory," as his soldiers, and later his political followers, loved to call him.

After the inauguration, the crowd rushed to the White House, where they surged back and forth in noisy enthusiasm, and climbed up on the damask-covered chairs, to get a better view of the President. It was a good-humored but disorderly throng, made up largely of men who felt that at last the masses of the people had a representative in the White House, and that they, instead of the well-to-do and

socially superior classes, would control and direct the government. AL

Events of Jackson's Administration 

Jackson helped along this feeling by dismissing many of the old office-holders, who had held their posts for years, and by filling their places with his own supporters, who now began to call themselves Democrats. He also pleased the 

Western and Southern people by preventing the recharter of the Bank of the United States, which they could not help regarding as an institution run by rich Easterners, to oppress the poorer people, who were trying to make a start in the newer lands of the West. 

When the national debt was paid off and the government began to have a surplus, Jackson pleased these same people by distributing this surplus money among the States. 

Neither his overthrow of the bank nor his distribution of the surplus was very wise, for they led to an increase in specu-

. 

lation, and in “wild-cat" banking which brought on a severe financial panic soon after Jackson left office; but they were popular at the time.

In other respects, as we shall read later, Jackson's record as President was excellent, and he helped mightily to increase the strength of the Union and the affection of the people for it. Whether wise or unwise he carried the confidence of the common people with him in whatever he did, especially the confidence of those who, like himself, had labored to build up a new America in the heart of the 

Western wilderness.

UNIT IX HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY AND THE EFFECT OF THOSE ACQUISITIONS 

HE central fact of American history from 1800 to 

The thirteen original States all lay east of the Appalachian Mountains and extended only two or three hundred miles from the seacoast. By 1850, the United States had pushed its boundaries westward almost three thousand miles and occupied the heart of the North American continent from the Atlantic to the Pacific. No other nation has ever increased in size so swiftly, so inevitably, and with so little dependence on war as a means of expansion. In this unit we shall follow the different steps in the progress across the continent. The 

Louisiana Territory, Texas, New Mexico, California, the

Oregon Country these are the milestones in our march TH 

T1850, is that of national expansion. to the Pacific.

The Louisiana Purchase 

The Treaty of 1783, made at the end of the Revolution, confirmed our right to the interior of the continent as far as the Mississippi, and so added to the original States a territory even larger than that which they occupied. A great many people doubted whether Americans could ever occupy all this region and still keep the Nation united; but as we have read, they soon proved their ability to do so. 

Even before this nearer Western country held anything more than scattered settlements, the courage and foresight of President Jefferson added some eight hundred thousand square miles to our territory, by the purchase of Louisiana.

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 205 

This occurred in 1803. The negotiations were conducted in France, for that country had lately acquired from Spain all its claims to the vast region between the Mississippi and the Rocky Mountains. 

A great New-Yorker, Robert R. Livingston, whom we have already met as the friend and financial backer of Robert Fulton, was our Minister to France. He was, it is said, Napoleon's favorite among all the foreign representatives in Paris. Certainly he had great influence with the 

Emperor, and he succeeded in persuading him to sell Louisiana to the United States, where a less skillful diplomat might have failed. 

Lewis and Clark's Expedition

Louisiana came to our Nation without a struggle, at a cost of only fifteen million dollars; first, because Spain in its enfeebled state felt unable to make good its claim to so distant a possession, and second, because Napoleon was so deeply involved in his costly and seemingly endless wars in Europe that he could see no chance of making any use of the American wilderness he had acquired from Spain. 

The boundaries of Louisiana were extremely indefinite; for no one knew exactly how the mountain ranges or the rivers ran. No white men had ever explored the more distant portions of the territory; so the first thing that Jefferson did was to send an expedition to find out just what our new purchase contained.

The expedition was to ascend the Missouri River to its source in the mountains; to cross the mountains if possible, and make its way to the Pacific; to make maps of all the region it explored, and to report on the nature of the country, its people, its animals, its vegetation, its climate, and its possible mineral wealth. There were forty-three persons in the party when it left St. Louis in 1804. It was com-

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 

manded by two army officers, Meriwether Lewis and 

William Clark. The latter was a brother of George Rogers

Clark, who had done so much in the Revolution to win the western country for the United States. 78 1000 120°

MAP OF LEWIS AND CLARK'S EXPLORATIONS 

To the Pacific at Last 90° By The expedition started up the Missouri in May. 

October it had reached the great bend in the river near the present city of Bismarck, North Dakota. Here Lewis and

Clark spent the winter among the friendly Mandan Indians. 

Early in the spring they pushed on, guided by a young Indian squaw called the Bird Woman (Sacajawea) who was of the greatest help to the expedition. Early in the fall they reached the headwaters of the Missouri, far up among the mountains. There again they found friendly Indians 

Shoshones, this time, who furnished them with guides to lead them through gaps in the mountains to the rivers that flow into the Pacific.

It was a hard and toilsome march through dense forests and over precipitous cliffs, but they came at last to a river

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 207 

they named the Lewis. Down that river they floated in improvised boats to the Columbia, and so to the sea where the second winter was passed. 

Then in the spring they set out on the return march. It was two years and a half after leaving St. Louis when they arrived there once more. They had traveled seventy-five hundred miles and brought back with them an extraordinary amount of interesting information about the country through which they had passed. 

The Pike Expedition 

Another party, led by General Zebulon Pike, explored the southern part of this unknown region. Pike pushed across the prairies of what is now Kansas and Colorado until he saw looming up before him the lofty snow-covered range of the Rocky Mountains. One of the highest peaks of these mountains still bears his name. Then, as winter was coming on, he turned southward. His party suffered much from the cold and more than once came near starvation. 

At last they reached Santa Fé, which was in Spanish territory. The Spanish officials put Pike and his men under arrest, but on learning that they had no designs against Spain, but were only exploring the new American territory, they released them and permitted them to return to St. Louis. 

These expeditions gave Americans more exact ideas of the character and the vast extent of Louisiana, but it was thirty or forty years before there were any considerable settlements there, except in the vicinity of New Orleans and of St. Louis. Around these centers two new States grew up and were admitted to the Union, Louisiana in 1812 and 

Missouri in 1821.

The Oregon Country 

Meanwhile other questions arose.

What was the north-

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 

ern boundary of Louisiana which divided the United States in its enlarged form from the British territories of Western Canada; and to whom should the lands west of the Rocky 

Mountains belong, those lands in the valley of the Columbia River which Lewis and Clark had explored? It became necessary to settle these questions, and in 1818 President Monroe made a friendly treaty with Great Britain by which the forty-ninth parallel of latitude was agreed on as the boundary between the United States and Canada, from near the end of Lake Superior to the summit of the Rocky 

Copyright, C. H. Nichols

PIONEERS ATTACKED BY INDIANS ON THEIR WAY ACROSS THE PLAINS TO OREGON 

Mountains. It was not so easy to determine what should be done about the Oregon Country, as the region between the mountains and the 

Pacific Ocean was called. The 

United States claimed it, (1) because an Amer- ican sea captain, Robert Gray, had discovered the mouth of the Columbia in 1792, giving this great river the name of his ship; (2) because Lewis and Clark had been the first to travel across this region and to follow the course of the Columbia River from the mountains to the sea; and (3) because since 1811 Americans had had a fur-trading post at 

Astoria near the mouth of the Columbia.

Great Britain claimed the coast and the country behind it, on the ground that Sir Francis Drake had discovered it, and that British sailors, Cook, Vancouver, and Mackenzie,

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 209 

had explored it during the eighteenth century. There were also British fur-traders scattered here and there through the country.

The two nations could not come to any settlement of their claims. They agreed, therefore, to permit people from both 

Canada and the United States to settle there and to try again at the end of ten years to agree upon a boundary line. 

By this treaty the United States, which only twenty years before had its frontier at the Mississippi River, a boundary which many people thought would be permanent, secured an actual foothold on the shores of the Pacific Ocean; for it was certain that however the question was finally settled, a part, at least, of the Oregon Country would fall to the 

United States. 

Americans in Texas

Only a few years later, events began to prepare the way for another enormous increase in our territory, this time to the southward. Texas, a rich and fertile region, larger in area than France or Germany, lay next to our recently acquired Louisiana. Texas was a part of Mexico, and Mexico, long one of the richest provinces of the Spanish Empire, at last won its independence in 1821.

Though Mexico claimed, rightfully enough, the sovereignty over Texas, there were very few Mexicans living there. It was so thinly settled that it was often a hundred miles or more between the Mexican villages. Attracted by the promise of wealth to be got from this almost unoccupied region, American pioneers crossed the Sabine River and began to settle in Texas as early as 1821, while Spain and Mexico were still at war. General Sam Houston and 

Stephen F. Austin were the leaders in this new emigration. 

The reports of the beauty and fertility of the land which they sent back to their friends led more and more Americans

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 

to follow them. Within ten years there were twenty or thirty thousand of them comfortably settled in Texas, and the Mexican officials there grew very uneasy. At the rate the Americans were coming they would soon own all the best land in Texas, and crowd the Mexicans out altogether.

So the Mexican government made laws to stop any further immigration from the United States, and began to treat the settlers who were already there unfairly. The roughand-ready pioneers from the Southwest who had made homes in Texas were not the kind of people to take such treatment meekly. There were several years of angry quarreling, and then the Americans rose in revolt. 

Texas Wins its Independence

Mexico sent an army northward to subdue the rebellion 

THE ALAMO 

and there was a year or two of fighting. 

In the course of this war occurred the siege of the Alamo, a stone building in the town of San Antonio, where a party of Americans was surrounded and besieged by a much more numerous force of Mexicans. The besieged men made an heroic resistance; they did not surrender until almost the last man was dead from wounds or starvation.

The Mexicans murdered the few survivors, and from that

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 

211 moment "Remember the Alamo!" became the war-cry of the Texans.

The end came at the battle of San Jacinto (April 21, 1836). Eight hundred Texans under General Houston defeated six times their number of Mexicans. The broken 

Mexican army abandoned Texas, which was declared an independent republic with General Houston as its president.

The Texans did not really want to be independent. They wanted to become part of the United States, and they asked that their republic might be admitted as a State in our Union. In order to understand what happened when they made that request, we must know what was the political situation in the United States at that time. 

Martin Van Buren Elected President

When General Jackson retired from the Presidency in 

1837, he was succeeded by Martin Van Buren,¹ the first president that New York gave to the Nation. Van Buren was a public man of long experience. He had been Governor of his State, Senator, Secretary of State, and Vice- President. He was a close friend and faithful follower of 

Jackson, who really selected him to succeed himself in the leadership of the Democratic Party and in the White House. 

Van Buren made a good President, but he took office at an unfortunate time. There had been a period of wild speculation in land and of careless banking, during Jackson's term of office, and "Old Hickory's" own management I Martin Van Buren was the son of a village innkeeper in Kinderhook, New York. He was born in 1780 and died in 1862. He was active in politics all his life; served in the New York legislature and as Attorney 

General and Governor of the State. He was also for a time in the 

United States Senate, and was Secretary of State and Vice-President under Jackson. He was President from 1837 to 1841.

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 

of the financial affairs of the government had made matters worse instead of better.

In 1837, the boom broke; there was a severe financial panic. Hundreds of banks and thousands of business men were ruined, and trade and industry fell into a long depression all over the country. This served to turn the voters against the Democrats, and in 1840 their opponents, who called themselves Whigs, carried the election. 

The Log Cabin Campaign 

They had not chosen, as their candidate, either Henry 

Clay or Daniel Webster, their two greatest leaders, but Gen- HARD CIDER CON

The Whigs made much of the simplicity of his character and his way of living. 

Part of his home in Ohio was said to be built of logs, and the Whigs used a log cabin, with a jug of hard cider by the door, as the central feature of their noisy and stirring campaign. The "log cabin" candidate, Harrison, was elected by a large majority; but he lived only one month after his inauguration and was succeeded by John Tyler, who was the
