Vice-President. Tyler was a Virginian, really more of a

Democrat than a Whig, though he had quarreled with Jackson, and had been nominated with Harrison in the hope of bringing some Southern Democratic votes to the ticket. 

John Tyler Becomes President 

President Tyler lost no time in quarreling with his Whig supporters, too. He could not agree with the leaders of the party on anything; he vetoed one bill after another which had been passed by Whig votes in Congress; and within a few months all the members of his cabinet except one had resigned. 

DANIEL WEBSTER 

The single exception was 

Daniel Webster, the Secretary of State. He remained in office until he had finished negotiating a treaty with Great 

Britain settling our northeastern boundary between the State of Maine and the Canadian provinces of Quebec and New Brunswick. The exact line of division between the two countries had been in dispute ever since the end of the Revolution. While the region had been covered with lonely forests that had not made so much difference, I Daniel Webster was born in 1782 in New Hampshire and was educated at Dartmouth. He became a very famous lawyer and one of the greatest of orators. During most of his life he lived in Massachusetts. 

He was elected to Congress and then to the United States Senate of which he was for many years a member. In 1841-42 and again in 1850-52, he was Secretary of State. He died in 1852. As a constitutional lawyer, Webster's reputation was so high that he has been called the "Expounder of the Constitution."

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 

but now people were beginning to clear away the trees and make farms there, and the boundary line had to be agreed upon. The treaty that fixed the line is called the 

Webster-Ashburton Treaty from the name of the two men, American and British, who made it. As soon as the treaty was signed, Webster, too, resigned his office. 

Texas Asks to be Annexed 

President Tyler thus found himself a man without a party; for both Democrats and Whigs regarded him as a traitor. It was while political affairs were in this confused state that the petition of Texas to be admitted to the Union was received. The President was in favor of annexing 

Texas at once, and so were most Southerners, whatever their party.

They saw an opportunity to add an immense extent of rich territory to the Union. Moreover, this territory was suited to growing cotton and other Southern crops. Slavery was sure to be extended thither indeed, most of the settlers had already taken slaves with them. The admission of Texas would add to the political strength of the South and of the slave-holding interest in Congress; and the Southerners were already becoming anxious about the future of slavery.

Ever since the Constitution was adopted, with its necessary compromise on the question of slavery, the effort had been made to keep the number of free States and of slave States precisely balanced.' But most of the new territory in the West and Northwest was not suited to the use of slave labor, and the Southerners welcomed the opportunity of adding to the Union a vast area in which slavery might I Ohio and Louisiana had been admitted together; Indiana and Mississippi, Illinois and Alabama, Maine and Missouri, Arkansas and Michigan. 

Later Florida and Texas balanced Iowa and Wisconsin.

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 215 

thrive, to balance the growing free States of the Northwest. 

On the other hand, many Northerners earnestly opposed annexation. They foresaw that it would lead to trouble and perhaps war with Mexico, and they had come to disapprove strongly of slavery, and did not wish to see any more slave territory added to the Union. 

In the spring of 1844, President Tyler made a secret treaty with the Texans, providing for the immediate admission of their State; but when he asked the Senate to ratify it, that body refused to do so by a large majority. At once annexation became the political issue of the moment, and the presidential campaign of 1844 was at hand.

The election of 1844 was fought out on issues of national expansion. The Democrats nominated James K. Polk, who had been Speaker of the House of Representatives and Governor of Tennessee. They declared boldly for the immediate annexation of Texas. That pleased the Southern people. Then the Democrats appealed to Northern and Western voters by demanding the right to occupy new land in the far Northwest. "The Oregon Country must be ours, as far north as the boundary of Alaska," they said. That boundary was at the latitude of 54 degrees, 40 minutes, and one of the popular cries of the campaign was, "Fifty-four Forty- —or Fight!" 

Over the Oregon Trail

The Oregon question, which the United States and Great Britain had left undecided by the Treaty of 1818, must now be settled. American settlers had begun to move into the region, led by missionaries, like Marcus Whitman, who were equally interested in saving the souls of the Indians It is interesting to note that the nomination of Polk was the first piece of news sent over Morse's new electric telegraph line between Baltimore and Washington.

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 

and winning the rich valleys of the Columbia and its tributary rivers for the United States.

These settlers traveled in parties of a hundred or more, over the long and difficult Oregon Trail, across the prairies and the mountains. They went in trains of heavy wagons, with canvas tops, which they called "prairie schooners"; 

LOGGING IN THE GREAT EVERGREEN FORESTS OF THE OREGON COUNTRY 

one or two wagons to each family, loaded with furniture and provisions for the journey, which often took three months. 

Sometimes they had to fight with hostile Indians, and that is why they went in companies, for a single immigrant would have been helpless against the savages. 

When the Indians attacked them, the emigrants would drive their wagons into a great circle, and hiding behind them, as if behind breastworks, they would shoot at the

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 217 

enemy, who, mounted on their ponies, would ride, whooping, yelling, and discharging their arrows or muskets round and round the improvised fortification. Some parties of white men and women were surrounded and massacred, but most of them succeeded in getting safely through to Oregon. 

By 1844 there were probably five or six thousand Americans in that country.

The British settlers were few, but among them were many fur-traders, and they had several large and well-fortified trading-posts. The British still claimed all the Oregon Country as far south as the Columbia River, and were preparing to defend their possessions against the increasing stream of American immigration. Unless the dispute was quickly settled, it might easily lead to war. 

James K. Polk Elected President ¹

The Whig candidate in 1844 was the venerable statesman, Henry Clay. He began by opposing the annexation of 

Texas and then, fearful of losing Southern votes, he agreed that if the people wanted it he would not object. He was beaten in the election because the Democrats stood stoutly for national expansion, while the Whigs were neither clearly for nor definitely against it.

The election of Polk was taken as a national verdict in favor of annexation, and when Congress met in December, it promptly voted to admit Texas to the Union; so that was accomplished before the newly elected President was inaugurated. 

¹ James K. Polk was the first "dark horse" candidate for the Presidency.

A "dark horse" is the politician's name for a candidate whose nomination, quite unexpected, comes as the result of the impossibility of agreeing upon any of the more conspicuous candidates. Polk was born in North Carolina in 1795 and died in 1849. He was a lawyer, and lived in Tennessee. He served in Congress and as Speaker of the House of Representatives and was also Governor of Tennessee before being elected President.

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY The Oregon Boundary Settled 

The first thing that Polk did was to settle the Oregon question by means of a treaty with Great Britain. The two countries very sensibly agreed to compromise the dispute. The United States did not insist on fighting for the line of 54° 40′, as the Democrats had promised it should, and the British consented to moderate their claims, too. In the end it was decided that the forty-ninth parallel, already the boundary between Canada and the United States east of the mountains, should also be the boundary all the way to the Pacific. 

War with Mexico over Texas Mexico 

Affairs in Texas were not so amicably settled. could not prevent us from annexing Texas, though it would have liked to do so, but its government insisted that the boundary between Texas and Mexico was at the Nueces River, while President Polk, supporting the claims of the Texans, held that it was at the Rio Grande, a hundred and fifty miles farther south.

He sent some American troops under General Zachary Taylor to occupy and hold the disputed region, and Taylor took up a position on the Rio Grande opposite the Mexican town of Matamoras.

Early in May, 1846, a force of Mexicans crossed the river and attacked Taylor, who promptly drove them back again. 

The President sent a message to Congress saying that armed Mexicans had invaded the United States, and asking for a declaration of war. On May 13, Congress voted that the two countries were already at war, owing to the hostile action of Mexico. 

Scott in Mexico

The war that followed was a succession of victories for the

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 

219 American troops, who were better armed, better led, and far more intelligent as soldiers than the Mexicans. Taylor moved steadily southward defeating the enemy at Monterey and Buena Vista. 40 9120

SCENE OF THE MEXICAN WAR 

Meanwhile another American army, made up of enthusiastic volunteers, mostly from the Southern States, was sent by water to the port of Vera Cruz. General Winfield Scott was in command. This army fought its way from the coast

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 

up through the mountains during the winter of 1846–47 and appeared before the Mexican capital early in May. 

General Santa Anna offered a brave resistance, but the 

Americans won one victory after another — at Contreras,

Cherubusco, Molino del Rey, and Chapultepec. The Mexi can army was scattered, and Scott entered the City of 

Mexico in triumph. It was clear that the Mexican cause was hopeless, and in February, 1848, a treaty was signed which confirmed the claim of the United States to the Rio 

Grande as its southern boundary.

This treaty that of Guadalupe Hidalgo - did far more than that; for while the fighting was going on round the City of Mexico, events were happening which led to the occupation of both New Mexico and California by the Americans. 

California in 1848

California had long been recognized as a part of Mexico. 

A number of devoted Spanish missionaries had gone into that vast territory and established missions among the Indians. Some of the substantial stone churches and mission houses that they built are to be seen in California today - at Santa Barbara, San Gabriel and elsewhere. There were twenty-two such missions between San Diego and 

San Francisco.

California was very thinly settled. Few white people except the priests had entered the country, and there were almost as many American pioneers as Mexicans among them.

When news of the war with Mexico reached California, these American pioneers determined to seize the country for the United States. They called on Captain John C. Frémont, of the United States Army, who was then in California with an exploring party, to help them.

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 221 

Frémont the Pathfinder

Frémont had spent the previous five years in a succession of journeys through the Rocky Mountain country, under the orders of the government. He had explored the whole country from the Columbia to the Colorado, and had discovered a number of practicable passes through the mountains. He had visited and mapped the Great Salt Lake 

Basin, and made his way in the face of extreme difficulties and hardships across the Nevada desert, and over the pathless range of the Sierra Nevadas into California. The Indians had told him that the trip was quite impossible, but he and his little party of brave men made it at the risk of their lives.

Frémont was a picturesque and romantic figure. People called him "the Pathfinder," and his exploits had made him almost as well known in Europe as in America. 

Conquest of California

He had already had trouble with Mexican officials who had tried to drive him out of California. He was quite ready to put himself at the head of the American settlers, and in a brief campaign he defeated the few Mexican soldiers who opposed him, seized Monterey, the little capital of California, and was declared Governor by his victorious followers. 

Not long afterward, Commodore Stockton, of the Navy, appeared at Monterey with his frigate. Then General

Kearny, who had already overrun New Mexico with a small force of American soldiers, arrived, having made his way over the fifteen hundred miles of desert and mountain trail between Santa Fé and Los Angeles. Kearny took command, and he was in effective occupation of California when the war came to an end.

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY The Gadsden Purchase 

Since United States forces had driven the Mexicans out of both New Mexico and California, our representatives declared that these vast regions must be ceded to this country. 

Mexico was in no position to resist. We did, however, pay 

Mexico the sum of fifteen million dollars for these great provinces, which was little enough for what we got. The new accessions covered more than 529,000 square miles, a greater area than that of the original thirteen States. The 

States of California, New Mexico, Arizona, Utah, and

Nevada have been created from it, and it includes also portions of Colorado and Wyoming.

A few years later, the United States bought some more territory from Mexico, along the southern line of New Mexico and Arizona, in order to settle a dispute about the actual frontier between the two countries. We acquired nearly thirty thousand square miles by this transaction, which is called the "Gadsden Purchase" from the name of the American diplomat who negotiated it. For this we paid 

Mexico ten million dollars. 

Gold Discovered in California

Hardly had California become a part of the United States when news reached the East that gold had been discovered there, on a ranch in the Sacramento Valley, owned by Captain John A. Sutter. The gold was found in the bed of a stream where some of Sutter's men were putting up a sawmill. Further investigation proved that the find was important; there was a great deal of gold in the hills around the Sacramento Valley.

It was the first discovery of gold in paying quantities in the United States, and the Nation went wild as reports of the increasing value of the mines were received. Hundreds of pioneers hurried to the spot where gold had been found,

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 223 

deserting their farms and the little towns which they had begun to build here and there in California. Sailors in San 

Francisco Harbor deserted their ships and rushed to Sutter's 

Fort. Poor Captain Sutter found his land overrun, pits dug all over his ranch, and his water rights disregarded by the prospectors who used the stream to wash out their gold. 

The "Forty-Niners' 99 During the next year- 1849 thousands of gold-seekers poured into California from the East and even from Europe. Some traveled in sailing vessels round Cape Horn, and landed in San Francisco, whence they hastened to the gold-fields. Others went by way of the Isthmus of Panama, where many of them fell sick and died from tropical fevers. 

Still others went overland in prairie schooners, on horseback, or even on foot. Many died on the way, for there were hostile Indians, and the perils of thirst and starvation to be faced as the trail crossed the Western deserts; and an epidemic of cholera found many victims. The long and lonely road to California was marked by many a grave and by the bleaching skeletons of the pack animals which had perished along the way.

Nevertheless, it is said that eighty thousand persons reached California during that year of 1849. Mining camps sprang up in every direction, and a score of towns appeared in which food, tools, and supplies were sold to the eager prospectors. As a rule the men who stayed in the towns and sold goods to the miners made more money out of the gold strike than any one else. Some large fortunes were made and gold worth millions of dollars was taken out of the 

California hills, but thousands of the Argonauts, as they were called, never found a paying claim, and got nothing but hardship and disappointment out of the gold rush.

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 

California Becomes a State

By 1850 there were so many people in California that it was admitted as a State. San Francisco had grown to be a city of twenty thousand people, and in its harbor were vessels from every civilized country in the world. As is usual in a new country where mines have been discovered, there was a great deal of lawlessness and violence among the Bogart

BB 

SAN FRANCISCO IN 1850 

California mining camps. Many of the gold-seekers were rough and desperate characters, and they came in such numbers that there was no organized police force in the State able to control them, and no courts upon the justice of which dependence could be placed.

As a result the more orderly people organized so-called "vigilance committees," made up of men who wanted some security for life and property and were willing to fight for it. 

These vigilance committees acted without the forms of law,

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 225 

and were very severe on any offenders they caught. Hangings were extremely common in the days of the gold rush, often on the slightest of evidence; but the vigilantes did restore order, and gradually the state government grew strong enough to take control of the situation. 

General Zachary Taylor Elected President 

The war with Mexico was a brilliantly victorious war in the field, and it added an immense territory to the Union. 

It was never a really popular war, however, except in the extreme South. Many people felt that it had been unnecessary; that our Government had really bullied a weaker nation into fighting; and that the war had been part of a plan to add a great stretch of slave territory to the United States. 

These people voted against the Democrats in 1848, and the Whigs were successful. Their candidate for President was General Taylor, who had won a military reputation in 

Mexico. President Taylor lived only a little more than a year after his inauguration. He was succeeded by Millard 

Fillmore, the Vice-President. He was the second citizen of 

New York to become President. 

The United States in 1850

At this time it will be interesting to pause in our story to see what the United States was like, between 1850 and 1860.

There had been important changes in the life of the people, in the ways of conducting business, in the schools, and in many other respects since we last viewed the country as it was about 1830.

On the economic side the changes were wrought by the remarkable progress of mechanical invention, the substitution of machines for hand labor, the rapid removal of industry from the homes of the people to shops and factories, and the spread of railways and telegraph lines over the

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 

country. These things are all part of what is called the "industrial revolution." It was not a revolution accompanied by fighting and bloodshed as political revolutions — like our own in 1776usually are; but it brought about much greater changes in the daily life of the people and their ways of earning a living than any political revolution ever did. 

Remarkable Inventions 

The first railway began to operate in 1830. When it proved to be a success, it meant the gradual disappearance 

AN EARLY FORM OF REAPER AND HARVESTER 

of the stagecoach and the freight wagon, drawn by horses, which had been for a long time essential parts of American life and American business. In 1831, Cyrus 

McCormick invented his reaper, and that soon came to replace thousands of horses on the farms of the country. Two years later, in 1836, Ericsson invented the screw propeller which in course of time revolutionized ocean commerce, and made man able to sail the seas without his age-long dependence on a favorable wind.

We have already spoken of Morse's invention of the telegraph in 1844. Two years later, Elias Howe perfected the sewing machine, which at once cut in half the labor of the housewife, and came in time to take most of the manufacture of clothing out of the home and transfer it to the factory. The weaving and spinning of cloth and the making of boots and shoes followed the same course. In 1830, most

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 227 

of the cloth was made by women in the home, and shoemaking was done by local cobblers in their little shops. By 

1860, the case was very different. Factories had not wholly replaced home industry, but they were fast doing so. 

Growth of the Cities 

One result of all this was the increase in the population of the cities. Places at the terminals or junction points of railways, or in locations favorable for manufacturing, grew rapidly. A new class of people filled these large towns- those who worked with machines, on railways, or in the various shops and factories. It was made up partly of native Americans, who left the towns and country villages to engage in a kind of labor that gave them more ready money than they were accustomed to, and partly of immigrants from Europe; for it was during these years that the flood of immigration that has so greatly speeded the growth and development of the United States began to pour into the country. 

A PONY EXPRESS RIDER 

The mail was carried by horsemen from California to Kansas City across fifteen hundred miles of wilderness and mountain range before the transcontinental railroads were built. 

The changes of which we have spoken were most noticeable in the East - in New England, New York, New Jersey, and Pennsylvania. West of the Alleghenies farming still remained the principal occupation, for land there was fertile and cheap, and power for manufacturing, whether through waterfalls or steam, was not easily found. In the 

South, also, the old order still prevailed.

The slave-owners, who controlled everything in that part of the country, loved the life of leisure and liberality on

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 

their great plantations, and dreaded any change as likely to upset the carefully arranged social system that was based on slavery. They discouraged manufacturing in the South, bought whatever goods they needed from Europe or from the North, and maintained in 1860 the same aristocratic, agricultural life that they had followed in 1830. The "poor whites" of the region were quite unable to bring about any change in their way of living even if they had wanted to do so.

Better Schools 

The generation of which we are speaking saw a decided improvement in the intellectual life of the Nation. Great interest was shown in schools.

AN OLD-FASHIONED FIRE ENGINE USED BEFORE THE CIVIL WAR 

Better buildings were erected, broader and more useful courses of study were introduced, and teachers were at last carefully educated for their work. This was especially true in the growing towns and cities, but even the remote rural schools were greatly improved. The first high school had been established in Boston in 1821, and by 1860 there were a great many such schools. Normal schools for teachers also made their appearance about 1840. The name of Horace Mann is conspicuous in this movement for better education; he gave his life to the cause of good schools, and made the school system of Massachusetts, where he lived, a model for the rest of the country. 

You must understand, however, that even the best schools of 1860 were not like the schools children are used to

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 229 

today. Almost every child now has eight or nine months of schooling for at least ten years of his life, and most children attend the high school as well. They enjoy well-lighted, comfortably warmed schoolrooms, and specially trained teachers give them instruction in a wide variety of subjects. 

There are even schools where young people can be taught useful trades. Things were much simpler in 1860. Not many children went beyond the lower schools, or learned much besides reading, writing, spelling, arithmetic, and perhaps a little geography or history. Only a few went to high school and fewer still to college. But education was growing better year by year, and the different States were beginning to lay the foundations for the excellent school systems we have today.

American Literature 

The interest in intellectual improvement showed itself in 

American literature, too. A number of authors appeared whose work not only delighted their fellow citizens, but won recognition and applause in Europe. 

Most of them were New England men, for during this period that section of the country took the lead in literary production. Among them were Henry Wadsworth 

Longfellow, the favorite poet of 

America; Nathaniel Hawthorne, our first really great novelist; 

Oliver Wendell Holmes and James 

Russell Lowell, who wrote both poetry and essays; Ralph Waldo HENRY W. LONGFELLOW 

Emerson, poet and philosopher; John Greenleaf Whittier,

HOW WE ACQUIRED NEW TERRITORY 

the Quaker poet; Henry D. Thoreau, charming writer on nature subjects; and Herman Melville, romancer and novelist. Walt Whitman, who was a native of New York, wrote a strange, striking sort of verse which is more appreciated now than when it was written; and from the South came Edgar Allan Poe, poet and writer of tales, and William 

Gilmore Simms, novelist.

It was an age of oratory, too, and famous orators like 

Daniel Webster, Edward Everett, and Wendell Phillips made addresses that are so fine they can fairly be called literature. American men of letters turned to writing history also. George Bancroft wrote his great history of the 

United States; William H. Prescott wrote of the conquests of Mexico and Peru; John L. Motley told the story of the 

Dutch Republic; and Francis Parkman wrote delightfully the history of France in the New World. In no other period of our history have so many authors of really first-rate quality been active as in the years just before the Civil War.

UNIT X  AND THE ATTEMPTED DISRUPTION OF THE UNION 

UR P to this time you have been reading of the continual growth of the United States in territory and population. It spread across the continent from the Appalachians to the Pacific Ocean, and in 1850 it occupied the same area it does today, except for Alaska, which was not purchased from Russia until 1867. Its population increased from 

4,000,000 in 1790 to 26,000,000 in 1850. As a nation it was steadily growing stronger and apparently more united. 

There were, however, influences at work all the time in the other direction — toward the weakening of the Union and the growth of hostility between the North and the South. 

These influences were the doctrine of States' Rights and the institution of slavery. In this unit you are to learn about both these things.

States' Rights - North and South 

You will remember that from the first there had been two parties in the country- the "strict constructionists" who believed that the Federal Government should have no authority except what the Constitution expressly granted it, and the "broad constructionists" who believed that the Nation should exercise all power that was not expressly denied to it by the Constitution. 

As early as 1798 the Kentucky and Virginia Resolutions took the ground that no State was obliged to obey a federal law of which it disapproved. A few years later, the Embargo and the unpopular War of 1812 stirred up New Eng-

land to a point where secession from the Union was openly advocated. The Hartford Convention in 1814 was almost as strong for the right of a State to nullify a law it did not like, or to withdraw from the Union if it pleased, as South Carolina was in later years. 

Some twenty years later, the State of Georgia practically defied the Federal Government in the matter of removing the Indian tribes from its soil. The Indians had treaties with the Government confirming their right to a certain territory in Georgia, and the Supreme Court again and again decided that the State had no right to compel or persuade the Indians to give up those rights. Georgia persisted, and in the end, the State won its point, since it had the sympathy of Andrew Jackson who was then President. 

The last of the Creeks and Cherokees were removed beyond the Mississippi. 

Chief Justice Marshall's Decisions

The upholders of States' Rights as against the power of the Nation would have stood a much better chance of getting their ideas generally accepted if John 

Marshall had not been for thirtyfour years Chief Justice of the 

United States. Marshall, who was appointed by President Adams in 1801, was a strong 

CHIEF JUSTICE JOHN MARSHALL I 

1 John Marshall was born in Virginia in 1755. He was educated at 

William and Mary College and was a captain in the Continental Army during the Revolution. He was a lawyer and stood high in his profession. 

He was a strong Federalist, and was appointed as an envoy to France; was elected to Congress and was for a time Secretary of State. In 1801, President Adams appointed him Chief Justice of the Supreme Court. served thirty-four years in that office and died in 1835. He

Federalist, and a firm believer in the supremacy of the National Government. Moreover, he was a man of extraordinary ability, and of so much personal force that while he was on the Supreme bench, he was able to dictate the opinions of the court on constitutional matters. 

Even judges who were themselves Democrats, and inclined to a strict construction of the Constitution, yielded to his arguments and sustained his views. In a series of important decisions, Chief Justice Marshall laid down the principles that the National Government was supreme except as to powers expressly reserved to the States; that the Supreme Court had a right to declare acts of Congress unconstitutional and consequently of no effect; and that the Federal Government might possess many "implied powers" - powers which might be thought necessary to the efficient and satisfactory exercise of rights that the Constitution had expressly granted. 

McCulloch vs. Maryland 

For example, in the case known as McCulloch vs. Maryland, Justice Marshall held that the Government might charter and control a National Bank as a part of its undoubted power to maintain an effective financial system for the welfare of the Nation, though the right to establish corporations was nowhere mentioned in the Constitution. 

This decision was unanimous in a court, five of whose seven members had been appointed by Jefferson or Madison as strict constructionists.

The effect of Marshall's decisions was to strengthen at every point the authority of the Federal Government, and to accustom the people to the idea that according to the law, that Government was supreme except in local matters. It is no wonder that Jefferson, the strict constructionist, thought Marshall a "very dangerous man," and lost his temper over more than one of his "Federalist" decisions.

Nullification in South Carolina

In 1832, the doctrine of extreme States' Rights came to an open test of its strength. South Carolina fiercely objected to the tariff of 1828the "Tariff of Abominations" 

which, its leaders said, was drawn so as to make the South pay tribute to the Northern manufacturers. John C. 

Calhoun, its ablest citizen, took the lead in the protest. He declared that the tariff bill was so injurious to South Carolina that the State had a right to nullify it which meant to refuse to permit it to be enforced within its borders. 

This brought the question squarely to a head; was a State so far independent that it could nullify a federal law whenever it chose to do so? Was the 

Constitution, as Calhoun said, a compact between sovereign States," from which each could withdraw at will, or did it create a nation, united and indivisible, to which the States were clearly inferior? 

The "Reply to Hayne"

JOHN C. CALHOUN¹ 

The question was debated in the Senate; Senator Hayne, of South Carolina, spoke in support of the theory that the 

Constitution was only a "compact. He was answered by

99 I John C. Calhoun was born in South Carolina in 1782, and was a graduate of Yale. He was elected to Congress in 1811, became Secretary of War in 1817, and was Vice-President from 1825 to 1833. Later, he was for many years a United States Senator, and he was Secretary of State under 

President Tyler. Daniel Webster, often opposed to him politically, said, on his death in 1850, that he was of "unspotted integrity and unimpeached honor," without a "selfish motive or a selfish feeling."

ever seen. 

Daniel Webster, Senator from Massachusetts, and probably the greatest parliamentary orator the country has In a famous speech that lasted through three sessions of the Senate, Mr. Webster made an argument in behalf of the Union as a true nation, which thrilled the country, and seemed to most Americans a complete reply to the doctrines of Calhoun. He closed with the cry, "Liberty and Union, now and forever, one and inseparable!"

Nevertheless, South Carolina was unconvinced; it still held that a tariff for protection was unconstitutional, and that any State might refuse to obey a law it considered unjust or contrary to the Constitution. So the legislature voted that after January 1, 1833, no duties should be collected at the Charleston custom-house, and that any federal officer who tried to collect them should be arrested. 

Jackson Crushes Nullification

President Jackson now took a hand. He was a Southerner, and, within reason, a States' Rights man; but he was devoted to the Union, for which he had fought at New Orleans, and he had a thorough distrust of Calhoun, with whom he had quarreled. At a dinner arranged by some 

Southern leaders who were sympathetic with South Carolina, he had startled his hosts by giving the toast: "Our 

Federal Union; it must be preserved." Now he sent word to Charleston, "If a single drop of blood is shed in opposition to the laws of the United States, I will hang the first man I can lay my hand on who is engaged in such treasonable conduct."

Every one knew that Jackson meant what he said, and South Carolina began to hesitate. Congress made it easier to withdraw from the dangerous position the nullifiers had got into, by reducing some of the tariff duties to which the State had objected; and in the end South Carolina repealed

its nullification resolution more quietly than it had passed it.

The Beginnings of Slavery 

This might easily have put an end to the extreme ideas of nullification and secession if it had not been for slavery, which soon came to be an issue far more dangerous to the unity of the Nation than any that had gone before. 

The first Negro slaves were brought to Virginia in 1619. 

From that time onward the institution spread throughout the colonies; at one time or another slaves were held in almost every one of them. In the North, however, there were never very many; only a few rich families owned them, and they were usually employed as domestic servants. By the time of the Revolution, slavery was outlawed in most of these Northern colonies; by the beginning of the nineteenth century it was abolished everywhere north of Delaware and Maryland.

In the South, on the other hand, slavery spread, and established itself firmly. The planters of tobacco, rice, and indigo thought that they could not raise those crops without Negro labor, and white people found outdoor work so exhausting in the hot weather that they were glad to have slaves to do it for them. 

Anti-Slavery Feeling in the South

At the same time there was a feeling among the best and wisest Southerners that slavery was wrong morally and harmful economically. Both Washington and Jefferson objected to slavery as a permanent institution and looked forward to a gradual emancipation of the Negroes. 

The influence of such men might have led to the peaceful abolition of slavery, even in the South, if it had not been for the invention of the cotton gin by Eli Whitney in 1793.

That machine made the cultivation of cotton so profitable that the entire South seemed suddenly to turn into one vast cotton field. By 1860, the United States furnished two thirds of the cotton used in the world. 

How Cotton Strengthened Slavery

The enormous expansion of cotton-growing caused a great increase in the demand for slaves. The value of an ablebodied Negro rose from three hundred dollars to one thousand dollars or even two thousand dollars. The South saw its wealth, its prosperity, its social structure dependent on slavery. Men usually succeed in persuading themselves that what is good for their own material interest is also good in itself; so the Southerners came to believe that slavery was not only necessary to the economic prosperity of their section, but was also the proper kind of relation between black and white. They convinced themselves that the Negro was far better off as a slave than he could be as a free man. 

Slavery and the Constitution

The Constitution was strangely silent about slavery. 

That was one of the compromises necessary in order to get a Constitution adopted at all; for the Southern States even in 1787 were sensitive about the subject, and would not have come into the new Nation if there had been any threat of interference with slavery. The African slave-trade was, indeed, forbidden, but the prohibition was not to go into effect until 1808. The only other reference to the institution was the provision that three fifths of the slave population, called "all others" in the Constitution, should be counted in determining the representation of the Southern States in Congress. It was agreed that slavery was a local issue to be left absolutely to the individual States. The Nation was not to interfere.

Effect of Slavery on the South 

Slavery was on the whole a satisfactory system for the great planters, who worked wide stretches of land with a single crop, and that crop one which required a great deal of hard manual labor in the field. In spite of a certain wastefulness, since a great many slaves had to be maintained who were either too young or too old for hard work, slave labor was far cheaper than free labor would have been. It is true that the slaves needed constant oversight, direction, and discipline; but it cost little to maintain them, for food was cheap and abundant, and the simple clothing they wore amounted to almost nothing in the way of expense. 

One of the worst evils of slavery was the effect it had on the poorer white people. There was little chance for a white man to get employment as a mechanic or craftsman, for almost all the trades were carried on by slaves trained to the work on the plantations belonging to their owners. 

Since the black men were all obliged to work and work hard, many of the whites who ought to have been workingmen came to have the idea that labor was unworthy of a white man's dignity; and since they had no other proper means of livelihood, these men became merely shiftless and povertystricken.

Of course many of them lived on farms of their own which they worked; but all the really fertile soil was bought up by the great planters; so the "poor whites," as they were called, had to scrape along as best they could on unproductive acres among the hills or the sand barrens. There was no class of independent and industrious small farmers and artisans in the South to compare with that in the North. 

Life of the Planting Aristocracy 

The well-to-do planters formed a social and political aristocracy. They had plenty of leisure, and they spent it

either in amusing themselves with hunting, horse-racing, and social entertainments at one another's plantation houses, or in the practice of government. They filled most of the important offices and displayed, as a class, a great deal of political ability. Whatever their differences on other questions, they were sure to act together whenever the interests of their class were threatened. For many years 

Southerners directed the policies of the National Government. Eight of our first twelve Presidents were from the South.

Life on the plantations was extremely pleasant for the masters and their families. There was usually money enough for the lavish, hospitable life the Southern people enjoyed. With an abundance of food, an abundance of leisure, and plenty of servants to do the work of the household, the mansions of the slave-owners were very comfortable places in which to live.

There was no little luxury of dress among them; and, as always happens in an aristocratic society, there was an ease and charm of manners in the South which visitors from abroad found delightful in contrast with the more reserved and less refined manners of the commercial and industrial North. The open-handed hospitality of Southern gentlemen and ladies made a deep impression on all such visitors. 

The Southerners were charming hosts, and enjoyed entertaining congenial guests. A Senator from Georgia once made a remark characteristic of the class to which he belonged. He said that there was no need of a hotel in the town near which he lived. Visitors of good family and proper breeding were always welcome in his mansion, and no others were wanted in town on any excuse. 

Life of the Slaves 

The slaves lived in "quarters," at some distance from the

'great house." These were rarely better than log cabins of one or two rooms with an earthen floor. The field hands always had enough to eat and to wear, though the food was often coarse and the clothing hardly more than cotton overalls and heavy boots.

The house-servants were of another, and, as they thought, higher class. They were better dressed, better fed, and better treated. Often relations of confidence and affection existed between the master and mistress and their houseservants. The love of Southern men and women for the 

Negro "mammies," who had been their nurses in childhood, is proverbial.

On plantations where the owners were considerate and kindly, the life of the slaves was far from unhappy. The 

Negroes are inclined to be a cheerful and sociable race. 

They had their own means of recreation, in which singing, dancing, and banjo-playing played a large part. They took great satisfaction in their religion also, which was of a very emotional sort. There was much singing about it; the quaint and melodious Negro "spirituals" are musically very interesting indeed. They were fond of praying, too; at a 

Negro prayer meeting the congregation would frequently pass into almost hysterical religious enthusiasm. 

Slaves were often treated with the greatest kindness by their owners; and the advocates of slavery were always pointing out how the Negroes were never "out of a job" as free workmen sometimes were, and how they were always sure of shelter, food, medical attention and care, even after they were too old to work.

All that is true; but there were also plantations where the owners were hard taskmasters, where the overseers were brutal and merciless, where the whip was in constant use, and the slaves were exposed to abuse of every kind. Add to this, that the slaves were, of course, deprived of any educa-

tion except in some trade, and that they were in constant danger of being separated from those they loved, husbands being sold away from their wives, and children from their mothers, and you can see that the lot of the black people was often intolerably hard. 

Slavery first appeared as a menace to the Union in 1820, when Missouri sought admission as a State. Slaves were already owned there, and the people of Missouri insisted that it must be admitted as a slave State. There was strong opposition to that, for Missouri reached far toward the Northwest, and the people of the North had made up their minds that there should be no slavery in that part of the country.

The Missouri Compromise 

There was a long and excited debate in Congress, and the passions of both the advocates and the opponents of slavery were much aroused. In the end there was a compromise, engineered by Henry Clay who was then Speaker of the House. According to this arrangement Missouri was to be admitted with slavery permitted; but thereafter there should be no slavery in United States territory north of 36° 30' of latitude which is the southern border of Missouri. 

For nearly thirty years the slavery question was carefully kept in the background in Congress, but during those years there was a great growth of anti-slavery sentiment in the North. Before 1830 there were in existence societies pledged to bring about the abolition of slavery; and these societies increased in number and enthusiasm as the years went on. A number of very effective orators and writers rose to lead the movement William Lloyd Garrison, who published The Liberator; Wendell Phillips, an agitator who was as bold as he was eloquent; Charles Sumner, who became a Senator from Massachusetts; Elijah Lovejoy, who

was killed by a hostile mob in Illinois (in 1837) for printing an Abolitionist paper; and others. 

The Abolitionists 

These men were on fire with hatred of slavery. They declared that it was a sin for the Nation to permit slaveholding. Garrison called the Constitution "a covenant with death and an agreement with hell," because it recognized the existence of slavery. Both he and Phillips demanded that if slavery could not be abolished the Union should be broken up, and that the Northern States should form a nation without the curse of slavery.

Only a small minority of Northerners were Abolitionists of this violent stripe. The mass of the people was long indifferent or even hostile to the movement, and most of the leading men, merchants, lawyers, and clergymen were shocked at the idea of forcing the South to free the slaves, and also at the idea of breaking up the Union. The feeling against slavery, however, grew steadily stronger. Most 

Northern men came to agree that although it might be their duty to let the institution alone where it already existed, it was certainly their duty to prevent it from extending into any new territory.

Slavery and States' Rights 

Among the Southern people the anti-slavery agitation in the North aroused both alarm and anger. The Southern people thought that their rights as citizens were menaced, and that sooner or later the North would try to rob them of their property in slaves and destroy the entire social system, based on slavery, in which they believed. Some of them were as violent in their language as Garrison or Phillips. All of them began to say that if the agitation did not stop, the Southern States would be justified in "seceding"

or withdrawing from the Union. They repeated Calhoun's old argument that the Constitution was a "compact," from which any State might withdraw if its interests were threatened. 

The Compromise of 1850 

The annexation of Texas and the Mexican War, with the great accessions of territory that followed, brought on a fresh crisis. The South demanded the right to take slavery into all the vast region taken from Mexico. The sentiment in the North was that this should not be permitted, and 

David Wilmot, a Pennsylvania Congressman, proposed a law called the Wilmot Proviso, that slavery should be forbidden in all the territory acquired from Mexico. 

Congress did not pass the Wilmot Proviso, but the country was stirred from end to end by the discussion of it. The question came up in acute form when in 1850 California sought admission to the Union. Though the Southerners had insisted that they had a right to take slaves into California and own them there, the Californians themselves did not want slavery and meant to prohibit it in their State Constitution.

There were angry debates in Congress over the admission of California and over the policy to be followed in New 

Mexico, Utah, and Arizona. At last another compromise was agreed upon; Henry Clay, now an old man, was conspicuous in arranging this Compromise of 1850, as he had been in the case of the Missouri Compromise. 

The Fugitive Slave Law and the "Underground Railway"

The bill yielded two points to the North. California was to be admitted as a free State, and the slave trade was to be prohibited in the District of Columbia. It gave two points to the South. Slavery was to be permitted in the rest of the

Mexican cession if the inhabitants voted in favor of it, and a very vigorous Fugitive Slave Law was to be passed, giving Southern slave-owners the right to use the federal power to get back runaway slaves who had escaped into the North. 

Daniel Webster, also an old man now, supported the compromise and was bitterly denounced by the Abolitionists in consequence. 

From Siebert's Underground Railroad. Copyright by The Macmillan Company FUGITIVE SLAVES ARRIVING AT A STATION OF THE "UNDERGROUND RAILROAD" 

Most people hoped that this compromise would settle the agitation and quiet the angry feeling that had disturbed the country. It did nothing of the sort. The people of the 

North made no pretense of obeying the unpopular Fugitive Slave Law. If a slave got safely across the Ohio River, few 

Northerners were willing to help in returning him to bondage.

Many actively assisted such runaway Negroes to escape into Canada. There was an organization called the "Underground Railway," composed of persons who were always

ready to receive Negroes who were sent on to them, to hide them from officers of the law, to feed them and shelter them until they could be safely passed on to the next "station" on the road to Canada and freedom.

When escaped slaves were arrested in Northern towns, they were often taken away from the officers by mobs of indignant anti-slavery people, and many States passed laws that gave such Negroes the right to a trial by jury, which the Fugitive Slave Law expressly denied them. It was extremely rare for a jury to vote to send the arrested Negro back into slavery. 

Uncle Tom's Cabin

Of course all this made the Southern people very angry. 

They complained, with good reason, that the North was not carrying out the federal law, and their threats of secession grew louder.

In the midst of this political excitement appeared Harriet 

Beecher Stowe's novel, Uncle Tom's Cabin. This book added fuel to the flames. Though it was not entirely onesided, since it did give some kindly pictures of plantation life, yet it presented also many of the hateful and cruel aspects of slavery. Its effect was to condemn slavery as a system, and it made a great impression on readers everywhere. More than two hundred thousand copies were sold within a year, and the book was widely read in Europe as well as in the United States. Southerners condemned 

Uncle Tom's Cabin as unfair, but throughout the North it was accepted as a true picture of the evils of slavery. 

Rise of the Republican Party

In the election of 1852, the Democrats, who stood firmly by the Compromise of 1850, and the right of slavery to exist without interference, easily elected their candidate, General

Franklin Pierce, of New Hampshire. The Whig Party was already breaking up. Its leaders dared not take a determined position of any sort on the all-absorbing issue, and neither Northern nor Southern people had any confidence in the party.

Within a few years it disappeared completely, and a new party arose which took the name of Republican. It contained anti-slavery Whigs like Senator William H. Seward, of New York, and Abraham Lincoln, of Illinois; and former 

Democrats like Governor Salmon P. Chase, of Ohio, and 

Senator Charles Sumner, of Massachusetts, who were equally opposed to the extension, or even the existence, of slavery.

The Republicans were not an Abolitionist party, however. Their platform only insisted that not another inch of 

United States soil should be given over to the slave-holders, and that the Federal Government should do nothing to protect or encourage slavery, which must be confined to the States where it existed, and be recognized only by the laws of those States.

In 1856 the Republicans entered the Presidential election with John C. Frémont, the "Pathfinder," as their candidate. 

They lost, and James Buchanan, of Pennsylvania, was elected by the Democrats. The Republicans carried most of the Northern States, however, and threatened to be really dangerous to the victorious Democrats by the time of the next Presidential election.

The Kansas-Nebraska Bill 

In 1854, while Pierce was President, Senator Stephen 

A. Douglas, of Illinois, threw a bombshell into Congress with his Kansas-Nebraska Bill. This bill, which organized two Territories, Kansas and Nebraska, out of a portion of the Louisiana Purchase, declared that the Missouri Com-

promise was no longer in effect, having been virtually repealed by the Compromise of 1850. It went on to state that slavery was not any longer prohibited north of 36° 30' as everybody had supposed it was, and that the people of the two new Territories should decide, when the time came to make them into States, whether slaves might be owned there or not. 

This idea of Douglas's, which was popularly called "squatter sovereignty," meant that Congress could no longer have any authority over the introduction of slavery into United States territory; that all territory not already organized into free States was open to slave-holders if they wished to enter it, and that the last chance of a peaceful settlement of the controversy over slavery was gone. 

STEPHEN A. DOUGLAS I 

The Struggle over Kansas

Congress passed the bill, and the first result was a desperate battle between freedom and slavery on the plains of 

Kansas. Nobody ever tried to take slaves into Nebraska; but hundreds and finally thousands of men from Missouri, Arkansas, and some other Southern States hastened to move into Kansas, with a view to voting slavery into it. 

At the same time, thousands of people from New England, New York, and the Middle West poured westward,

I Stephen A. Douglas was born in Vermont in 1813. He removed as a young man to Illinois where he early became active in politics. He was a member of the Illinois Legislature, a judge of the State Supreme Court, and a Congressman from 1843 to 1847. From 1847 to his death in 1861, he was a United States Senator, and in 1860 was a candidate for President.

Almost equally determined to win Kansas for freedom. every one who went to Kansas in those days was a partisan, even more interested in settling the political future of the State than in making homes and finding prosperity there. 

They all went armed or had arms sent to them; and from the very first Kansas became a battlefield. 

Kansas Becomes a Free State

There were frequent clashes between the two parties and no little blood was spilt. The slavery party was at a disad- 

Courtesy Dept. of Agriculture SOD HOUSES 

Early settlers in Kansas and Nebraska often lived in houses made of turf cut from the prairie soil vantage, because slave-owners hesitated to move into Kansas in large numbers while it was possible that the State would in the end outlaw slave property; but armed bands of "border ruffians" from Missouri made up the lack of genuine citizens by crossing the border to vote whenever an election was held.

There was political trickery as well as violence in Kansas, and for a time a proslavery government was in power, upheld by President Pierce, although there was never an actual majority of the voters behind it. In the end, however, the

Free-State Party grew too strong to be counted out; and after three years of disorder and civil strife, Kansas voted slavery out by a decisive majority. In 1861, it was admitted to the Union.

The Dred Scott Decision 

While the excitement over Kansas was at its height, the Supreme Court handed down a decision that aroused fresh indignation in the North. This was the famous Dred Scott decision. The case was that of a slave who had been taken into free territory by his master and who sued for his freedom on the ground that he could not be held as a slave on free soil.

The opinion of the Court on the case itself was not of importance, for it declined to assume the authority of deciding it; but in the course of his remarks Chief Justice Taney laid down these three statements of the law: first, that a slave was not a person, but a chattel or piece of property; second, that the Missouri Compromise was unconstitutional, since Congress had no right to legislate concerning slavery in the Territories of the United States; and third, that a master had the same right to take his slave into a free State, without losing his property rights in him, as to take thither a horse or a cow or a dog.

Rise of Abraham Lincoln 

The Dred Scott decision caused the greatest excitement among the ever-increasing numbers of anti-slavery people of the North. It seemed to yield everything to the extremists of the South, and to put slavery beyond any possibility of control. The Republicans believed it to be bad law, but it was law because the Supreme Court had pronounced it. 

The issues presented by the decision were everywhere discussed. They were the chief substance of a famous series of

LINCOLN'S FIRST HOME IN ILLINOIS 

A log cabin built by his father and himself in 1831 debates in Illinois, in 1858, between Senator Douglas, who was a candidate for reëlection, and Abraham Lincoln, who opposed him. It was in these debates that Lincoln first appeared as a great leader. The ability and force of his speeches made a deep impression on the country. In his speech accepting his nomination, he made the remarkable statement: "A house divided against itself cannot stand. I believe this government cannot exist permanently, half slave, and half free. I do not expect the Union to be dissolved. I do not expect the house to fall. I do expect it will cease to be divided. It will become all one thing or all the other." 

John Brown's Raid

If the North was stirred by the Dred Scott decision, the South was even more upset by the John Brown raid in 1859. 

Brown was an old man of seventy, a fanatical abolitionist, who had taken part in many of the skirmishes over slavery

in "bleeding Kansas." He was deeply religious, and had brooded so long over the evils of slavery that he imagined himself called of God to put an end to it. 

He planned to aid the Negroes to escape from their masters, and to establish a rallying-place for them among the mountains of Virginia. With this purpose, he gathered a party of some twenty men, and seized the United States 

Arsenal at Harper's Ferry.

His scheme was hopeless, of course. The Negroes did not flock to him; he was surrounded and captured by a body of troops under Lieutenant-Colonel Robert E. Lee; and he was finally convicted of conspiring against the Government and hanged.

Many people in the North thought of John Brown as a martyr in the cause of liberty; but throughout the South his raid created both alarm and indignation. The Southerners did not understand that he had no substantial support in his rash venture. They were convinced that he was the agent of important men in the North, and that it was part of the anti-slavery program to stir up in the South slave insurrections with all their horror and bloodshed.

The Campaign of 1860 

Amidst these controversies and passions, the election of 

1860 approached. The Republicans nominated Abraham 

Lincoln. Their platform declared for the restriction of slavery to the fifteen States where it existed, and the guarantee of free institutions everywhere else. 

The Democratic Party split in two. The Northern wing of the party insisted on nominating Senator Douglas. The 

Southern wing would not accept him, for they had begun to suspect him of not being so much devoted to their view of the Constitution as they had at first thought. So they nominated a candidate of their own — Vice-President Breckinridge.

A fourth group, made up of old Whigs who still hoped to compromise things somehow, also nominated candidates 

John Bell and Edward Everett. 

Lincoln is Elected 

With the Democratic Party divided, Lincoln's election was inevitable. He had not nearly so many popular votes as the other three candidates combined, but he carried almost every 

Northern State and had a clear majority in the electoral college. His election was the signal for secession in the South.

The leaders had already laid their plans. They declared that the Republican victory meant that sooner or later their slaves would be set free against their consent, and

When he saw the proof, Lincoln remarked, "Well, that expresses me better than any I have seen.'

They went on to repeat the arguments of the South 

¹ Abraham Lincoln was born in 1809 in Kentucky. With his father, always a restless pioneer, he moved first to Indiana and then to Illinois. He had little education except what he got for himself by reading. By turns he worked on pioneer farms, or as a flatboatman, surveyor, and storekeeper. 

He studied law in his spare time from other occupations and became a lawyer. Always interested in politics, he was for some years a member of the 

Illinois Legislature, and was sent to Congress in 1846. He became a leader of the Republican Party in Illinois, was an unsuccessful candidate for United States Senator in 1855 and again in 1858. He was elected President in 1860, reëlected in 1864, and was assassinated a year later.

Carolina nullifiers that the Constitution was only a compact between the States, from which any State might withdraw if it felt itself oppressed or unjustly treated. That time, they said, had come. Secession was the only remedy. 

The South Secedes 

South Carolina was the first to go. Its legislature passed an ordinance of secession on December 20. The Gulf 

States, Alabama, Mississippi, Louisiana, Texas, and Florida, quickly followed. Georgia delayed a little and so did 

Virginia, North Carolina, and Tennessee. There was much strong Union feeling in those States, and much hesitation about taking the fatal plunge. In the end, they all seceded, and so did Arkansas, though not until after Lincoln was inaugurated and showed that he meant to use force in order to bring back the seceding States. 

No one

The consternation in Washington was great. knew quite what to do. The Government remained silent while the seceding States seized the arsenals and customhouses belonging to the United States. The President, old 

James Buchanan, was puzzled, worried, and uncertain about his duty. He did nothing to check secession, recover the seized government property, or assert the authority of the United States in the States that had defied it. Before the day came for Lincoln's inauguration, a Southern

Confederacy had been formed by a Congress meeting at 

Montgomery, Alabama, and Jefferson Davis, of Mississippi, and Alexander H. Stephens, of Georgia, had been elected 

President and Vice-President. It was evident that if the 

Union were not to be dissolved, war was necessary.

I. What was the doctrine of States' Rights? Give as many instances as you can of its appearance in early American history. 

2. Do you think it was the proper view of the relations between the States and the Nation, or not? Give your reasons. 

3. What did Chief Justice Marshall think about it? Tell something of his services to the Nation, and of his character and personality. 

4. What effect did his famous decisions have on the development of the Nation? 

5. What was nullification and what led South Carolina to try it? Who was the leader of nullification in South Carolina? 

6. What did Daniel Webster have to say about States' Rights and nullification? 

7. What did President Jackson do about it? 

8. Why did public feeling about slavery in the South change between 1790 and 1830? 

9. What did the Constitution say about slavery? 

10. What effect did slavery have on the "poor-whites" of the South? 

II. Describe the life of a rich planter in the South.

UNIT XI  

LAVERY and States' Rights at last produced the effects SLA they had threatened so long. The South seceded, and the North went to war rather than see the Union dissolved. 

It was to be a long and bloody war. In this unit we shall follow the events of the first two years. They were years of discouragement, during which no amount of sacrifice and no quantity of bloodshed seemed to bring the struggle any nearer a conclusion. You will, we hope, get from it some idea of the desperate character of the fighting, and of the patience, wisdom, and skill with which Abraham Lincoln guided the affairs of the Union through this crisis. Toward the end of the period, we shall find him proclaiming the freedom of the slaves — a proclamation which only victory on the battlefield could make good. 

The Superior Strength of the North

In 1861, there were nearly 22,000,000 people in the States that remained loyal to the Union, and only 9,000,000 in the Southern Confederacy more than a third of whom were Negro slaves. The area covered by the Confederacy was only one third of that of the States remaining in the Union. 

The wealth of the North was also much greater than that of the South. Its people were not occupied in raising a few great agricultural crops. Besides much diversified farming, they were engaged in commerce, manufacturing, mining, and industries of many kinds. 

The natural resources of the North, apart from the fertility of the soil, were greater, for coal, iron, gold, and silver

were already being mined in large quantities. There were few factories in the South, which had always been dependent on other States or other countries for manufactured goods; while in the North there were mills, factories, and mechanics in great numbers, so that everything needed in war, from shoes to gunboats, could be easily produced. 

Finally the railway systems and the banking system of the North were much more advanced than those in the South. Consequently, it was much easier for the Union 

Government to get the money it needed and to transport its men and supplies to the front than for the Confederate Government to do the same thing. Indeed, the South had no way of getting ready money except by selling its crops principally its cotton - and when, early in the war, the Union navy blockaded the coast and so put a stop to the export of cotton, the South was hard put to it to raise any money at all. Finally, there was a well-equipped navy in the hands of the Union, while the South had no navy and few ships of any kind. 

Southern Advantages

With all this superiority in man power, wealth, and materials, it may seem strange that the Northern armies were not at once victorious, but the South had some advantages, too. Their people were intensely devoted to the cause of secession; at first, indeed, they were more in earnest than the people of the North. Many Northerners still doubted whether the Government had a right to coerce seceding States, and whether, if it had that right, it was worth while to fight a war to maintain it. 

The Southern troops also were on the defensive in their own country, which they knew well; and an army in defense, on its own ground, does not need so many men as an army of invasion, which is often far distant from its base of supplies.

The Southern man also was more military in his tastes and more inclined to military service. The Southern generals, most of them West Point graduates who had seen war service, were, in the first years of the war, better equipped for leadership than the Northern officers, many of whom were volunteers from civil life. 

What the South Hoped for

The Southern leaders, moreover, counted on two things to bring them victory. They did not believe the North would undertake or support a long war just to keep the Union intact; and they did not believe the world could get on without Southern cotton. "Cotton is King," they said. 

If there were no cotton for the Northern mills, they thought Northern business men would demand that the war should cease. If there were no cotton for the great English and 

French mills, they thought England and France would interfere in their behalf. In both these ideas they were quite mistaken; but it took the experience of war to prove it to them. 

Lincoln's Inaugural

When Lincoln reached Washington, he found everything in confusion. President Buchanan had literally done nothing either to prepare for war or to reach a peaceful settlement with the seceding States. Worried statesmen were proposing all sorts of compromises in the hope of averting war, but Lincoln made it clear that he would not compromise. The States that had gone out must come back, or he would use the federal power to bring them back into the Union.

At the same time there was nothing loud or boastful in his attitude. His inaugural address was an eloquent plea, gently and kindly expressed, for the reunion of the States

under the old flag. He was willing to promise the South not to interfere with slavery in the States where it existed; but he would not agree that any State, at any time, for any reason, could leave the Union except aș an act of revolution.

Lincoln's Cabinet 

Lincoln's Cabinet contained men of various party attachments. Senator Seward, of New York, was Secretary of

State; and Governor Chase, of Ohio, was Secretary of the 

Treasury. Both were Republicans, and both gave faithful and valuable service to the Union. Gideon Welles, the

Secretary of the Navy, was a Democrat, but a strong Union man; and when his first Secretary of War proved unsatisfactory, Lincoln appointed another Union Democrat, Edwin M. Stanton, to the post. He meant to make use of all the talent and all the political strength that he could call to the defense of the Union. Stanton was a peculiarly difficult man to work with, irritable, stubborn, and not always fair, but Lincoln managed him with unfailing tact, and used his energy and honesty and driving power with great effect throughout the war.

The new President was a man of small experience in high office; but he had qualities that made him a great executive, and as a manager of men he has had few equals. He learned quickly, and his patience, his tolerance, and his shrewd common-sense helped him to solve successfully difficult problems that many a more brilliant and more widely experienced public man would have found too much for him.

The War Begins 

For more than a month, Lincoln took no open steps against the Confederacy, and he was accused by impatient people of indecision and slothfulness. His plan, however,

was to make the South take the first hostile action, which it did on April 12, 1861. On that day, Confederate troops opened fire on Fort 

Sumter in the harbor of Charleston, South Carolina, that was still occupied by a Union garrison; and obliged the commander, Major 

Anderson, to surrender. 

Lincoln's patience was now justified. 

The North, hitherto doubtful and uncertain, arose in wrath at the news that Sumter had been fired on; and the border States of Maryland, Kentucky, and Missouri - all slave States, and at times inclining to the Confederacy began to 

ABRAHAM LINCOLN 

The famous statue by Saint-Gaudens in Lincoln Park, Chicago swing the other way. In the end all three remained loyal to the Union. 

The President at once issued a call for 75,000 volunteers, and many more than that number offered themselves. 

Half-equipped and untrained in war, these men were hurried forward, most of them for the defense of Washington. 

Meanwhile President Davis also issued his call for troops

and began to organize an army to cover Richmond, Virginia, which had been finally chosen to be the capital of the Confederacy.

It was mid-July before these armies were in any condition to fight, and even then they were more like raw militia than like real armies. They advanced on one another, nevertheless, and met at Manassas on the banks of a creek called 

Bull Run, on July 21. General Beauregard commanded the

Confederate, and General McDowell the Union army. 

Bull Run and General McClellan

The battle was a confused engagement in which men fought bravely, but without much intelligence. It ended in Harrisburg
