THE SCENE OF WAR IN THE EAST 

the defeat of the Union army, which fell back in a kind of panic toward Washington. The defeat of Bull 

Run was all that was needed to arouse the fighting spirit of the North. 

The President called for 

300,000 men and the call was promptly answered. 

General McClellan, an officer who had had professional training at West 

Point, and had already done good service in driving Confederate bands out of the western mountains of Virginia, was called to Washington and put in command of the Army of the Potomac, as it was henceforth called.

McClellan was a good organizer and a thorough disciplinarian. He devoted himself to making a real army out of

the abundant volunteer material that was offered him, and he succeeded admirably. He did not venture to attack the 

Confederates until he had built up an army in which he had confidence. On their part the Southern generals, Johnston and Beauregard, were busy organizing their own army, and made no attempt to move against Washington during the fall and winter of 1861-62. At this time it was President 

Davis's idea not to take the offensive, but to wait for the stopping of cotton exports to produce its expected effect on the North and on England. 

The Union Blockade

Cotton was steadily becoming scarce. While the Northern army was marking time, learning the rudiments of war under McClellan, the navy was extremely busy blockading the seaports of the South so that cotton could not get out, nor much-needed military supplies get in. The blockade was so effective that cotton exports from the South fell from $202,000,000 in 1860 to less than $4,000,000 in 1862. 

Now and then a swift, well-handled ship would slip out of a Southern port with a cargo of cotton, and get by the blockade under cover of storm or darkness. When that happened, the cotton sold for very high prices in England, but most of the money went to the "blockade-runner," and not to either the cotton planter or the Confederate Government. 

There were many daring sea captains who undertook to run the blockade, but few of them escaped capture, and as the blockade tightened, month by month, their number grew smaller and smaller.

The Trent Affair 

Still Great Britain took no action to interfere between the combatants. The British Government was openly sympa-

thetic with the South, and among the wealthy classes and the aristocracy the feeling was even stronger. Prince Albert and Queen Victoria, and some statesmen like John Bright were firmly set against going to war with the United States, and among the masses of the people even the cotton spinners who were thrown out of work by the shortage of cotton the cause of the South was unpopular, because in the public mind it was linked with slavery. 

Once it seemed as if Great Britain might take sides 

against the Union. An American warship stopped a British vessel, the Trent, and Captain Wilkes took from it two Confederate agents, Mason and Slidell, who were on their way to Europe to seek aid from either France or 

England. These men were brought back to Boston and put 

THE STARS AND BARS" in prison. 

Confederate Battle Flag high-handed action.

The British Government protested very sharply against this 

If our Government had replied in a similar tone, no one knows what might have happened; but President Lincoln, remembering how the United States had fought the War of 1812 in protest against just this sort of "search and seizure," ordered Mason and Slidell released, and informed Great Britain that Captain Wilkes had acted without any authorization. So that trouble blew over without leading to serious consequences.

The Monitor and the Merrimac In March, 1862, there occurred a very important naval engagement in Hampton Roads at the mouth of the James River. When Virginia seceded, the Virginia authorities

had seized the United States Navy Yard at Norfolk, together with ships and supplies worth some $10,000,000. 

Among the ships was one named the Merrimac. The Confederates took this vessel, and ingeniously made it over into an "ironclad." They covered her hull with plates of iron, built a strong ram of the same material over her bow and armed her with fifteen cannon.

Then the Merrimac - renamed the Virginia went out to attack the Union squadron of five ships that was blockading the entrance to Chesapeake Bay. 

The wooden ships of the squadron were helpless before the ironclad. Two were sunk and three driven aground. 

The next day the 

Virginia returned, intending to complete its work by sinking the grounded vessels, but she was met by a strange craft that had arrived in Hampton Roads during the night.

This was an iron vessel, too, but of a quite new type, designed by an engineer named John Ericsson, and called the Monitor. It had been secretly built in the New York Navy Yard. 

Painting by Halsall, in the Capitol

THE MONITOR AND THE MERRIMAC 

The first naval battle between two ironclads

The Monitor had a low flat deck, almost at the level of the water, and a round turret amidships armed with two very large cannon. People called her "a cheese-box on a raft." There was a hot fight there in Hampton Roads.

Neither ship could sink the other, but the Merrimac or Virginia was so badly damaged by the Monitor's fire and by the loss of its iron ram that it withdrew to Norfolk. 

---

This celebrated battle was watched with interest by the whole world. It proved the value of ships of iron as compared with those of wood. Ever since that day the warships of the world's navies have been built and armored with iron or steel. 

Fighting in the West

While McClellan still hesitated to attack in the East, some lively fighting went on in the West. There were many scattered battles or skirmishes in Missouri and Kentucky, as a result of which both these States were occupied by 

Union troops.

Both sides were anxious to keep control of the Mississippi River and of the important streams, like the Cumberland and the Tennessee, which flow into it. If the 

Union forces should be able to get such control, they would cut off the western part of the Confederacy from the rest, and prevent the South from using these rivers the natural means of transportation in that region, where railways were still few. 

General Grant Emerges

The Confederates had built two forts to command the 

Tennessee and Cumberland Rivers - Fort Henry and Fort 

Donelson. General Grant, who commanded the Union army in that vicinity, moved unexpectedly against them in the middle of winter, and though they were bravely defended, he took first one and then the other, together with 15,000 prisoners and much material of war. This was the most important victory yet won by the Union arms. 

Following the capture of these forts, Grant moved forward to face a strong Confederate army under General A. S. Johnston. There was a bloody battle at Shiloh (or 

Pittsburg Landing) that lasted two days (April 6, 7, 1862).

At first the Confederates seemed to be winning; but Grant got timely reënforcements, General Johnston was killed in battle, and in the end the Confederates retreated into Mississippi. 

Farragut at New Orleans

Cincinnat 

INDIANA 

These battles gave the Unionists command of all the important rivers, north of Vicksburg in Mississippi. At the same time they won the lower Mississippi, too, including the city of New 

Orleans. This was accomplished by the navy. Captain Farragut 

soon to be made an admiral 

at- tacked the forts at the mouth of the Mississippi, with a powerful fleet of eighty-two ships and gunboats. When he found that he could not reduce them by gunfire, he boldly ran past them, breaking the great iron chains with which the channel had been obstructed. The taking of 

New Orleans itself then became an easy matter. 

These victories were of great value to the North,

WEST since they struck a blow at the Confederacy that was more serious than was realized at the time except by educated military men. The eyes of the public were still fixed on the armies that faced each other between Washington and

Richmond, and it was thought that battles elsewhere were of only minor importance. 

Lee and Jackson

At last, after long months of preparation, McClellan 

GENERAL ROBERT E. LEE 

was ready to move. The Army of the Potomac, more than 100,000 strong, was in splendid condi- tion. McClellan's plan was to transport the army by water to the peninsula of Yorktown 

where Cornwallis had surrendered to Washington eighty years before and at- 

tack Richmond from the East. 

The Confederate Army of Northern Virginia was now commanded by General Robert E. Lee, 1 Robert E. Lee, born in Virginia in 1807, was a son of the Revolutionary cavalry general, "Light-Horse Harry" Lee. He was educated at West

Point, served with distinction in the Mexican War, and was a lieutenantcolonel in the United States Army when Virginia seceded. Lee opposed secession, but felt that his duty was to follow his State when it left the Union. In 1861, he was major-general in command of the Virginia troops; in 1862 he became commander of the Army of Northern Virginia. A brilliant soldier, he won, as a man, the love of his troops and of the Southern people. After the war, until his death in 1870, he was president of Washington University in Virginia. In his honor the name of the college was changed after his death to Washington and Lee University.

- a very brilliant officer who had seen almost forty years of service in the regular army, and who had reluctantly cast in his lot with secession when his State - Virginia - left the Union. He had an army as large as McClellan's; for that general had to leave a strong force to defend Washington, which was threatened by a Confederate army under General 

Thomas J. Jackson (Stonewall Jackson).¹ 

Jackson was a commander of real genius, and his victories in the Shenandoah Valley, over the Union generals who opposed him, had alarmed the North and made Lincoln and his advisers fear for the safety of Washington, which could easily be approached from the mouth of the Shenandoah Valley. 

McClellan Fails in the Peninsula

In the peninsula campaign McClellan met with only misfortune. He was outgeneraled by Lee, who suddenly called back Jackson to reënforce him. In a series of battles lasting for seven days (in June, 1862) the Union Army was so roughly handled that McClellan decided to withdraw toward Washington again. 

There was deep disappointment in the North over

McClellan's failure, and corresponding elation in the South. 

Lee and Jackson determined to follow up their victories by attacking McClellan. They did so, and after a second victory at Bull Run, won by Jackson's brilliant strategy, the Army of Northern Virginia pushed on across the Potomac into Maryland.

But McClellan on defense was more dangerous than on the offensive. He met Lee at Antietam (or Sharpsburg), and though the battle was not decisive, Lee felt it wise to fall back again into Virginia. 

¹ Jackson got his name at the battle of Bull Run, where his division stood "like a stone wall," when the rest of the army seemed about to give way before the Union attack.

The Emancipation Proclamation 

President Lincoln took this occasion to issue a proclamation of emancipation which he had long been considering. 

The plan of freeing the slaves in the seceding States was frankly a war measure. The Southerners, by using their slaves to produce food and other supplies made it possible to put almost their entire white man-power into the army. 

Painting by Carpenter, in the Capitol

LINCOLN READS THE EMANCIPATION PROCLAMATION TO HIS CABINET 

Stanton Chase Lincoln 

Welles Smith Seward Blair Bates 

Lincoln believed that if he declared the freedom of the slaves, many of them would leave the plantations, and refuse to work any longer for their masters.

It is true, of course, that Lincoln hated slavery, and believed it to be morally wrong and economically ruinous, but he felt always that his first duty as President was to maintain or restore the Union. He delayed emancipating the slaves as long as there was the slightest hope of securing the return of the seceded States without his doing so.

Even now the Emancipation Proclamation (issued in September) was conditional. It freed the Negroes in the 

States that were in rebellion, but not in the loyal States like 

Maryland and Kentucky. Moreover, it was not to go into full force until January 1, 1863. If any seceded State returned to the Union before that date, it was not to apply to that State. Of course none of them did return, and on 

January 1st, emancipation throughout the Confederacy was proclaimed.

Effect of the Proclamation 

This step naturally aroused not only the country but the world. It created the greatest indignation in the South, and even in the North there were those who denied that the President had any power to free the slaves. It was obviously a revolutionary thing to do, and could be defended only as a war measure taken against States that were in open warfare against the Government.

However, it was in general received with great enthusiasm in the North, where it was clearly understood that slavery was at the bottom of all the bloodshed and suffering through which the Nation was passing. On Europe it had a remarkable effect. Hitherto the war had seemed to Europeans merely a struggle between two groups of States over a matter of constitutional interpretation. The Emancipa tion Proclamation made it a battle over a great moral issue that of the right or wrong of Negro slavery. 

After the emancipation of the slaves there was never any reason to fear that the governments of Great Britain or

France would dare to come to the open support of the South. Public opinion in those countries was henceforth on the side of the Union.

Outline Summary I. The strength of North and South compared 

(a) Superior wealth, industrial development, and transportation facilities of the North (b) Superior man-power of the North (c) The South at first more united than the North, and more military in its tastes (d) The South on the defensive (e) The South believed the North and the world could not get on without its cotton 

2. The first year of the war (a) Lincoln's Inaugural; his Cabinet 

(b) Bombardment of Fort Sumter 

(c) Battle of Bull Run; the North awakes (d) The Union navy blockades Southern harbors (e) The Trent affair; trouble with Great Britain avoided (f) The fight of the Monitor and the Merrimac (g) General McClellan organizes the Union Army, but hesitates to fight 

3. Emancipation 

(a) Military successes of General Grant in the West (b) Admiral Farragut with his fleet seizes New Orleans (c) General Lee defeats General McClellan near Richmond and drives him back; General Jackson threatens Washington (d) Lee and Jackson checked at Antietam 

(e) Lincoln issues the Emancipation Proclamation 

Questions 

1. Compare the North of 1860 with the South, in population, manufactures, and wealth. Why had the North outstripped the South in these respects? 

2. Did the South have any advantages on its side? If so, what were they? 

3. What two reasons gave the South hope of victory? 

4. Contrast Buchanan's attitude toward nullification or secession with that of Andrew Jackson.

5. Compare the feeling in the North before and after Fort

Sumter was fired on. What effect did the defeat of Bull Run have on the North? 

6. In what ways was General McClellan successful as a commander; in what ways unsuccessful? 

7. Why was the blockade of Southern ports undertaken? 

8. Give an account of the Trent affair. Why did President Lincoln give up Mason and Slidell to Great Britain? 

9. Tell the story of the battle between the Monitor and the

Merrimac. Why was this so important a battle in naval history? 

10. What was the great object of Grant at Fort Donelson and 

Shiloh, and of Farragut at New Orleans? 

II. Do you think General Lee was right or wrong in following his State (Virginia) out of the Union? Give your reasons. 

12. Give some important facts about General Stonewall Jackson. 

13. Why did not Lincoln emancipate the slaves when the war first began? On what ground did he do so in September, 1862? 

14. What effect did the Emancipation Proclamation have on the 

North? The South? Europe?

Activities 

Give oral reports on Robert E. Lee, Stonewall Jackson, George 

B. McClellan.

If time permits, have the children make special reports on important battles or campaigns which the teacher may select.

Two men, one who has been a sailor and the other a Western farmer, discuss whether Lincoln should have surrendered Mason and Slidell. The sailor thinks he should not; the farmer thinks he did right. Dramatize.

Write two editorials on the Emancipation Proclamation, one for a strong Republican paper in the North, and one for a Democratic paper in Maryland which thinks he had no right to issue the proclamation.

Make a list of important battles mentioned in the text, showing which was the victor in each. Give any reasons you can think of why the South should have been encouraged at the end of 1862, and any reasons why the North should have been encouraged. 

Show why the battle between the Monitor and the Merrimac

was important both to the conduct of the war and to naval construction for many years to come.

On a large map show the places named: Richmond, Charleston, Vicksburg, New Orleans, Bull Run, the Peninsula, Antietam,

Forts Henry and Donelson, Corinth, Mobile. Show why it was so important for the North (a) to close the Southern seaports by blockade and (b) to get control of the Mississippi. 

For your notebook. Write brief accounts of Abraham Lincoln,

Robert E. Lee, and Stonewall Jackson. Use an outline map and mark the places of important battles as you read about them. 

Show the position of the Union and Confederate armies in Virginia in April, 1862, and in September, 1862, the Union Army almost besieging Richmond in April and forced to the defense of Washington in September. Show the position of Union and Confederate armies in the West in the same months. On a time line show the important battles and other events of 1861 and 1862.

UNIT XII  

ITH this unit we bring to a close the first part of our WITH book. The long war comes to an end with the victory of the Union and the preservation of our country an undivided nation. You will read of many famous battles and of many great soldiers and statesmen. The greatest of them all is Abraham Lincoln with whose death at the hands of an assassin our story closes. 

The Alabama 

It was said at the end of the last unit that the government of Great Britain and France did not venture to give the Confederacy any open assistance, but they did permit a good deal of aid to be given secretly. 

The South had no navy of importance, but it succeeded in fitting out a number of ships to act as privateers. These ships captured and sunk many merchant vessels of the United States that were engaged in commerce- two hundred and fifty-eight in all. The most famous of these Confederate cruisers was the Alabama. This vessel was actually built with British money in a British shipyard and sent to sea with a British crew, though its commander, Captain Semmes, was an American from the South. 

The Alabama cruised the sea for two years, taking refuge in British or French ports when pursued. It destroyed nearly sixty American merchant ships. At length the 

United States man-of-war Kearsarge overtook the Alabama in the British Channel, and, in a hot sea-fight off the French port of Cherbourg, sunk her.

Our Minister to Great Britain, Charles Francis Adams,

told the British Government bluntly that its conduct with respect to the Alabama amounted to making war; and after the Civil War was over, Great Britain had to pay the United States $15,500,000 for the ships the Alabama had destroyed. 

Napoleon III in Mexico 

The French Emperor Napoleon III was frankly friendly to the South; and while we were engaged in the war he took advantage of the situation to send an army to Mexico, and obliged the Mexicans to consent to have an Austrian Archduke, Maximilian, for their 

Emperor. This was the firstand last time that any European Government ever tried to defy the Monroe Doctrine.¹ 

When the war was over and the Union was victorious, Napoleon III found that he would have to call home his troops or fight the United States. He

GENERAL "STONEWALL" JACKSON 

chose the former course, and Maximilian's empire promptly collapsed. The Mexicans rose against him, took him prisoner, and shot him.

Lee Wins Two Great Battles 

The year 1863 was marked by hard fighting in both the East and the West. General McClellan had at last been removed from the command in the East. Great as his services had been in organizing the Army of the Potomac, he was slow to act and unwilling to press the fighting. He was also disrespectful to the President when urged to take the offensive. 

I See Unit VI, page 144.

None of the generals who succeeded him — Burnside, Pope, or Hooker was a match for Lee. Every time they met him they were beaten. At Fredericksburg and at Chancellorsville the Confederates won great victories, though in the latter battle they met with a terrible loss in the death of Stonewall Jackson, who was killed in the moment of victory.

Lee is Repulsed at Gettysburg 

After Chancellorsville, Lee determined to invade the North. He moved quickly past the Army of the Potomac 

From a contemporary engraving

THE BATTLE OF GETTYSBURG 

which was standing in defense of Washington, crossed 

Maryland, and appeared suddenly in Pennsylvania. He was moving toward Philadelphia with an army of 75,000 men, when he came face to face with the Army of the Potomac (now under General Meade) which had turned northward to meet him.

The armies fought at Gettysburg. The battle which

lasted for three days (July 1-3) was one of the bloodiest in the history of war. Lee attacked the strong positions of Meade again and again, but he could not carry them. 

At length his losses were so great that he felt obliged to retreat. The final assault, on July 3d, commanded by 

General Pickett, with 15,000 men, has been called "the high tide" of the Confederacy. When it was repulsed, the 

North drew a long breath of relief; for the fear of what would follow a Confederate victory was profound.¹ 

Grant Takes Vicksburg 

Meanwhile, in the 

West the last steps were being taken to win complete control of the Mississippi 

River. Almost all of it was in Union hands, but the stronghold of

GENERAL ULYSSES S. GRANT 2 

Vicksburg, high on the bluffs above the river, was still held by a Confederate army under General Pemberton.

I In November, 1863, a National Cemetery was dedicated at Gettysburg, wherein to bury the bodies of the men who fell there. It was on this occasion that Lincoln delivered his famous Gettysburg Address. (See Appendix C.) 

2 Ulysses S. Grant was born in Ohio in 1822. He graduated from West 

Point and served in the Mexican War. After reaching the rank of captain in the United States Army, he resigned and was in private life when the Civil War began. He was given a commission as colonel of an Illinois regiment and rose rapidly to the rank of brigadier and then major-general. 

In 1864, he was made lieutenant-general in command of the armies of the United States. In 1868, he was elected President and was reëlected in 

1872. He died in 1885.

Against this fortress General Grant directed his campaign. 

He tried one plan after another, but they all failed, so difficult was the approach to the city. At length Grant moved his army to the south of Vicksburg, assisted by the Union gunboats, which ran the batteries of the forts as Farragut had run those below New Orleans.

Then Grant came up behind the city, and after defeating General J. E. Johnston, who was trying to bring up reenforcements, he laid siege to the fortress. For a month or two it held out; but food and ammunition failing, it was forced to surrender. This was on July 4, the day after the battle of Gettysburg; the two great victories greatly elated the North and correspondingly depressed the South. "The Battle Above the Clouds"

Soon after the fall of Vicksburg, General Grant was sent to Chattanooga to take charge of the campaign there.

This city was the most important of the few railway centers in the South; its possession was of great value to either army. The Union general, Rosecrans, had tried in vain to take it; at Chickamauga he was soundly beaten by General

Bragg. It was in this battle that General G. H. Thomas, a Virginian who had remained loyal to the Union, got his name, "the Rock of Chickamauga," because his division stood firm and prevented the defeat from becoming a rout. 

After Grant arrived, the face of affairs changed. He restored the army to fighting condition, planned an assault on Bragg's strong position on Lookout Mountain, and won, through the gallantry of the soldiers in Hooker's, Thomas's and Sherman's divisions, a very brilliant victory. The charge of Hooker's men up the face of Lookout Mountain through the fog and mist, was one of the great exploits of the war.

The men called it "the battle above the clouds."

Grant Made Lieutenant-General 

Not long after this battle, Grant was called to Washington, made Lieutenant-General, and put in command of all the Union armies. President Lincoln, who had almost despaired of finding a man able to face Lee, and capable of defeating him, believed he had found the man at last. 

Grant's plan of campaign was simple, but well thought out.

He was to take command in Virginia, and hold Lee "in a vise," as he expressed it, while the armies of the West, under General W. T. Sherman, his able lieutenant both at 

Vicksburg and Chattanooga, moved eastward through

Georgia. This would cut what remained of the Confederacy in two, and then permit Sherman to march northward and take Lee and Richmond in the rear. 

The armies of the Union were now much larger and better equipped than those of the South, for the superior resources of the North in men, money, and materials were beginning to tell. Grant felt that the Confederacy could not long resist the kind of hammering he planned. 

Grant and Lee Fight in Virginia 

This great double campaign began in May, 1864. Grant led his army toward Richmond, faced at every step of the way by Lee. There was a succession of terrible battles. among the forests and swamps of tidewater Virginia the 

Wilderness (May 5, 6), Spottsylvania (May 8-18), and Cold 

Harbor (June 3). The losses on both sides were severe, especially among the Union troops who were attacking. 

Grant was not discouraged; he did not fall back when Lee checked him at the Wilderness, but sent back word to President Lincoln: "I propose to fight it out on this line if it takes all summer."

It did take all summer and more; for though Grant by virtue of his greater numbers obliged Lee to fall back until

he had reached the outer defenses of Richmond, he could not squarely defeat him, and the fortifications round Richmond were too strong to be taken by direct assault. So 

Grant moved the greater part of his army south of the James River, and began what amounted to a siege of the 

Confederate capital. 

Sherman Takes Atlanta and Marches to the Sea 

Meanwhile Sherman, farther south, was meeting with complete success in his part of the campaign. In a series of da

TREAT masterly marches and maneuvers, he forced back the Confederate army under J. E. Johnston until he threatened 

Atlanta, the capital of Georgia, and a very important place from a military point of view. There at last some severe fighting occurred, and Sherman was victorious. Atlanta fell on September 2, 1864.

Having scattered the army opposed to him, Sherman next cut loose from all his communications and led his army straight across Georgia to Savannah, living "off the country" on the way. This famous "march to the sea" was an extraordinary piece of strategy. Not only did it cut the 

Confederacy in two, from north to south, leaving nothing but the two Carolinas and southern Virginia in open resist-

ance, but it devastated a wide stretch of territory, and destroyed an immense quantity of crops and other supplies that might otherwise have gone to the support of Lee and his army.

As the Union army swept along, it left behind it a swath sixty miles wide and three hundred miles long in which all railroads and telegraph lines were demolished, hundreds of houses and barns burned, and thousands of farms stripped of all their food supplies and live stock. It was a terrible experience for one of the most fertile sections of the South. 

The memory of it lives there even to this day; but Sherman declared that it was a war measure that was justified because it greatly shortened the agony of the conflict now drawing to its end. 

Farragut at Mobile Admiral 

While Sherman was approaching Atlanta, news came of the final success of Union arms in the Southwest. 

Farragut, with a strong fleet, entered the harbor of Mobile, the last stronghold still held by the Confederacy on the waters of the Gulf of Mexico. He reduced the ships, forts, and shore batteries that defended the port, and received its surrender on August 5. 

In November occurred the Presidential election. President Lincoln had been renominated, and General McClellan was the Democratic candidate. With Lincoln as candidate for Vice-President on the ticket was Andrew Johnson, military governor of Tennessee, who represented excellently the thousands of people among the mountains of Virginia, Kentucky, North Carolina, and Tennessee who had been loyal to the Union throughout the war.

These people were mostly poor and extremely independent. They owned no slaves, and had no interest in slavery. When the crisis came, they followed the Union

ADMIRAL DAVID G. FARRAGUTI 

rather than the slave-holding aristocracy. In Virginia they were so numerous that the western part of that State split off, and was admitted to the Union in 1863 as the independent free State of West Virginia. 

Lincoln is Reëlected

During the campaign Mr. Lincoln felt grave doubts of his reëlection. The North had been obliged to resort to conscription, or draft, since the number of volunteers was no longer sufficient to fill the ranks of the armies; and conscription was not popular. In New York City it was re- 

I David Glasgow Farragut was born in Tennessee in 1801. He was a midshipman in the navy at nine years of age, and at his death in 1870 had seen sixty years of active service in the navy. He took part in the War of 

1812 and the Mexican War. Though a Southerner by birth, he remained loyal to the Union in 1861 and was the most distinguished of the naval commanders in the Civil War.

sisted by armed mobs, and soldiers had to be called in to suppress these "draft riots." 

Then, too, the terrible losses sustained in Grant's battles against Lee had discouraged many people, who feared that a complete victory in the war was impossible. But the success of Sherman's campaign caused a change in feeling, and in November Lincoln was triumphantly reëlected. The brief address which he delivered on the occasion of his second inauguration is one of the most famous of Lincoln's speeches. It was an eloquent and touching plea for reconciliation and reunion between the States of the North and the South.

The End of the War Approaches 

After Lincoln's reëlection the end of the war came quickly. Sherman turned north through the Carolinas, driving Johnston's army before him and cutting off the hope of any reënforcements or supplies for Lee from that quarter. 

General Sheridan, at the head of a strong cavalry force, swept up and down the Shenandoah Valley, destroying everything of value that might have gone to Lee's support. 

Penned up within the lines of defense around Richmond by Grant's army, Lee saw his stock of food and ammunition dwindle every day without hope of replenishment. Many of his men were sick and many others, realizing that defeat was inevitable, deserted. 

On April 1, 1865, Grant assaulted Lee's lines at Petersburg, and carried them. The end was at hand. Lee told 

President Davis that Richmond could no longer be held.

Davis and his Cabinet escaped from the city and fled southward. Lee drew off his army to the westward, but 

Grant and Sheridan were in instant pursuit. On April 3, United States troops entered Richmond.

Lee Surrenders 

Lee, in his retreat, got no farther than Appomattox Court House, seventy-five miles from Richmond, when he found himself again surrounded. His shattered army, half-starved, half-clothed, was in no condition to fight. On April 9, he surrendered with all his men to General Grant. 

The Union commander granted most generous terms. 

All Confederate soldiers and officers were permitted to leave at once for their homes, after giving their parole not to take up arms again against the Union. They were allowed to take their horses, too, when they owned any, "to help them with the spring planting." Grant also sent 

25,000 rations to the Confederate camp, and forbade his own men to engage in any noisy rejoicing over the surrender of Lee. In every way he showed kindly feeling and consideration for the conquered enemy, and General Lee and his men deeply appreciated his conduct. 

When Johnston surrendered to Sherman, a few days later, he and his soldiers received the same generous treatment. Never has a hard-fought war come to a close with more friendly feeling among those who actually fought one another in the field. 

Lincoln is Assassinated

The news of Lee's surrender, which meant, of course, the end of the war, was received with an outburst of joy in the North, and with a definite sense of relief in the South, for the burden of the long conflict had weighed heavily on both sides. 

The rejoicing was suddenly turned to grief when, on 

April 14th, President Lincoln was assassinated in Washington. The murderer was J. Wilkes Booth, an actor by profession, and an ardent Southern sympathizer. He shot the 

President from behind, as he sat in a box at Ford's Theater. 

The wound was mortal, but Mr. Lincoln lived on, uncon-

scious, until the next morning. Booth made his escape from the theater by leaping to the stage and getting out at the rear door; but he was pursued, hunted down, and shot dead while resisting capture.

Character of Lincoln 

The death of Lincoln plunged the country into mourning. 

He had had much criticism to face; but as the war wore on, THE LINCOLN MEMORIAL AT WASHINGTON 

The thirty-six columns represent the States in the Union at the time of Lincoln's death. In the cornice are wreaths bearing the names of the present States, fortyeight in number. There is a colossal statue of Lincoln by Daniel C. French within the building. the people came to have deep confidence in his honesty and his wisdom, and great affection for the simple nobility of his character. Sprung from the humblest ranks himself, the common people always thought of him as one of themselves; while the leaders of thought and action had come to recognize that the President was the strongest, sanest, and wisest among them.

His death was a special blow to the South; for he was an advocate of the kindest and most generous treatment of the seceded States. The story of the years after the war would have been a far happier one for the South if Lincoln, instead of the Republican leaders in Congress, had directed the processes of Reconstruction.

The Results of the War 

The Civil War has sometimes been called the Second American Revolution. It was indeed as revolutionary in its effects on the country as the war of 1776. It abolished slavery, for while it was in progress the Thirteenth Amendment to the Constitution prohibiting slavery everywhere in the United States was proposed, and by December, 1865, it had been ratified.

It destroyed the old and rich social system of the South which was founded on slavery, and caused a new and widely different South to arise from the ruins of the old. 

The war and the abolition of slavery led in the end to a complete change in the character and political balance of our civilization. Before the Civil War the Union was founded on agriculture, and the political power was in the hands of the people who lived on the soil. A change began to take place even while the fighting was still going on, and it has grown more and more apparent as the years pass. The 

United States has become a great industrial republic. The cities and large towns control legislation and set the pace for our modern life. It is the business man and manufacturer rather than the farmer and planter who are powerful politically. 

Finally, the Civil War decided once for all the nature of the Constitution. The Union is not a compact between sovereign States, but a Nation, one and indivisible, as Marshall and Webster and Lincoln declared it to be.

Whatever the framers of the Constitution thought about it, that interpretation was settled as the final one on the battlefields of the Civil War.

UNIT XIII  

HE civil war between the Northern and Southern TH States came to an end with the fall of Richmond, the flight of the Confederate Government, and the surrender of General Lee and his army to General Grant at Appomattox in the early days of April, 1861. 

Problems of Reconstruction

The war settled two great questions that had long agitated the country. It caused the abolition of Negro slavery everywhere in the United States; and it put an end to the doctrine that the Constitution was a mere compact between the States, from which any State might legally and peaceably withdraw if it chose to do so. 

At the same time the war left some very difficult problems to be met. They were the problems of the " “reconstruction" of the Southern States. How should a proper form of government be restored to them? On what terms should they take their place again in the Union against which they had fought for four years? What should be done about the millions of Negroes, no longer slaves, but free men and future citizens of the United States? In this unit we shall see what answers were found to those questions. 

Unhappy Conditions in the South

The conditions in the seceding States were most unhappy at the close of the war. Armies had marched back and forth over all of them from the Mississippi to the Atlantic Ocean, and great battles had been fought in all of them except Florida and Texas. There had been much destruction of

299 property, especially in Virginia, Georgia, and the Carolinas. 

Houses had been burned, railroads torn up, farms and plantations despoiled, livestock seized for army use, the cotton industry, the mainstay of Southern prosperity, destroyed. 

These are the inevitable results of a long and hotly contested war, in the area where the actual fighting occurs. 

Besides this destruction of property, the Southern people faced a great money loss in the freeing of the slaves. They had represented a value to their owners of two or three billion dollars before the war. That amount of capital wealth was at one stroke wiped out, and with no compensation to the slaveholders.

The Confederate currency was, of course, valueless when the Government at Richmond fell, and the South had no money with which to do business or to carry on while the ravages of war were being repaired. There was little wealth left except the bare land; and the slaves, on whom the 

Southerners had depended for the cultivation of the land, were dispersed, and often unwilling to work for their old masters even for wages.

Break-up of the Social System The entire social system of the South was overthrown by the abolition of slavery, and it was bound to be a long time before a new one could be established to meet the strange new conditions. Thousands of families, once rich, were reduced to poverty and sometimes to destitution. At the same time the Negroes, free at last, but ignorant and unused to responsibility, without any land of their own, or any experience in earning their own living, were confused and helpless. They did not know how to use their new-found liberty. Some of them even thought it meant that they would somehow be fed and clothed without having to do any work at all.

How was the South to be Treated?

Nothing could be done to restore life in the South to order and prosperity until it was decided on what terms the seceded States were to reënter the Union, and by what means local government should be reëstablished. Were the 

Southern States still to be regarded as members of the Federal Union, though so lately in revolt against it; or had they forfeited all their rights as States, which they could regain only on terms laid down by the victorious North? 

How were the Southern white people to be treated? Were they still citizens of the United States, entitled to vote and to form their own State Governments, or had they lost their civil rights until Congress pleased to restore them?

What should be done about the freedmen; how were they to be helped to learn how to support themselves? Were they to be regarded as full citizens with the same right to vote that the white people had, or were they to undergo a period of preparation and education first?

It is easy to see that these were all very hard questions to answer. There was need for great wisdom and generosity and mutual understanding if they were to be answered so as to restore the South quickly, peaceably, and happily to its old place in the Union. Unfortunately at the very beginning the country lost its wisest and strongest leader. 

Within a week of Lee's surrender, President Lincoln was assassinated by a hot-headed, half-insane Southern sympathizer, J. Wilkes Booth. 

President Andrew Johnson 

The Vice-President, Andrew Johnson,' at once succeeded

I Andrew Johnson was born in North Carolina in 1808. His parents were poor, and he received no schooling in his boyhood. After his marriage, with the help of his wife, he acquired a fair education. He removed to 

Tennessee, where he went into politics as a leader of the farmers and

to the presidency. He was by no means so well qualified as Lincoln for the difficult task of guiding the country through the period of reconstruction. He was a self-educated, self-made man from the mountain country of the 

THE CAPITOL, SEEN FROM AN AIRPLANE 

The Library of Congress is shown at the top of the picture South, which had been loyal to the Union throughout the war. He had plenty of native ability and strong patriotic feeling. 

But instead of having Lincoln's wonderful patience and tolerance, Johnson was hasty and tactless of speech. Lincoln was a remarkable manager of men. He understood human nature thoroughly and knew how to conciliate oplaboring men of the mountain country, where few slaves were owned. 

He was Mayor of Greenville, a member of the State Legislature, and in 1842 was elected to Congress. Later he was elected Governor of the 

State and (in 1857) a United States Senator. President Lincoln appointed him Military Governor of Tennessee in 1862, and two years later he was elected Vice-President, succeeding to the Presidency on Lincoln's assassination. He was again chosen Senator from Tennessee in 1875, but died a few months later.

ponents and to win the confidence and the support of the people. Johnson quarreled readily, and made enemies more easily than friends. He had not the all-important gift of persuading and guiding public opinion. Finally, although thoroughly loyal, he was after all a Southerner and so was never completely trusted by the Northern leaders. Almost from the moment that he became President he was at odds with Congress. 

Views of Lincoln on Reconstruction

Even if Lincoln had lived, there would have been disagreement between him and Congress. He had made it clear before his death that he regarded the seceded States as still members of the Union. In his view, they could not legally withdraw from the Union, and had never actually done so. 

He was ready to let any State set up its own government and send representatives and senators to Washington, just as soon as one tenth of the voters in that State had taken the oath of loyalty. As commander-in-chief of the army he granted pardon to any citizen who would take that oath, excepting only the officers of the Confederate Government, and men who had joined the seceding States while holding civil or military posts under the United States Government. 

He seems not to have thought it wise to give the right of suffrage at once to all the Negro freedmen. 

The Republican leaders in Congress or at least some of the strongest of them - did not at all agree with this program. Led in the Senate by Charles Sumner of Massachusetts, and in the House by Thaddeus Stevens of Pennsylvania, they took the ground that the Southern States had lost all their rights as States by seceding from the Union. 

They were to be regarded as "conquered territory," and could only be readmitted by submitting to terms much more severe than those the President suggested. The Republican

leaders also thought it would be necessary very soon to give the vote to all the Negroes in order to protect them in their new position as free men.

The President's Plan 

As nearly as we can tell, President Johnson's views were much like those Lincoln would have held if he had lived; but he lacked Lincoln's power with the people of the North and 

Lincoln's gift of quiet but effective leadership.

During the summer, while Congress was not in session, Johnson went ahead with his own plans for reconstruction. 

He granted amnesty to Southerners who would take the oath of loyalty, making the same exceptions that Lincoln had made. He appointed provisional governors of several 

States. These governors called conventions that repealed the old ordinances of secession, agreed to ratify the Constitutional Amendment abolishing slavery, and voted that the Confederate war debts should not be paid. The conventions also provided for the election of legislatures to take over the control of the State Governments and choose senators for 

Congress. Tennessee, Virginia, Arkansas, and Louisiana all took these steps before Congress met in December; according to the President's view they were once more members of the Union exactly as before the war.

The Plan of Congress 

As soon as Congress met, it showed that it did not mean to agree with the President. Sumner and Stevens had a reconstruction plan of their own. Under their leadership, Congress refused to admit the senators and representatives elected by the four States that had accepted President Johnson's terms, and voted that Congress and not the President must control the reorganization of the South. 

The congressional plan of reconstruction was outlined in

five important acts. First it passed the Civil Rights Bill. 

This gave the Negroes the right to use the courts for the recovery of debts owed to them or of damages for wrongs done to them. It also provided for military protection to the 

Negroes in case any State refused to assure them all their rights as citizens. 

UNITED STATES SENATE CHAMBER 

The President vetoed this bill, on the ground that all the States had not been represented in Congress when it was passed; but it was made a law over his veto by a two-thirds vote.

The Congressional Plan Wins 

Congress also passed a bill creating a Federal "Freedman's Bureau," to assist the former slaves in maintaining their rights and in getting a proper start in life under the new and difficult conditions they had to face. The Presi-

dent vetoed that bill, too, because he found too much military control of the South in its provisions. At first the 

Senate upheld his veto; but when he vetoed a second bill containing many of the same provisions, Congress passed that over his veto.

In June, 1866, Congress voted to submit a Fourteenth 

Amendment to the Constitution. This amendment gave the Negro every right of citizenship that the white man possessed except that of the suffrage; but it provided for reducing the representation in Congress of any State that did not permit the Negro to vote.. It also provided that no man who had once sworn allegiance to the United States and had then taken part in secession should be permitted to hold any office until his disability had been removed by act of Congress.

Next Congress passed the Reconstruction Bill. This bill put all the Southern States under military governors with troops under their command. It also provided that if any 

State should ratify the Fourteenth Amendment, and adopt a constitution that gave the Negroes the right to vote, it might be readmitted to the Union. The President vetoed this bill, also, but it was immediately passed over his veto. 

Under this arrangement all the seceded States except 

Virginia, Mississippi, and Texas were “reconstructed” and took their places again among their sister States. 

Finally, in 1869, a Fifteenth Amendment was proposed and adopted, making the Negro a voter everywhere in the United States. So far as federal law went, he became the full political equal of the white man.

The Quarrels of President and Congress 

By the enactment of these laws, one after another, generally in the face of President Johnson's disapproval, Congress had successfully maintained its claim to be the only

power that could determine the ways and means of reconstruction. It was able to do this because the Republicans composed more than two thirds of both Senate and House, and because Sumner and Stevens and the other "radical" leaders had been able to unite the Republican Party in open opposition to the President. 

Passion ran high during these years. President Johnson, convinced that Congress was acting unfairly and unwisely, and sometimes in defiance of the Constitution, publicly attacked the motives and the conduct of the Republican leaders. On the floor of Congress, these leaders replied with equal vehemence. They believed, and they persuaded the 

Republicans of the North to believe, that the President, as a Southern man who had been a Democrat before the war, was trying to help the Southern leaders, whom they still called "rebels," back into power, at the expense of the 

Negroes whom the war had set free.

Stevens, who was a man of immense force and determination, with a very sharp and bitter tongue, was most conspicuous in the long quarrel, and his continual abuse of the President in the end aroused very strong feeling against 

Johnson in the North. 

Impeachment of the President

Not satisfied with having imposed its will on the President in the matter of reconstruction, Congress went further and deliberately encroached on his proper powers as President.

When he removed from office some officials who had been openly or secretly hostile to him, Congress passed the Tenure-of-Office Bill, which forbade the President to remove any civil officer without the consent of the Senate. 

Mr. Johnson denied the power of Congress to limit his constitutional authority in this way, and defied the new law by dismissing his Secretary of War, Edwin M. Stanton, who

was in open sympathy with Sumner and Stevens. There was a long and angry quarrel over this act. It ended by the impeachment of the President by vote of the House of Representatives. 

UNITED STATES HALL OF REPRESENTATIVES 

The President, on the charges of misconduct in office brought by the House, was tried, according to the Constitution, in the Senate, Chief Justice Chase presiding. If two thirds of the Senate had voted to sustain these charges, Mr. 

Johnson would have been removed from office, and Senator Wade, President of the Senate, would have succeeded him. 

But seven Republican Senators would not follow the rest of their party to that extent. They honestly believed that 

Congress had gone too far, and that the President was this time in the right. They voted for his acquittal, and the impeachment failed by one vote. A few weeks later, Thad-

deus Stevens, already a very old man, and Mr. Johnson's most implacable enemy, died.

General Grant Elected President 

With the failure of the impeachment, the violent phase of the quarrel between the President and Congress came to an end. His term of office was nearly completed, and since he had not any warm personal support even among the Democrats, he was not a candidate for President in the election of 

1868. The Republicans nominated General Grant, the military hero of the Civil War, and he was elected by a great majority over Governor Seymour of New York, the Democratic candidate.

The Carpet-Baggers 

Meanwhile, how did the reconstruction plans of Congress work out in the South? Not well at all, unfortunately.

The Southern white people, who felt that they had been unjustly treated, did not try to coöperate in establishing orderly government. In fact, many of them were prevented from doing so, if they would, for they were still disfranchised as "rebels." On the other hand, the vote had been given to all Negroes, though most of them were still too ignorant and unused to self-government to use their new power intelligently. 

Under these conditions a number of Northern men who were politically ambitious moved into the South, took up their residence there, and by establishing their influence over the Negroes got themselves elected to office. The 

Southerners called these men carpet-baggers," because they were said to have nothing but the clothes they brought with them in the traveling bags made of carpeting which every one used in those days. The State Governments which these men set up, with the aid of Negro votes, were called "carpet-bag governments."

Disorder and Corruption

A few of the carpet-baggers were honest men who tried to act fairly, but most of them were greedy and unscrupulous adventurers. The governments they controlled were corrupt and extravagant. The property-owners of the South which meant the white people were heavily taxed, while the money so secured was often stolen or wasted. 

There was much disorder and crime, too, for the strangers, who were governing the States for their own profit, took no interest in restraining the vicious elements of the population. For several years the respectable part of the South was under a kind of "reign of terror," quite at the mercy of the corrupt, the idle, and the unscrupulous, both white and black. Unfortunately, as part of the congressional plan, United States troops were still stationed in parts of the South; and they could usually be called on by the carpetbag governors to prevent any resistance to their authority. 

Slowly, however, the problem worked itself out. The white people of the South began to assert themselves. they took the required oath of loyalty, they got back the right to vote, and as they were in the majority in most of the States, they were able gradually to get control of the government and drive the carpet-baggers out of office. In 

States where they could not do this, they formed secret or semi-secret organizations, like the famous Ku-Klux Klan. 

The Ku-Klux Klan 

As The Klan, which had its origin in Tennessee, was at first a kind of "vigilance committee." Its members rode about at night, hooded and wearing sheets of white cotton cloth. 

They punished people who were known to be vicious or criminal, by whipping them or giving them coats of tar and feathers. Then the Klan began to terrorize the superstitious 

Negroes so that they dared not vote at the elections. 

¹See Unit IX, page 224.

In the end it became itself so lawless and violent that the intelligent and respectable people of the South helped to break it up; but it did its part in restoring the control of the elections to the white voters.

The End of Reconstruction 

All through Grant's two administrations — for he was reelected President in 1872 - these restless and disorderly conditions prevailed in the South, and United States troops remained there to prevent any oppression of the Negro freedmen by the white population. 

As the years passed, and the passions that had been stirred by the war began to die down, the people of the North came to understand that the Nation could not become united and peaceful while the federal power was being used to prevent the most intelligent and enterprising Southern people from managing the affairs of their States. Matters came to a crisis in 1876, when a new President was elected.

The Disputed Election of 1876 The Republicans nominated Governor Rutherford B. 

Hayes of Ohio; the Democrats nominated Governor Samuel 

J. Tilden of New York. The election was so close that its result depended on the votes of three Southern States, South

Carolina, Florida, and Louisiana, where federal officers still remained in control of the elections. 

There were two sets of returns; according to the local election officials the Democrats had carried these States and Tilden was therefore elected. According to the report of the federal officials the Republicans had won and Hayes was elected.

The dispute was carried to Congress for settlement; while feeling ran so high in the country that many people feared

that there would be an armed revolt whichever way it was decided. Congress debated the difficult situation for two months, and then voted to leave the decision to an Electoral Commission consisting of five senators, five representatives, and five justices of the Supreme Court. 

SUPREME COURT CHAMBER 

The commission met, heard the evidence in favor of both sets of returns, and then voted, 8 to 7, that the electoral votes of all three States should be counted for Governor Hayes. He was accordingly chosen President by a single electoral vote 185 to 184. 

Federal Troops Withdrawn

The Democrats were very angry at this decision. They insisted that the election had been "stolen," and some of the hot-headed ones called for open resistance to the inauguration of Governor Hayes. The leaders were wiser, however. They would not listen to any plans of violence,

and when the 4th of March came, Governor Hayes was quietly inaugurated as President.

One of his very first acts was to order the withdrawal of all federal troops from the South. That meant that the white voters resumed control of the elections and of the local governments in every Southern State. 

The Solid South

One result of the reconstruction policy of the Republican leaders was that these white voters of the South almost all became Democrats. For many years the South steadily voted Democratic, and even today it is very unusual for the Republicans to elect their candidates in any of the States of the old Confederacy. That is what we mean when we speak of the "Solid South." It is pretty solidly Democratic. 

The Southern legislatures have also found ways to prevent most of the Negroes from voting. They did not violate the 

Constitution by disfranchising them on account of their color, but they set up qualifications of education and property-owning which very few of the freedmen could reach. The Negroes of the South have made remarkable progress since the war. A great many of them are now prosperous and well educated. Nowhere is their right to hold property or to use the courts in defense of their rights denied; but in the South they do not yet vote in large numbers, and they have none of the political power that the radical Republicans thought they had given them after the war. 

Progress of the Negroes

Even in the South, however, the Negroes have in large numbers become owners of land and successful farmers; they engage in various kinds of business and have established banks in some of the larger towns. 

Many of them have

come to the North in search of still wider opportunities in business and industry, and have done well. Negro doctors, lawyers, teachers, and clergymen are numerous, and Negroes have made their mark in art, music, and literature. 

It is easy to see the signs of Negro influence on much modern art and music. Perhaps the best-known leaders of their race have been the educators men like Booker T. Washington, Major Robert Moton, and W. E. Burghardt du Bois.

These men, by bringing education and culture to the descendants of those who were once slaves, have helped more than any others to raise the black man toward the level of his former masters.

Outline Summary 

1. The struggle between President Johnson and Congress over reconstruction (a) Conditions in the South in 1865 (b) Lincoln's views on reconstruction, substantially adopted by Johnson (c) Views of Stevens, Sumner, and the Republican majority in Congress (d) The President vetoes 

unsuccessfully the reconstruction bills passed by Congress (e) The Tenure-of-Office Bill and the impeachment of Johnson (f) Election of General Grant; the congressional plan of reconstruction wins 2. Results of the congressional plan of reconstruction (a) Federal soldiers occupy the South (b) Government of the South by "carpet-baggers," supported by the enfranchised Negroes (c) Widespread corruption in the public service (d) White citizens of the South combine to regain control of their States; the Ku-Klux Klan (e) The disputed election of 1876 (f) Public opinion supports President Hayes in withdrawing troops from the South; the white voters resume control of the government of their States
