UNIT XIV DEVELOPMENT OF THE NATION'S NATURAL WEALTH 1865-1900 AGRICULTURE STOCK-RAISING LUMBERING RAILWAYS MINING 

HE Civil War is a true dividing line in the history of politics, in trade, or in social life was the same after the war. The country began to develop in new ways and along lines very different from those of the past. There were great changes even in so long-established and well understood industries as farming and the raising of livestock. We shall read in this unit about the most remarkable of these changes. 

The Homestead Act 

The Western country was well filled with farms before 

1860. Most of the States beyond the Allegheny Mountains had been carved out of public land, owned by the United States. The Government had been generous in distributing this land to the people. It had been sold to settlers for not more than $2.50 an acre, and much of it at half that price. 

Nevertheless, there had been some grumbling among the settlers at having to pay anything at all for the land. They thought that any one who endured the labor and hardship of clearing and cultivating a wilderness farm ought to have the land for his pains.

In 1862, while the war was still going on, Congress passed a Homestead Act which provided that any one who took up a quarter-section of the public land (one hundred and sixty acres), lived on it and farmed it for five years, should be

given a deed to it free of charge. Of course this led to a new flood of immigration, mainly to the States beyond the Mississippi; for only in those States was any public land left. 

There were many people of old American stock among those who took up land under the Homestead Act; but there were also a great number of immigrants from Europe, especially Germans and people from the Scandinavian countries.

The Grain Farms of the West 

The land in these Western States was prairie land, generally flat and fertile. There were no forests to be cleared 

PRIMITIVE PLOUGHING 

off, and almost every acre was fit for farming if there were rain enough. It was as good land as any in the world for growing wheat and corn and those became the chief crops of the region.

This accounts for its rapid settlement. 

The farms were generally large one hundred and sixty acres, at least and some families were able to get two or three quartersections, if there were grown-up children who could take up claims as well as their father. In this way millions of acres of wild land were brought under cultivation in the years following the war. The great States of Minnesota, Kansas, Nebraska, Iowa, and the Dakotas became an enormous farming country. Corn and wheat were produced in such quantities and at so low a cost that Eastern farmers

began to find it no longer profitable to raise such crops except for their own use. The "abandoned farms" of New 

England and Northern New York generally date from these years, when their owners left them to find more fertile land in the West. 

Machinery on the Farms

The These great farms could hardly have been successfully cultivated under the old system of agriculture; but in the years just before and just after the war a number of machines were invented which made it possible for one man to do work that had formerly required half a dozen men. mechanical reaper of Cyrus 

McCormick was the first of these machines. That was followed by the improvement of the threshing machine, by the harvester, invented by the brothers 

Marsh, by the "twine binder" of J. F. Appleby, and by many other useful if less important improvements in machinery. 

These inventions came to be combined in one great machine, drawn first by ten or more horses, and in later 

Courtesy Cleveland Tractor Co.

PLOUGHING WITH A TRACTOR 

years since 1900-by gasoline tractors. These harvesting machines reap the grain, deliver it to a binding table, where the binder ties it into sheaves or bundles of a proper size, and then drops them to be gathered up and stacked in readiness for threshing. There are even "combines," in which a threshing machine is attached to the harvester, so that the

grain is threshed at the same time it is cut. The invention of the self-binder alone — the last of the important improvements in harvesting machinery made it possible for a farmer to raise and handle twice as much wheat as he could before, with the same amount of labor. 

Under these circumstances wheat-growing became a great industry. Wheat was no longer grown by the farmer, only as part of his crop, and chiefly for his own use. The grain farms of the West raised nothing but wheat, which was sold for cash; and the wheat farmer bought whatever else he needed food, clothing, and so forth, with the money he got for his wheat.

Wheat also became one of our principal exports. We could raise a great deal more than we could possibly use at home, and since the land had cost the farmer nothing but his labor, and was extremely fertile besides, we could offer our wheat in foreign markets at a very reasonable price. 

In 1860 we sold wheat and flour worth $19,000,000 abroad. 

By 1900 we were exporting $140,000,000 worth every year, though we had to meet the competition of Russia, Canada, Australia, and Argentina. 

Farming in the South

Agriculture in the South passed through no such sudden expansion. For some years, indeed, it lagged behind what it had been before the war. The great cotton and tobacco plantations were largely broken up, because their owners could no longer afford to operate them without slave labor. The smaller farms that took their place could not produce so much or so well as the plantations had done; many of them were tilled by tenant farmers, black or white, who had neither the capital nor the intelligence to get the most out of their land.

The disorders and conflicts of the reconstruction period

also delayed the revival of Southern agriculture; but it came in time. It was accompanied by a general use of the new farm machinery, and by the introduction of new crops.

Cotton and tobacco are no longer the sole mainstays of the Southern farmers. They have found that money can be made quite as surely from peanuts, from semi-tropical fruits, and from early vegetables for the Northern markets. 

The old staple crops have not been abandoned, however. Corn, cotton, tobacco, rice, and sugarcane are still grown, and cotton continues to be the chief money crop of the South. Improvements in farming methods and in farm machinery, and the opening of new lands in the great States of Texas and Oklahoma have made it possible to grow nearly twice as much cotton as in the days when the Southern statesmen were proudly proclaiming: "Cotton is King." 

Copyright, Underwood & Underwood

A REAPING AND THRESHING COMBINE" 

Raising Cattle on the Ranges 66 The raising of cattle for the market was another industry that made a remarkable growth in the years that followed the war. 

West of the wheat country, stretching out toward the Rocky Mountains, was a region where the rainfall was too uncertain to make farming profitable. 

This land was covered with the tall buffalo grass, however, which made excellent grazing for cattle, and since no one

had claimed it for agriculture, the grass was free to the cattleman.

On this vast "open range" great ranches sprang up, with thousands upon thousands of cattle belonging to each ranch. 

These cattle fed together on the ranges, but they had first been "branded" with the owner's mark, and every summer there was a "round-up" where the steers were collected and separated into the several herds.

This work, as well as the general care of the cattle on the range, and the calves at the home ranch, fell to the "cowboys" as they were popularly called. 

The "Cowboys"

These picturesque cowboys lived a rough, out-of-door life, almost always on horseback, watching the herds, protecting the animals against Indians, cattle thieves, or beasts of prey, attending to the branding or rounding-up of their special charges, and driving the herd at last to the often distant railway by which the cattle were to be shipped to market.

It was often the custom to keep cattle on the more southern plains of Texas during the winter, and then to drive them north in the spring, grazing as they went, until they came to the corn country of Kansas or Nebraska, where for a few weeks before being marketed, they would be fattened on corn. Great "cattle-towns" grew up at Dodge City, Kansas, and Ogallala, Nebraska, where these Texas cattle were sold to the ranchers who fattened them. 

Taking the Cattle to Market 

Once ready for market, the cattle were loaded on long trains and taken to the stockyards at Chicago, or Omaha, or 

Kansas City, or St. Joseph. There they were slaughtered and made into beef; though sometimes they were taken all

the way to the seacoast and put into ships "on the hoof" to be carried alive to the markets of London or Liverpool. 

The business of "meat packing," as the preparation of cattle and sheep and hogs for sale as meat is called, became a very important one. Before the war its center had been at 

Cincinnati; but Chicago and Kansas City soon took the lead. The stockyards in these cities are vast in extent, and millions of animals are slaughtered every year. 

The "open ranges" of the West did not outlast the nineteenth century. The illegal enclosure of grazing land by some of the cattle-raisers, the demand of actual settlers for much of the range land, and the growing expense of doing business in the haphazard way of the rancher brought the system to an end. Cattle are now raised on enclosed ranches properly owned and fenced, and the cowboy, whose romantic figure has been made popular by the writings of Roosevelt and Wister and the pictures of Frederic Remington, no longer “rides the range." He is, however, one of the most picturesque types that the American frontier, ever moving westward, has produced.

Indian Wars in the West 

The extension of cattle-raising into this Western country, caused the extinction of the buffaloes that used to roam over it. The hardship thus brought upon the Indian tribes, who regarded this vast prairie as their hunting ground, led to a succession of conflicts between the Indians and the cattlemen. The Government was then obliged to restore order, and as the Indians were by this time on the war-path, these conflicts led to more than one Indian war of some importance. 

In the North it was the Sioux who were most troublesome; in the South, the Apaches. The Government wished the Indians to stay on certain reservations which it had set apart for them. The Indians often declined to do so. The

most famous of these wars was that with the Sioux in 1876, when the Indians, led by their chiefs, Rain-in-the-Face, Crazy Horse, and Sitting Bull, wiped out a force of United

States cavalry under General Custer at the battle of the Little Big Horn River. Of course the Indians were conquered in the end. Some of them fled to Canada; the rest went back peaceably to their reservation. 

The Mining Industry Grows 

It was during these post-war years also that people learned how vast was the mineral wealth of the country. 

Iron ore and coal had long ago been found and mined in

Pennsylvania and elsewhere, and gold had been discovered in California; but now almost every year brought news of great beds of valuable minerals. Gold was found in 

Colorado and later in Nevada. Immense deposits of silver were uncovered in the great Comstock Lode in Nevada, and smaller quantities in many other mountain States. Iron was found in northern Minnesota - free iron in such abundance that it could actually be scooped up out of the mine in steam shovels and sent to the furnaces without the trouble of smelting it. Iron ore was also discovered in great deposits among the hills of northern Alabama. Rich copper mines were opened in northern Michigan, in Utah, and in Arizona. 

The discovery and use of all these valuable metals added tremendously to the wealth of the Nation. Mining towns sprang up almost overnight where these discoveries were made. Many of them, particularly in the districts where gold and silver were found, have already had their day and have been abandoned, as the deposits of metal became exhausted. They were lively, often lawless places, filled with adventurers and worse, for the precious metals always seem to attract a shifty and greedy sort of population to the search for them. In some of Bret Harte's stories you can

read about the life that went on in these mining camps in California and Nevada, where gambling and drinking were the chief amusements of the gold-seeker.

The iron and copper industries — particularly the former - have caused the growth of many large and prosperous cities; not at the mines, however, but at the points where the iron is smelted, refined, and made into steel. Pittsburgh, Cleveland, Birmingham, and many smaller cities have grown great because of the iron industry. 

The Forests are Leveled for Lumber

Still another industry that developed widely during these years was that of lumbering. Hitherto, timber sufficient for the needs of the country had been cut by individual farmers or by men who did business on a small scale among the forest growth of the Eastern States. Now the demand for lumber for houses, for furniture, for boxes in which to ship the increasing manufactures of the Nation suddenly began to mount. Great lumber companies were formed to operate in the pine or hardwood forests of Maine, Michigan, northern New York, and Wisconsin. By and by came the discovery that wood pulp could be made into a serviceable and cheap paper and into "pulp board" for making boxes and packing cases.

The forest area of the country shrank rapidly as these companies cut their way through it. Americans have always been wasteful of timber - largely because there seemed to be an inexhaustible supply of it when the country was first settled. An enormous quantity of very fine timber was cut down and burned up as the farms of the East and Middle West were cleared. There was nothing else to do with it, for there was then more than any one could use. 

The same wasteful methods have been followed by most lumber companies, however. It has been the practice to

clear everything, full-grown trees and saplings, off the land, making no provision for reseeding or replanting. Often through pure carelessness fire has been permitted to run through the cut-over land, burning soil as well as brush, until all its fertility is destroyed. Great stretches of once valuable timber land have thus been changed into a barren desert.

Fortunately, lumbermen have grown wiser with the rapid disappearance of the timber, and nowadays they are often careful to protect and even replant the land they have cleared. Lumber camps are also cleaner, more decent places than they used to be. The life in a lumber camp, miles distant from anything like civilization, must always be rough and hard; but the modern woodchopper has a variety of good food instead of an unvarying diet of pork and beans and pie and tea; he has a fairly comfortable bunk in place of a shakedown of dry spruce boughs, and a well-built camp instead of the rough and drafty shanties his predecessors lived in back in the seventies and eighties of the last century. 

The chief sources of lumber today are the forests of the South which are still far from exhaustion and the splendid fir and spruce forests of the Pacific Coast. 

Growth of the Nation's Wealth

It is obvious that this rapid expansion of national wealth from agriculture, stock-raising, mining, and lumbering must have had its effect on business and social life all over the country.

It struck a blow at agriculture in the East, because food crops could be grown much more cheaply in the fertile free land in the West. At the same time it helped to build up manufacturing industries in the East, for the supply of power and of labor was more abundant there, and that section was nearer both to the center of population and to the

seaports from which manufactures for our foreign trade could be exported.

Of course the growth of all these industries stimulated railroad-building, for the many bulky products of the West had to be brought East, and all the machinery and manufactured goods the West needed had to be taken thither. 

Railways spread from one end of the country to the other, criss-crossing everywhere in the thickly settled States, and even reaching (in 1869) to the Pacific Coast. In 1860 there were only 30,000 miles of railway. In 1900 there were 

198,000 miles.

Commerce Expands 

The increase in national wealth gave a tremendous impetus to domestic business and to foreign commerce. The 

United States had always been an exporter of cotton and tobacco. Now it began to sell its grain, its meat, its copper, its machinery and its manufactured goods to the other countries of the world, and it got money enough from them to buy large amounts of foreign goods in exchange. 

Population grew rapidly. The news that there was free land to be had in the West attracted millions of immigrants from Europe; and as our manufacturing industries grew more rapidly than the natural increase of population, other millions came across the Atlantic to find work in our mills and foundries. Between 1870 and 1900 the population almost exactly doubled — from 38,558,371 to 75,994,575, and the national wealth increased at least three or four times. 

Politics during these thirty years grew to be more and more affected by business and financial interests. Politicians no longer talked of questions like that of slavery, or of State rights and federal power. The tariff, the coinage, and the commercial policies of the Government interested them. 

We shall have more to say about all these things in later

units; but from this brief summary you can get some idea of the great changes that the years from 1865 to 1900 brought into American life.

We have spoken above of the remarkable growth and expansion of our railway system, during the Civil War and after its close. Much of the building carried on during the war was done to help the Government carry on the fighting. New roads were built to make it easier to carry soldiers to the front or from one part of the country to another, and especially to hasten the movement of food, ammunition, and all kinds of supplies from the fields and factories of the North to the armies in the field. 

The Transcontinental Railways 

The greatest project of all was the Union Pacific Railroad, which was to link the distant States on the Pacific Coast with the rest of the country. When the war began, there was no railroad west of Omaha. Between that city and 

San Francisco stretched fourteen hundred miles of prairie, desert and wilderness, crossed from north to south by several ranges of mountains from 10,000 to 14,000 feet in height.

To build a railroad across such difficult country seemed like an almost hopeless undertaking, especially at a time when the country was involved in a great and costly war. 

But it was felt that it must be done in order to bring the Far 

West into touch with the rest of the Union.

In 1862 Congress incorporated the Union Pacific Railroad 

Company to build a railroad westward from Omaha. At the same time some wealthy and enterprising Californians, among them Leland Stanford and Collis P. Huntington, organized a company to build eastward from San Francisco. 

To both companies Congress voted subsidies — as high as $48,000 a mile in the mountain country to help them construct the roads. It also made to them considerable

grants of public land along the line of the rails, which the company could sell to prospective settlers. 

The work was pushed with energy; twenty thousand men were employed during the concluding year. In 1869 the lines approached each other from east and west; on May 10 they met at Promontory Point, near Ogden, Utah, and the final spike, made of gold, was driven in the presence of an enthusiastic crowd. 

THE MEETING OF THE UNION PACIFIC RAILS 

Other transcontinental lines, the Northern Pacific and the Atchison, Topeka and Santa Fé, were planned at about the same time, and construction was begun on both. They progressed more slowly, however, and all work on them was stopped by the financial panic of 1873. It was not until some ten years later that they reached the Pacific Coast. 

How Railway-Building Helped the Country 

The benefits that followed the construction of these roads are clearly to be seen. They hastened the development of

the Pacific Coast, one of the richest and most fertile parts of the Union, and they also helped to build up chains of advancing civilization through the immense unoccupied territory that lay between western Nebraska and the Sierra 

Nevada Mountains. Mining camps and cattle ranches were no longer the only outposts of population in this region. Farms followed the railway lines wherever farming was profitable, and towns sprang up wherever mining, cattle-raising, or agriculture made them necessary. 

Perhaps the chief benefit was in drawing our vast country more closely together, in making it easy to travel back and forth from the Atlantic to the Pacific and in encouraging the exchange of the products of the East and the West. 

Before the advent of the transcontinental roads California was a month's journey or more from Chicago. With their completion it was brought within five or six days of that city. Today fast trains travel between Chicago and San 

Francisco in less than three days. Without the railways it would have been hard to keep a region so distant and so inaccessible by other means attached to the Union. 

Abuse of Government Aid 

You have read about the very generous aid the Government gave to these railways while they were building. There seemed to be excellent reasons for such aid at the time. 

The roads were thought to be essential to the development of the Nation, and yet they cost so much to build that it was feared private capital would hesitate to spend so much money on roads that passed through a nearly empty country. 

Unfortunately, though, the policy led to a good many abuses. The public lands granted to the railways were not always honestly handled by the companies; but the chief abuse was connected with the construction companies formed to do the actual work. These companies were

usually made up of the same men who were directors of the railway companies. As directors they let contracts for construction work to themselves as constructors, and at exorbitant rates. It is said that it cost $58,000,000 to build the Central Pacific road from San Francisco to Ogden, but that the construction company was paid $120,000,000 for the work.

Most famous of all these companies was the Crédit Mobilier, formed to build the Union Pacific. To prevent the Government from interfering with the operations of the road and the construction company, the latter used some of its stock to bribe some influential members of Congress to protect the interests of the company. The fact somehow became known, and led to a public scandal which destroyed the reputation of a number of well-known politicians, including the Vice-President of the United States. This was in 1872.

The Dark Side of Railway-Building 

Following the Civil War railroad-building went forward at a great pace all over the country. The entire railway system of the South, destroyed in the war, had to be rebuilt, and new roads were projected and constructed in every direction. Much of this building was necessary to take care of the rapidly increasing internal commerce of the country. Some of it, however, was purely speculative. 

The railroads already existing had been exceedingly prosperous during the war. Their profits had been so great that people seemed to think any kind of railroad could make money, whether it was a necessity to business or not. It was the easiest thing in the world to sell stock for a new railway; and a good many roads were built into territory that could not profitably support them, or between cities that already had an adequate railway service.

The result was that after a few years these roads got into trouble, and some of them went bankrupt. In the end, however, the situation worked out well enough. The smaller, less profitable roads were taken over by the stronger roads, and great railway systems began to arise. 

Consolidating the Railways 

Small early railroad companies were formed to build lines between cities fifty or a hundred or even two hundred miles 3487 

RAILROAD TRAINS, OLD AND NEW 

On the right is the DeWitt Clinton, the first locomotive on the New York Central Railroad, with its train of coaches. This train ran from Albany to Schenectady in 1831. On the left is a modern express the Twentieth Century Limited. 

apart. They had not the resources to improve their service, and they were rarely particular to arrange their trains to accommodate one another. In a journey of five hundred miles it might be necessary to "change cars" two or three times, with perhaps a tedious wait at each change.

When these small roads were taken over by a larger company, or united into a single long system, the service

improved immediately. Better tracks were laid, larger locomotives built. Trains ran faster and more regularly, and over longer distances. Today one can travel from New 

York to Chicago, from Chicago to San Francisco or New 

Orleans, or from Boston to Miami, Florida, without leaving the car in which he began the journey. 

The process of consolidation has gone on steadily. There are great systems like the Pennsylvania Railroad or the New York Central that control from 10,000 to 20,000 miles of railway. It is even proposed to divide the whole national system between some twenty or twenty-five corporations, and the Government, through the Interstate Commerce Commission, is trying to work out a plan to that effect. So far it has not succeeded. 

Water-Borne Traffic Diminishes

Transportation by water has had some extraordinary development, and some decay. The use of rivers and canals has not increased since the Civil War. The Mississippi and 

Ohio Rivers, which were at one time used extensively by steamboats carrying both passengers and freight, are now used very little except by barges, towed by small steamers, for the transportation of bulkier freight, especially coal and lumber.

The reason for this change in transportation methods is that the railways give so much quicker and more satisfactory service, because the rails can be laid wherever business requires them, whereas the rivers can only serve the towns that are on their banks.

Canals, too, are only rarely used; most of those that were built a hundred years ago have long been abandoned. The most famous of them all, the Erie Canal, rebuilt and enlarged by the State of New York, still does a great deal of business, but only with bulky freight for which no one is in a hurry.

Transportation by water is cheaper than by rail, but Americans are usually willing to pay the higher charges to get their freight promptly delivered. Perhaps the time will come when we shall turn again to the waterways for reasons of economy; but it has not yet 

MODERN STEAM TOW BOAT 

These boats tow long strings of freight barges. In the background is a section of the St. Louis waterfront. come. Commerce on the 

Great Lakes 

In one direction transportation by water has flourished; that is on the Great 

Lakes. These splendid bodies of water make a perfect deepwater highway between East and 

West. Steamships, almost or quite as large as trans-Atlantic liners, ply back and forth between Buffalo, Cleveland, Detroit, Chicago, Milwaukee, and Duluth. Great cargoes of coal, iron, lumber, wheat, copper, and miscellaneous goods are carried between these great cities and many smaller ones, and people are using the passenger steamers more and more when they can spare the time; for the trip through the lakes is almost equal to an ocean voyage. 

The canal that was built at Sault Ste. Marie to carry shipping around the swift water between Lake Superior and Lake Huron is one of the busiest waterways in the world.

It was opened just before the Civil War; in 1860, 403,000 tons of shipping passed through it. In 1929, the tonnage amounted to 92,000,000, and it increases year by year. On some days forty or fifty large vessels, besides many smaller craft, pass through the locks at Sault Ste. Marie.

By widening and deepening the Chicago River and connecting it with the Illinois River, a waterway has been provided down which quite good-sized craft can pass from the Great Lakes to the Mississippi River and so on to the Gulf of Mexico. Another scheme, much discussed but not yet undertaken, would deepen the Welland Canal around Niagara Falls, and build another around the rapids of the St. Lawrence River above Montreal. This would enable ocean-going steamships to cross the Atlantic and sail up the St. Lawrence and the Great Lakes to Cleveland, Detroit, Chicago, and Duluth without any change of cargo — and of course to make the voyage in the other direction equally well. 

As for the tonnage used in our lake traffic, it compares even with that employed in our foreign commerce. Before the World War set us to building ships to carry on the interrupted trade with Europe, there were actually more American-owned ships sailing the Great Lakes than the oceans of the world. Today the tonnage on the Lakes amounts to about 3,000,000 tons, a merchant navy greater than that of all but two or three of the nations of the world. 

The Telegraph

Railways and steamboats are not the only means of speedy communication between the different parts of our great country and between it and the rest of the world. 

Telegraph, cable, and telephone lines do not bring people face to face, but they enable them to communicate with one another with extraordinary rapidity. All these means of bringing people into closer touch with one another have

been marvelously developed since the days of the Civil War. 

You read in an earlier unit of this book¹ about the invention of the electric telegraph, and of its first use in 1844. 

It had already come into very common use by 1860 and the necessities of the war made the extension of the system even more rapid. It was important to keep all the armies in constant touch with one another and with Washington, and the demand of the people for constant and prompt information about what was going on at the front led to the construction of many new telegraph lines. 

After the war, the rapid increase in railway-building encouraged the growth of the telegraph system. Wherever the rails went the telegraph wires followed; for the quick transmission of messages and orders was essential to the safe and speedy operations of the trains. People came to use these wires more and more for their own business too, and the newspapers, eager to collect news from all parts of the country as promptly as possible, used them even more than private citizens did.

Today there is hardly a village or hamlet so small that it is not served by the telegraph. Modern business and the modern newspaper would be impossible without it. Its effect in drawing the country closer and ever closer together is obvious. When a man in New York can send a message to a friend or business associate three thousand miles away in San Francisco and get an answer back in an hour or even less, it is impossible to think of the Pacific Coast as being the remote and inaccessible place our grandfathers thought it. 

The Atlantic Cable 

As soon as the telegraph was proved a success on land, the idea began to occur to people of imagination that it might be used somehow to bridge the ocean and bring the con- 

I See Unit VII, p. 175.

tinents of the world into closer touch with one another. It was an American, Cyrus W. Field, who first worked out a way of carrying telegraphic messages under the sea. He spliced a number of copper wires together so as to make them into lines hundreds of miles in length, covered them with a waterproof substance, and so made cables that could be laid under water without damage to the wires within. 

A special ship was made with a great reel at the stern on which the cable was wound. Then the ship sailed slowly along paying out the cable as it went, which, of course, sank to the bottom of the sea. There were failures and disappointments before complete success was achieved. 

As early as 1858 a cable between Ireland and Newfoundland gave satisfactory service; but then it broke somewhere on the floor of the ocean.

LANDING THE ATLANTIC CABLE AT NEWFOUNDLAND 

This steamship is the Great Eastern, the largest of her day, but only about half the tonnage of the Leviathan. 

The Civil War prevented any more experiments, but in 1866 another line was successfully laid, and since then there has been no interruption in communication by telegraph between Europe and the United States. Moreover, cables have been laid in every direction, beneath the Pacific and Indian Oceans as well as the Atlantic, and every civilized country is now in immediate communication with every other one.

The Telephone 

Another remarkable invention was the telephone. This, too, was invented in America, by Alexander Graham Bell.

In Boston, in 1876, he first showed the world that the human voice could be carried over electrically charged wires. The original telephone line was only a few hundred yards long; but the principle was sound and improvements were rapidly made. Now it is possible to talk across continents and oceans, and here in the United States the telephone is so essential a part of our daily life that there is hardly a house without it.

Of course the telephone has the advantage over the telegraph that not only messages but conversations can be transmitted by it, and that after a connection has been made communication is not only speedy but instantaneous.

More wonderful, or at least more mysterious, than either is the wireless telegraphy, which we speak of as the radio. 

Very early in the twentieth century an Italian, Signor

Marconi, showed us how to use the invisible vibrations that are continually passing, or can be made to pass, through the ether around us. These waves travel with the speed of light, so that they pass completely round the earth in less than a second of time.

By the proper kind of apparatus they can be caught and turned into sound waves which we can hear. In this way messages can be sent through the air to and from ships at sea, or from one end of the country to the other. The only difficulty about them is that they cannot well be directed toward a single point, but go out in every direction from the place from which they start. That is an advantage when a ship in distress is calling for help, but it is not so satisfactory when you want to send a messsage that you want one person only to receive.

The widest development of radio has been to furnish

popular entertainment; the ether is alive, day and night, with musical concerts, lectures, and other programs, which, if you own a radio set, you can make audible in your home.

The States are Drawn Together 

The effect that all these remarkable improvements in transportation and communication have had on the social and political life of the United States cannot be exaggerated. 

If we add to them the invention and development of the automobile, which came somewhat later the first motor cars were built toward the close of the nineteenth century we shall see that people today are living in a very different sort of world from that of the early days of the Republic. 

The most striking effect of all is one of which we have already spoken the drawing of the Nation into a far closer unity than it ever knew before the Civil War. People now travel by rail or motor car easily and swiftly from one part of the country to another. Northerners have become at home in the South, Easterners in the West. People, however far removed from one another, can send messages and even talk together. Things that happen in California are known and discussed within a few hours in New England. 

The Civil War determined that there should be no actual disunion between the States. The railways, the telegraph, the telephone, the motor car, have brought about a degree of real union between the various sections which would have been impossible without them.

UNIT XV THE UNITED STATES BECOMES AN INDUSTRIAL COUNTRY The Old United States an Agricultural Country 

P to the time of the Civil War, the United States was first of all an agricultural country. Farming was the principal occupation of the original colonists, and it continued to be that of the Americans after they had created an independent nation.

There were millions of acres of fertile untouched soil among the forests and plains west of the Appalachian Mountains. Any one could have a farm who would take the trouble to pick out the land he wanted, and clear it. 

The rich valleys of the Ohio, Wabash, Tennessee, Cumberland, Mississippi, Illinois, Missouri, and other rivers became filled with settlers whose only idea was to own land and grow crops on it. Probably four people at least out of every five who lived in the United States in 1860 were directly or indirectly dependent on agriculture for their living. 

Manufacturing and commerce were important along the coast, particularly in New England, New York, and Pennsylvania. Cotton and woolen mills were common along the rivers of New England, the iron and steel industry was beginning to establish itself among the hills of Pennsylvania. 

There were factories scattered through New York and around Philadelphia. Shipyards were busy along the coast, and trade became brisk between ports like Boston and New 

York, with Europe, the West Indies, China, and other places across the sea. But after all, most Americans lived

WE BECOME AN INDUSTRIAL COUNTRY 

by farming or planting, and the country was rural in the character of its civilization. Great cities were few, and the manufactures of the country were by no means equal to supplying everything the people needed. 

Changes that Followed the Civil War 

After the Civil War, however, a great change began to make itself felt. The Great West was no longer unoccupied. 

PART OF A GREAT STEEL MILL 

In time it became even thickly populated from the agricultural point of view. All the best land was taken up and laid out in farms. Only the Far Western regions, where the rainfall was too uncertain to make farming successful, remained unoccupied. No longer could an ambitious young man find a home for himself and his family by merely traveling west and settling down on a strip of rich prairie land.

The rapidly growing population of the country had to turn to other means of support. Industry and manufacturing

WE BECOME AN INDUSTRIAL COUNTRY 347 

grew very rapidly. Great iron and steel mills sprang up at Pittsburgh, Cleveland, and other cities. Factories for making textile goods, silk, shoes, and leather goods appeared in several parts of the country. Great flour mills clustered around the falls at Minneapolis. Shops for making locomotives and machinery of all kinds were built. 

Small towns grew large and large towns became great cities. 

Year by year the growth of industry increased, until today the United States is really an industrial country; a nation of manufacturers rather than of farmers. More than half the population is "urban" or city-dwelling. Not more than one person in four now lives on a farm. 

Growth of Industry During the Civil War 

Industry began to make great progress even during the war. There was immediate need for guns, ammunition, and military supplies, for rails for new railroads, for great quantities of clothes and shoes for the soldiers, for machinery to make all these things, and to replace on the farms the men who had gone into the service. Foreign trade was more or less interrupted, and it became necessary for us to manufacture many commodities that we had formerly imported. In- In war-time prices are always high and profits large; so business men turned eagerly to manufacturing. With the end of the war the growth of industry did not stop. The country, aided by increased immigration, grew rapidly in population and in the demand for goods of all kinds. vention flourished. New machines of all sorts were produced to enable factories to turn out goods in greater quantities and at larger profits. Agriculture was still the chief occupation in the West and South, but the East and parts of the Middle West became more and more a great industrial workshop.

WE BECOME AN INDUSTRIAL COUNTRY The Iron and Steel Industry 

The most remarkable growth, perhaps, was that of the iron and steel industry. The year before the war began the product of our iron mills amounted to only about $37,000,- 000. Ten years later it had increased sixfold. By the end of the century it amounted to more than $800,000,000. In 

1929, it had risen to $4,300,000,000. 

Before the Civil War steel was scarce. Steel, as perhaps you know, is a purified form of iron into which a proper amount of carbon has been introduced. That makes it very much harder and tougher than iron and less brittle or breakable. It was not until an Englishman named Bessemer invented a process for purifying melted iron by driving a blast of cold air through it that steel could be easily and cheaply made. This Bessemer did in 1858, and only a few years later American mills began to use his process. great many years almost all steel was made in this way, and because of its superiority over the old cast iron, it came to be used for almost every purpose that iron had formerly served. 

For a

The Bessemer process, however, though it is well adapted to some kinds of iron, is not so good for iron that contains a large amount of phosphorus as a good deal of iron ore does. So in later years another process called the "openhearth process" was invented by a man named Martin, and the greater part of American steel is now made in that way. 

Why Steel Mills Locate near Soft Coal Mines 

Iron cannot be smelted without the use of fuel, of course, and the best sort of fuel for that purpose is coke, which is made from soft or bituminous coal by extracting the gases that are naturally a part of it. The iron industry required the bringing of the ore and the coke together, and as it costs more to transport an equal value of coke than of iron, the

WE BECOME AN INDUSTRIAL COUNTRY 

349 mills did not grow up around the iron mines unless there was soft coal near as well. 

In the case of Pittsburgh in Pennsylvania and Birmingham in Alabama iron and coal were both found near by, so great rolling mills and steel foundries sprang up in and about these cities. Cleveland and Chicago also built up important steel industries, because the cargoes of iron could easily be brought down by water from the rich mines in Minnesota to be used with the coke from mines not too far away. 

Andrew Carnegie

Pittsburgh was the first great center of the industry; not only because of its evident advantages, but also because of the energy, enterprise, and business genius of its iron masters. The most famous of them all was Andrew Carnegie, who was born in Scotland, came over to this country as a boy, and became a railway telegrapher. He was among the very first to see the tremendous possibilities of the steel industry, and aided by capital supplied by men who had confidence in him, he established himself at Pittsburgh.

The growth of his enterprises was rapid and extraordinary. 

Long before his death he was one of the richest men in the world. His mills became, early in this century, the center around which the United States Steel Corporation, the greatest producer of steel in the world, was formed. 

The Many Uses of Steel

Steel is used so widely in modern life that our age is sometimes called the age of steel - as it also is called the age of steam or the age of electricity. All our transportation by land and sea is based on steel, for rails, locomotives, cars, bridges, and ships are all built of it. Modern warfare is dependent on it, for guns of all sorts, from pistols to the most powerful artillery, could not be made without steel; while

WE BECOME AN INDUSTRIAL COUNTRY 

bullets are often steel-jacketed and heavy projectiles are made of the same substance. Warships are built of steel and armored with belts of still harder steel made by special processes. 

All the machinery with which our thousands of mills and manufactories are filled is made of iron or steel. Bridges, though occasionally built of masonry, are usually built from 

Courtesy Colorado Fuel & Iron Co. 

BLAST FURNACES AND ORE YARDS 

end to end of trusses and girders of steel. All edged tools are of steel. In our cities steel has replaced stone in the raising of great buildings; and the use of strong steel frames, bolted together and later faced with stone or concrete, has made it possible to erect skyscrapers that are forty, fifty, and even seventy and eighty stories high. 

Steel workers are well paid, for their work is important, laborious, and highly skilled. It cannot always be pleasant or healthful, for the mills are noisy, smoky places, and the men who handle the molten iron are constantly exposed to

WE BECOME AN INDUSTRIAL COUNTRY 

exhausting degrees of heat. But ingenious machinery has relieved them of much of the very heavy labor that was once required of them. There was formerly much unrest and dissatisfaction among the steel workers, owing to the hard conditions of their life and labor. This reached its peak in the great strike at Carnegie's Homestead Mills near Pittsburgh in 1892; but the relations between employers and men are now much improved, and seem likely to remain so. 

Cotton Mills in New England

The textile industry of the United States had its earliest large development in New England, though woolen mills were also common in New York and Pennsylvania. The manufacture of cotton especially became a New England business. Water power was abundant on many of the rivers in those States, and made it possible to run the mills more economically than elsewhere. Lowell, Lawrence,

Manchester, and Fall River grew from villages to great industrial cities in the years between 1840 and 1880, and scores of smaller places had each its cotton mill to help its growth and prosperity.

Of course the early manufacturers of cotton cloth had to face the competition of the English mills, which were already well established, and able to produce their goods more cheaply than the Americans could. With the help of a protective tariff, which put heavy duties on the English cloth, the American industry soon became prosperous. By 1860 there were more than a thousand cotton mills in the United States, and goods worth $115,000,000 were being made every year. At the end of the century the value of their product had increased to nearly three times that amount. 

Cotton Mills in the South

After 1880, the South began to challenge New England's

WE BECOME AN INDUSTRIAL COUNTRY 

leadership in the cotton industry. Water power was common in the South, too; especially in the region at the foot of the long range of the Appalachian Mountains. Mills built there were much nearer the cotton-fields, so that the cost of transporting cotton to the machines was small; and the costs of living were so much less in the Southern country that people were willing to work for smaller wages. 

Under these conditions the cotton industry grew rapidly in Virginia, North Carolina, and South Carolina. Today 

THE SPINNING ROOM OF A COTTON MILL 

more of the coarser kinds of cotton cloth are made in the South than in New England, and not a few mill-owners have transferred their business from Massachusetts or Rhode Island to North Carolina. The finer and more expensive cottons are still made in the North, where there are advantages of climate and a larger supply of skilled labor for the production of such goods. 

From the beginning the cotton industry has made use of child labor in the mills. There are many operations in spinning and weaving cotton that are so simple that a child of

WE BECOME AN INDUSTRIAL COUNTRY 353 

ten or more can attend to them. Since children are paid much less than grown people, it is economical for the millowner to employ children when he can. In many cases parents do not object because the earnings of the children make the family income so much larger. 

Public opinion in the United States, however, is strongly against the employment of young children in factories. 

One by one the industrial States of the North have made laws forbidding it, and obliging children to attend school until they are at least fifteen or sixteen years old. There is no real child labor in the Northern mills today. It still exists in the South; but we may expect that it will not do so much longer.

The Woolen Industry 

For two hundred years after the settlement of our country, most of the woolen cloth used in America, that was not imported from England, was "homespun," made on looms in the homes of the people. There were no woolen mills in the United States before the Revolution and few until the War of 1812, when the need for woolen cloth and the stopping of imports from Europe gave opportunity to establish mills in our own country. 

It was thirty years more before factory-made woolen cloth was as common as homespun; but from that time forward the change was rapid. The Civil War led to another great development in the industry, for the Government needed and bought immense quantities of cloth to make uniforms and overcoats for the soldiers. The amount of wool woven in the mills jumped from 85,000,000 pounds a year to more than 200,000,000 pounds, and many large fortunes were made by woolen manufacturers during these years. 

The industry, almost always well protected by tariff duties, continued to increase after the war was over. Mills

WE BECOME AN INDUSTRIAL COUNTRY 

were most numerous in New England, New York, and Pennsylvania, but they were to be found in other Northern States as well. The value of their annual product has grown to considerably more than a billion dollars a year. For some reason our best woolen cloth is not even yet as fine as that made by the English manufacturers. Perhaps the climate has something to do with that; but we make a great deal of cloth of fine quality every year, as well as much that is not so good.

We have been especially successful in the weaving of woolen carpets. American inventors have shown great ingenuity in adapting the power loom to that kind of work; and American rugs and carpets, woven by machinery, are finer than any other floor coverings except the wonderful rugs made on the hand-looms of the Orient. 

Growth of the Silk Industry

The United States has also built up a very great silk industry since the close of the Civil War, in the face of continual competition from the long-established manufacturers in France and in Japan. This is the more remarkable because no raw silk whatever is produced in this country, and the mills must bring their material from halfway round the world. In 1860, only about $6,000,000 worth of silk was manufactured in our country; but since then the output of our mills has increased more than one hundred times. 

Paterson, New Jersey, is the chief seat of silk manufacture, but there are silk mills in many of the States in the eastern part of the country.

In these later years the making of artificial silk or rayon, from cellulose or wood fiber has become almost as great an industry as that of silk manufacture, and there are great rayon mills in the South as well as in the North, in Tennessee as well as in New Jersey.

WE BECOME AN INDUSTRIAL COUNTRY 355 

Leather and Shoemaking

Another very important source of national wealth is leather and the products that are made from it. The growth of all the leather industries— and particularly shoemaking was helped by the remarkable increase of cattle-raising on the open ranges of the West, of which you read in the last unit. The skins of cattle are, of course, the principal sources of leather, and the millions of steers that were raised in the grazing country of the West provided, when they came to market, a very plentiful and cheap supply of skins for tanning into leather for the manufacturers.

Shoemaking was a hand trade, carried on in little shops, until 1845 or later. Then began the invention of a succession of ingenious machines — the leather roller, the lasting machine, and various stitching machines for sewing the different parts of the shoe together. Of course the result was the passing of shoemaking out of the home and into the factory. The growth of the industry since the Civil War has been extraordinary. There are some fifteen hundred factories in the country, mostly in Massachusetts, New York, or in the vicinity of St. Louis, and more than 350,- 

000,000 pairs of shoes are made every year. 

No industry is more firmly based on the ingenious use of machinery. The ordinary factory uses at least sixty different kinds of machines; and it is interesting to know that almost every important machine is made by a single company, the United Shoe Machinery Corporation. This company owns most of the principal patents, and sells or leases the machines it makes to the manufacturers. On the leased machines the shoe manufacturers pay to the company a rental, or a royalty on the product of the machines. 

The American shoe has been called "the standard machine product of the world." If you live near a shoe factory

WE BECOME AN INDUSTRIAL COUNTRY 

you will find it most interesting to visit it and watch the machines, each performing its own task and turning out in the end the perfect, shapely shoe. 

Advances in the Printing Trades 

Just as the tireless American inventors and builders of machinery had taken the making of cloth and shoes out of the homes and the little shops of the people, and created great industrial cities, full of mills and factories to do the manufacturing business of the country, so they revolutionized many other trades and occupations as well. The printing of the early years of the Republic, down nearly to the days of the Civil War, was done in small shops, with presses not very different from the one that Benjamin Franklin had worked by hand in Philadelphia, long before the Revolution. 

In 1845, however, Hoe invented the rotary press, which could be operated by steam and later by electric power. The rotary press has been continually improved since that time, by attachments which make it possible to fold and stitch together the printed sheets, while the speed with which it works has greatly increased. The modern newspaper press is a huge machine, which can print and fold many thousands of papers every hour. 

The Modern Newspaper 

Other inventions have helped to make the modern newspaper very different from the small journals of four pages that our great-grandfathers knew. In 1876, Christopher

Sholes built the first typewriter, which has made the writing of letters and the preparation of copy for books and newspapers far easier and more rapid than it formerly was. Then came the invention of the linotype machine by Mergenthaler, a contrivance that permits a compositor to set type

WE BECOME AN INDUSTRIAL COUNTRY 357 

mechanically with four or five times the speed of the old printer standing at his case and setting type by hand. 

Added to these great improvements in printing methods, an ingenious system for collecting news by telegraph, telephone, and cable from all over the world, has been organized by newspaper owners, thus making possible the great metropolitan newspapers of the present day. There are journals in New York and Chicago that print every day half a million copies, each of thirty, forty, or even sixty pages, filled with the news of the world. Even in the smaller cities the newspapers are surprisingly complete in equipment and in content.

Practically every one in the United States reads his daily newspaper; and though it may be doubted whether the opinions of the editors have as much influence in shaping public opinion as they had in the days before the Civil War or immediately after it, the great newspapers perform a valuable service in keeping Americans well informed about whatever is going on in all parts of the world. 

The Era of the "Trusts"

We have tried to show you some of the steps by which the United States, at least in the North and East, became an industrial nation, making a continually increasing use of machinery and of mechanical power in the production of all kinds of goods. We ought also to tell you about one other feature of our industrial development, the growth of great business combinations.

Almost as soon as the Civil War was over, business combinations began. We have read about the way in which small railroad companies came to be joined together into great railway systems. Something of the same kind happened in many other lines of business. Even in the textile and shoemaking industries, which are today freer from big

WE BECOME AN INDUSTRIAL COUNTRY 

combinations than any others, there were fewer mills and factories in 1900 than in 1865. Those that remained, however, were far larger.

In the iron and steel business, combination came quite early. The great Carnegie mills were joined with others to form the United States Steel Corporation, and the immense steel business of the nation has fallen chiefly into the hands of this corporation and the Bethlehem Steel Company. 

The telephone companies are practically all united under the American Telephone and Telegraph Company; the telegraph business is conducted by only two companies. 

The Standard Oil Company, formed by John D. Rockefeller, absorbed scores of corporations producing gasoline and kerosene from petroleum. Most of the sugar refineries were acquired by a single corporation; the meat-packing industry was taken over by four or five great concerns, and the same thing happened in many other lines of business. These great combinations were at first called "trusts" because they were organized under boards of trustees who took over the stock of the smaller companies and gave the stockholders trust certificates in their place. The first trusts were formed as early as 1879 and they rapidly increased as the advantages of the system were recognized. 

The Sherman Anti-Trust Act. 

The purpose of the trusts was to make larger profits by putting a stop to the often ruinous competition between so many small companies all fighting for their share of the business. The trust could buy its supplies more cheaply, could maintain prices without fear of being undersold by competitors, and since it dealt in such large quantities could produce and distribute its goods at a lower cost. It was also in a better position to bargain with labor unions, since it offered the principal and sometimes the only opportunity for employment in the industry to which it belonged.

WE BECOME AN INDUSTRIAL COUNTRY 359 

The people were suspicious of the trusts. They seemed to be aiming at a monopoly in all the great industries of the country, and people have always had a justifiable fear of monopolies. Their financial and economic power was understood and it was feared that this power would be used to keep down the wages of labor and raise prices to the people who bought the goods of the trusts. The State legislatures began to pass laws to prevent or limit their organization; and in 1890 Congress passed the Sherman Anti-Trust Act with the same end in view. Under these laws the Standard 

Oil Trust, oldest of them all, was dissolved by the courts, and others were so much hampered in their activities that they, too, went to pieces.

Consolidation of Business Goes Forward 

But the movement toward consolidation into great business companies was only halted; it was not stopped. The "trusts" gave place to "holding companies" in which a new organization was formed to buy or control the stock of the smaller corporations. The American Telephone and 

Telegraph Company is the oldest and most famous of these holding companies. They grew rapidly in number and size, and another wave of public opposition arose as they increased. While Mr. Roosevelt was President, Congress passed more laws to prevent the great holding companies from establishing monopolies (in 1903). The President was able to prevent a proposed union of two great railways the Northern Pacific and the Great Northern. A few years later (1911) the courts forced the Standard Oil Corporation and the American Tobacco Company to break up their powerful holding companies, and people began to think that perhaps they had seen the last of the great combinations. 

The economic forces were too strong for political control, however. New ways of consolidation were found, and the

WE BECOME AN INDUSTRIAL COUNTRY 

Nation gradually became used to the idea of combination for the sake of economy. Under the watchful eye of Congress and the State legislatures, these combinations proved less dangerous than had been expected. Wages were not lowered and prices did not increase as much as it was feared they would. Today the production and distribution of the necessities of life are largely in the hands of a few great companies in each industry, and consolidation is still going on as fast as ever. It is now opposed principally on the ground that it reduces the number of independent manufacturers and merchants, and makes an increasing proportion of American citizens employees of some one else, rather than owners and managers of their own businesses.

UNIT XVI THE BENEFITS AND THE PROBLEMS OF WE IMMIGRATION 

´E have spoken of the very rapid development of the United States both in agriculture and industry during the period between 1865 and 1900. That extraordinary growth would not have been possible if it had not been for the constant stream of immigrants from Europe during those years. At least 12,000,000 foreigners came to America to find work and make homes for themselves in this period. 

In 1882 alone almost 800,000 arrived. You can see what that meant in the way of filling up the vacant spaces in the Western farming country and supplying much-needed labor for railway-building and for the new mills and factories that were rising on every hand. 

Immigration in the Early Years

The United States has always attracted emigrants from Europe. A great new country, with plenty of land for all, it naturally offered hopeful opportunities to poor men, ambitious of doing better for themselves than they could in the crowded countries of Europe, where it was hard for a man without land or money to get ahead. Our first immigrants came mainly from the British Isles and Germany; but not very many could come at one time, for travel was costly, slow, and dangerous until steam was applied to navigation. 

With the appearance of steamships on the Atlantic, about 1840, immigration became easier, cheaper, and more comfortable. Then, too, certain things happened in Europe that turned thousands of men toward this country. There

was a severe famine in Ireland, where the potato crop failed, and in despair of getting food enough at home, throngs of Irish men and women took ship for America. 

In Germany, about the same time, there was a revolutionary movement in favor of a republic, which was, however, suppressed by the royal government; but as a result a great many dissatisfied and discouraged people decided to come to America, where they could have the kind of government they liked. 

The Irish and German Immigrations

Soon after 1845, these two streams of immigration — from Ireland and Germany - began to pour into the United States. They were joined by another stream, not so large, but very considerable, from the Scandinavian countries, Norway and Sweden. The first comers found plenty of work, and almost free land if they wanted it. They wrote glowing accounts of their good fortune to their friends at home, and more and more immigrants arrived. Before 1840 there had never been as many as 100,000 immigrants to our country in one year. By 1854 there were over 400,000. 

These new citizens settled almost wholly in the North; they did not like slavery and did not wish to live in a region where work was done almost entirely by Negroes. The 

Scandinavians pushed on into the wheat country and took up farms.

So did some of the Germans, while others remained in the cities as artisans or traders. The Irish stayed mainly in the cities or large towns, and soon came to be one of the chief sources for the supply of labor there. 

Immigration and the Civil War

Most of the immigrants became naturalized voters as soon as it was possible for them to do so; and their numbers were sufficient to make it worth while for the politicians of both

parties to try to gain their support. The Irish usually became Democrats; but the greater part of the Germans though not all — and the Scandinavians were attracted to the Republican Party.

With the opening of the Civil War it became evident that these new citizens were to be of great assistance to the North, where almost all of them had settled. Many served as soldiers in the Union army, and those who did not enter the army, by their labor on the farms and elsewhere helped to maintain the supply of food and carry on the necessary activities of business. Immigration had added some 

4,000,000 to the population of the North between 1845 and 1860, and this addition to its man-power was of the greatest importance in the struggle to preserve the Union. Perhaps without it the North would have found it impossible to win. 

After the Civil War immigration continued to increase. 

Hundreds of thousands of farmers from Great Britain, Germany, and Scandinavia poured into the new West, to take up land under our liberal Homestead Act, and develop our great corn and wheat country. Millions were attracted to our growing industries. They toiled in the construction of 200,000 miles of railway, in the mines of coal and iron and copper and gold and silver and lead; in the great steel mills and cotton factories and shoeshops. They came so fast that, in spite of the growth of both agriculture and business, there was always an abundance of labor to be had on the farms and in the shops. 

The Chinese

All this time there was no attempt to limit or restrict immigration. All who wished to come were welcomed. 

Americans took pride in the flood of men and women who were pouring into their country to find new hopes and new homes in the land of liberty.

Newcomers were

About 1882 there came a change. thronging into the country so fast that American labor took alarm. It was feared that so many immigrants would enter the United States that there would not be work for everybody, and that wages would fall. The feeling was especially strong on the Pacific Coast. Chinese had begun to come in large numbers to California, and they were willing to work so hard and needed so little money to live, that white workingmen foresaw ruin for themselves if something was not done to stop the Chinese immigration. 

There were riots and disorders in consequence, and feeling rose so high that Congress thought it best to pass a law forbidding the immigration of Chinese laborers. In the same year (1882) the first attempt to control immigration from Europe was made. The new law put a head tax of fifty cents on all immigrants, and forbade entirely the entry of those who, by reason of extreme poverty, disease, or a criminal record, were undesirable as residents of the United States.

Immigrants Come from Eastern Europe 

For a time this law caused the number of immigrants to fall, and the business depression of 1893-96 brought the numbers down still further. With the return of prosperity, the tide of immigration rose again. To add to the flood, the great steamship companies, in competition for business, offered very low rates for steerage passengers and even had agents in the principal European cities to encourage and persuade people to emigrate.

Under these conditions the number of immigrants to enter the country rose year by year; for several years after 1905 it amounted to more than a million persons annually. At the same time the character of the immigration began to change. Many still came from Great Britain, Ireland, Ger-

many, Sweden, and Norway; but many more from Italy, Greece, Austria-Hungary, Poland, and Russia. These people took up the task of furnishing the heavy labor needed in construction work, in road-building, and in the coal mines and steel mills of Pennsylvania and Illinois. 

Copyright, Underwood & Underwood

ELLIS ISLAND IMMIGRATION STATION, IN NEW YORK HARBOR 

Problems of the Newer Immigration

These changes in the character of immigration brought about new problems which the United States had not previously had to face. These newcomers were very different in race and language, in traditions and customs from the people who already lived here. They were unfamiliar with the ideas of government on which this Nation was founded, and not too well fitted to become voters in a democratic republic. They were unaccustomed to the standard of living that prevailed here, and they were willing to work for wages lower than American laboring men required if they were to feed and clothe their families properly.

These later immigrants showed a tendency to collect in certain districts in all the large cities. In these foreign

colonies little or no English was spoken, and people followed the habits and ways of life they had brought with them from abroad. They often remained quite alien to all the life around them, and they neither understood the American people who were their neighbors nor were understood by them.

When they became voters they had no intelligent idea of the candidates or the issues that were presented to them, and generally voted as they were told to vote by political bosses of their own race, who frequently used the power thus given them for their own corrupt or selfish ends. 

How those Problems are Disappearing For many years this new immigration was a source of danger to our social and political system; but it is becoming less dangerous every year. The second generation grows up here among us, goes to school, learns to speak English and to absorb the ideals and customs of the country. These younger people insist on a standard of living as high as that of the rest of the Nation, and they are by no means so easily led by shrewd and corrupt bosses. For a good many years, however, the character of this new immigration gave cause for much uneasiness, and, as we shall see later, it led Congress finally to pass laws which made it impossible for more than 150,000 foreigners to enter the country in any single year.

It is interesting to know that some of the men who have done the most to make America great have been immigrants who came here with little except their clever brains and their will to work. Among them are Albert Gallatin, a famous statesman and financier of the early nineteenth century; 

John Ericsson, the inventor of the screw propeller; Carl

Schurz, soldier and statesman; Michael Pupin, the great electrical genius; and Edward W. Bok, editor, author, and philanthropist.

Make an immigrant "Hall of Fame" — a list of immigrants who have contributed to the history of the country. Examples are Albert Gallatin, John Ericsson, Carl Schurz, Michael Pupin, Jacob Riis, and E. W. Bok. Get photographs of as many of the men or women chosen as possible. See Our Foreign-Born Citizens, by Annie E. S. Beard. 

Give an oral report on "The Journey to America." See The

From 1850 to 1860, 88 per cent of our immigrants came from Great Britain, Ireland, and Germany, less than one per cent came from Italy, Austria-Hungary, Poland, and Russia. Between 1890 and 1900, 31 per cent came from the first-named countries and 55 per cent from the last-named. Explain why the earlier immigration more easily became Americanized.
