UNIT XVII DIFFICULTIES RAISED BY OUR GROWTH THE IN WEALTH AND INDUSTRY 

HE revolution that the Civil War and the years of reconstruction wrought in the agricultural, industrial, and political conditions of the United States, introduced many new problems into the government of the Nation, the States, and the local communities. Let us consider some of these problems, one by one. 

New States Admitted

The Homestead Act of 1862, the great mineral discoveries in many of the mountain States, and the rapid growth of population on the Pacific Coast led to the organization of many new States in the far West between 1864 and 1896. 

Nevada was admitted in 1864, Nebraska in 1867, Colorado in 1876. In 1889 the old Territory of Dakota was divided into two new States, North and South Dakota; and in the same year Montana and Washington were admitted to the Union. Then followed Idaho and Wyoming, both in 1890, and Utah in 1896.

Thus more than 900,000 square miles of land in the West, which had been sparsely settled Territories when the Civil War began, was divided into ten States, equal members in the Union with the thirteen original States and the twentytwo that had been admitted previous to 1864. 

The Farmer's Difficulties 

The farmers, miners, and stock-raisers who formed the bulk of the population of these new States lived under un-

usual conditions and had new problems to face. They were isolated from one another as the people in the older part of the country were not. Farms and ranches were very much larger than in the East; houses were often miles apart; towns and even villages were few; the immense distances of the West, still thinly settled, made social intercourse and political or economic coöperation difficult.

These Western farmers were not, like the older farmers, chiefly engaged in making a livelihood, and able to sell 

Courtesy of Cleveland Tractor Company

HARROWING BY TRACTOR ON A WESTERN FARM 

what they wished to sell to near-by markets. They raised great crops of wheat and corn or thousands of cattle, all of which they wanted to sell, but which they had to send hundreds or even thousands of miles to market. They were dependent for prosperity on the railroads; on good, efficient transportation at fair rates. They were often dissatisfied with the service the railroads gave them, and were especially discontented with the freight rates, which they considered too high.

The money problem was another difficult one for the Western farmers. Most of them had bought land or made improvements on borrowed money. This money they had

to get from the East, for their own part of the country was too young to have accumulated much capital. They had to pay high interest on their loans, for the money-lenders preferred to lend to the industrial businesses which seemed more likely to be profitable than farming. Money was scarce in the West, and as time went on, and greater and greater crops of wheat were raised, prices tended to fall. 

So the farmer was in constant need of better credit and more currency in order to carry on his business successfully. 

It was not only the farmer of the new West, but his brothers in older States like Illinois, Wisconsin, Indiana, Michigan, and Iowa, who found the situation difficult. 

The Granger Movement

Under these conditions, naturally, the farmers began to form organizations to help one another, and to exert what influence they could on politics. The Granges, first formed in 1867 as benevolent societies, soon began to take an active part in fighting for lower railway rates on agricultural products, and in many States the farmers were able to elect representatives to the legislatures who passed laws fixing the rates at a considerably lower level than they had reached heretofore.

These laws were not always fair, because they were based on hostility to the railroads rather than on a real effort to understand both sides of the case. Although the railroads fought them bitterly, and in the so-called Granger cases, carried the fight as far as the Supreme Court of the United States, the courts almost always ended by upholding the right of a State to fix railway rates within its borders, if the rates charged by the roads seemed to be exorbitant. 

The Farmers' Alliance 

Some ten or twelve years after the formation of the

Granges another association arose, called the Farmers' Alliance. This organization took an active part in politics from the outset, but it was more interested in financial than in railroad legislation. The farmers were feeling the pinch of something like poverty, for the prices of their crops continued to fall while the interest on the money they had borrowed was as high as ever. 

Courtesy of Los Angeles Chamber of Commerce

MODERN HARVESTING ON A LARGE SCALE 

They felt the need of more money, and of cheaper money. 

Many of them joined the Greenback Party, which was organized in the late seventies to persuade the Government to issue quantities of paper money, based, not on actual gold or silver or even on Government bonds, but on the mere promise of the Government to accept it or to redeem it in real money if presented to the Treasury. 

Greenbacks and Free Silver

The Greenback Party was never able to get the Nation to vote for its schemes of unlimited paper money. Ten years later, another party arose which took the name of Populists. 

This party also had its chief strength among the farmers of the West and South. It, too, wanted cheaper money and

more of it; but the plan it proposed was the free coinage of silver into dollars. Since the amount of silver in a dollar was worth only about sixty-four cents in gold, the Populists reasoned that the silver dollars would make all money cheaper; that prices would rise, and that it would therefore be easier for those who owed debts or mortgages to pay what they owed.

The "free silver" movement gained a great deal of strength after 1890 when the situation of the farmers was at its hardest. As we shall see later, the presidential election of 1896 was fought out on that issue. Free silver was beaten; but shortly afterward times began to improve. 

For many years the condition of agriculture was prosperous; it is only of late years that another fall in prices has aroused discontent in the West and led the farmers to interest themselves as a class in national politics. 

The Troubles of the Railways 

The troubles that disturbed the relations between the farmers and the railways were not the only ones that the era of railway-building between 1865 and 1890 brought on. 

The railways had never been really profitable investments before the Civil War, but during the war and the years following it they made a great deal of money. A perfect fever of railroad-building began. Immense amounts of capital went into new roads, and the owners tried to make all they could out of them.

You will remember reading ¹ about the help the National

Government gave to some of the more costly roads like those to the Pacific Coast. You will remember, too, how some dishonest men found ways to get more than they should have got from the Government by making the building of these roads unnecessarily expensive. 

I See Unit XIV, pages 328-30.

There were other abuses, too. Sometimes speculators got hold of a road and "watered" the stock. That means that they issued and sold a great deal more stock than the true value of the road represented. Of course it was impossible to earn money enough to pay dividends on this watered stock, and the road became bankrupt, ruining many innocent stockholders. This is what happened to the Erie 

Railroad in the early seventies.

There were other practices that aroused public resentment. Some roads favored certain cities over others in their rates, to please officers of the road. Sometimes they gave special rates or rebates to certain customers who had private influence with the management. The Standard Oil 

Company was charged with getting control of the petroleum industry in Pennsylvania and Ohio through securing secret terms from the railroads, much more liberal than their competitors could get. 

Bad Practices of the Railways

More railroads were built than were actually needed at the time. As a result competition between them grew so keen that rates were often cut to absurd figures in order to get business.

In 1876, one could travel from Boston to 

Cleveland 700 miles - for $6.50, and cattle were shipped from Chicago to New York for a dollar a carload.

When the railroads tried to meet this difficulty by "pooling," which means agreeing to divide the business between them according to plan, at a fixed rate, there was a loud protest from the public, who objected to paying the new rates, and thought the whole arrangement unfair and "monopolistic."

Another practice of the roads which aroused resentment was that of charging less proportionally for a "long haul" between cities where they had to meet competition, than for

a "short haul" on their own line where they had no competitor. Sometimes, in fact, the charge was not only relatively but actually less. In some cases freight was moved a thousand miles for less money than for a hundred miles; and you can see why people thought that most unfair, and took measures to regulate the charges and the practices of the railroads. We have already spoken of the 

Granger cases, in which the courts decided that the States had a perfect right to fix rates and pass laws for the railroads to obey. This decision was followed by numerous State laws, all designed to prevent the railroads from watering their stock or agreeing among themselves to make freight rates that were thought to be too high. 

The Interstate Commerce Commission 

In the end the National Government was also drawn into railway regulation, for so many systems covered two or more States that the conflicting laws of these different States were often confusing both to the railways and the people who used them. In 1887 the Interstate Commerce 

Commission was established. This commission does not interfere with rates between points in the same State; but it has power to fix rates between places in different States and to see to it that the roads violate none of the provisions of the Interstate Commerce Act, which forbade pooling, the giving of rebates, and the other objectionable practices you have been reading about.

Under the control of the Interstate Commerce Commission almost all the abuses of early days have disappeared, and the railways have regained the friendly confidence of their customers, the people of the United States. 

The Tariff Becomes Highly Protective 

The tariff for many years presented a political problem.

Before the Civil War tariffs were generally low, according to present-day ideas.

When the war came on and a greatly increased revenue was needed, the duties were quickly raised. Under the high tariff of 1862 the manufacturing industries of the country became very prosperous, and when the war was over, their owners determined to see that the duties were not reduced. 

They were men of wealth and of wide influence, and they used that influence to keep Congress in favor of protection as the permanent policy of the country. 

The farmer, of course, had far less interest in protection. 

We imported very little in the way of farm products, and the farmer needed no duty on wheat or potatoes or corn. It might be argued that a protective tariff was of no use at all to him, while it tended to increase the price of many things he had to buy; but the Western farmer, at least, had become 

Republican because of the Civil War, and he accepted the doctrine of his party. He was more interested in reducing railway rates and in agitating for more money either of paper or of silver than in studying the tariff question. 

The laboring men in their turn were told that their employment and their wages - decidedly higher than those in Europe depended on the prosperity of the manufacturers, and that this prosperity in turn depended on protection. 

Many of them, therefore, were willing to vote for candidates who were pledged to support a high protective tariff. 

The Tariff in Politics 

From 1876 to 1896 it may be said that the tariff was the chief issue that divided the two great parties. The Republicans were strong protectionists. The Democrats were generally advocates of “a tariff for revenue only." Grover 

Cleveland, the only Democratic President between 1861 and 1913, was a firm opponent of high protection, and dur-

ing his second term (1893-1897) a tariff bill was passed which, though it did not meet his views, was lower than those which Republican Congresses had enacted. 

With the disruption of the Democratic Party over the free-silver question in 1896, the Republicans gained a new lease of power, and protection, to which they were committed, became more firmly established than ever as a national policy. Since the World War, the rapid increase of manufacturing in the South and the demand of the farmers for tariff duties on foodstuffs that were being imported into the United States - particularly the wheat and dairy products of Canada - have converted many Democrats to protection. As a party they still advocate lower duties than the Republicans do, but they no longer stand for a tariff "for revenue only." The tariff bill passed in 1930 levied duties higher than ever before, chiefly owing to increased rates on agricultural products.

Why there was no Labor Problem One Hundred Years Ago 

Labor problems also arose after the Civil War. In the early days of our country there were no such labor problems as there are today. All the manufacturing weaving, shoemaking, metal-working, and other similar work — was done by hand workers, either in the home or in small shops where the master and a few apprentices or journeymen worked together.

The factory system, in which machinery was used, bringing many workers together in a single establishment, came about very slowly. Even as late as 1840, probably half the manufacturing in the United States was done by the old methods of hand labor.

After 1840, however, things moved more rapidly, and the necessity for prompt production of large quantities of goods during the Civil War caused great factories to rise all over

the North. Even then the formation of a labor or wageearning class was delayed by the fact that there was still so much free farm land to be had in the West. So long as a man could easily become an independent farmer if the pay and conditions of industrial life did not suit him, a labor class like that of England and other European nations was unlikely to form.

Gradually, however, the free farming land all became occupied. The factories grew larger and larger and required more people to work in them. Many of the new immigrants who poured into the country were mechanics, who had no desire for agricultural independence. So a wage-earning class, who preferred to work in the factories, came into being. 

Rise of a Wage-Earning Class 

Once formed, the wage-earning class soon began to organize to improve the conditions under which it had to labor. 

The men who worked in separate industries formed “unions,” by means of which they hoped to be in a better position to demand higher wages or shorter hours from their employers. 

Often the employers would not grant what was asked for, either because they could not afford to or because they wanted more of the profits of business for themselves. 

When this situation arose, the members of the unions often "went out on strike." They refused to work any longer under the existing conditions, and tried to prevent any other workmen from taking their places. The strike was something new in the United States, and at first it alarmed the Nation very much; for people feared that strikes would become worse and worse and so cause constant disorder and a breakdown of business.

The great railway strike of 1877 was the first to spread over a great part of the country. Railway workers all the way between New York and Baltimore in the East and

Chicago in the West stopped work because the roads tried to cut down their wages. There was some rioting and destruction of property before the strike was settled, and both militia and federal troops had to be used to keep order. 

After that experience there was no doubt that there was a labor problem, and a difficult one, to be faced. Strikes

For a number of years, the strike was the usual means taken by labor to gain its ends. No year passed without a 

SECTIONAL VIEW OF ANTHRACITE COAL MINE 

The larger buildings are the "breakers' where the coal is sorted and prepared for market. 

To the left is the shaft or entrance. Seams of coal are represented by the broad black lines. "Tunnels," starting from the shaft, are bored across these seams and "gangways" are bored into the seams themselves. number of strikes in different parts of the country. They were most serious in times of business depression, such as the years between 1892 and 1896. Then occurred the great strike of steel workers in the Carnegie mills at Homestead, Pennsylvania, and the strike of car builders and railroad men at Pullman and Chicago. On both occasions soldiers had to be called out to restore order.

Strikes were also frequent among the coal miners, whose work was hard and whose pay was often inadequate. 

Strikes are expensive; they mean loss of money to both employers and workmen, often hardship and suffering to the latter, and great inconvenience and uneasiness to the public at large. As time went on, both employers and labor leaders came to understand this, and efforts were made to avoid strikes and to settle all disputes about wages and working conditions by arbitration. These efforts did not always succeed — for men are often stubborn when they disagree - but progress has been made. It is a long time since there has been a great nation-wide strike. Most differences between employers and employees are now settled in friendly conference. 

Labor Organizes into Unions

The progress of the movement for a united labor class has gone steadily forward since its beginning about 1870. It is favored by the better education of workingmen, since attendance at the public schools became compulsory, by the concentration of workers in great industrial cities, by the establishment of newspapers written and edited for workingmen, and by better means of communication between members of the unions all over the country. The first great national organization of union workers was the Knights of Labor, founded about 1870. That organization has now been superseded by the American Federation of Labor which arose some ten or twelve years later. 

The purposes of these organizations, as of the labor movement generally, are all good. They aim at getting better pay for all workers and at preventing unhealthful and otherwise unsatisfactory conditions of labor. They try also to tie the workers together, so that when they have occasion to bargain with their employers for terms of pay or concern-

ing hours of work, they can act unitedly, in what is called "collective" bargaining, instead of losing their influence through division into small groups. They try also to secure laws that will protect workingmen in their rights, and that will prevent the employment of little children in industry, or the overworking of women in mills and factories. 

Labor Legislation

Labor laws have been passed in increasing numbers both by State legislatures and the National Congress. We have not room to tell you about all of them; but among others are laws limiting the hours that women and young people can be made to work; laws obliging employers to keep their factories well lighted and sanitary; laws requiring safety appliances to reduce the danger of accidents caused by machinery; and laws to establish systems of insurance against such accidents.

Industrial education has been encouraged in many States and cities; and excellent public schools have been established where boys and girls can learn the fundamentals of whatever trade they wish to follow. All over the United 

States people have come to understand that it is the duty of our Government to protect the wage-earners against the dangers of ignorance and injustice.

Labor is Patriotic in the United States It is interesting to observe that here in the United States the labor organizations have been almost invariably attached to the principles on which our Government was founded. In Europe such organizations have often, perhaps usually, fallen into the hands of Socialists, and even Communists, and have threatened at times to overthrow the governments and the social systems of the nations in which they exist. In this country the opposite has been the

case.

The Federation of Labor has been the firm supporter of our form of government - the democratic republic and it has gained its ends, not by defying the Government, but by using the means our political system offers to voters to bring about whatever changes or improvements they desire.

Outline Summary 

1. Agricultural problems (a) New States admitted, 1864-1896 (b) Difficulties, financial and economic, of the farmers (c) The Granger movement and the Farmers' Alliance (d) The Greenback movement, strong in the West (e) The "free-silver" movement strong in the West and South 

2. Problems in transportation (a) Abuses in railway-building (b) Loose and unfair practices in railway management (c) The Interstate Commerce Commission brings order out of chaos 

3. Tariff problems (a) Increasingly protective character of the tariff (b) The Democrats as a "tariff for revenue" party (c) Protection becomes established as the economic policy of the Nation 

4. Problems of labor (a) No labor class one hundred years ago (b) Rise of a wage-earning class as a result of industrial progress (c) Labor fights its battles against the employers by means of strikes (d) Labor organizes the Knights of Labor, and the American Federation of Labor (e) Labor and industrial legislation (f) Patriotic character of American labor organizations Questions

UNIT XVIII , 1885-1900 

OOTH in politics and finance the United States had some troublesome situations to meet between 1865 

Boome troublesome to meet between 18ag BOTH and 1900. 

Let us take the political questions first. We have already read of the long and difficult conflict over the reconstruction of the South. That was the most dramatic political issue of the time; but there was another problem that had to be met — that of political corruption. 

The Spoils System

The "spoils system" was firmly entrenched in American politics before the Civil War. That system meant the turning out of office of almost every Government official from the highest to the lowest whenever a President of a different party was elected. "To the victors belong the spoils," one politician declared in defense of this practice; while others argued that “rotation in office" was the only fair thing in a democracy, where there ought to be no official class, and where every citizen might hope to have his turn in Government service.

Of course rotation in office meant that much of the time the Government was in the hands of newly appointed officers, unfamiliar with their duties and often without any qualifications except fidelity to their political party.

In the years following the Civil War, there was much corruption as well as incompetence among the office-holders. 

It was a time when money was being made easily and quickly. Many of the newly rich promoters and speculators were willing to pay for favors from the Government,

city, state, or national, and many politicians found it hard to resist the opportunity of making money dishonestly. 

While General Grant was President (1869-1877) cor ruption spread like an infectious disease through Washington. The President himself was scrupulously honest, but he failed to see through some of the men about him, and although he was an able soldier, he was not well fitted to deal with shrewd and cunning politicians.

There were scandals in the Treasury Department, where the officials permitted whiskey manufacturers to evade the payment of the excise tax; in the Post-Office Department, where excessive payments were made to contractors who carried the mail over certain routes, and in the War Department, where the Secretary avoided impeachment only by resignation. There were scandals in Congress, too; some members were shown to have taken money or stock from railway companies that wanted certain legislation for their benefit. The popular resentment at these scandals nearly gave Tilden, the Democratic candidate for President, the victory in 1876. 

Two Ohio Presidents, Hayes and Garfield

Under President Hayes conditions were much better. An honest man himself, he surrounded himself with honest officials. He was an enemy of the spoils system, and did all he could to prevent the politicians from getting incompetent party workers into the Government service. 

In 1880, the friends of General Grant tried to procure for him another term in the Presidency. The objection of the people to a third term for any man, since Washington and Jefferson had thought two terms enough, was too strong, however. The Republican nomination finally fell to General James A. Garfield, who had fought in the Civil War and had long been a Congressman from Ohio. He was elected

over the Democratic candidate, also a Civil War veteran, General W. S. Hancock. 

President Garfield was no sooner in office than the spoils system began to give trouble. The Collectorship of New 

York was vacant. The President had his candidate whom he appointed. The Senators from New York, who belonged to another faction of the party and had worked for General Grant's nomination, insisted on their right to control the choice of office-holders in their own State. They fell out with the President and resigned.

In the midst of this quarrel, General Garfield was shot by a man named Guiteau, who proved to be a half-crazy individual, disappointed because he was not appointed to an office for which he had applied. The President was shot on 

July 2, but lingered between life and death for more than two months.

Evils of the Spoils System Recognized Garfield was succeeded by the Vice-President, Chester A. 

Arthur. The new President was one of the faction to which the New York Senators, Conkling and Platt, belonged, and 

President Hayes had removed him as Collector of the Port of New York for permitting too much political interference in his office.

It was feared, therefore, that he might prove unsatisfactory as a President; but he proved to be a dignified and able executive who showed an unexpected sympathy with efforts to reform the spoils system.

The death of President Garfield at the hands of a disappointed office-seeker had shocked the Nation and convinced it of the danger of a system that every four years turned the National Capital over to a horde of small politicians hunting for office. Public opinion obliged Congress to pass a law reforming the civil service by means of a commission, which

should hold examinations to determine whether candidates for office were intelligent enough to discharge its duties properly, and limiting appointments to those approved by the commission. Nor could a person so appointed be removed, so long as he did his work well. This law, which 

President Arthur signed in 1883, took most of the positions in the departments at Washington quite out of politics, and it has since been extended from time to time to cover a great many other positions in the civil service. 

Cleveland the Reformer President 

In 1884 the Democrats nominated for President Grover Cleveland, who as Governor of New York had won a great reputation for honesty and devotion to public duty. He was opposed by James G. Blaine, the most brilliant of Republican leaders, who had been 

Speaker of the House, Senator, and Secretary of State under 

President Garfield. Mr. Blaine was opposed by members of his own party, who thought he had been somewhat tainted by the corruption in Congress ten years before. They supported 

Cleveland on the issue of "reform," and he was elected the first Democratic President since 1861. 

GROVER CLEVELAND 

President Cleveland proved to be as good a reformer at ¹ Grover Cleveland was born in New Jersey in 1837. He became a lawyer and lived in Buffalo, New York. After filling several offices in Buffalo, including that of mayor, he was elected Governor of New York in 1882. 

He was elected President in 1884, defeated for reëlection in 1888, and again elected in 1892. He died in 1908.

Washington as at Albany. There were no political scandals during his term of office, and he carried out a thorough cleaning-up of the General Land Office. This had been loosely conducted for many years, and a great quantity of public land in the West had got into the hands of people who had no right to it.

Though he made an excellent President, Cleveland was defeated for reëlection by General Benjamin Harrison, the Republican candidate. This was because Cleveland made his campaign on the issue of a revision of the tariff laws downward; and the voters were still in favor of protection. 

Corrupt Politics in the Cities 

Corruption in politics was quite as notorious in the cities as at Washington during the late sixties and the seventies. 

It was a time of very strict party feeling, and the "bosses" who managed to get control of the party machinery could do much as they pleased, since voters rarely went over to the opposite party.

Independence in voting was further discouraged by the system of balloting then in use. Each party printed its own ballots on paper of a size or shape or color that made it easy to tell whether the voter was casting the ballot he was expected to. Thus the boss could keep a close watch on his followers, who rarely dared to vote anything else than the 'straight ticket." 66 Another element of strength for the boss lay in the immense number of immigrants, recently naturalized, who crowded the larger cities. These voters, ignorant of our politics, were likely to vote as they were told by their leaders, who were usually lieutenants of the boss. 

In this way many city governments fell into the hands of unscrupulous and dishonest men who stole or wasted the public money with impunity. The most sensational case

was that of New York City, which a ring of politicians, headed by the famous "Boss Tweed," ruled absolutely for several years. During that time millions of money were dishonestly distributed among the ring and its followers, and valuable franchises for street railways and other public utilities were sold for millions more. In the end the ring became so bold that the anger of the voters was aroused

against them. Public-spirited men - among them Samuel 

J. Tilden, later Governor of New York-set themselves to overthrow the thieves. Proof of their rascality was found and presented in court. Tweed and some of his lieutenants were convicted and sent to jail, and the ring was broken up.

The Secret Ballot Introduced 

The repeated evidences of corruption in the use of the ballot finally convinced every honest man that genuinely

secret voting was necessary. 

With that in force, the politicians who bought votes, or who tried to control the votes of their followers, could no longer be sure how any one was voting, and could not discipline any citizen who wished to vote independently.

The best system seemed to be the Australian ballot, so called because it was devised in that country. By this system the State prints all the ballots, and lists on each ballot the names of all candidates for every office. The voter makes a cross against the names of his choice, folds the ballot and drops it into the box. No one can tell for whom he has voted.

This system was introduced by law in Massachusetts in 1888. Other States have followed one by one, until secret voting after the Australian plan is now almost universal in the United States.

Financial Problems 

Financial problems occupied much of the attention of the American people during these years of which we are speaking. They began to be pressing at the very outbreak of the 

Civil War. The expense of carrying on the war was sure to be enormous; the ordinary revenue of the country would not begin to meet it. Ways and means had to be found to raise money in large amounts and at once. It

The first step was, of course, to borrow money on the notes of the Government. Then the tariff rates were raised on all commodities, in order to bring in more revenue. was through this means that the Nation first became accustomed to the idea of very high duties on imported goods; even the protective tariffs of earlier years had carried rates that seem low to us today. The excise taxes on whiskey and tobacco were increased, and new taxes levied on a variety of articles that had never been taxed before. Another source

of revenue was the income tax, which was imposed on incomes of more than $600.

All these taxes brought in money, but not fast enough. 

Expenses mounted while Congress was discussing them. 

The Government continued to borrow money from the bankers on notes and bonds until by the end of the war there were "loans bearing five different rates of interest and maturing at nineteen different periods of time." 

Still more money was needed, and finally the Government decided to issue paper money without any reserve of gold or silver behind it. These notes (called "greenbacks" because the backs were printed in green ink) were mere promises of the Government to pay at some time or other their face value; but they were made legal tender for any and all debts, and were issued in large quantities. By the end of the war greenbacks amounting to $400,000,000 were in circulation. Of course the greenbacks rose or fell in value as the credit of the Nation rose or fell; that is, according as the 

Union seemed likely or unlikely to win the war. At times they were worth only about sixty-four cents on the dollar measured in gold; and the prices of everything rose accordingly. By 1865 food stuffs, coal, shoes, clothing, and other necessities were costing more than twice as much as at the beginning of the war. 

By all these means the great sums required to fight the war were somehow raised; but when the fighting was over, the Union found itself faced with a national debt of $2,846,- 000,000, a sum which to people of that day seemed a crushing burden. 

The National Bank Act

A very important war-time measure was the National Bank Act passed in 1862. There had been no central bank in the United States since 1836, and the State banks which

were in existence when the war broke out were quite unable to carry on the financing of business satisfactorily. Moreover, there was no standard currency, for the notes of the State banks were accepted at all sorts of rates, depending on the distance from the State of issue and the credit of the individual bank that had printed them. 

The National Bank Act permitted the charter of national banks to groups of stockholders, who were to invest their capital in bonds of the United States and might then issue bank notes up to ninety per cent of the value of the bonds they owned. Later the old State banks were driven out of business or forced to become national banks by putting a tax on their note issues.

The Bank Act was carefully drawn, and it succeeded in building up a group of strong, safe banks all over the country and in providing a standard form of currency that was of equal value throughout the United States. There have been some changes in its provisions, but it is still in force; the national banks which you see in every city and large town today are chartered under it. 

Greenbacks and Specie Payments

During the war and for some years thereafter the Gov ernment suspended all specie payments, which means that it would not redeem its paper currency in gold or silver as it always does in ordinary times if requested to do so. When the greenback bills were issued, gold and silver dollars promptly disappeared from circulation because they were worth more for other purposes than they were as money. 

Even the dimes, quarters, and half-dollars followed them. 

The Government was driven to issuing paper money in place of them, too, and these little pieces of paper (called 'shin-plasters"), postage stamps, and bits of copper or nickel stamped by private business men served as small change for many years after the war.

It was not until 1879 that the Government had accumulated enough gold in the Treasury to make it safe to begin paying out coin again on demand. That is what is meant by the "resumption of specie payments." It was feared that there would be a great rush by the holders of paper money to get gold for it; but that was not the case. The credit of the United States was by this time so good that few people bothered to ask for gold money. The paper money had become "as good as gold" when it was understood that gold could really be had for the asking. 

Hard Money vs. Soft Money

At the same time, it was planned to retire the greenbacks by destroying them as fast as they were returned to the Treasury, but this was not done. The country needed them still, for the "hard money" (gold and silver) and the national bank notes were not sufficient to furnish all the currency that was needed to carry on the business of the country. 

The United States was developing very fast commercially and industrially during these years. Great enterprises were being undertaken and plenty of money was necessary to their financing. Even with the help of the greenbacks there was none too much currency in circulation. Statesmen and financiers were much occupied in trying to find means of increasing the supply of money, and of arranging it so that it would be expanded whenever more was needed in business, and diminished when the special need of it had passed.

The difficulty was to do this and still keep all money sound and worth what it purported to be worth. It is easy enough to issue plenty of money if you do not care about preserving its actual value. There were several schemes of that sort proposed. One was to keep all the greenbacks in circulation and then to issue as much more. That would

have kept them far below the value of the gold dollar as they were in war-time. They would have been "cheap money," and, as always happens in times of cheap money, prices would have risen and people who had debts to pay could have paid them in money that was of less value than that which they had borrowed.

This plan was popular in some parts of the country, particularly in the South, where most people were in debt and in financial distress, and in the West, where the farmers owed money that they had borrowed to buy, enlarge, or improve their farms. The Greenback Party was organized to elect men to Congress who would vote for this plan, and there were a good many in both the old parties who favored it too. In 1876, 1877, and 1878 the Greenback Party was strong in many of the agricultural States; but the sentiment in favor of a sound and stable currency was too strong, and after the successful resumption of specie payments and the consequent revival of business, the Greenback Party faded away. 

Gold and Silver as Money in the Early Years

A more important movement for cheap money was that which advocated the free coinage of silver. In the early years of our history gold and silver were of equal standing in our coinage. The Government would turn any amount of either metal into coins free of charge when it was presented at the Mint. The ratio between them was fifteen to one fifteen ounces of silver being estimated as worth one ounce of gold.

Under this system silver was somewhat overvalued, and so, as it was worth more as money than for commercial uses, while exactly the opposite was true in regard to gold, almost all the money coined and used was silver. In 1834 the ratio was changed and made sixteen to one. This in turn slightly

overvalued gold, and so the silver money rapidly disappeared from use and was replaced by goid or bank notes.

In 1873 Congress voted to discontinue coining silver dollars at all, because no silver was being presented at the Mint; it was more valuable for other purposes than money. 

Curiously enough, the price of silver began to fall almost immediately, owing to the great quantities of it that were being taken out of the newly discovered mines in the West. 

Within a few years it was far below the old ratio of sixteen ounces of silver to one ounce of gold. 

The Free Coinage of Silver Demanded

Those who wanted more and cheaper money were quick to see the chance offered by the fall in the price of silver.

They began to demand that the free coinage of silver at the old ratio of sixteen to one be resumed. That would mean that any one could bring silver worth sixty or sixty-five cents to the Treasury and have it coined into a dollar. The result would be just what it would have been if the Greenback Party had succeeded in having a fresh lot of paper money printed and issued. There would be an immense quantity of dollars coined, no one of which was really worth a dollar. Prices would go up at once, and debtors could pay their debts in money that was worth much less than that which they had borrowed. 

The proposal was very popular in some parts of the country in the West and South particularly. The Populist 

Party, formed about 1890 to speak for the rural population in those sections, made "free silver" one of the important planks in its platform. It had many advocates in the 

Democratic Party too, and some among the Western Republicans.

The panic of 1893 and the hard times that followed added

fresh strength to the movement, for the numbers of those who were in debt and financial distress increased. In the election of 1892, Mr. Cleveland was again elected President, defeating General Harrison as Harrison had defeated him in 1888. President Cleveland had no sympathy whatever with free silver. He was firm in his opposition to every proposal to increase the circulation of money by coining dollars that were not worth their full value, and he was able to persuade Congress to repeal the Sherman Law of July, 1890, which required the free coinage of a limited number of silver dollars every year. 

The "Sound Money" Election

By 1896 the Democratic Party was split squarely in two on the question. The Western and Southern wings of the party proved the stronger. In the national convention they pledged the party to the free coinage of silver and nominated for President William J. Bryan, an eloquent young 

Congressman from Nebraska. The Republicans nominated William McKinley,' who had been notable in Congress for his devotion to the principle of protection and had since been Governor of Ohio.

The campaign was fought wholly on the issue of free silver. Many of the Cleveland Democrats left their party, but their places were taken by Populists, who were in complete sympathy with Bryan and the Democratic platform. 

The Republicans became the party of "sound money," and had the support of all the strong business and commercial interests in the East and Middle West. The result was 

I William McKinley was born in Ohio in 1843. He left college to join the army in the Civil War and rose to the rank of Major. He became a lawyer and was a member of Congress from 1877 to 1891. Later, he was twice elected Governor of Ohio, and twice elected President (1896 and 1900). He was assassinated while President in 1901.

in doubt to the end, but "sound money" triumphed and 

McKinley was elected by a decisive majority.

It happened that almost immediately a wave of prosperity swept the country. Business improved; the prices of agricultural as well as industrial products began to rise; new discoveries of gold in Alaska and the Klondike helped to increase the stock of gold money, and so to relieve the situation. Within a few years the conditions that had led to the demand for free silver disappeared, and with them disappeared the enthusiasm for the double standard of money. In 1900, Congress passed a law that made gold the single standard on which the currency of the Nation should rest.

UNIT XIX EDUCATION, CULTURE, AND SOCIAL LIFE 

TH 1865-1900 HE changes in the manner of life of the people of the United States during the years between 1865 and 1900 were those that might have been expected in a country that was fast "growing up"; becoming less provincial, more thickly populated, and better supplied with the means and the leisure for the development of intellectual, artistic, and social interests.

Education Before and After the Civil War Very distinct advances were made in the matter of public education after the Civil War. In colonial times education had been a class affair. Few except those who were to be ministers or professional men were taught more than to read, write, and cipher; illiteracy was common. Schools improved, however, as the country developed. The foundations of our present public-school system were laid in New England, and later in New York, Ohio, New Jersey, and Pennsylvania, before the Civil War; but there was an unwillingness on the part of many taxpayers to vote the money such schools needed. In many communities there were practically no schools except small elementary schools, often under the supervision of some church, and occasional seminaries or academies, where a considerable tuition was charged.

After the Civil War the growth of the public-school system was rapid. It spread everywhere in the North and was introduced into the South where it was unknown before.

EDUCATION, CULTURE, AND SOCIAL LIFE 407 

Normal schools, previously few in number, were organized to train teachers, who had formerly had no special preparation for their work. Towns began to build substantial and even handsome buildings to house their schools, and to furnish them with new and better teaching equipment. The standard of teaching rose, and new studies were introduced. 

THE RED CROSS BUILDING, WASHINGTON, D.C. 

About 1890 the practice of furnishing textbooks to the school children free of charge at the expense of the town was introduced, and it is now general. 

High Schools Increase in Numbers

Public high schools were also organized in increasing numbers. Before the war there had been only about three hundred in the whole country. It was the common opinion then that most children did not need an education so advanced as the high school gave, and that those who wanted it should pay for it, and not expect the taxpayers to furnish it to them. But people now began to see that in a democ-

EDUCATION, CULTURE, AND SOCIAL LIFE 

racy, where nearly every one took part through his vote in the government of the country, education was not only desirable but essential; and that only through the public schools could all children, rich or poor, be assured of a chance to get that necessary education. 

At the same time people generally became more ambitious for their children, and demanded for them opportunities to get instruction in all sorts of subjects. High schools multiplied all over the country, and there are now few towns of any size where one cannot be found. 

State Universities and Agricultural Colleges Nor was the higher education in colleges and universities neglected. Even before the Civil War a number of States had established State universities as part of the educational system, and after the Civil War many more were founded, particularly in the South and West, where endowed private colleges were not so common as in the East. These universities gave instruction in a great variety of subjects, including law, medicine, engineering, and agriculture, and at a cost far below that of the older colleges. 

While the war was going on, Congress passed a law granting a certain amount of public land to each State, the income from which should be used to support schools of agriculture and mechanics. The States were quick to establish such schools, sometimes as independent institutions and sometimes as departments of the State universities. 

The influence of these schools on agriculture has been very great. They have taught better methods of farming and stock-raising, shown the farmer how to cope with plant and animal diseases, weather conditions, and insect pests, and helped to develop new varieties of plants that will bear larger and better crops.

EDUCATION, CULTURE, AND SOCIAL LIFE 409 Technical Schools and Women's Colleges 

With the growth of the great mechanical industries and the increasing demand for competent men to undertake the engineering of railway systems, waterworks, and other public improvements, the need for better technical education became apparent; and a number of excellent technical schools sprang up in different parts of the country. The 

Massachusetts Institute of Technology, the Carnegie Institute of Technology at Pittsburgh, and the Case School of 

Applied Science at Cleveland are examples; and there are many others.

The higher education of woman advanced rapidly during these years.

It had begun with Mary Lyon's School at Mount Holyoke, Massachusetts, and Emma Willard's School at 

Troy, New York, both founded about 1830; but for long there was a strong prejudice against giving girls the same advantages in collegiate education that boys had. With the establishment of the State universities the barriers began to fall. It was not long before girls were admitted to them on the same terms as boys. Then a number of excellent colleges wholly for women were established, Vassar during the Civil War, Wellesley and Smith soon after, and more and more as time went on. 

CLARA BARTON 

A great many of the smaller private colleges, particularly in the West, admit women on exactly the same terms as men. Coeducation is the rule in more than half the colleges of the country, and the right of a girl to get any amount of education of which she is capable is no longer questioned by any one.

EDUCATION, CULTURE, AND SOCIAL LIFE Libraries and Museums 

The period about which you are reading was remarkable also for the general establishment of public libraries all over the country, and of museums of art in many of the principal cities. The effect of these institutions on the culture of the 

Nation has been very marked. Reading, which was once the habit of only a few, has become universal, and the appreciation of the beautiful in art, confined to a very small circle of educated people in the days before the Civil War, is now taught in the schools and is widespread among Americans of all classes. 

In some States — Massachusetts, for example — there is no town without its public library; and there are more than ten thousand public libraries in the United States. 

One of the ways in which Andrew Carnegie spent the enormous fortune he made in steel was to give money for the erection of library buildings all over the country. 

The Growth of Newspapers 

Newspapers grew notably in number, size, and importance during the closing years of the nineteenth century. 

The practice of advertising in the newspapers became very general and brought the owners large revenues. This they used to pay the cost of collecting news from all over the world and to add a constantly increasing variety to the contents of their papers. If the people of the United States are not the best-informed people in the world, it is because they do not take advantage of all the opportunities of acquiring information that the newspapers offer them.

The New Architecture 

During the middle years of the last century art and architecture were neglected in the United States. The labor of clearing the continent and establishing a business

EDUCATION, CULTURE, AND SOCIAL LIFE 411 

and political system capable of sustaining a rapidly growing nation left Americans little time to enjoy or cultivate the graces of life. The taste shown in the buildings, public or private, of this period was deplorable, and not much can be said for the pictures that our artists painted or the statuary they designed.

A revival of interest in art followed the war. Architects like Richard Morris Hunt and H. H. Richardson arose to teach our people that buildings could be beautiful as well as substantial. They aroused the ambition and purified the taste of scores of younger men. When the city of Chicago celebrated, with its World's Fair of 1893, the four hundredth anniversary of the discoveries of Columbus, the beauty of the buildings and the landscape work gave surprising proof of the ability of American architects and helped wonderfully to educate the millions who visited the Exposition in the meaning of art.

Great Names in Art 

Painting and sculpture advanced at the same time, largely under the influence of French art. During these years 

Paris was the admitted artistic center of the world. All young men who were interested in the production of beauty found their inspiration and much of their education in the studios, schools, and museums of that city. The result was a remarkable amount of excellent painting and sculpture, and the appearance of several artists of undoubted genius.

The landscape painting of George Inness, the marine pictures of Winslow Homer, the portraits of John S. Sargent and E. A. Abbey, the unusually graceful and delicate paintings and etchings of James McN. Whistler, the varied art of John La Farge, and the splendid statuary of Augustus Saint-Gaudens are achievements of which any nation might be proud.

EDUCATION, CULTURE, AND SOCIAL LIFE 

Literature After the War

In literature we made no great improvement. The writers of the last third of the century rank no higher than those of the middle third- Longfellow, Hawthorne, Poe, Emerson, and the others of whom you read in an earlier chapter.

But there was much sound and interesting writing by men like "Mark Twain" (Samuel Clemens), W. D. Howells, Joel Chandler Harris, F. Bret Harte, and others.

It is interesting to notice that few of these authors had what can be called a wide outlook on life. Their ideas were influenced by their environment and they wrote largely about people or conditions of life in the particular part of the country in which they had spent their youth. They were, in fact, "provincial." "Mark Twain" was the most original and versatile of them all, but his most characteristic work had as its background the Mississippi River, which he knew as a boy and on which he sailed in his youth as a river pilot. Bret Harte wrote little of importance except stories of the California mining camps. Joel Chandler Harris confined himself to the tales told by the Negroes of his home in Georgia. 

Thomas Nelson Page did not venture far from his old Virginia home in his books, nor did Mary Wilkins or Sarah Orne Jewett attempt anything more than pictures of the 

New England life familiar to them.

The Changing Social Life 

The changes in the social life of the country were such as grew out of the increasing wealth of the people and their tendency to flock into the cities and large towns as industry became centered there.

For the first time there came to be a numerous wealthy class, not living on the land as the old planters of the South had done, but concentrated in the large cities. These peo-

EDUCATION, CULTURE, AND SOCIAL LIFE 413 

ple had the leisure and the inclination to amuse themselves. 

They were fond of traveling by steamship to Europe, a diversion which was quite new; for in former times few Americans ever crossed the ocean and then only when business called them. But the new steamships, swifter and more luxurious than any craft ever seen before, made travel delightful instead of dangerous and uncomfortable.

Rich people also flocked to such pleasure resorts as Newport, Saratoga Springs, and Bar Harbor, where they built beautiful summer homes and found amusement in yachting, riding, or - at Saratoga in horse-racing.

Amusements of the People 

The popular amusements of the time were different from those of an earlier age. The theater still had its place in the larger cities, but people everywhere enjoyed the Negro minstrel shows, in which singing, dancing, and farce were intermingled. The performers were usually white men with blackened faces. The traveling circus, with its animal show, its clowns, and its acrobats was even more popular; millions of men, women, and children looked forward to the annual visit of the circus with delight. 

Outdoor sports became popular too. The game of baseball spread like wildfire over the country. Every village, every school, had its nine, and many baseball clubs were formed. Thousands attended the games played in the cities by teams of professionals. Lawn tennis was introduced from England and later golf from Scotland, and both games found a cordial welcome. 

In the eighties, the bicycle was invented- or rather perfected and for a number of years the streets and roads were full of boys and girls and even men and women pedaling cheerfully about their errands of business or pleasure. 

Almost every one was a-wheel; but the introduction of the

EDUCATION, CULTURE, AND SOCIAL LIFE 

automobile about 1900 put an end to the popularity of the bicycle.

The Women Begin to Organize 

During the years from 1865 to 1900 women came to take a much more conspicuous part in activities outside the home than in the past. They interested themselves in all sorts of charitable and social welfare work. They formed clubs for reading and study, and often came to be better informed and more cultivated than their husbands. They organized themselves to work for all kinds of reform and social betterment. 

One of the strongest and most useful of these organizations of women was the Woman's Christian Temperance Union. Under the lead of Frances 

Willard this association spread all over the United States, and was very effective in encouraging temperance and 

FRANCES WILLARD arousing public opinion against the evils of the traffic in liquor. Another was the Red Cross, which devotes itself to the work of relieving suffering and want, both in time of war and when floods or droughts or fires or earthquakes cause hardship and sickness. Of course men as well as women work for the Red 

Cross, but its most active organizer and first president was Clara Barton, who had already an honorable record as a war nurse both in our own Civil War and in the war between 

France and Germany in 1870.

Women were deeply interested, too, in work among the needy and the ignorant in the poorer quarters of our great cities. They established "settlement houses" in those dreary streets, and busied themselves in nursing the sick

EDUCATION, CULTURE, AND SOCIAL LIFE 415 

who could not afford other help, in caring for little children in day nurseries and kindergartens, in teaching older persons to speak and write English, in maintaining clubs and gymnasiums for boys and girls and cooking and housekeeping classes for the women. The most famous of these useful institutions is Hull House in Chicago, which was established and managed by a very remarkable woman, Jane Addams.

The movement to gain political rights and a greater degree of legal independence for women also began to gain momentum. For many years a few "advanced" women, like Susan 

B. Anthony, Julia Ward Howe, and 

Elizabeth Cady Stanton, had tried to interest their sex in the cause of votes for women. They had little enough success at first, but gradually they made headway. By the end of this period, women were awakening to the 

ELIZABETH CADY STANTON 

desirability of changing laws that withheld from them rights that men enjoyed, and beginning in considerable numbers to demand the suffrage. 

Men's Organizations

Among the men, too, social organizations increased rapidly. Fraternities, usually secret in character, and often accompanied by elaborate rituals and made attractive by arrangements for mutual insurance or other financial benefits, grew up and numbered their members by the million. 

Patriotic orders flourished, also; the Sons of the Revolution, the Society of Mayflower Descendants, and the Grand Army of the Republic (made up of veterans of the Civil War) are examples of this kind of organization. There were

EDUCATION, CULTURE, AND SOCIAL LIFE 

many others, and they all had an excellent effect in stimulating an interest in the history of the country and a devotion to the principles on which it was established. 

Finally civic organizations, associations of business men, of workingmen, of citizens interested in the improvement and beautification of their home towns, or in the reform of social and governmental abuses, were established on every hand. The passion for "organization," which foreigners find to be characteristic of Americans, declared itself very conspicuously during these closing years of the nineteenth century. The people of the United States, feeling at last that the Nation was in no danger of division and sure of a great future, were setting themselves to forget sectional differences, to know one another better, and to prepare for unified effort in a hundred ways toward making the country a better place in which to live.

Activities Ask some elderly man or woman to talk to the class about the schools of fifty years ago, as he or she remembers them.

Collect pictures of famous buildings - for example Trinity 

Church, Boston (Richardson); the State capitols at Albany, N.Y., and Lincoln, Nebraska, the World's Fair buildings, great New 

York skyscrapers, the Lincoln Memorial in Washington, and so on. Study them in class and show why each is worthy of admiration.

UNIT XX THE UNITED STATES BECOMES A WORLD 

POWER 

E are so used to thinking of the United States as one of the greatest of world powers that we do not always realize how recently it became so. For many years after the Constitution was adopted, our country was small in population and wealth as compared with the leading nations of Europe. The early statesmen understood that this was so, and bent their efforts to avoid any entanglement in world politics. They had a proper fear of being drawn into the quarrels of stronger nations, since that might lead to wars that would threaten the safety of the country and the success of our experiment in democratic government. 

Views of Washington, Jefferson, and Monroe 

Washington, you will remember, would not take sides in the wars that accompanied the French Revolution, and in his Farewell Address urged his countrymen to be careful not to become involved with any foreign nation as an ally. Jefferson repeated Washington's warning against “entangling alliances." His chief reason for buying the great territory of Louisiana was to get rid of either France or Spain as a near neighbor. Monroe, in enumerating the principles of the "Monroe Doctrine," had in mind the necessity of keeping the European nations at a distance while America developed in its own way, neither interfering in European politics nor permitting itself to be interfered with. 

We shall now follow the course of events by which the United States, almost in spite of itself, came to be recognized as one of the world powers, no longer isolated between

its two oceans, and indifferent to the political affairs of the rest of the world. The underlying cause was, of course, the remarkable growth of the country in wealth and population; for no nation can become great in man-power and moneypower, in commerce and industry without being drawn by interest and necessity into close and constant relations with other nations everywhere.

Purchase of Alaska 

The first acquisition of territory outside our own broad band of country between the Atlantic and the Pacific came WASH.

ALASKA COMPARED IN AREA WITH THE UNITED STATES 

in 1867, when Alaska was purchased from Russia for $7,200,000. Russia had occupied this region of 600,000 square miles as part of its conquest of Siberia in the eighteenth century. It was in North America, to be sure, but only across the narrow Behring Straits from the Russian possessions in Asia, and as no one else had any claim upon it, the Russians had seized it.

In course of time, however, Russia became tired of being responsible for Alaska. It was too far from home to be defended if any other nation should covet it, and it had never paid for the trouble of occupying. So Russia was glad to sell it to us, and Mr. Seward, who was then Secretary of 

State, hastened to buy it.

At that time many Americans thought it a foolish thing to do, for Alaska was supposed to be a frozen, barren waste, valueless except for a few sealskins taken on its islands. 

Time has proved it much more valuable than that. A good deal of the land can be farmed successfully, and the United States has profited through traffic in furs, fish, timber, and gold far beyond any expectations based on knowledge of the country at the time of the purchase. There are large coalfields in Alaska, too, which will sometime be of very great value. 

American Interest in Cuba

The eyes of Americans - particularly Southerners had also been turned toward some of the fertile islands of the West Indies. As early as 1854 some of the leading statesmen had suggested that it would be desirable to annex Cuba, an island belonging to Spain, against which it was in more or less continual revolt. The slaveholders thought that the annexation of Cuba would greatly strengthen the institution of slavery. The Civil War interfered with any plans of that sort, however. 

In 1870, while General Grant was President, he negotiated a treaty with the Government of Santo Domingo, another West Indian island that had in turn been the property of Spain and France, with a view to its annexation by the United States. But Congress would not agree, and the negotiations had to be broken off, much to General Grant's disappointment.

The Cuban Revolution 

It was twenty-five years later that events brought about our intervention in the West Indies. Since 1895 a rebellion against the authority of Spain had been going on in Cuba. 

This oldest of the Spanish colonies in the New World, and (with Porto Rico) the last remaining Spanish possession there, was constantly misgoverned and exploited by the mother country. For forty years revolts had been frequent, though never successful.

Americans knew enough about what was going on in Cuba to be deeply sympathetic with the Cubans. Stories of the cruelty of the Spanish governors and soldiers in the fight against the rebels aroused widespread indignation in this country. A great many adventurous young Americans went to Cuba to take service with the rebels; and there was a strong feeling that the United States ought to interfere to bring about Cuban independence. "Remember the Maine"

President McKinley, who was then in the White House, protested again and again to the Spanish Government against the conduct of its military officers, who were laying waste the island and behaving with the utmost severity toward rebels and peaceable folk alike. He could get little satisfaction from Spain, but he did not want to involve the United States in war, and tried as hard as he could to avoid it. He might have succeeded if the United States battleship Maine, sent on a friendly mission to Cuba, had not been blown up in Havana Harbor on the night of February 15, 1898. Most of her officers and crew were lost with the ship.

It has never been satisfactorily determined what caused the explosion; but at the time the conviction was general in the United States that it was a Spanish plot, and that a

mine had been exploded against the side of the Maine. 

The country boiled with excitement. A declaration of war was demanded at once. "Remember the Maine" became the battle-cry of an outraged nation. 

War Declared with Spain

The President and Congress yielded to the popular clamor. Congress passed a resolution declaring that Cuba of right ought to be free and independent, and promising our aid to the revolutionists unless Spain at once withdrew its armies from the island. Spain refused to do so, and on 

April 25, Congress declared war.

The first hostilities occurred, not in or near Cuba, but quite at the other end of the world. Among the East Indies, off the coast of Asia, lie the Philippine Islands. This group of rich islands also belonged to Spain, and as in the case of Cuba, the inhabitants were in revolt. It happened that our Asiatic squadron, made up of several small cruisers commanded by Commodore George Dewey, was lying in Hongkong Harbor, about seven hundred miles from the 

Philippines, when war was declared. 

The Victory at Manila

By order of the Navy Department, Dewey set sail at once for Manila, where a Spanish naval squadron was stationed. 

He lost no time, but pushed boldly into Manila Harbor in defiance of possible mines and torpedoes, and engaged the Spanish ships. In a few hours his guns had battered their way to victory. The Spaniards surrendered with the loss of most of their ships and some four hundred men killed. 

Only seven Americans were wounded, and none of our ships were badly damaged. This battle was fought on May 1, 1898.

Dewey's victory aroused great enthusiasm in the United

States. Congress made him a rear-admiral, and soon after an admiral, and dispatched twenty thousand soldiers under General Merritt to help him capture and hold the city of Manila. When the American army arrived, there was a brief, but lively campaign. The Spanish troops fought bravely, but they were outnumbered. On August 13,

Manila, the capital of the Philippines, fell, and with it passed the control of the entire archipelago. Spain had lost its last possession in the Orient. 

Fighting in Cuba

Meanwhile, the campaign in Cuba was going forward. 

Together with our regular army, which was small but efficient, we had the services of some two hundred thousand militia and volunteers who had joined the colors; but only a small part of these men ever reached the shores of Cuba. 

The War Department had no prepared plan of campaign, and it was uncertain just where troops would first be landed; but the question was decided by the news that Admiral Cervera, at the head of a Spanish squadron of six large cruisers, had evaded the naval blockade we had begun to establish on the Cuban coast and reached safely the harbor of Santiago, on the southeastern shore of the island. 

Our own fighting fleet, under Admiral W. T. Sampson, with Commodore W. S. Schley second in command, hurried thither and formed a strong blockade round the narrow entrance to the harbor. At the same time General Shafter was ordered to transport his troops to Santiago and coöperate with the navy in capturing the city and the Spanish ships. 

The Rough Riders at San Juan

It was well into June before Shafter had his men landed and ready to advance. Once ashore they showed pluck and

daring, advancing through a very difficult jungle country to the Spanish positions around Santiago, which they took
