one after another in a series of small but exceedingly lively engagements.

Next to the regulars, who fought bravely as always, the brunt of the fighting fell on the volunteer cavalry, called popularly the "Rough Riders." This regiment, made up of Western cowboys and young college men, was raised by 

Theodore Roosevelt, a young New Yorker who had already made his mark in public life. He was its lieutenant-colonel and later its colonel, after its first commander, Leonard Wood, had received promotion.

The Naval Battle of Santiago 

The victories of the Americans at Siboney, El Caney, and San Juan Hill convinced the Spanish commander that he 

I Theodore Roosevelt was born in New York City in 1858. He graduated at Harvard in 1880, and was a member of the New York legislature. 

He was appointed United States Civil Service Commissioner, Commissioner of Police for New York City, and Assistant Secretary of the Navy, before he led his regiment of Rough Riders in the Spanish-American War. 

In 1898, he was elected Governor of New York and in 1900, Vice-President. 

He became President on McKinley's death in 1901 and was elected President in 1904. After leaving the Presidency, he traveled in Africa and in the Brazilian wilderness. In 1912, he was the Progressive candidate for 

President. He advocated the entrance of the United States into the World 

War as early as 1915. He died in 1919.

could not hold the city; so Admiral Cervera was advised to take his squadron out and try to break through or avoid the American squadron, which had been strengthened by the arrival of the battleship Oregon. The cruise of this ship all the way from the Pacific Coast, around the end of South 

America and up to the West Indies, had been breathlessly watched by the Nation, and when it joined Admiral Sampson's fleet the rejoicing was great.

As a matter of fact, Cervera was in no condition to fight; for his ships, neglected and ill-equipped by an incompetent Spanish Government, were hopelessly inferior to the American fleet. Out of Santiago Harbor he came, nevertheless, on July 3. Once out he turned westward in a desperate attempt to escape; but the American ships closed in, pursued him relentlessly, and by their gunfire drove one ship after another ashore until the whole Spanish squadron was destroyed.

Within a fortnight Santiago surrendered, which was fortunate; for the American army, riddled with malaria, typhoid, and tropical fever, was no longer the army of El Caney and San Juan, and would have found it hard to maintain the siege much longer. 

Another American force had meanwhile been dispatched under General Miles to occupy Porto Rico, which it did with no serious opposition. Already the Spanish Government, without resources to carry on the war any longer, was negotiating for peace, and on August 12, the armistice was signed.

Peace Brings us New Territories 

The treaty of peace, signed in December, surrendered Cuba to Cubans; but ceded Porto Rico, the Philippines, and the little island of Guam in the mid-Pacific to the United States in return for $20,000,000 paid by us to Spain. Thus

the United States became possessed of two important colonial outposts, one of them six thousand miles distant, on the very coasts of Asia, and both inhabited by people quite unlike Americans in race, language, habits, and social organization. It was to most people a quite unexpected result of the war, and to many an experiment of very doubtful wisdom.

The step was taken because President McKinley and his advisers felt that Porto Rico must not be allowed to fall into the hands of any other European nation, now that Spain had lost its hold on it; and that the Philippines would be unable to maintain their independence if it should be granted to them.

The Philippine Rebellion 

The people of Porto Rico submitted very willingly to the rule of the United States; but the Filipinos resisted it, or at least a great number of them did. Their leader was 

General Emilio Aguinaldo, who had been in revolt against the Government of Spain in earlier years. It took the 

American army several years to suppress the rebellion and restore order in the archipelago; for among the forests and jungles of that tropical country, it was not easy to force the Filipinos into a decisive battle, or to find the roving bands that made up their forces. In the end, however, Aguinaldo was captured by General Funston, and the revolt came to an end.

Helping Cuba 

Our troops also remained in Cuba for several years, under the command of General Wood, who was appointed governor of the island until a republican form of government should be established. During those years we were of great help to the Cubans, restoring order, cleaning up the

ruined towns, and teaching the inhabitants the principles of sanitation and local self-government. When, in 1902, the Cuban Republic was at length organized, our soldiers withdrew, leaving the island a healthier and more orderly place than it had ever been before. 

We Annex Hawaii and Part of Samoa 

At the same time that we acquired the Philippines we also got possession of two other groups of islands in the 

Pacific, the Hawaiian or Sandwich Islands, and a part of the Samoan group. Neither came to us as a direct consequence of the war with Spain, though the strategic value of the Hawaiian Islands in the middle of the Pacific was of especial importance to us after we had occupied the Philippines. 

There were many Americans already living in Hawaii,

TYPICAL PHILIPPINE SCHOOL 

Before the American Occupation the descendants of missionaries, or merchants, or sailors who had gone there in earlier years. These Americans had overthrown the old Hawaiian monarchy and established a little republic of their own in 1893. At that time they had asked to be annexed to the United States, but President 

Cleveland would not consent. President McKinley had new reasons for agreeing to their request, and in 1898 the islands became one of our possessions. Tutuila and a few smaller islands of the Samoan group were annexed in 1899. 

World Power and "Imperialism"

Thus suddenly and unexpectedly the United States became a world power, with possessions widely separated from

it by leagues of salt water; possessions, moreover, which it seemed certain could not for a long time, if ever, become States in the Union. As we have said, many people objected to our keeping the Philippines. These people were called "anti-imperialists," because they said that the Nation had embarked on a career of imperialism, or the government of subject peoples, contrary to all its traditions and to the true principles of a democratic republic. Former 

President Cleveland was an anti-imperialist; so were a majority of the Democratic Party and not a few who had always been Republicans.

Imperialism was really the leading issue in the election of 1900, in which McKinley and Bryan were again the candidates. President McKinley was reëlected. With him, Theodore Roosevelt was elected as Vice-President. Roosevelt had been Governor of New York since his return from military service in Cuba.

Our Policy Toward the New Possessions The policy of the United States toward its new possessions was intelligent and kindly.

PHILIPPINE SCHOOL 

Of the type built by the United States 

Every effort was made to win the good will of Porto 

Ricans and Filipinos. A great deal of money was spent in building schools and supplying them with 

American teachers, for the mass of the people both in 

Porto Rico and the Philippines were unable to read or write. 

Doctors and sanitary en- gineers were dispatched, too, and under their instruction the homes of the people and the streets of the cities were

made clean and healthful. Many tropical diseases, which are really the result of uncleanliness and ignorance about infection, almost wholly disappeared. 

Trade, especially with the United States, was encouraged, and the islands came to enjoy a prosperity they had never known, and a degree of order and good government to which they were quite unaccustomed. Steps were taken also to instruct the natives in the principles of self-government, and little by little, as they proved able, the direction of local affairs was put into their hands, and legislatures were established, which made most of the laws, subject, however, to a veto power in the hands of the governor appointed from Washington.

Problems of the New Century The United States, then, faced the twentieth century with a new spirit and under new conditions both at home and abroad. We had settled the chief of our domestic problems and had attained a unity of thought and feeling unknown since the days when the question of slavery had divided us into two hostile sections. We were no longer an isolated nation in the heart of the continent, but a world power, with its navy on every sea, and a voice in the political future of Asia as well as America. We had taken upon ourselves the duty of guiding and caring for millions of backward peoples, who must be our wards and not our fellow citizens. We had assumed responsibilities for the peace and order of distant parts of the world. 

The anti-imperialists were the defenders of the old traditions and policies of the Republic. Those traditions and those policies we seemed suddenly and inevitably to have outgrown. Much of our history since 1900 has been the story of the continued expansion of our world power, an expansion we have not so much willed, as found ourselves unable to escape.

Assassination of McKinley 

Within a few months of President McKinley's second inauguration, while visiting a great exposition at Buffalo, New York, he was shot by a half-crazed anarchist named

Czolgosz. A few days later - September 19, 1901 - he died, and Theodore Roosevelt became President. Mr. 

Roosevelt, who was only forty-three, was the youngest man who had ever filled the presidential office. He was in complete sympathy with the new outlook of the United States and its wider participation in world affairs. He proved this by the energy with which he pushed forward the building of the Panama Canal.

The Panama Canal 

For many years the project of uniting the Atlantic and Pacific Oceans by a ship canal across the narrow Isthmus of Panama had fascinated statesmen and engineers. It had even been suggested as a possibility by Samuel de Champlain as early as 1600.

In 1881 a French company headed by Ferdinand de Lesseps, who had built the Suez Canal between the Mediterranean and the Red Seas, a few years before, undertook the task of digging a canal at Panama. A great deal of work was done, but the labor proved more costly and difficult than had been expected, and the workmen died like flies from the deadly tropical fevers to which they were exposed. The company finally became bankrupt. 

The experience of de Lesseps led people to believe that it was impossible to build a canal in so unhealthy a spot as the Isthmus of Panama. It was suggested that it would be much better to locate the canal farther north across Nicaragua, where use could be made of the San Juan River and Lake Nicaragua. An old treaty with Great Britain provided that neither country should ever exercise any exclusive

We Undertake to Build the Canal President Roosevelt felt that a canal ought to be built, and the country thought so too. The voyage of the 

Oregon during the early days of the Spanish War had shown how inconvenient and dangerous it was to have our naval forces in the two oceans so separated that it took a month or more for one fleet to reach the other in case of emergency. The President, however, did not intend to build a canal unless it was to be the property of the United 

States, and Congress agreed with him. 

Fortunately Great Britain was willing to yield her rights in the proposed canal and John Hay, our Secretary of State, negotiated a treaty that gave the United States a free hand. Then the question arose whether the canal should be built at Panama or Nicaragua. Congress could not decide and left the choice to the President, who promptly chose the Panama route. Much had lately been learned about the control of yellow fever and other tropical diseases, and the Army Medical Corps assured the President that Panama could be made a healthy place where men could work. This assurance finally determined Roosevelt's decision.

A Misunderstanding with Colombia Arrangements now had to be made with the Republic of Colombia, of which the province of Panama was a part. The 

Colombians saw a chance to drive a hard bargain with the United States, and insisted on terms that President Roosevelt thought unfair and exorbitant. He refused to agree to them and the project was apparently at a standstill. 

There occurred, however, an opportune revolt of the

people of Panama, who were eager to have the canal built, and willing, too, to get for themselves all the money the United States would pay for the right to build it. Roosevelt used the naval power of our country to prevent Colombia from suppressing the rebellion; the independent Republic of Panama was formed, and we at once entered into 

GAILLARD CUT, PANAMA CANAL 

fresh negotiations with its government, which were immediately successful. The treaty was signed on November 18, 1903. 

How the Canal was Built 

Work on the canal soon began. The Medical Corps of the army was as good as its word. Under the able direction of Colonel Gorgas, the diseases that had wiped out the laborers on the French Canal were not only controlled, but virtually banished. Not more than a score of cases of yellow fever occurred during all the years the canal was building. 

The death rate and the sickness rate among the jungles of Panama were below those of the most healthful part of the United States. It was a remarkable triumph for organized sanitation, and for the scientific treatment of tropical diseases.

The construction work on the canal was carried out by the Engineer Corps of the army under the command of Colonel Goethals, who was quite as competent for his task as Colonel Gorgas was for his. There were great difficulties to be overcome, but one by one they were met and conquered.

TORTOLA 1. 

Bique Bay

CEAN PACIF First an enormous dam was built to impound the waters of the Chagres River, and form a lake deep enough to float the largest ships. This lake reached back to the line of hills that forms the backbone of the isthmus. These hills, four or five hundred feet in height, had to be cut through.

Huge steam shovels were used for the excavation; but the rock proved to be slanted at such an angle that the earth on top of it was constantly sliding down into the canal bed. 

The banks had to be cut back much wider than had been planned and that made the undertaking more costly than was expected. Colonel Goethals, however, kept patiently at work until he had a waterway the banks of which would not slip.

At either end of the canal great locks had to be built of masonry and concrete, for the canal level is about eightyfive feet above sea level, and ships have to be raised that distance by means of a series of locks. These locks had to be large enough to accommodate the largest battleship, which means that each must be almost a thousand feet long. 

There are three at either end of the canal. 

What the Canal Cost 

It took about ten years to finish the canal, and approximately $400,000,000 were spent on it. There are fortifications at Gatun at the Atlantic end, and at Balboa at the Pacific end to protect the canal from being destroyed by an enemy in time of war. A strip of land several miles wide on either side of the waterway has been granted by the Republic of Panama to the United States and it is part of our national territory. It is called the Canal Zone. 

Value of the Canal to the United States

Of course the Panama Canal is of the greatest military value to the United States, since it would bring our naval forces on the two oceans into close touch, in case of war. 

Commercially it has been even more serviceable than was expected. It is already so much used by ships of all nations that plans are being considered to build another canal, this time at Nicaragua, in order to relieve the Panama Canal

from the constantly increasing traffic. A great volume of freight passes through it from our Eastern cities to the west coast of South America and our own Pacific Coast. Trade over both these routes has been greatly stimulated. The 

Mississippi Valley has profited less, because the Mississippi River has not yet been developed for the use of seagoing vessels above New Orleans. When that is done the river ports in the heart of the continent should find the canal very useful to them also. 

The "Free Tolls" Controversy

When the canal was opened there was a great discussion about the tolls that should be charged. Many Americans 

Courtesy of Isthmian Canal Commission

GATUN LOCKS, PANAMA CANAL 

thought that "coastwise" traffic between one port of the United States and another ought to pass through the canal free, or at least for a lower rate than that which foreign ships had to pay. Congress even passed a law to that effect in 

1914; but Woodrow Wilson, who was President at the time, insisted that good faith required that the canal should be open to all shipping on equal terms. He carried his point and Congress repealed the law it had made.

A Great Achievement 

The building of this great canal is one of the most important events in the history of the country. It was an achievement in sanitation and in engineering of which any nation might well be proud. It was a useful service to international trade, for it brought European ports far closer in point of time to the South American ports in the Pacific and to our own Western harbors, and opened a new route to Australia and the Orient. It was a long step also toward extending our influence in the Caribbean Sea and upon the nations surrounding it. It is in this direction that some of our most striking advances as a world power have been made. 

The Treaty of Portsmouth

Before passing on to look at the problems in our domestic affairs and our relations with our neighbors in America that marked the opening years of the twentieth century, there should be mentioned twò interesting episodes connected with our emergence as a world power.

We have read of the enthusiasm with which President Roosevelt welcomed our entrance into world politics and of his energy in putting through the Panama Canal. He showed his interest in making good our new place in the world in two other ways.

In 1904, Russia and Japan went to war over their respective claims to the control of Manchuria in Asia. The war was a bloody one; several great land battles and one naval battle were fought. The Japanese were getting the best of it when President Roosevelt took the unusual step of offering to help in bringing about peace. Both nations accepted, and the final meeting of the Japanese and Russian peace commissioners was actually held in the summer of 1905 on American soil, at the United States Navy Yard at

Portsmouth, New Hampshire. For this service Roosevelt was later awarded the famous Nobel Prize, given each year to some one who has done something important to advance peace.

Our Battleships Circle the World 

Two years later, in 1907, the President decided to send our great fleet of sixteen battleships on a trip around the world, to visit the harbors of all the maritime countries. 

He did not do this in a boastful spirit, but with the desire to acquaint nations all over the globe with the fact of our naval strength and our ability to sustain, if called upon, our new place as a world power. The fleet made a voyage of forty thousand miles, the longest ever undertaken by so powerful a company of ships.

UNIT XXI THE UNITED STATES AND ITS SOUTHERN NEIGHBORS 

N this unit we shall read about the way in which the United States has got on with its many neighbors to the southward - the people of Mexico, Central America, the West Indies, and all the republics of South America. 

Although these people live on the same side of the world with us, we had only occasional and not very close relations with them during our early history as a nation; but year by year we have found ourselves drawn nearer and nearer to them, first to Mexico, and then, since our war with Spain and our acquisition of possessions in the Caribbean Sea, to all the others. 

Our Policy in the Caribbean Sea

After the war with Spain, we established something like a protectorate over the infant Republic of Cuba. There was to be no interference whatever, so long as its experiment in self-government went smoothly, but the United States kept, and Cuba conceded, the right to intervene in case disorder arose, with which the Cuban Government could not deal. We also kept certain coaling stations for our fleet; especially at Guantanamo Bay, where we maintain a naval station that would be of the greatest importance for the protection of the Panama Canal, in case of war. 

At various times, our Government has thought it necessary to set up temporary protectorates here and there in. the Caribbean area, to keep order or to restore financial conditions that had broken down. This has usually been done with the excuse of maintaining the principles of the

Monroe Doctrine; for when a little Central American or 

West Indian state becomes bankrupt, or a revolution, attended by destruction of property, breaks out, it is almost certain that citizens of one or more European countries will have claims of damages to be settled. We are and always have been unwilling to see European naval expeditions come to this side of the Atlantic to enforce the collection of such claims. To avoid that we have ourselves stepped in to keep order and to help find the money to discharge any debts owed to foreign countries. 

Protectorates and Receiverships

The process has been for American bankers to advance the money, and for agents of our Government to take charge of the collection of customs and taxes to see that they were honestly used to pay both the necessary expenses of government and the money advanced by the bankers. These "receiverships" have been uniformly successful, though many Americans object to them as "imperialistic" interferences with other nations' affairs.

It was during the Roosevelt Administration in 1905, that this policy was inaugurated. Santo Domingo was the first little republic to be helped out of its difficulties. Soon after we performed the same service for Nicaragua and Honduras in Central America. In 1915 our marines and financial agents were called to Haiti to restore order and set the Government on its financial feet, and they have been there almost ever since that time. A second visit was made to Nicaragua to quiet a revolution that was threatening to destroy the Government. 

Nicaragua and the Virgin Islands

We have always felt a special interest in Nicaragua, because the time may come when another interoceanic canal

will have to be built across that country. In anticipation of that event, we made a treaty with Nicaragua, as long ago as 1914, by which, in return for $3,000,000, we acquired the sole right to build a canal in Nicaraguan territory. We also got control of several islands off its shores, which would be valuable points of defense if the canal were ever constructed.

Our strategic position in the Caribbean was further strengthened by the purchase from Denmark of its West Indian Islands. This little group, the most important island of which is St. Thomas, is called the Virgin Islands. 

Twice before, once in 1865 and again in 1902, we had offered to buy them, but Denmark refused to sell. It was in 1917, while the World War was in progress, that the Danes changed their minds and parted with the islands in return for $25,000,000. The Virgins are of no particular value commercially, but they command the trade routes from Europe to Panama, and can easily be fortified. 

What Our Policy is, and What Others Think of It 

Not only have we been interested in extending our power in the Caribbean; we have also always been ready to offer our help in settling disputes between the republics of Central America. In this way we brought about a peaceful agreement between Panama and Costa Rica where trouble arose over a disputed boundary line. We also assisted in settling a quarrel that Costa Rica and San Salvador had with

Nicaragua over the action of the latter country in granting us the right to build a canal over its territory without consulting them. Finally we came to a friendly understanding with Colombia, which was still resentful over the loss of Panama, and paid her a sum of money to compensate her for it.

It is fair to say that in all these affairs we have tried to

act with consideration toward our weaker neighbors, and that we have often done them some very real service. At the same time it must be admitted that we have sometimes blundered, through not understanding the thoughts and feelings of the people of Latin America. The Central 

American republics, too, jealous of their independence, have often been suspicious of our motives and resentful of 

HALL OF FLAGS, PAN-AMERICAN BUILDING 

our intervention. 

That is probably inevitable, for the

United States is so much larger and more powerful, that they are inclined to fear lest it will sometimes use its power perhaps to harm them, perhaps to annex them.

We need not say that our country has no remote intention of doing either of those things, and many of the more intelligent people of Central America understand us and trust us. 

Relations with South America 

Our relations with the South American republics have generally been friendly and often cordial. When, in the early years of the nineteenth century, they won their independence from Spain under the leadership of Bolívar and

San Martín, the sympathy of the United States was immediate and warm. Henry Clay expressed it so eloquently that he became the idol of the South Americans. 

The Monroe Doctrine at first made us friends in South 

America; Brazilians, Chileans, and Argentinians were not yet quite confident of their newly won freedom, and they appreciated our announcement to the world that we should permit no foreign nation to threaten America.

Little by little, as the Doctrine seemed to be extended by our statesmen to furnish an excuse for exercising a sort of benevolent, "big brother" protectorate over some of the

Caribbean countries, the South Americans became doubtful whether the Doctrine were a good thing after all. By this time they had grown strong enough to feel perfectly able to take care of themselves, and there is a party in almost every South American republic which feels that we are more dangerous to their country's complete independence than any European power is. However, we have never intended to apply the Doctrine in such a way as to offend a South American Government, and there is no wish on our part to do so.

The Monroe Doctrine in Venezuela 

In 1895, while Grover Cleveland was president, the

United States came to the support of the Republic of Venezuela in a boundary dispute between that nation and Great Britain, whose colony of Guiana adjoins Venezuela. 

We had already been asked by Venezuela to use our good offices to persuade Great Britain to arbitrate. The Secretary of State, Richard Olney, undertook the task, and in the course of his correspondence with the British Foreign Minister asserted our special interest under the Monroe 

Doctrine in the difficulties of our Southern neighbors.

The British Government denied that we had any right

to intervene and declined to arbitrate. Thereupon President Cleveland sent a message to Congress asking for authority to make a study of the dispute, for the purpose of securing the adoption of the proper boundary. The language of his message was sharp and determined. For a few days it was thought that Great Britain might take offense, and that war might ensue. But Great Britain did not wish war, or even a misunderstanding, with the United States. Her officials were instructed to give our commissioners every opportunity to study the records they wanted to examine and in the end it was proved that the British Government's side of the dispute was correct instead of that of Venezuela. 

Venezuela Again in Trouble

A few years later, in 1902, Venezuela was in trouble again. This time several European nations had claims against her for property of their citizens, destroyed in the course of one of Venezuela's frequent revolutions. Great

Britain, Germany, and Italy sent naval vessels across the water and announced that they would blockade the Venezuelan coast until the claims were paid. Roosevelt was then our President. While he admitted that the 

Monroe Doctrine did not mean that no South American nation could be punished for misconduct toward a European power, he was determined that this dispute should also be arbitrated before the international tribunal established at 

The Hague in 1899.

The Germans in particular were unwilling to do that, so the President ordered Admiral Dewey with the entire American fleet into the Caribbean and informed the German Ambassador that the United States would take a hand in the affair unless the case went to arbitration. The threat was sufficient; arbitration was accepted.

45I 

The Pan-American Congress

As early as 1881, James G. Blaine,' then our Secretary of 

State, had a vision of a plan for political and economic coöperation among all the nations of the New World. He issued invitations to a Pan-American Congress, to be held at Washington; but after the murder of President Garfield, he resigned his office and his successor recalled the invitations.

When Blaine returned to Washington, in 1889, as Secretary of State under President Harrison, he at once renewed his efforts to bring the nations of America, North and South, into some sort of friendly organization. The first 

Pan-American Congress met in Washington in 

October, 1889. Not a great deal was accomplished, but there was frank discussion of trade relations and steps were taken to bring about some changes in the tariff laws of the various countries in order to encourage mutual trade. 

Photograph by Harris & Ewing

THE PAN-AMERICAN BUILDING IN

Washington and Jefferson University, and became an editor in Augusta, Maine. After serving in the State legislature, he was sent to Congress in 

1863. He was Speaker of the House of Representatives, 1869-75, and 

Senator from Maine, 1876-81. He was twice Secretary of State, in 1881, and 1889-92. In 1884, he was the Republican candidate for President.

quarters in a beautiful building in Washington. The most important achievement of the congresses is the agreement entered into by all the nations of North and South America, to submit their disputes to arbitration instead of going to war about them. Less success has attended the effort to carry out a plan for mutual reduction of tariff duties, for the purpose of encouraging trade. However, trade has been encouraged in other ways; it is much greater now than formerly, and there is a better spirit of friendliness and confidence among the nations, owing in large part to the sessions of the Pan American Congress, where the public men of North and South America meet, consult, and form helpful friendships.

Growth of Trade with South America 

The influence of our closer relations with South America can be seen in the steady increase of our export trade to the southern republics. In 1900 it amounted to only 2.79 per cent of our entire trade; by 1920, it had grown to be 

7.58 per cent. In the same year 14 per cent of our imports came from South America. As the South American nations grow in wealth, in population, and above all in stability and freedom from revolutions, we may hope that our relations will become still closer and still more profitable to them and to us.

Our Relations with Mexico 

After all, the nation to the southward with which we have had most to do is our next-door neighbor, Mexico. You have read of our war with Mexico in 1847-48, following our annexation of Texas. That war led to the acquisition of an immense block of territory comprising the present States of California, Nevada, Utah, Arizona, and parts of 

Colorado and New Mexico.

Of course the war left behind it a great deal of hard feeling. The Mexicans were long resentful over the forced surrender of so much of their territory. But when during our 

Civil War the French Emperor, Napoleon III, used his army to oblige Mexico to accept an emperor, the Austrian Archduke Maximilian, the Mexicans had cause to appreciate our friendliness. As soon as our own war was over, we notified Napoleon that he must withdraw his troops at once. Rather than go to war, he withdrew them, Maximilian was executed, and Mexico regained her independence.

That episode restored much of the old good feeling between the two nations, and for many years after that our relations with Mexico were peaceable. Indeed, a great many Americans went into Mexico to help develop its oil wells, its silver mines, and its coffee plantations. When in 1911 a revolution overthrew Porfirio Diaz, who had been for thirty years President and virtual dictator of 

Mexico, American investments in Mexico had come to be very large.

The Mexican Revolution of 1911-16 

Diaz was overthrown in 1911 by a party that accused him of oppression and corruption and of granting Mexico's richest resources to foreign capitalists, European and American. 

The President who succeeded him was Francisco Madero, an honest man, but not strong enough to control the situation. In little more than a year he was overthrown in turn by a counter-revolution led by a general in the army, Victoriano Huerta. A few days later, Madero died under very suspicious circumstances, in prison. It was generally believed that Huerta had had him murdered.

All this happened just as Woodrow Wilson was about to be inaugurated President. From the first, Wilson re-

fused to recognize Huerta as President of Mexico, though all the European nations did so quite promptly. The partisans of Madero, led by General Carranza, were already in revolt against the usurper, and it was plain that President Wilson sym- pathized with them. "Watchful Waiting" 

Wilson did not offer to interfere in the situation and announced that his policy would be one of "watchful waiting," but nothing would induce him to recognize the Huerta administration as the lawful one. In the midst of this tangled situation some 

American marines from a

were arrested without cause by Mexican officials, and 

Huerta stubbornly refused to offer apology or express regret.

President Wilson at once dispatched a squadron to make a naval demonstration before the city of Vera Cruz, and some marines were landed to occupy the city until an apology was forthcoming. Huerta was still stubborn. 

Three South American republics, Argentina, Brazil, and

Chile, offered their services as mediators; President Wilson at once accepted them and withdrew our ships from Vera Cruz. Before mediation had proceeded very far, Huerta 

I Woodrow Wilson was born in Virginia in 1856. He graduated at 

Princeton. For many years he was a professor of history and government at Princeton and president of the University, 1902-10. He also wrote many books on American history and the science of government. In 1910, he was elected Governor of New Jersey, and in 1912 and 1916 he was elected President. He died in 1924.

found his position grown too difficult. He gave up the government to Carranza and went into exile in Spain. 

The Expedition Against Villa 

Even after Carranza became President, Mexico remained disturbed. A clever and determined robber chief named 

Pancho Villa kept the northern states of the Republic in disorder with his raids, and the soldiers of Carranza could not catch him. Villa was accustomed sometimes to cross the border and raid American property. 

At last he became so bold that President Wilson determined to teach him a lesson. A large part of the regular army with some militia from Texas was sent to guard the border, and by arrangement with Carranza a strong American force under General Pershing entered Mexico in pursuit of Villa. This force did not succeed in catching him either, and after a while it had to be withdrawn. 

Copyright, by Harris & Ewing

GENERAL PERSHING 

After a year or two of continued disorder, General Obregon - later to become President of Mexico himself overtook Villa and crushed his army. Something like peace descended on Mexico, and has since been maintained.

Better Relations with the New Mexico 

In 1917, the Mexicans adopted a new constitution which declared all the natural wealth of the country- oil, silver, and so on, to be the property of the nation, and voided all the concessions and sales of land which Diaz had made to foreign capitalists. A great many American investments were affected by this law, for American oil companies and

mining companies had bought a large part of Mexico's richest land.

For several years thereafter our Government was occupied in trying to get Mexico, through its courts or its President, to recognize the rights of Americans in the property they had bought in a perfectly legal way. At first the Government had no success, and at times our relations with Mexico were a little strained by the dispute. Eventually an agreement was reached by which the American companies leased their properties from the Government (the money they paid amounted to an additional tax), and everything was amicably settled.

In late years our relations with our southern neighbor have become more cordial than ever before, owing to the tact and good sense of our ambassador to Mexico from 

1927 to 1930, Mr. Dwight W. Morrow.

UNIT XXII DOMESTIC PROBLEMS OF THE NEW CENTURY 

ITH the beginning of the twentieth century the WH ortain the social structure of the country owing to the exhaustion of the vast supply of free farming land on the one hand, and the growth of great industrial cities on the other. By 1900 almost all the land that was available for agriculture had been taken up; it was no longer possible for the immigrant or the man dissatisfied with his farm or his job in the East to go out into the West and possess himself of a piece of fertile land for no more than the labor of cultivating it. The last great area of public land in the Indian Territory had been thrown open to settlers in 1889. This is the region now known as the State of Oklahoma. For many years it had been preserved as a reservation for the Indian tribes who had moved westward as the country filled up behind them. 

The Rush into Oklahoma

As the Indians had made little use of the wide stretches of fertile prairie, the Government decided to give individual allotments of land to the Indians, and to purchase from the tribes who had held title to the soil of the Territory all that was not so allotted, and offer it for settlement to white men. 

On the day when this land was to be opened to settlers, a veritable army of eager land-seekers had collected along the border and federal troops had to be put on guard to prevent men from stealing across the line and squatting on desirable tracts ahead of the lawful time. When the word was given, the crowd poured across the border in wagons, on horse-

DOMESTIC PROBLEMS 

back, or even on foot, and scattered in every direction across the prairie to seize land for themselves. 

Within twenty-four hours sizable tent cities had sprung up where Guthrie and Oklahoma City now stand. By the next year there were 258,000 people in Oklahoma and by 

1900 there were 790,000.

It was not until 1907, however, that Oklahoma was actually admitted as a State. In 1912, it was followed by New 

Mexico and Arizona. These two States were the last to remain as Territories. They were part of the region we took from Mexico in 1848, but they had grown slowly, for their mountains and their extremely dry climate made them uninviting to settlers looking for farming land. With their admission the entire territory of continental United States was organized into States. 

Making Farms by Irrigation 

The supply of naturally arable land having been exhausted, attention was turned to great stretches of land 

Courtesy of Chicago, Burlington and Quincy Railroad

IRRIGATING ORCHARD LAND 

Showing the ditches that carry the water through the entire tract

DOMESTIC PROBLEMS 

461 in the Far West, which might be made fertile by irrigation. 

Much of this land in New Mexico, Arizona, Idaho, Utah, and Colorado was barren for lack of rainfall. The soil itself was rich enough; it needed only water to make it fertile. 

So in 1902 the Reclamation Act was passed by Congress. 

This act appropriated money from the sale of public lands for the carrying out of irrigation projects; and it provided for making the fund self-sustaining by collecting payments from the settlers whose land was to be made valuable by this means.

These works usually consist of great dams, built of concrete, to hold back the water in the streams among the mountains, thereby creating reservoirs from which water can be carried down into the almost rainless valleys. Some of these projects are marvelous feats of engineering. For example, there is the monster Roosevelt Dam on the Salt River in Arizona which is 284 feet high, the Elephant Butte Dam on the Rio Grande, in southern New Mexico, and the Arrowrock Dam, in Idaho. which are even higher, and the Gunnison Tunnel which carries water under a mountain range in Colorado to fertilize a valley on the farther side. There has also been a great deal of work done in draining swamp lands in the States farther east, and a considerable amount of good farm land has thus been reclaimed. 

Conservation of Natural Resources 

President Roosevelt was an enthusiastic advocate of the reclamation policy; he was even more interested in the movement for the conservation or saving of our national resources. Americans have always been wasteful 

Courtesy of Seattle Chamber of Commerce

CONSERVING WATER POWER 

A conduit that carries water to a hydro-electric plant in the Western mountains 

of the generous natural resources of the country, particularly the timber, the oil, and the coal with which it was so liberally provided. By the beginning of this century, wise men saw that something must be done to make us more economical, if we were not to find our national wealth exhausted. 

President Roosevelt was active in bringing this necessity to the notice of the people. He appointed a commission on conservation, and encouraged legislation in Congress to create national parks and national forest reserves on the public land that still belonged to the Nation. He and his successor, President Taft, also withdrew large amounts of public land from sale, because they contained, or were supposed to contain, valuable mineral or oil deposits which ought to be preserved for the public

DOMESTIC PROBLEMS 

463 profit rather than turned over to speculators. By these means immense quantities of timber, oil, and water power are now under government control, and can be used only under government regulations which are drawn in order to prevent waste.

The forest reserves are valuable because they prevent private enterprise from stripping the mountain slopes for their timber. Bare slopes permit the water to run off too quickly when the snow is melting or heavy rains come, and cause disastrous floods in the spring and almost dry watercourses in the summer and fall. 

The Bureau of Mines has also been of great service in helping the Government to locate lands underlaid by valuable minerals, and by encouraging mine-owners to use economical and safe methods of extracting the ore.

Making Rural Life More Comfortable 

The gradual shifting of population from the farms to the towns has been a striking factor in the history of the United States. In 1790, only 3.4 per cent of the population lived in communities of 8000 or over. By 1860, this proportion had risen to 16 per cent. By 1900, it was 31 per cent, and in 1920, it was about 47 per cent. If the census definition of 2500 population in a village as an "urban" community be taken, more than one half our people are urban rather than rural.

Efforts have been made to stem this continual drift of population to the cities, and to encourage people to go "back to the land." Though these efforts have not been very successful so far as the statistics show, they have at least made life in the country a great deal more convenient and comfortable than it used to be. 

For example, the Government has established a system of free delivery of mail to farmhouses all over the country;

DOMESTIC PROBLEMS 

it has added to that a parcel-post system which gives to the farmer a service for the delivery of heavy and bulky packages such as the express companies furnish in the cities. 

Roads have improved astonishingly since 1900, largely owing to the aid given by the Federal and State Governments. Most farms are now within reach of good hard roads suitable for travel at any season of the year; which means a decided saving of time and expense in getting produce to market.

Innumerable mechanical inventions have made the labor of farm work less exhausting and furnished better means of transportation. The automobile, the farm tractor, the gang-plow, all kinds of harvesting machinery, and the milking-machine are examples of the benefits invention has conferred on agriculture. 

Services of the Department of Agriculture

The Department of Agriculture and the state colleges of agriculture have put scientific research at the service of the farmer. Their bulletins, and the advice of their specialists, which is freely given, have shown the farmer how to improve the seed he uses and the livestock he raises. They have taught him effective ways of dealing with diseases of plants and animals, and with insect pests. They have introduced him to new and profitable crops - Kaffir corn and alfalfa are notable examples. And through the

Weather Bureau and the monthly crop reports they have helped him to foresee growing conditions and to understand what is likely to happen when he comes to sell his produce. 

There have been great improvements, too, in the means of marketing farm products. The establishment of creameries and cheese factories has helped many a farmer to dispose profitably of his surplus milk. The building of great elevators for the storage of grain, often owned by

DOMESTIC PROBLEMS 

465 

Photograph by U.S. Army Air Service AIRPLANE SPRAYING COTTON FIELD TO KILL BOLL WEEVILS 

associations of wheat-growers, has enabled farmers to hold their crops for better prices. The development of the packing industries has stabilized the market for meat, and the invention of refrigerator cars and of cold-storage plants makes it possible to ship meats, eggs, and fruit and to keep them fresh even in the hottest weather. 

Coöperation Among the Farmers

Coöperation among farmers and fruit-growers has also had a part in getting better prices for their produce. By pooling their crops and putting the sale of them in charge of experienced market-men, a coöperative association can reap many of the advantages that large business concerns enjoy; it can buy what its members need at wholesale prices, and sell what they have to sell at the proper time and on the best terms. There is a great future for coöperation in 

American agriculture especially in dairying and fruit-growing; but it has not yet been developed as it has in many 

European countries in Denmark, for example. 

So, though people still continue to move from the farms

DOMESTIC PROBLEMS 

into the cities, because more money is to be made there, and more variety of life and entertainment is to be found there, living, for those who remain in the country, has become easier, pleasanter, and generally more profitable. 

Problems of the Cities 

The fast-growing cities have had many new problems to solve. Among them are the difficulty of keeping pace in house-building with the increasing stream of population, the task of organizing satisfactory transportation systems to get the throngs of people back and forth on their errands of business or pleasure, and the necessity of keeping the public services the fire and police departments, the water supplies and the public health and sanitation work equal to the demands made upon them. 

Although much remains to be done before they become satisfactory places in which to live, our cities have met their problems fairly well. It must be admitted, however, that what has been done has in many cases been done extravagantly and wastefully, for city government in the United States has often fallen into the hands of men who are corrupt or inefficient. Progress has been made, however. The old slums, made up of ramshackle, unsanitary houses, are fast disappearing; most cities have planning boards of capable men which are widening streets, designing park systems, and establishing the orderly development of business and residence districts.

The lighting of American cities is usually good, for we make great use of electricity. In the larger cities railways, elevated above the street or sunk below them in costly subways, have made rapid transit possible from the heart of the city to its outskirts and its suburbs. The architecture of business buildings and of homes is much more tasteful than it used to be. In New York and

DOMESTIC PROBLEMS 

467 Chicago, particularly, there are many great buildings which are as remarkable for their originality and their grace or massiveness as for their extraordinary height. 

Guarding the Public Health 

Perhaps the greatest advance has been made in the protection of the public health and the supply of clean, pure

Water is often brought from specially constructed reservoirs, fifty or a hundred miles away, and delivered in unlimited quantity. New York draws a part of its supply from the hills far up the Hudson River; Boston from streams that formerly flowed into the 

Connecticut River; Los Angeles gets its water from the

DOMESTIC PROBLEMS 

Sierra Nevada Mountains far across the State of California. 

Excellent sewerage systems, the intelligent control of infectious diseases, fine hospitals, where the poor can have free treatment, and public instruction in hygiene have all helped to make disease less common or at least less fatal in the cities than in the country, where the conditions would appear to be far more healthful. The urban death rate is almost everywhere below the rural rate. 

The Cost of Living 

Life in the modern city is, however, considerably more expensive than it used to be. Rents are high because the city is crowded and land is valuable. All the conveniences and advantages of city life cost money, which, of course, is reflected in the tax rate. Much of the food has to be brought from long distances, and must pass through many hands, each of which takes a profit from it on the way to the consumer. These are the causes of the “high cost of living," of which you often hear people complain. They are the price that must be paid for the advantages of life in a modern city.

Labor Problems — the Coal Strikes 

Clashes between capital and labor were frequent during the opening years of the century. It was a period when great combinations of capital were being formed in many industries. Labor often found itself at a disadvantage in dealing with these new combinations or trusts; and even when the working people were not in any way oppressed, they were, with good reason, fearful of the power the trusts gave to capital. Wherever labor was well organized, and able to influence legislation by the exercise of its voting power, it was not badly off; but in some industries it was unfortunately situated.

DOMESTIC PROBLEMS 

469 This was especially true of the coal industry, where the pay was generally small, the conditions of work hard, and the organization of the workmen incomplete. In 1902 there was a great strike among the hard-coal miners of Pennsylvania, who demanded better pay, shorter hours, and the right to deal with their employers through a union. For five months no coal was mined, and there was danger that the Eastern towns and cities would face the winter with no coal in their bins.

That danger made it clear that the miners and the mineowners could not be allowed to fight out their differences without any thought of the public who needed the coal, and whose money made it possible for both employers and workmen to exist. President Roosevelt stepped in. He threatened to seize the mines and operate them through the Government if the strike were not settled. That threat brought both sides to terms. They submitted their dispute to a coal commission appointed by the President, which gave the miners a considerable part of their demands. 

Labor Insists on the Right to Organize 

In 1913 there was another serious strike in the coal mines of Colorado, and a little earlier there was a long strike in the woolen mills at Lawrence, Massachusetts. If these strikes were not chiefly caused by the refusal of employers to permit their men to organize unions or to deal with the unions when they were organized, that refusal was responsible for most of the bitterness with which they were conducted.

Labor saw capital continually increasing its power through combination, and it was determined to compel the recognition of its own right to organize. That was the only way in which it could hold its own in the struggle for higher wages and better working conditions.

DOMESTIC PROBLEMS 

Maintaining the Interest of the Public

This period was marked also by the awakening understanding of the fact that the public had some rights in the matter, and also by the increasing disposition of the Government to interfere in the quarrels of capital and labor in order to protect those rights.

We have seen how President Roosevelt interfered to compel arbitration in the coal strike of 1902. Both he and his successors used their influence more than once to bring about the arbitration of labor disputes. 

In 1903, partly as a result of the strike of the previous year, a Department of Commerce and Labor, with a secretary sitting in the Cabinet, was formed to study the problems of labor and of business and to help the Government in dealing with them. Ten years later, the department was divided, and since then we have had a Secretary of 

Commerce and a Secretary of Labor as well.

Capital Combines on Every Hand 

The people of the United States were, however, less stirred by the efforts of labor to perfect its organization all over the country than they were by the attempts of capital to build up great and powerful combinations in one industry after another.

The Standard Oil Company had been obliged, in 1892, to dissolve the original trust agreement by which companies that produced four fifths of all the petroleum in the country were united in one organization. In 1899 it tried to do 

It formed a "holding the same thing in a different way. company" to take over all the properties of the different oil companies it controlled. Then came the organization of the monster United States Steel Corporation (1902) which combined almost all the great steel mills in the United States under a single board of directors.

DOMESTIC PROBLEMS 

471 The railroads also were heard from. Mr. E. H. Harriman, who had taken the bankrupt Union Pacific Railroad and made a prosperous road out of it, arranged to buy the Southern Pacific and the Central Pacific, too, so that he would control all the business between California and the East. He and James J. Hill, who owned the Great Northern, engaged in a famous stock-market duel for the ownership of the Northern Pacific Railroad, also bankrupt. Hill won, with the help of J. P. Morgan, the great banker, by securing a majority of the stock. He therefore controlled all the rail lines that led to the northern part of the Pacific Coast. He organized the Northern Securities Company to hold the stock of the roads he owned. 

Roosevelt's Campaign Against the New "Trusts"

The Nation was alarmed at all these great combinations of capital, for it feared that monopolies of oil and steel and railway transportation would mean higher prices for everything and even the control of the Government at Washington by a few men of great wealth. What President

McKinley would have done in this emergency, if he had not been killed by an assassin, we do not know; but President

Roosevelt acted promptly. He ordered an immediate suit in the Federal Courts to break up the Northern Securities

Company, and in the end the Government won. At the 

President's suggestion Congress passed new laws to prevent the formation of any combination that could control the greater part of the product of any industry and to forbid railways from giving rebates and other favors to powerful industrial companies.

A suit was also brought against the Standard Oil Company, and in 1911, after some years of litigation, that combination was also broken up.

During the summer of 1902, the President made a tour

DOMESTIC PROBLEMS 

of the country speaking everywhere of the danger of the great corporations commonly called "trusts," and of the necessity of regulating very strictly the power of capital to enter into combinations that might grow into monopolies. 

He was an energetic and convincing speaker, and he aroused the public opinion of the Nation as it had never been aroused on a question of economics. 66 

Progressive" Policies

The ideas of Roosevelt on the conservation of the Nation's resources (see page 462) and on the control by the Government of private business were something new in American politics. They were called "progressive" policies, because they aimed at an increasing use of the federal power to shape the conditions of the Nation's life. They were immensely popular, because Roosevelt himself was a very effective leader of public sentiment and because they seemed to the people to meet the new problems of the time in a satisfactory way. Wealth was growing fast and exerting a dangerous influence on public life. Roosevelt stood forth as the champion of the people against the power and the greed of wealth.

So he was triumphantly reëlected President in 1904, and he remained throughout his term a popular and successful executive. There was a strong minority in his own partythe Republican — that did not like him and thought he was carrying his interference with private business too far; but it could not successfully challenge his control. 

In 1908, Roosevelt was able to dictate his successor, as Jackson had dictated the election of Van Buren in 1836. 

The new President was William H. Taft, of Ohio, who had been a federal Judge, Governor-General of the Philippines, and Secretary of War in Roosevelt's Cabinet. He was chosen by a large majority over William J. Bryan, for the third time the candidate of the Democrats.

DOMESTIC PROBLEMS 

473 

The Taft-Roosevelt Quarrel 

President Taft had pledged himself to continue the Roosevelt policies, and in his own way he did so. The suit against the Standard Oil Company was pressed, and a verdict for the Government obtained. But Taft had neither the energy nor the enthusiasm of his predecessor, and he had friends and advisers among the wing of the Republican Party that did not like 

Roosevelt. One of Roosevelt's close friends, Gifford Pinchot, head of the 

Forestry Bureau, accused Taft's Secretary of the Interior of trying to break down the conservation policies of Roosevelt. There was a quarrel. 

Taft stood by his secretary; Roosevelt listened to Pinchot.

When 1912 came, bringing another presidential election, Roosevelt openly opposed the renomination of Taft and

Roosevelt refused to accept the decision and put himself at the head of a new party, which called itself the Progressive Party. The campaign was hot and full of excitement.

With the Republican Party split squarely in two, only one thing could happen. The Democratic candidate was elected. Woodrow Wilson, a new man in politics, who had long been a student and writer of history, President of I William H. Taft was born in Ohio in 1857. He was a graduate of Yale

He was President from 1909 to 1913 and Chief Justice of the United States from 1923 to 1930, the only man to hold both these great offices. He died in 1930.

Legislation for Labor

President Wilson's ideas on capital and labor were much nearer those of Roosevelt than of Taft; he was even more advanced than Roosevelt. With his approval Congress passed the Clayton Act, in 1914, which forbade all sorts of agreements between corporations to control trade or to restrict it, and made it illegal for the same persons to be directors of more than one company engaged in the same industry. This act also forbade manufacturers to favor certain customers, or to persuade their customers not to handle the goods of competing manufacturers. The object was to protect old-fashioned competition in trade against the new methods of combination. 

Congress also passed the La Follette Seamen's Act, in 1916, obliging shipowners to pay better wages and give their sailors better living conditions aboard ship; and the Adamson Act, which provided that railway workers employed on roads that were in two or more States should not work more than eight hours in a day.

Government Operation of the Railways 

When the United States entered the World War, in 1917, President Wilson who had been reëlected in 1916carried government control of industry further than had ever before been suggested. In order to make sure that the railroads should serve the needs of the Government first of all, and coöperate perfectly in the preparations for war, he issued a decree taking over the railroad properties, and putting them under the direction of his Secretary of the Treasury, Mr. McAdoo, who was also made Director of Railways.

DOMESTIC PROBLEMS 

475 As long as the war lasted and for a year or more thereafter, the Government operated the railways, using, of course, the same officers and employees to do the actual work. As a war measure it worked well, for it enabled the 

Government to use the railways for its most immediate needs, but the Government was not so careful about keeping the rails and the engines and cars in good condition as the owners had been. So when the roads were returned to their stockholders, in 1920, considerable money had to be spent to restore them to first-class condition. 

Labor Problems Since the World War 

Since the war, union labor has had its troubles and its successes. It has had to fight a movement among employers in favor of the "open shop." That means a shop or factory in which not union labor alone is employed, but which is "open" to organized and unorganized workmen alike. It has also had to face the practice of some employers who, in case of a strike, get from the courts an "injunction," or order, forbidding the strikers to meet in numbers on the street or to do anything to annoy those who are willing to take up the work the strikers have left. 

There has been a great deal of discussion about "government by injunction," as this practice is called. Some people believe that it is simply using the courts, which ought to be impartial, to help the employers against their workmen; while others look on it as a proper means of preventing disorder and perhaps violence in connection with labor disputes.

On the whole, however, the problems of capital and labor seem to be settling themselves gradually. Strikes and lockouts are less common than they used to be, because both parties see how costly and foolish they are. Employers pay better wages and deal more justly with their

DOMESTIC PROBLEMS 

men than they did in the past; and when differences arise, they are usually settled by friendly conference or by arbitration.

On the other hand, people are not so much afraid of great combinations of capital as they used to be. They have come to think that it is not the size, but the conduct, of a corporation that has to be watched. Now that the principle of government regulation of great business corporations is firmly established, the people rely on the Government to prevent such combinations from taking improper advantage of them.

UNIT XXIII REFORMS IN FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, AND SOCIAL LIFE THE 

HE matters we read about in the previous unit were by no means the only ones that remained unsettled during the early years of the present century. Let us look for a little at some other problems - financial, political, and commercial — that the people of the United States had to deal with during that period. 

The United States Becomes a Creditor Nation 

In matters of finance great changes have occurred since 1900, owing to the fact that the United States has grown in a quarter of a century from a debtor nation to a creditor nation. That is to say, that in the former days we used to owe a great deal of money to foreign nations, whereas they now owe us a great deal more than we owe them. 

We came to owe money to the rich people of England, France, Germany, and other European nations, because at the time when we were building thousands of miles of railway every year and increasing our industries and our factories so fast, we had not enough ready money ourselves, and had to borrow it from those who had. We sold them bonds and stocks in great quantities and a very large sum of money had to be sent across the ocean every year to pay the interest on the bonds and the dividends on the stocks. 

When the World War came, the people of those countries, from whom we had borrowed, needed money at once and they sold back to us a great many of the bonds and the shares of stock. So we no longer owe them as much as we

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 481 

did. Moreover, as the war went on, the Governments of 

France and Great Britain had to borrow many millions of dollars from us with which to pay the cost of fighting; and since the war we have been so much more prosperous than Europe that we have lent more money to help the people of those countries rebuild and repair their own industries, injured by the war.

The Federal Reserve System 

We could not have played the part we did in the war or since if we had the unstable banking system we had before our own Civil War, or even the old National Bank system that we had when the twentieth century opened. Fortunately, in 1913 Congress passed a bill organizing what is called the Federal Reserve System. It is difficult to explain this system so that you may really understand it; but what it accomplished is this: It gave us all the advantages of a strong central national bank without actually creating such a bank. Americans have always been afraid of a great national bank, for fear of the influence it might have on politics.

The Federal Reserve System established twelve Reserve banks in different parts of the country, and by this means made it somewhat less easy for money to flow into New York, where it could be borrowed and used by the brokers of Wall Street for speculation on the stock exchange. It has given us a much more elastic currency than we had before; when more money is needed in circulation the 

Reserve System supplies it; when there is need for less, the circulation of money is "contracted" or diminished.

The system, which was worked out by bankers and members of Congress together, has served us excellently, and made our banks safer, stronger, and more useful than ever. 

All National Banks are members of the Federal Reserve

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 

System, but unfortunately only a few of the State banks have joined it. It cannot produce its best effects until all banks, whether chartered by the States or by the Nation are members.

In 1910, the Government established savings departments in connection with the post-offices, so that people need not be dependent on private banks for their savings. 

Most people still prefer, however, to put their money in the private banks, which pay a higher rate of interest and are usually entirely safe.

The Income Tax 

Among the principles of the Democratic Party under 

Bryan and the Progressive Republicans under Roosevelt, was the federal tax on the yearly income of people who were rich or well-to-do. Congress passed a law providing for an income tax, as far back as 1894, but the Supreme Court held that the Constitution forbade any such "direct tax" unless it was levied in proportion to the population of the several States. Of course a tax levied in that way would defeat the purpose of the advocates of an income tax. They wanted to get money from those who had most of it, and in some States in New York and Pennsylvania, for example there were many more such people than their share of the total population would indicate. 

So the movement began for a 16th Amendment to the Constitution, permitting Congress to lay an income tax on individuals and business corporations. By 1909 it had grown so strong that Congress proposed such an amendment to the States. Four years later the necessary number of States had ratified it, and the income tax became a most important part of our system for raising a national revenue. 

Some of the States have adopted the idea, too, and people who live in those States have to pay two taxes on the amount
