At the top is a mooring mast for dirigibles. of their yearly income State. one to the Nation and one to the The extraordinary expenses that arose during the World War obliged the Government to resort to new and unheard-of forms of taxation. We spent about $25,000,- 

000,000 on the war ourselves, and we lent nearly $10,000,- 000,000 to Great Britain, France, Belgium, and Italy, our allies against Germany, Austria, Turkey, and Bulgaria. 

We shall learn more about that when we come to read the story of the war itself. 

The Immigration Problem 

During these early years of the century the Nation began

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 

to understand that there was a rather serious immigration problem to face. Between 1900 and 1914 something like 

12,000,000 immigrants poured into the country. Most of them were absorbed by industry to do the heavier and rougher sorts of work; but it was doubtful how long that could continue. American labor leaders began to get uneasy, for they saw the danger of an immigration so large that it would take jobs away from native workingmen or force them to accept the lower wages which the immigrants were willing to take.

Most of these 12,000,000 newcomers were from southern or eastern Europe. Though they were industrious and thrifty, they spoke no English, understood little of our ways of life and next to nothing of our political system, and were content with what we should call a very low standard of living. They were clannish and crowded into "foreign colonies" in the larger cities. No one finding his way into these quarters would have thought himself in America at all.

We Decide to Limit Immigration 

For these reasons, the movement to limit foreign immigration began to gain strength. Our forefathers, who thought of the United States as a refuge for the oppressed of all the earth, would have been shocked at such an idea; but they could not have foreseen the flood of alien people that was by this time pouring in on us and threatening to overwhelm us by its numbers. 

In 1917 a law was passed providing that only immigrants who could read and write their own language should be admitted. At that time the war had pretty nearly put a stop to immigration anyway; but after the war the problem became serious again. The "literacy tests" did not reduce the stream of immigrants sufficiently. So in 1924

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 485 

another law was passed which limits the total number of immigrants to 150,000 a year, and limits the number from any one nation to two per cent of the natives of that nation who were living in the United States in 1890. Americanization

At the same time Americans did not meet this problem of immigration merely by cutting down the number of foreigners they would receive. You have heard something of "Americanization," no doubt. Americanization means an organized effort on the part of people of public spirit to help all immigrants, especially those who live in the "foreign colonies" spoken of above; to be sure that their children go to our public schools and to get the older persons to attend night schools, or other classes, where they can be taught to speak and write English, and where they will be given instruction in the kind of government we have, in our ideals of political and social democracy and in the ways and customs of living in this country. A great deal of very useful work of this kind has been done in all the great cities of the land.

New Political Methods Initiative and Referendum 

The period from 1900 to 1920 was remarkable for the many changes and reforms that were made in our political methods both in the States and the Nation. Most of these changes were brought about by the wave of "progressive" sentiment that swept over the country during those years. 

The aim of these changes was to increase the influence of the individual voter in political affairs as against the party organizations or "machines," and to bring about more regulation of the business and life of the people by the Government.

Some of these reforms were suggested as far back as the

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 

days of the Populists in 1890, but they were actually put into practice only after the rise of Bryan to power with the Democrats and of Roosevelt with the Republicans. They were first tried in the Western States, for the West is naturally more venturesome and ready for change than the older East. Several of these reforms came to us from Wisconsin, where Robert M. La Follette, first Governor and then Senator, was especially earnest and successful in the cause of political reform.

Among these reforms are the initiative, the referendum, the recall of elected officers, the direct primary, the short ballot, and the commission government in cities. 

The initiative means that if enough people would like to have a certain law enacted, they can by petition, properly signed, present it to the legislature to be acted upon. The referendum provides a way of getting the law brought before the people if the legislature declines to pass it. If enough voters sign a request for a referendum, the legislature must submit the bill to a popular vote at the next general election; and if the people vote for it, it becomes a law. 

This is an entirely new way of making laws, and it would have shocked the men of 1787 who made our Constitution; for they had more distrust of the wisdom of the common people than confidence in it. So far the initiative and referendum have not been introduced into national law-making; but many States have tried them, and on the whole with good, though not always satisfactory, results. 

The Recall of Elected Officers 

The recall provides that if an elected officer a mayor or a governor, for example does not give satisfaction to a sufficient number of citizens, these citizens may petition for his recall. The matter is then put to a popular vote in the middle of his term, and if a majority votes against

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 487 

him he is removed from office. Some of the Western 

States have even applied this idea of the recall to judges; but the general feeling is that the courts should be kept out of politics, and that judges should be secure in their positions unless they do something worthy of impeachment by the legislature. The recall is less popular than the initiative and referendum, but a number of States and cities have adopted the idea. 

The Direct Primary

The direct primary is one of the changes that came to us from Wisconsin. It was proposed because so often in 

USNAVY 

Courtesy, U.S. Navy Department

A GREAT DIRIGIBLE 

former days, the conventions to nominate state or local officers were controlled by the bosses, or leaders of the "machine." These men frequently nominated unfit candidates, often for corrupt or at least selfish motives. 

The direct primary does away with the nominating convention. Any man who desires the nomination, and can get the required number of voters to sign his "papers," has

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 

his name put on the primary ballot and the members of each party, at an election held a month or more before the general election, select from the self-proposed candidates the ones to appear on the party ticket. 

The direct primary is in fairly general use today. It has to a certain extent weakened the influence of professional politicians and party bosses; but it does not always produce good candidates, for the voters, if more honest, are no wiser than the delegates to the conventions used to be, if indeed they are as wise. Moreover, the cost of carrying on a private campaign for the primary nomination often discourages a poor man from presenting himself even when he is well qualified for office. 

The Short Ballot

The short ballot has not made so much progress as the other reforms we have spoken of, though it is one of the soundest of them all. That is because it is unpopular with those who think that as many offices as possible should be filled by popular vote. The short ballot means that only a few of the most important offices shall be filled in that way, and that the governor or mayor shall appoint many officials who are now elected by popular vote. 

In this way the chief executive becomes directly responsible for the conduct of affairs through his appointed subordinates, and the people are able to vote for or against a few men with whose records they are familiar, instead of for a long list of candidates who are in many cases mere names which mean nothing to the electors.

New Methods of City Government 

The greatest weakness of American government is shown in the management of our cities, where masses of voters, often ignorant and careless, have submitted to the control

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 489 

of political bosses who are interested only in making money for themselves and their friends, through the corrupt use of the power the offices give them. One means of improving the character of municipal government is through the commission plan, by which the affairs of the city are placed in the hands of four or five men, instead of the clumsy system of mayor, aldermen, and common councilmen, which is the old-fashioned form of city government. Often the commission appoints a "city manager" to conduct the public works and the financial affairs of the municipality; and he is usually a competent business man with large experience and no political ambitions.

This commission system has been tried with some success in hundreds of cities, including the great city of Cleveland, with a population of nearly 1,000,000. In most of the larger cities, however, the political influence of the bosses has so far been strong enough to prevent the adoption of the idea. 

Direct Election of Senators

Three remarkable reforms have been made in our national political system, and two of them required constitutional amendments before they could become effective. When our Constitution was written, the election of United States Senators was given to the state legislatures. After the 

Civil War, as the organization of our politics grew more and more democratic, a demand arose that the senators should be chosen by direct vote of the people. This was one of the principles advocated both by the Democrats under Bryan and the Progressive Republicans. In 1913 an amendment to the Constitution was adopted which transferred the power of election from the legislatures to the people, where it still remains.

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 

Women Get the Vote

The right of women to vote as well as men was proclaimed far back in the nineteenth century by such famous women as Susan B. Anthony, Julia Ward Howe, and Elizabeth Cady Stanton. For years the woman suffrage movement made little progress, for the women themselves were not deeply interested. But little by little they were awakened to the importance of the matter, and their leaders found themselves helped by many men who were convinced that it was only just that women should have the vote. 

One by one some of the States in the West - Wyoming, Utah, Colorado, Idaho, Washington, and California gave the vote to women. The Progressive Party of 1912 put woman suffrage into its platform. The movement grew; the excitement of the World War, in which women did so much useful work, helped it along. In 1919, Congress proposed an amendment giving women the same right to vote that men possessed. A year later a sufficient number of States had ratified this amendment and it became part of the Constitution. Women have since been elected governors of States and members of Congress, and have filled very many less important offices in state legislatures and municipal governments.

The third reform in national affairs was the establishment of a budget system in 1921. Previously Congress had appropriated money without very much inquiry about where it was to come from, and sometimes the Government actually had to borrow money to get enough for expenses. 

The budget system is something that every business concern and a great many private households have as a matter of course. The probable revenue of the United States is carefully calculated, the probable needs of each government department are taken into account, and then the Director of the Budget tells Congress just how much

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 491 

money it can afford to appropriate and what the share of each department ought to be. The Congressmen did not like the idea very well at first, but they have all come to see that it is a very much more sensible and business-like way of attending to the appropriation of money than the old way was.

Trunk Lines and Electric Railways 

Transportation by land and sea has passed through some interesting and important changes since the new century came in. With 

1900 the great era of railwaybuilding had pretty nearly come to an end. 

The 30,000 miles of rail that were laid before 1860, by 1900 had been extended to 200,000 miles. 

Almost every part of the country that needed

VIEW OF AN OIL FIELD 

The lofty derricks support the machinery for drilling the oil-wells. a railway or could make one profitable was supplied. Building did continue at a slower pace, however, until 1915; since then almost no new lines have been constructed. 

The tendency has been to consolidate the existing roads into great "trunk line" systems. There are four or five of these systems between the principal cities of the East and Chicago or St. Louis; there are three or four similar systems in the South, and as many crossing the Western plains and mountains to the Pacific. The Interstate Com-

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 

merce Commission is at work on a plan for dividing all the roads in the country among some ten or twelve great combinations, taking care that none shall have a complete monopoly of the business in any important part of the country. The commission is finding it very hard, however, to arrange a division satisfactory to itself and to the railroads; it still remains a problem for the future. 

During the early years of the century there was a wide development of street railroads operated by electricity running from city to city all over the country. Some of the railways also planned to use electricity instead of steam for motive power; but the expense of changing from steam to electricity is so great, and the cost of electric power is so much more than that of steam power, that only here and there, generally in the neighborhood of great cities, has the change been made.

Meanwhile the electric roads have had to meet the competition of the automobile. A great many of them have 

Courtesy of National Museum

AN AUTOMOBILE OF 1898 

had to go out of business, because so many people now prefer to drive in their own motor cars instead of traveling more slowly and less comfortably by trolley.

The Coming of the Automobile 

The automobile industry has had a most astonishing development during this twentieth century in the 

United States. The improvement of the gasoline engine, invented in 1867, made automobiles possible in great numbers. Cars were first seen on our streets about 1893, but

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 493 

for some years thereafter they were crude and clumsy affairs. 

People laughed at them at first, and they got out of order so easily that one could not depend on them to go any great distance.

Even in 1900 there were still comparatively few in common use, but they grew more attractive in appearance and more reliable in performance every year.

When Henry 

Ford began to make a stout little car that would sell for less than a thousand dollars, the new means of transportation began to be popular. Today there are at least 25,000,000 automobiles owned in the United States.

Growth of the Motor Industry 

The motor-car industry employs, either in the manufacture or the care and repair of cars, between 3,000,000 and 4,000,000 men, and billions of capital are invested in it. 

The increasing use of the automobile has revived the interest in road-building. In 1900 there were comparatively few really first-rate roads in the country - few that were more than passable except in dry weather. Today every State in the Union has built fine hard roads of macadam or concrete between its principal towns and there are national "highways" by which the motorist can cross the continent or go from end to end of the country with speed and comfort.

The Federal Government has come to the aid of the roadbuilders too. By the Federal Aid Acts of 1916, 1919, and 

1921 the Government appropriates about $75,000,000 a year to be distributed among the States for road-building, each State agreeing to spend a dollar of its own for every dollar it receives from Washington. In some recent years half a billion dollars have been spent by federal, state, or local appropriation to improve, repair, or replace the roads and bridges of the country.

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS New Agencies of Transportation 

Business has found the use of trucks on these fine new roads very profitable. The transportation of freight, if not too bulky or over too great distances, is often cheaper and quicker by automobile truck than by rail. There is also the advantage that the truck can go directly from the door of the factory or the store, to the door of the customer, so a great part of the freight business of the country is now carried on in this way. 

Travel by bus is also becoming popular.

Many of the street-railway lines within the cities

Photograph by courtesy of General Electric Co. 

AN ELECTRIC LOCOMOTIVE ON THE CHICAGO, MILWAUKEE, AND ST. PAUL RAILROAD 

have replaced their trolley cars with automobile buses, and bus lines ply back and forth between almost all the cities and large towns in the country. 

Naturally the growth of the automobile industry has had a pronounced effect on older methods of transportation. The horse and wagon have almost completely disappeared from our roads except in remote districts. Electric trolley lines, once prosperous, no longer pay; many have become bankrupt and have been abandoned.

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 495 

The railways have found their passenger business very much diminished, and their freight business has not increased as it might have been expected to do. There will always be need for the railways, however; though some of you who read this book may see the time when all will be using electric locomotives instead of the steam engines you are familiar with today.

Man Takes to the Air 

One other means of transportation, more wonderful than any of those we have spoken of, was developed during these busy years of the twentieth century 

the navigation of the air. That, too, was made possible by the invention of the gasoline or internal combustion engine, for the keeping of a plane afloat in the air depends on the speed with which it can be driven, and the lightness of the engine which drives it forward. 

Americans have been conspicuous in working out the principles of flying and in the practical application of them to successful flying machines. Professor Samuel P. Langley, a scientist connected with the Smithsonian Institution at Washington, contributed as much as any one to the world's knowledge of the natural laws that must be observed in the construction of the airplane. In 1903, two other Americans, Wilbur and Orville Wright, were the first to build a plane that would actually fly.

ONE OF THE EARLIEST AIRPLANES (1910) 

The span of the wings was 42 feet. This machine developed a speed of 40 miles an hour, and was driven by engines of 27 horse-power. The weight, loaded, was about 1100 pounds.

The advance in the science and the art of aviation has

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 

been marvelous. The Atlantic Ocean has been crossed again and again; the most remarkable flight, perhaps, was that of Charles A. Lindbergh who flew alone from New 

York to Paris in 1927. Airplanes were used with brilliant success during the war. After peace came, they were still further impro- ved until regular flights on schedule are now made every day between the principal cities in the United

N-X-211 RYAN NVP Louis 

States and in Europe. 

That there is a great future

LINDBERGH AND HIS PLANE 

both for the airplane and the great airship or dirigible balloon is now admitted even by those who began by saying that flying would never be anything but a dangerous adventure.

The time may come when people will travel by airplane in as great numbers as they now travel by rail or automobile. 

Growth of American Trade 

The export and import trade of the United States, which is carried chiefly by water, of course, has expanded marvelously since 1900. In that year our exports amounted to $1,394,483,000, and our imports to about $850,000,000. 

By 1920 the exports had increased to considerably more than $8,000,000,000, and the imports to more than $5,000,- 000,000. This great growth in trade was owing in part to

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 497 

our constantly increasing business with our new possessions - Porto Rico, the Philippines, Hawaii — and partly to the development of our manufactures for which new markets were found in all parts of the world. The war of 1914-18 had its part in building up our trade also. During those years the fighting nations - especially Great Britain, France, and Italy — depended on us for great quantities of grain and other foodstuffs, and for guns, ammunition, and war supplies generally, which they were not themselves able to produce in sufficient quantities. 

The trade of these countries was also much interrupted by the war, and our merchants were able to sell a great many things to the nations of Europe and South America which they had previously bought from England or Germany. Part of those new markets we were able to hold even after the war was over; but our foreign trade is not now as great as it was in those days. Instead of the $8,000,000,000 of exports in 1920, we now export something more than $5,000,000,000. Our imports annually amount to a little more than $4,000,000,000. 

Shipping in the World War

The World War also caused a remarkable increase in American shipping. The two greatest maritime nations of the world, Great Britain and Germany, were at war. 

German shipping was driven off the ocean and Great

Britain had great difficulty in keeping enough merchant ships afloat to serve its own needs, to say nothing of the rest of the world. The United States was quick to seize the opportunity. Private citizens built a great many ships, and after we ourselves entered the war, the Government built even more through the Emergency Fleet Corporation, organized for that purpose. During the nineteen months of our participation in the war, 875 vessels were launched

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 

with a tonnage of almost 3,000,000. By 1920 American ships measuring nearly ten million in tonnage were afloat in the foreign trade and ships of a tonnage of 6,357,700 were engaged in our own coastwise trade. 

A Surplus of Ships 

After the war was over, however, we found it impossible to find use for all these ships. We were no longer sending such quantities of food, ammunition, and supplies across the water; and the European countries again became our competitors for ocean-borne traffic. A great many of the vessels, built so hastily to meet an emergency, had to be tied up to the wharves to rust away; some of them were dismantled and burned.

The Government was left with many ships on its hands that it could not sell, because private shipowners did not find the shipping business profitable any longer. For several years the Government operated the largest and most important steamships it owned, including three or four fine German ships which it had seized when we went to war with Germany, and these ships were consequently held up in our harbors. The Shipping Board was the branch of the 

Government that had charge of this business; but gradually this board has managed to sell off most of these ships to private owners, and it is now nearly out of the business of sailing ships about the world.

Our Present Merchant Marine 

It is more expensive for Americans to build and sail ships than for Europeans to do so, owing to the higher cost of labor here, and the higher standards of pay and treatment which our laws assure to our sailors. So shipowning does not attract American business men in ordinary times. For

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 499 

many years our Government would not consent to help shipowners with subsidies, as many of the European nations do; but since the war the Jones-White Merchant Marine 

Act has been passed. That act did something in the way of subsidies, providing for liberal mail contracts to American-built ships, exempting steamship companies from 

THE LEVIATHAN 

This great steamship measures 950 feet in length, and 100 feet in breadth. Her tonnage is 

64,100. Her engines develop 82,200 horse-power and drive her at a speed of 24 knots. Her crew number 1100, and in addition she accommodates over 3700 passengers. Camera Corporation; reproduced by courtesy of United States Shipping Board.) certain taxes imposed on other businesses, and permitting government loans to help in the construction of larger and swifter liners. The act has brought some encouragement to our merchant marine, and a number of very fine ships are now afloat in the foreign trade under the American flag. 

Until conditions change still more, however, we are not likely to challenge England or even Germany in the carrying trade of the world.

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS The Public Health Service 

The United States has faced a number of serious social problems during the years since 1920, with varying degrees of success. In the matter of public health and sanitation, as we have already read, the advance has been great. We have learned how to deal with many infectious diseasestuberculosis, diphtheria, and typhoid fever in particular and we have found out how to prevent much of the sickness and consequent loss of life among babies. We have the finest water supplies and the most complete sewerage systems in the world. There are boards of health in every city and most towns to see that people obey the regulations that are meant to prevent or control sickness. Everywhere hospitals have arisen where those who cannot pay may have proper care as well as those who can pay. 

We have succeeded so well that the death rate of our population has been greatly reduced. It is probably not over half as great as it was a century ago, and two thirds of what it was fifty years ago.

Care of Criminals and Defectives 

There has been great improvement, too, in the care of criminals and of people who are insane or not quite normal mentally. There are still some old, gloomy, unsanitary prisons in use, but the newer ones are well planned and well lighted. In many of them the prisoners are given work to keep them occupied and consequently more cheerful, and there is a distinct tendency toward a more humane and intelligent treatment of convicts.

We owe at least some of the recent improvement in prison conditions to Thomas Mott Osborne, a citizen of

New York, who gave years to a study of the prison systems of the country. He introduced into the prison at Auburn a kind of discipline that aimed, not at making the punishment

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 501 

of convicts as hard and cruel as possible, but at encouraging them to reform and become orderly members of society. 

Men who had no trades were taught them, and those who had, were permitted so far as possible to work at them while in confinement. Osborne's ideas have not been accepted everywhere; but they have had a wide influence in making the treatment of convicts less unfeeling than it used to be. 

Schools for the deaf-and-dumb, for the blind, and for children who are below the average mentally have been established in different parts of the country, and many a child, who under the old conditions would have grown up helpless, useless, and a charge on the charity of the community in which he lived, has been taught enough to make him able to do some kind of honest work and to support himself. Dr. Samuel G. Howe was the leader in educating the blind. His school, the Perkins Institution for the

Blind, in Massachusetts, was established in 1833, and it has served as a model for work with children afflicted in this way all over the world. 

Temperance Reform in America 

The United States may fairly claim to have taken the lead in temperance reform, for no other nation has tried so hard to control the habit of drink and to put a stop to the traffic in liquor. The temperance movement began in this country about one hundred years ago. At that time drinking was almost universal and the effects of intemperance in pauperism and crime were plainly to be seen.

The temperance movement began with small local societies the members of which took a pledge never to use strong drink; but it spread with great rapidity over the country. It was aided by traveling speakers, many of them men who had themselves been drinking men and had experienced all the unhappy results of intemperance. The

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 

most famous of these men was John B. Gough, an earnest and eloquent man whose temperance lectures converted thousands to abstinence.

In later years the women of the country organized the 

Woman's Christian Temperance Union, under the leadership of Miss Frances E. Willard. This association is one of the most determined and effective foes that the liquor traffic ever had to deal with. The influence of this organization in keeping the ideal of total abstinence from liquor before the eyes of the Nation has been very great. 

The Birth of Prohibition

At first the temperance societies devoted themselves to reforming only individual drinkers; but before long they began to work for the passage of laws making the manufacture of liquor illegal. Maine was the first State to pass a prohibitory law. That was in 1852, and it was brought about largely through the energy and enthusiasm of Neal Dow, one of the early leaders in the cause of temperance. 

Gradually other States followed Maine. Some of them, however, finding that prohibition did not work smoothly, repealed their laws; others, especially in the West and 

South, kept them. Even in States where prohibition was unpopular, laws were usually made which permitted towns or cities to forbid the sale of liquor within their boundaries. 

The Eighteenth Amendment

By 1913, about thirty States had laws restricting the sale of liquor; many of these absolutely prohibited it. 

During the World War the temperance movement gained ground fast, and Congress passed a law forbidding the sale of liquor during war-time. The fighting was over before this law went into effect, but in the meantime Congress had voted to submit a constitutional amendment to the States

503 prohibiting the manufacture and sale of liquor anywhere in the United States.

The response of the States was unexpectedly prompt and decisive. Many people thought the proposal a little premature; but apparently the Nation was in the mood to try to settle the question once for all. One by one the state legislatures ratified the Amendment until forty-six out of forty-eight had acted. On January 6, 1920, the Eighteenth 

Amendment was declared adopted, and liquor was outlawed in the United States. It has not disappeared, however, and the wisdom and efficacy of the law is still a matter of dispute.

Workmen's Compensation Laws 

Although we led the world in temperance reform, we were slow to accept the principle that accidents to workingmen in industrial occupations ought not to be a burden on the victim alone. The old common law took the ground that the workingman took the risk of accident when he accepted employment, and that he was probably injured through his own carelessness or that of a fellow workman. This was so often obviously unfair and unjust that other countries England and Germany passed laws as early as 1884 and 1897, making the industry itself, through the employer, bear part, at least, of the financial burden. 

The first of these workingmen's compensation laws, as they are called, to be passed in this country was that of Maryland in 1902. Other States have followed - for these laws are state, not national, laws — until almost every State in the Union has one. They provide that the employer shall make specified payments to the victim of accident in case of injury, permanent disability, or death. Most employers meet this expense through insuring themselves against such demands in a state or a private insurance

FINANCIAL, POLITICAL, SOCIAL REFORMS 

company. It is of course now to the advantage of the employer as well as the workman to avoid accidents, and the compensation laws have had a very marked effect in reducing the number of such accidents.

UNIT XXIV  

HE peaceful development of the United States, to TH which almost everybody believed the twentieth century was to be dedicated, was suddenly interrupted, in 1914, by the outbreak of the most terrible war the world has ever seen. In this war almost the whole of Europe was involved, together with the possessions of European nations in other continents, and into it the United States itself was finally and unwillingly drawn. 

Underlying Causes of the War 

The causes of this bloody conflict were many. They can almost all be reduced to the historic jealousies and animosities of the Teuton and the Slav in eastern Europe and of France and Germany in western Europe; and to the rivalries of the chief European powers in the struggle for industrial and commercial wealth and for rich and profitable possessions in Asia and Africa.

Austria, with the help and encouragement of Germany, was trying to extend her rule over some of the smaller and unruly Balkan countries to the south. Russia, the great 

Slav empire, was inclined to take the part of these little States against Germany and Austria. Both Russia and 

Germany had their eyes on Constantinople. France, smarting under her defeat by Germany in 1870, constantly nursed the idea of recovering the provinces of Alsace and Lorraine which had been taken from her. She was in alliance with Russia, because both nations feared the power of Germany.

Great Britain and Germany were also rivals for the trade of the world, and Germany, by her splendid fleet of merchant vessels and warships, began to threaten Britain's mastery of the sea both in peace and war. Finally Germany, grown strong and prosperous and ambitious under the rule of William II, felt that she had not her share of rich colonies in other parts of the world, and was frankly jealous of the great possessions of France and Great Britain. 

How the War Started 

All this tangle of hostilities and ambitions was rendered more dangerous because every important nation had a large and well-trained army at hand ready to fight at a moment's notice. For years Europe had been an armed camp, or, to use another figure, a pile of inflammable tinder, only waiting for a match to set it off. More than once between 1900 and 

1914 war was averted with the greatest difficulty, simply because the European statesmen did not dare to begin what they felt would be a terrible conflict, the result of which no one could predict. 

The match was finally tossed into the tinder on June 28, 1914, when a young Serbian shot and killed Archduke

Franz Ferdinand, heir to the throne of Austria, whom he considered an enemy of the Slav peoples. The Austrians suspected the Serbian government of knowing more than it should about the assassination of Franz Ferdinand, and the 

Austrian government, backed by the German Emperor, demanded that Serbia should take certain steps that would amount to a confession of its responsibility for the murder. 

When Serbia hesitated to do so, an Austrian army set out for the frontier. It seemed as if Austria meant to conquer and annex Serbia, as it had certain other smaller Slav States.

Now, however, Russia stepped in, told Austria to halt,

and began to mobilize her army to protect Serbia. Germany lost no time in ordering out her own great army and notifying Russia that she meant to support Austria. 

France, as the ally of Russia, called out all her troops, and threatened to attack Germany if Germany attacked Russia.

Courtesy of U.S. War Department DUGOUTS BEHIND THE FIGHTING LINES IN FRANCE 

For a week the issue was in doubt and desperate attempts were made by the British Government to find some way of settling the sudden quarrel without actual fighting. No compromise could be agreed upon, however, and by the first of August four of the five greatest European powers were at war, Germany and Austria against France and Russia. 

For a few days Great Britain hesitated; but when a German army, bent on invading France, started to march across Belgium, a little country whose neutrality in time of war had been guaranteed by Germany herself as well as by France and Great Britain, the question settled itself. 

Great Britain declared war on Germany.

Germany Invades France: Battle of the Marne 

We shall not attempt to describe in detail the events of the first two years and a half of this dreadful war. The German army swung across Belgium and France, and almost reached Paris; but it was turned back at the Marne by the French and British, fighting together. Then it dug itself into a line of trenches that stretched all the way across France from the sea to the Vosges Mountains. There it stayed for four years. The French and British could not drive the Germans out and the Germans could not break the line of their enemies, who were also in trenches of their own. were a million or more men in each line of trenches. were scores of bloody battles fought each year, and a constant cannonading day in and day out all that long time. 

The loss of life was terrific. There There

On the Eastern Front, where Russians and Serbians were fighting Austrians and Germans, the armies moved about more; they were not tied to their trenches as they were in 

France. A Russian army invaded Germany; it was driven out again and the fighting was transferred to the Polish provinces of Russia. Then an Austrian army invaded 

Serbia, but was driven back; a Russian army invaded 

Austria, but was driven back. In Poland Germans and 

Russians killed one another, but for a long time without decisive results.

The War at Sea 

At sea the British navy proved strong enough to keep the German navy shut up in its seaports. Once it ventured out and there was a great naval battle off the coast of Jutland. 

The British ships drove the Germans back to cover, but did not destroy them as they had hoped to do. Meanwhile, German submarines were getting out into the open sea and sinking many of the merchant ships on which Great Britain

depended for food. The ingenuity of the British navy was severely taxed to find a way of catching and sinking these dangerous little underwater fighters. 

Courtesy of U.S. Navy Department SMOKE SCREEN LAID BY DESTROYERS 

This proved a most effective means of concealing a convoy of transports from enemy vessels Meanwhile, Turkey and Bulgaria came in on the side of Germany, mainly because they did not like the Slav peoples and thought the fine German army would win anyway. 

Italy and Roumania joined the Allies (France, Great Britain, and Russia). There was bloody fighting at Gallipoli, near

Constantinople, which a British and French army tried in vain to reach. There was fighting in Syria, among the 

Austrian Alps near the Italian border, and all over the 

Balkan Peninsula. All Europe seemed to have gone warcrazy.

The United States Remains Neutral 

During these years of 1914, 1915, and 1916 the United States very carefully preserved its own neutrality. There seemed at first to be no possible reason for us to get mixed

American up in this suicidal struggle across the ocean. sympathy seemed to be largely with Great Britain and France. They were democratic countries, with governments and political ideals much like our own, while Germany and Austria were ruled by emperors who were felt to be wholly out of sympathy with the principles of democracy and popular government.

Moreover, the violation of the neutrality of Belgium by Germany had made a bad impression, and the submarine warfare of Germany aroused indignation because it meant the death of many men and women who were not soldiers or combatants at all. When the great British steamship 

Lusitania was torpedoed by a German submarine in the Irish Channel in 1915, almost all of its passengers were drowned, and a great many of them were Americans. That aroused even greater indignation in the United States. Ex- 

President Roosevelt said boldly that we ought to go to war with Germany on that account.

There were also many sympathizers with Germany in the country, for there are millions of Americans of German ancestry. Most of them instinctively took the part of the land where they or their parents had been born. 

Britain and Germany Interfere with our Ships

President Wilson was not swayed by the arguments of either party. He preserved our neutrality with the utmost care just as long as he possibly could. His reëlection to the 

Presidency in 1916, in the midst of the discussion, seemed to show that the majority of the people approved of his course. 

It was the German submarines that finally drove us into the war. From the beginning of the war both Great Britain and Germany had interfered with our commerce. The

President was kept busy for two years writing notes of indignant protest first to one Government and then to the

other. The British had blockaded the German coast in order to keep food and other supplies from reaching the Germans. Their navy permitted neutral vessels to go to 

Holland or Norway or Sweden through a narrow lane of water kept open in the English Channel and the North Sea; but if the commanders who patrolled this route thought a cargo was meant for future export to Germany, they stopped the neutral ship and prevented it from going any 

​A SERVICE FLAG 

Each star represented a member of the family in army service farther. The United States had done the same thing in the Civil War with ships carrying goods thought to be destined for the Confederacy by way of Mexico. 

Germany, on the other hand, set explosive mines adrift in the North Sea and in the Channel, and warned all nations that all the waters around Great Britain constituted a "war zone," and that her submarines would make it very dangerous for neutral vessels to enter these waters. According to all existing international law neither nation had the right to carry the blockading of the enemy's coast so far; but they continued to do so in spite of all the arguments of President Wilson.

The British only stopped our ships from going where they wanted to; they did not kill any Americans. When the submarines of Germany sunk a British passenger ship like the Lusitania, however, there were often Americans aboard who were drowned; for of course the little submarines could not rescue many of the victims of their torpedoes, even if they had wanted to. When early in 1916 they sunk the steamship Sussex, with the loss of several American lives, the President threatened to break off all relations with Germany, but the German Government promised not to sink any more passenger ships without giving them warning.

German Spies in the United States The United States had another cause for complaint when it was discovered that German agents in this country were busy plotting to encourage strikes in mines and in mills and factories that were making military supplies for the Allies; for during these years France and Great Britain were buying a great many things from us, as they had a perfect right to do. Men were caught putting bombs in the holds of vessels bound for France or England, and there were mysterious explosions in ammunition factories that killed a number of people. There was evidence that some of the diplomatic officers in the German embassy at Washington were directing these plots and President Wilson insisted that these men be recalled.

The Zimmermann Note 

Early in 1917 a message from Herr Zimmermann, the German Foreign Secretary, to the German Minister to Mexico was intercepted and made public. It instructed him to try to persuade Mexico to declare war against the United States, and promised that if Mexico would do so Germany would help Mexico get back Texas, New Mexico, and Arizona. This was an absurd suggestion to make. The 

Mexicans were too wise to try anything of the sort, but the publication of the message stirred up new hostility to 

Germany in the United States.

The activity of some American citizens of German birth did not help their own cause as they hoped. They argued that Americans ought to stop doing business with the Allied nations because the British blockade had prevented us from trading directly with Germany. They defended and sometimes assisted the agents who were trying to destroy American factories and to cause dissension between American employers and workingmen. Their arguments did not

convince many Americans, and their conduct exasperated rather than frightened the people of the United States. 

Germany Looses its Submarines

Early in 1917 our relations with Germany suddenly became more difficult than ever. It began to look as if the 

Central Powers, Germany and Austria, were going to win the war. Russia, badly beaten, lost heart and courage; she was on the verge of the great Revolution that overthrew the old empire of the Czars. Austria had overrun Serbia and was about to invade Italy. A German army had occupied 

Roumania and crushed all resistance. The trench lines still held in the West, but both France and Great Britain were finding the burden of war discouraging. If the German armies in the East were brought into France, could they not decide the issue?

The Germans thought they could. In December, 1916, they had proposed peace on terms the Allies would not accept, and President Wilson had, at the same time, made vain proposals for a conference to negotiate a "peace without victory" for either side. Now the German Government decided to wage war "ruthlessly." It withdrew the pledge it had given after the sinking of the Sussex. Henceforth, it announced, German submarines would sink any enemy vessels they met; persons who took passage on them must take their chance of drowning. One American ship a week might pass through the English Channel if it was painted in gaudy stripes so that it could be recognized. 

The United States Declares War on Germany 

President Wilson had warned Germany when the Sussex was sunk that he would break off all relations if unrestricted submarine warfare was permitted. When the German announcement was made, he promptly dismissed the Ger-

man Ambassador at Washington and recalled our Ambassador at Berlin. Between February 3, when this happened, and April 2, when Congress met, eight American ships had been sunk by submarines, with considerable loss of life. 

Nearly 1,000,000 tons of shipping were sunk in these two months. In April alone, ships-mostly British-with a tonnage of 800,000 were destroyed and many persons drowned.

This merciless warfare against merchant vessels turned the public opinion of the United States finally against Germany. Congress, on the advice of the President, declared war against the Central Powers on April 6, and the country ac- with the exception of a minority composed of pacifists and men and women of strong German sympathies cepted the decision with enthusiasm. It was felt that at bottom it was a struggle of democracy against autocracy; that the victory of Germany would be a blow at popular government from which it might never recover; and that the decision of William II and his military advisers to disregard not only international law but common humanity in their submarine campaign, made it impossible for the United States longer to remain neutral in the great struggle. 

We are Unprepared, Except for Our Navy

Our country was, however, in no condition of preparedness to enter on so terrible a war. The navy, to be sure, was fit and efficient; but we had only a few thousand soldiers in our regular army, and not many of our citizens had received any military training whatever. All naval vessels that were not needed to patrol our coasts and convoy our ships on their way across the ocean were at once dispatched to European waters. Under the command of Admiral 

Sims they did excellent service in the campaign against the submarines. They had no opportunity to take part in a

naval engagement, for the German fleet remained hidden behind the guns of its fortified harbors; but they were very useful in putting an end to the menace of the submarines. 

The Germans had expected to sink so many British and American ships that Great Britain could not get the food she needed to keep her people alive, 

nor would the United States be able to send soldiers across the Atlantic after they were trained. As it happened, the Allied navies worked out means of detecting and sinking the submarines so that each month the number of ships sunk by the Germans grew smaller. April, 1917, saw the peak of the German submarine campaign.

Copyright, by Harris and Ewing By the time the American troops were ready for service, the threat of defeat by means of the submarine was over. 

Only two American transports were torpedoed; two million men crossed the sea to the battle front with only three hundred casualties due to the submarines.

ADMIRAL SIMS 

We Build Ships as Fast as the Germans Sink them

For several months, however, during 1917, there was great anxiety. If the Germans could have continued to destroy 800,000 tons of shipping every month, they would certainly have won the war. Accordingly the Government and people of the United States set themselves to the task of building ships faster than the Germans could sink them.

The navy built hundreds of destroyers and "submarine chasers❞— small, swift boats that it was hoped could elude the torpedoes, and then drop bombs on the submerged submarine.

Other hundreds of ships were hurried off the stocks to

serve as cargo carriers to replace those vessels that had been sent to the bottom. Every shipyard along the coast hummed day and night with activity. Some of the old yards in New England, where wooden ships had been built years before, were reopened, and more wooden ships were created with astonishing speed. The Government, regardless of expense, built two or three immense plants where iron ships, all turned out according to the same model, were launched at the rate of almost one every day. Some of these ships were of great value to the Allies, hard pressed for tonnage; but many were not finished until the need for them was past.

Raising and Training Four Million Men 

The At first it seemed an almost hopeless task to raise and transport an army equal to the demands of modern warfare in time for it to be of service on the Western Front. 

German military men were sure it was quite hopeless, but President Wilson and his advisers tackled the problem with energy and determination. A law was hurried through 

Congress drafting every man between twenty-one and thirty-one years of age. There were exemptions made, of course, because of physical disability or because some young men were found to be essential to agricultural or industrial work at home; but about four million men were thus selected for military service.

For the training of these men as officers and soldiers, immense camps sprang up almost overnight in different parts of the country. 

As fast as the men in these camps had acquired the rudiments of military discipline and marksmanship they were organized into regiments and brigades, given a few months of drill and instruction in trench fighting, and hurried over to France, while fresh relays of drafted men were sent to camp to take their places. In France they

Photograph by courtesy of the War Department

AN AMERICAN AMBULANCE TRAIN CAMOUFLAGED AGAINST AIR ATTACK 

had more instruction before they were sent to the front. 

By June, 1918, just a year from the time the first draft was made, there were 700,000 American soldiers in France and 2,000,000 more nearly ready to cross the ocean. The speed with which this immense army was raised, trained, and transported across the water surprised European military men, both friends and foes. The question was, Would it stand fire and prove effective on the battle front? 

The world had not long to wait for an answer. 

How we Raised $22,000,000,000

As preparation for actual fighting meant also the manufacture of cannon, rifles, ammunition, and airplanes, those industries were speeded up by government order without any question of the cost. Extravagance was necessary, for

the United States had within a year to raise and equip an army equal to those the other nations had spent years in creating. 

The work cost sums that seemed at the time incredible; they amounted to no less than $22,000,000,000, all raised and spent within two years. Our Government also made loans to our Allies, France, Great Britain, Italy, and Belgium, amounting to some $10,000,000,000 more, and it had to raise that money also from its own citizens. 

To get together this vast sum of money, the Government began by increasing the tariff rates and the income and excise taxes, and it also laid many new taxes, such as one on inheritances, and others on deeds and similar legal documents and on various "luxuries" such as theater tickets, toilet articles, and even membership dues in clubs. 

Taxation, however, heavy as it was, supplied only a part of the money required. More money was raised by the issuance of government bonds (called Liberty Loans) sold directly to the people. The remarkable wealth of the Nation and the loyalty with which it supported the Government in the war were shown by the ease with which these loans were subscribed and even oversubscribed. Rich people, banks, and financial and industrial concerns took great blocks of the bonds; and all the way down through the mass of the people, each one did his share. 

Millions of poor, hard-working folk who could spare only a few dollars bought the stamps that the Government issued for five and ten dollars. These stamps were redeemable just as the bonds were. Communities competed with one another to see which could offer the largest amount of money to the Nation. Never was a war financed so lavishly and yet so easily from the pockets of the people. It was a remarkable proof of the strength and unity of a democratic people, once they are aroused by a crisis in their national life.

American Troops Meet the Enemy 

Supported thus generously by their fellow citizens at home, the new American army faced the enemy at last. 

A few battalions of our regular army had appeared in France in June, 1917, but it was a year later before we were able to throw our real strength into the fight. As early as March, 1918, some American divisions were ready. They were assigned to service with French or British armies who sadly needed reënforcements, but who found the man-power of their own nations very nearly exhausted. The American soldiers showed dash and courage. They were good fighters.

The Allied generals began to think that the fine qualities of these eager young men would make up for their brief and hurried training; and they were right. 

Requests came from all along the front for American divisions to help in holding the line. General Pershing, who had been selected by the President to command our forces in France, insisted, however, on keeping the bulk of our troops together as a distinct group of American armies. 

Marshal Foch, the great French general who was at this time in supreme command of all the Allied troops in France, agreed to this plan and assigned to General Pershing the eastern end of the long line of battle, from below Verdun nearly to Rheims.

Château-Thierry and Belleau Wood 

Late in May, 1918, the German commander, General

Ludendorff, ordered an attack on the line between Rheims and Soissons. The French gave way before it on a front of thirty miles; the Germans pushed on to the Marne River, only forty miles from Paris. In this emergency fresh American troops, the Third Division and a brigade of marines, were hurried forward. They met the advancing Germans at Château-Thierry and in a hot engagement stopped them

and turned them back. Then the Second Division came up to their support and the Germans were driven out of Belleau Wood to the north of Château-Thierry and Ludendorff's drive was halted once for all.

3-33-35-78-80-91) It was the turning-point of the war. A month later, the 

Germans advanced again, but this time they gained no ground at all. Eight American divisions faced them, and after a bloody battle drove them back in full retreat.

Foch seized the advantage. He had hundreds of thousands of fresh, eager American soldiers to rely on for reënforcements and he struck hard at the German line. It began to give way everywhere. The tired German soldiers, knowing at last that America was in the war, with perhaps millions of fighting men, lost courage. Their officers began to realize that they could not, with their war-worn troops, hold back this new enemy.

Taking the Salient at Saint-Mihiel 

General Pershing sent his newly organized First Field

Army against the projecting salient or bulge in the trench line at Saint-Mihiel. The Germans had held this salient for four years; but in three days the First Army had driven them out, taking 12,000 prisoners in the process.

Allied victories now occurred on every hand. The 

British on the left of the line, and the French in the center, broke through the famous Hindenburg Line of trench fortifications and sent the Germans reeling back. Allenby in Palestine wiped out the Turkish army that faced him. 

The Allied army in the Balkans beat the Bulgarians and pushed forward to invade that country. The Italians drove the Austrian army out of Italy in hopeless rout. 

The Battle of the Meuse-Argonne 

Meanwhile our own American armies were advancing steadily in their part of the vast battlefield. It was their duty to drive the Germans out of the forest of the Argonne, west of Verdun, and up the Meuse River. They performed this duty admirably. The fighting was continuous through the month of October; it was one unending battle with fresh troops thrown in as division after division had to be withdrawn exhausted and decimated by the fighting. We call it the battle of the Meuse-Argonne a battle lasting forty

days. The Americans were victorious. They drove the

Germans slowly but steadily backward, until early in November they took Sedan, where the French had been crushed by the Germans in the war of 1870. More than 

30,000 Americans were killed in this tremendous battle. 

Germany Yields

The war was now virtually over. The Allied armies farther west had driven the dismayed Germans quite out of 

France. Ludendorff saw the hopelessness of the situation.

Behind him Germany was in revolt, tired of the war, certain now of defeat. The Emperor, William II, abandoned his retreating army and took refuge in Holland. Revolution was proclaimed in Berlin. William II abdicated his throne, and Ludendorff begged Foch for an armistice- to prevent the invasion of Germany by the victorious enemy. The armistice was granted and signed on November II. was no more fighting after that.

The Peace of Versailles 

There 

The peace conference met at Versailles near Paris. President Wilson took the unusual step of attending it in person. 

He meant to use the opportunity to create a League of Nations, a kind of world parliament which should settle all questions between nation and nation and make war impossible; and he dared trust no other negotiators to carry out his purpose. He was, therefore, absent from the United 

States for several months, a step no other President had ever dreamed of taking.

The peace congress did not succeed in agreeing on the final terms until June 28, 1919, exactly five years after the day of the Archduke Franz Ferdinand's murder. The 

French Premier, Clemenceau, and the British Premier,

Lloyd George, did not at first approve of the League of Na-

tions, while President Wilson did not approve of some of their plans for punishing and weakening Germany. In the end the President had his way about the League; its organization was made part of the Peace of Versailles; but he had to give way on other points. 

Courtesy of the War Department HALL OF MIRRORS, VERSAILLES 

Here the Treaty of Peace was signed The treaty restored Alsace and Lorraine to France, gave the German colonies in Africa to Great Britain, and took Syria, Mesopotamia, and Palestine from the broken-down Turkish Empire, the first to be controlled by France and the other two by Great Britain. Austria was broken up. Bohemia (henceforth to be called Czecho-Slovakia) and Hungary were made independent states and the southern provinces were joined to Serbia to form the Jugo-Slavian kingdom. Poland appeared once more on the map as an independent nation and so did Finland and two or three of the

provinces of Russia along the coast of the Baltic Sea. With most of these changes in territory Wilson heartily agreed, for he believed in the "self-determination of peoples," which means the political independence of peoples, however small, who have the same language and religion and who are unwilling to form part of a different and more powerful nation. 

Americans on the Rhine

The treaty also obliged Germany to pay immense sums to France and Belgium as reparation for all the towns that had been destroyed and all the land laid waste during the war. 

She had finally to surrender her navy to be scrapped or sunk, to agree to disband her army and not undertake the training of another, and to permit Allied troops to occupy her strong places along the Rhine as pledges for carrying out the terms of the treaty. Hard terms they were, and Germany has never been reconciled to them; but it was beyond her power to resist and the government of the new German Republic signed the treaty reluctantly. 

Part of the line of the Rhine was assigned to American troops to hold and for several years American soldiers occupied the fortress of Coblenz and the country round it. The rest of the great army of 4,000,000 men was demobilized as rapidly as possible.

The Nation Shows Its Patriotism 

We should also remember the whole-hearted patriotism with which the Nation at home supported the armies at the Front while the war lasted. Not only did they subscribe more than $14,000,000,000 to the Liberty Loans, as we have already learned, but they submitted to a very thorough control by the Government of their food and fuel. That was done in order to make sure that there should be enough of everything for the soldiers in France and in the camps.

Herbert Hoover was the capable food administrator.

Under his direction larger food crops were planted and raised, the distribution of food was organized and controlled, and millions of people cultivated gardens in their back yards and bought only such quantities and kinds of food as he advised. It was a striking illustration of the power of public sentiment in a democracy, for almost every one denied himself things he wanted or did work to which he was not accustomed, because it would have seemed unpatriotic not to do so. 

Underwood and Underwood

HERBERT HOOVER' Relief Work at the Front 

The people also contributed generously to the work of the humanitarian agencies at the front and in the camps, and made it possible to serve the American army in this way as no other army was ever served. The 

Red Cross took the lead, of course, but religious associations- the Salvation Army, the Young Men's Christian Association, the Catholic and Jewish societies — all had their representatives with the soldiers, furnishing entertainment and such little luxuries as they could offer to the men in camp or trench, and giving counsel and comfort to the wounded or the dying.

The Government did its share of welfare work among the men, and it also organized an elaborate system of war-time insurance, so that the soldiers, by contributing a small part I Mr. Hoover was born in Iowa, in 1875. He graduated at Leland 

Stanford University in 1896 and became a successful mining engineer, practicing his profession in America, China, and South Africa. During the World War he was Director of War Relief in Belgium and of the Food 

Administration in the United States. From 1921 to 1929, he was Secretary of Commerce. He was inaugurated as President, March 4, 1929.

of their pay, could go into battle knowing that if they were disabled or killed, their families at home would not be left even temporarily in want.

We Reject President Wilson's League of Nations 

President Wilson returned home from Versailles to find that the treaty he brought with him was not popular. The 

League of Nations which he had succeeded in working into the treaty seemed to have more enemies than friends in the United States. The Republican leaders almost all opposed it, partly, of course, because it was proposed by a Democratic President, and they carried the great majority of their party with them. A great many Democrats also were lukewarm or hostile to it. Americans had been taught from childhood that it was dangerous for this country to have too close relations with European powers. 

The words of Washington, Jefferson, and Monroe were often quoted. The League seemed to most Americans to diminish our independence and make our national policies to some degree subject to the advice and consent of Great Britain or of France or of some other nation of Europe.

There was a long and heated debate in the Senate. Some senators were furiously hostile to the treaty. Others would have voted for it if the President had consented to certain reservations or conditions with regard to our membership in the League; but Mr.. Wilson would make no compromise. 

He insisted on the treaty as it stood, League and all. Angry at the opposition in the Senate, he started out on a tour of the country in order, through a series of speeches, to put his case before the people.

The strain of this tour was too great for him, enfeebled as he was by the exhausting labors of the war years. He broke down and was carried back to Washington an invalid, never

to regain perfect health. His enemies in the Senate proved too strong for him. When he refused finally to consider any of the proposed "reservations," enough senators refused to vote for the treaty to make the two-thirds vote necessary for ratification impossible. The United States remained technically at war with Germany until 1921, when a separate agreement restoring friendly relations between the two countries was signed. 

Three Republican Presidents

Meanwhile a presidential election had again occurred in 1920. The Democratic Party, depressed by the collapse of its leader in the White House, and divided on his policies, went down to defeat. Warren G. Harding, a Senator from Ohio, was elected President, only to die in office two years later and be succeeded by the Vice-President, Calvin Coolidge, former Governor of 

Massachusetts. In 1929, Coolidge was succeeded by Herbert Hoover, whose remarkable work as head of the Belgian relief organization and as Food Administrator during our participation in the war had been followed by eight years' service as Secretary of Commerce.¹ 

CALVIN COOLIDGE I 

I Calvin Coolidge was born in Vermont in 1872. He graduated at 

Amherst College, and became a lawyer. He was Mayor of Northampton, Massachusetts, and was often elected to the Massachusetts State legislature. In 1916 he became Lieutenant-Governor, and in 1918, Governor of Massachusetts. Elected Vice-President in 1920, he succeeded to the 

Presidency in 1923, upon the death of President Harding. He was elected 

President the following year, He now lives at Northampton, Massachusetts.

UNIT XXV 

AN N extraordinary thing in world politics during this twentieth century has been the persistence of efforts, again and again disappointed, to find some means of establishing and guaranteeing peace among the nations. The movement may be said to have begun long ago with the negotiation of treaties for the arbitration of disputes between different nations.

The United States and Arbitration 

The United States was among the first to resort to arbitration. The dispute with Great Britain over the Alabama claims was settled by arbitration; also a controversy with the same nation over the seal fisheries of Alaska in 1886. 

We helped to make arbitration compulsory among the nations of the Pan-American Union, and we obliged or per- suaded Great Britain to arbitrate its boundary dispute with Venezuela in 1895. These are only a few of the proofs we gave of our preference for friendly agreement or judicial settlement in place of force as a means of settling international differences. 

The Hague Conferences

In 1899 a conference of nations met at The Hague in Holland, at the invitation of the Czar of Russia, to try to establish some permanent tribunal to which nations could bring their quarrels for settlement. Our representatives took a leading part in drawing up the agreement that constituted such a tribunal, which was signed by almost all the civilized nations of the world.

The first really important case that was submitted to the Hague tribunal for decision - the dispute between Germany and Venezuela, in 1903 — was arbitrated as the result of President Roosevelt's influence with both nations. 

AT THE GRAVE OF THE UNKNOWN SOLDIER Arlington National Cemetery 

The second Hague conference, in 1907, also met in accordance with the suggestion of President Roosevelt. It was at this conference that the troublesome question of the limitation of armies and navies, which is still (1931) worrying the statesmen of the world, was first discussed. Little enough that was practical came of it at this time; but the 

American delegates were among the most sincere in endeavoring to reach some useful conclusion. 

How the League of Nations Works 

Until the League of Nations was established — at the suggestion of an American President - the United States

had always been foremost in the movement for peace among the nations. The country, however, has never been convinced that it should depart from its traditional policy of isolation from European governments. Although it is true that the League has not accomplished all that was hoped of it, it is a step in the direction of coöperation between the nations and it offers an opportunity for the statesmen of the world to meet at regular intervals to discuss the problems of international law and the best ways of avoiding such wars as that of which you have just read.

The League works through three bodies; the Secretariat made up of permanent officials who carry out the routine business of the League; the Assembly in which each of the fifty-five nations that are members is represented for the open discussion of international questions; and the Council, a smaller body which determines the questions on which the League is to act. The Council can decide nothing unless by unanimous vote - an obvious weakness, but one that cannot be avoided while nations remain as jealous of their own sovereignty and as suspicious of their neighbors as they still are. The Assembly meets annually at Geneva in Switzerland, the headquarters of the League; the Council meets more frequently whenever it appears to be necessary. 

The World Court 

The chief accomplishment of the League of Nations has been the establishment of the World Court, so called; a permanent body of eminent men always ready to serve in arbitrating any international disputes that are submitted to their judgment. A famous American, Elihu Root, had an important part in drawing up the rules that govern the court, and some well-known Americans - Chief Justice 

Hughes and Frank B. Kellogg, former Secretary of State have been among its judges. Nevertheless, the United

States has never agreed to submit all its disputes with other nations to the World Court. Three Presidents, Harding,

Coolidge, and Hoover, have recommended that it do so; but 

Congress has declined to join the Court except on terms of its own to which the other nations of the world would not consent. 

The Conference at Washington

Though we have remained outside the League and the World Court, we have not lost our interest in advocating and working for peace. In 1922 a conference on the limitation of naval armaments met in Washington, at President Harding's invitation. Japan, Great Britain, France, and 

Italy, the chief naval powers, were represented, and the conference succeeded in reaching an agreement on the number of warships each nation should build during the next ten years.

Another conference of the same sort was held in London in 1930. That settled little except that the United States should be entitled to have a navy equal in size to Great Britain's if it chose to build the necessary ships. 

The Washington Conference of 1922 also led to a Four- Power Treaty between France, Great Britain, Japan, and the United States, dealing with affairs in the Pacific and the Far East. These four nations agreed to respect one another's possessions in that part of the world; to submit all disputes between any of them to a conference of all four powers; and to stand together if the rights of any should be threatened by an outside nation. This treaty may prove to be of great service in preventing war in a part of the world where the chance of war might easily arise. 

The Kellogg Pact

One more step toward peace was taken in 1928 when

Secretary Kellogg, in consultation with M. Briand, the

French statesman, suggested to the nations of the world a pact or agreement by which all who signed it agreed to give up war "as an instrument of national policy." That means that no war would be begun to take territory from another nation, or to force any other nation to agree to do something it did not wish to do. A great many of the important nations have signed the Kellogg Pact. 

Pacific and Atlantic Photos KELLOGG TREATY OUTLAWING WAR SIGNED BY FIFTEEN NATIONS 

The Kellogg treaty outlawing war was signed in Paris at the Quai d'Orsay, with impressive ceremonies

Of course peace and good will are forwarded by many other means than treaties and congresses of statesmen. 

There are international associations of a dozen kindsfinancial, artistic, educational, scientific, religious, and social in which people from the different nations, who are interested in similar things, meet, exchange ideas, and become friends. The visit of a great scientist like Dr. Einstein

to the United States, to meet and compare notes with American learned men, is an influence toward world peace. So are the friendly receptions that the people of one nation give to the aviators of another when they fly safely across the sea. So are the arrangements by which the nations exchange postal services so that letters pass freely from one country to another; the agreement by which the navies cooperate to patrol the trans-Atlantic steamship lanes at the season when icebergs are a danger; the conferences that are always taking place between Americans and Europeans who are working on problems of public health, education, or scientific research.

All these things are a part of the slow but steady progress of the world toward peace and a good understanding between the nations; and in all of them the United States bears its part.

Our Duty to Our Country 

We have thus traced, rather hurriedly to be sure, the story of the United States from its small and weak beginnings to its present place among the strongest and richest nations of the world. We can be sure that the future will see our country becoming still stronger and more powerful. 

As its strength grows so will its responsibilities increase. It cannot help taking a larger and larger part in world affairs. 

It will also have to face domestic problems of the most serious character. Let us all determine to know as much as we can about these problems at home and abroad, so that when we come to be citizens of the great Republic, with votes that will have their part in choosing the men, who, as President or as members of Congress, are to deal with them, we shall use those votes intelligently and for the best interests of the Nation.

We are fortunate to have been born and to live in the

United States of America. 

great country, to guard its honor, and to serve it in whatever way we can. At the same time let us remember that our country has made mistakes in the past, and that it is our duty to profit by them, so that we may more successfully strive to improve the conditions of life both here at home and among the nations of the world. 

Let us never forget to love our
